Honor's Slayer

the whole thing, one page, no clicking

Disclaimer & Front Matter

Honor's Slayer
A continuation of Honor's Slayer by becuzitswrong

Arc One: Hades

Chapter 10: The dreams had started three days after the merging.

Buffy knew they were the Girl's memories and not her own because everything in them was wrong in small specific ways. The sky was the wrong shade. Fire was a miracle rather than a convenience. The men around the fire were short and dark and spoke in something that wasn't quite language, more like language's rough first draft, and Buffy understood every word of it the way you understand a dream, which is to say completely and then not at all the moment you wake up.

In the dreams she was the Girl. Not watching her. Being her.

She ran on bare feet across ground that had never seen a road and the running was joy, pure and uncomplicating, her body a perfect instrument tuned to exactly one purpose. The darkness around her breathed. She breathed back. The things that lived in the dark knew her smell and gave her a wide berth, not from weakness but from something older than fear, a recognition of hierarchy that didn't need to be argued or demonstrated. She was the thing that hunted the things that hunted men. The food chain had a roof and she was it.

She woke from these dreams feeling better than she had any right to.

That bothered her more than the dreams themselves.

"You're doing it again," Honor said.

Buffy looked up from where she'd been sitting cross-legged on the shuttle's deck, staring at nothing in particular. Honor stood in the hatchway with two mugs, Nimitz draped across her shoulders like an extremely opinionated scarf. The treecat's green eyes tracked to Buffy and his tail tip moved in what she'd come to understand was the equivalent of a nod.

"Doing what?"

"Going away somewhere behind your eyes." Honor crossed the space between them and sat, handing over one of the mugs. Hot chocolate. It had taken Scotty Tremaine approximately four hours after Buffy's return to full personhood to figure out that she shared Honor's feelings about coffee. He'd shown up with a small brick of compressed chocolate powder with the expression of a man presenting tribute to uncertain royalty, and Buffy had decided then that she liked him. "Fritz is calling it dissociative episodes. I'm calling it thinking very hard about something you don't want to talk about."

"Fritz needs a new hobby."

"Fritz's current hobby is writing seventeen pages of notes about your biology that he knows he can't show to anyone." Honor wrapped both hands around her mug. The morning outside the shuttle was the color of old bruises, the Hades jungle pressing against the canopy in every direction. Somewhere out in it something screamed, brief and final. Neither of them flinched. "He asked me to ask you whether the healing factor is always active or whether it requires some kind of trigger."

"Tell him it's always on." Buffy looked into her mug. "Like a pilot light. Most of the time you don't even know it's there."

"And when you're injured?"

"It turns into a bonfire." She paused. "That's not exactly right either. It's more like�" She stopped, frustrated. The vocabulary for this didn't exist in any language she'd grown up with and apparently hadn't been invented in the two thousand years since. "You know how your body normally handles damage? Like if you cut yourself, there's this whole process, clotting and scabbing and new skin, and it takes a specific amount of time and there are specific steps?"

"Yes."

"Mine skips the steps. Or does them all at once. I don't know which. I've never been able to watch it happening from the outside." She thought of Fritz's expression when he'd described watching her arm knit itself back together around the bones he was still trying to set. The memory made her a little queasy. "It costs something, though. Energy. That's why I eat so much. My body's running a repair shop twenty-four hours a day whether there's anything to repair or not, and when there's something big to fix it drafts every available calorie for the job."

"That explains the E-rats." Honor's voice was carefully neutral. The camp had gone through three weeks of rations in the first five days of Buffy's lucidity. Nobody had said anything directly to Buffy about it. She'd overheard Alistair McKeon doing some creative accounting with the supply inventory to paper over the gap, and had made a note to find some way to square that particular debt.

"Yeah." Buffy drank her chocolate. Outside the screaming had stopped. "Sorry about that."

"Don't be." Honor said it in the tone that meant the subject was settled rather than the tone that meant she was being polite about it, which Buffy was learning to distinguish. "If the caloric cost scales with the severity of the repair, then the transfusion you gave me cost you considerably more than a deciliter of blood."

Buffy hadn't thought about it in those terms. She turned the idea over. Fritz had cut off the transfusion when she lost consciousness, which meant the blood loss itself hadn't been what dropped her. Her body had been running the equivalent of a stadium's worth of repair work on Honor's arm and face and eye and then had simply run out of fuel mid-job and shut down non-essential systems, of which consciousness was apparently one.

"Huh," she said.

"Yes." Honor looked at her steadily. "So. Thank you."

The directness of it landed differently than Buffy expected. She'd been thanked before. Giles had thanked her with tea and careful understatement. Willow had thanked her with enthusiasm and hugs. Xander had thanked her by pretending she hadn't done anything particularly impressive and then going quietly and saying something in private that he'd never repeat in public. Each of those had felt like a language she was fluent in.

Honor's thank you was a different language. It had weight to it. It arrived like a handed object, something you were meant to hold onto.

"You're welcome," Buffy said, and found she meant it without qualification for maybe the first time in her life.

Nimitz made a small sound and Honor's expression shifted slightly in the way it did when the treecat was feeding her something through their link. Buffy watched the process with the same fascination she always felt. It was the closest thing to magic she'd found in this century, which was either deeply comforting or deeply ironic, she hadn't decided which.

"He says you were awake at three in the morning sitting outside the shuttle counting stars," Honor said.

"I wasn't counting them. I was looking for constellations." Buffy kept her voice even. "They're all wrong. I know that's stupid. I know it's been two thousand years and I know the stars move, I paid attention during that unit in ninth grade, I know why they're different. Knowing why doesn't make it less�" She stopped.

"Wrong," Honor said.

"Yeah."

Honor was quiet for a moment. Nimitz made another small sound, more like a question this time, his head tilting toward Buffy. "He wants to know if any of them are the same."

Buffy looked up despite herself. The canopy blocked most of the sky, but there was a gap to the east where something had fallen years ago and the jungle hadn't fully reclaimed it yet. A wedge of pale morning, a few stars still visible against the brightening edge. "Orion's still there," she said. "Mostly. The belt's shifted. And Sirius. I think that's Sirius." She pointed. "It's supposed to be the brightest star in the sky and that one's the brightest star I can see, so."

Nimitz bleekd softly. Honor said, "He thinks Sirius is a good anchor."

"Tell him I think he's a pretty good anchor too."

The treecat's tail curled at the tip. Honor's expression did something complicated and fond.

The camp was waking up around them. Buffy could hear it before she could see it, the specific sounds of military people moving through a morning routine with the compressed efficiency of people who'd done this on ships and stations and now were doing it in a jungle and had simply recalibrated the motions without particular complaint. She'd spent enough time watching them to have assigned everyone a distinct sound signature. Alistair McKeon's footsteps were deliberate and evenly spaced, a man who moved through space like he was always on a quarterdeck. Jamie Candless was quieter, hunter-quiet, which she respected. Andrew LaFollet was the quietest of all when he was focused, which should have impressed her more than it did, because she could still hear him coming from thirty meters.

She could not hear Scotty Tremaine until he was approximately four meters away, which was either the result of some deliberate technique he'd never mentioned or just the natural consequence of being a person who moved through the world assuming it was basically friendly. She hadn't decided which. Either way it was annoying.

He appeared around the shuttle's bow with his hair doing something structurally ambitious and a ration pack in each hand, offering one to Honor with the exact degree of deference that managed to communicate respect without being stiff about it, then turning to Buffy with the other.

"Still no coffee," he said. "In case you were worried."

"I was not worried."

"Good. There's something in the ration packs that claims to be a breakfast cereal but I think that's aspirational." He dropped to sit cross-legged a comfortable distance away, not so close as to crowd her, not so far as to signal discomfort with her presence. She'd noticed he always calibrated that distance carefully and never made a production of it. "How'd you sleep?"

"Fine."

"She was outside at three in the morning," Honor said pleasantly.

Buffy gave her a look. Honor's expression conveyed absolute innocence. Nimitz's expression conveyed that he found human social dynamics endlessly entertaining.

"Couldn't sleep," Buffy said. "I do that sometimes. I used to patrol at night to deal with it." She tore open the ration pack and looked at the contents with the pragmatic resignation she'd developed over the past few weeks. "Back home, I mean. Walking helped."

"You can walk the perimeter," Scotty said. "We've got a rotation but there's no regulation against extra sets of eyes. Especially�" he stopped.

"Especially eyes like mine," she finished.

He met her gaze directly. "Especially eyes like yours. Which can see in the dark and process threat data about four times faster than a human baseline. Yes. I was going to say that." He didn't look apologetic about having said it. "I'm not trying to make you feel like a weapon."

"I know." She ate whatever the cereal was claiming to be. "I am one, though. That's not something I can unknow." She looked out at the jungle, which had brightened now to its daytime shade, that particular green that was too green, too fecund, too alive, a green that hadn't learned any restraint. "I came to terms with that a long time ago. Being a weapon that makes her own choices about what to aim at. It's basically the whole job description."

"What happens when there aren't any targets?"

It was a better question than it probably sounded. She considered it seriously. "I've never actually had a gap long enough to find out. There was always another apocalypse." She paused. "That's not a figure of speech, by the way. Literal apocalypses."

"Multiple."

"I was seventeen. It was a busy year."

Scotty looked at her for a moment with an expression she couldn't entirely read. Not pity. Not disbelief, which she appreciated. Something more careful than either of those. "How old are you now?"

The question dropped into a silence. She was aware of Honor going still beside her.

"Seventeen," she said. "Technically." She ate another spoonful of the not-cereal. "I'll be eighteen in January, assuming anybody around here knows what January is."

"Grayson still uses the old calendar for liturgical purposes," Jamie said from somewhere behind the shuttle, which meant he'd been there for at least part of the conversation and had been too Grayson about it to announce himself. He appeared around the bow a moment later, looking somewhat unrepentant. "January is the first month of the year. You were born in January?"

"The nineteenth."

"Then in approximately six months you will be eighteen years old." He said it with a gravity that suggested this was important information which had been successfully captured and filed. "Steadholder Harrington, the Ensign asks whether you'll need him before the morning briefing."

"Tell him half an hour." Honor stood, gathering Nimitz, and looked down at Buffy with an expression that had some component of the same careful thing Scotty's had held, but older, more seasoned, something that had been through more rounds of the same fight. "The morning briefing will include you. Such as it is. We're still in the planning stages of getting off this rock and you should know what the options are."

"Options plural is encouraging."

"It's approximately one and a half options. But we're committed to optimism." She paused. "Are you all right?"

The directness again. Buffy had noticed that Honor asked that question the same way she issued orders, without hedging, without the social softening that turned it into mere courtesy. It was a real question that wanted a real answer.

"I'm working on it," Buffy said. Which was the most honest answer available and also probably the most correct.

Honor nodded once and left. Nimitz looked back over her shoulder at Buffy as they went, his green eyes holding something that wasn't quite readable but felt distinctly like encouragement.

Scotty was still there. He'd finished his ration pack and was now looking at the jungle with his hands loosely on his knees.

"You don't have to stay," Buffy said.

"I know."

She ate the rest of the not-cereal. Somewhere in the canopy above them something was making a sound like a distant bell, rhythmic and incongruous. She'd heard it every morning since she'd been coherent enough to notice things. She still didn't know what made it.

"What's that sound?" she asked.

He tilted his head, listening. "No idea. We looked for it the second day. Couldn't find the source." He glanced up into the green. "McKeon thinks it's some kind of bird. Fritz thinks it might be an insect colony. Andrew thinks we shouldn't be spending resources on finding out." He paused. "I just like it."

"Me too," Buffy said.

She hadn't known that until she said it.

~~~

The morning briefing was held in the larger of the two shuttles, which had been stripped of most of its passenger fittings to create something that functioned approximately like a planning room if you were willing to extend the definition of planning room to include exposed conduit runs and the persistent smell of recycled air and jungle damp.

There were eleven of them, counting Buffy. She'd met most of them in the scattered and nonlinear way of someone who'd spent the first part of her time here as a feral apex predator, which meant some introductions had already happened but bore reviewing under better circumstances. She sat slightly outside the main cluster, not by anyone's direction but by her own habit, the position that gave her sightlines to all the exits and let her see everyone's face.

Nimitz, from his perch on Honor's shoulder, watched her do this with what felt like professional appreciation.

Alistair McKeon ran the briefing with the concise competence of someone who'd been distilling complex problems into clear points his entire adult life. The problem was, as Honor had described, approximately one and a half options.

The camp was located on Hades, a prison planet at the center of a gravity well that made casual departure impossible without a ship capable of compensating for it. They had two shuttles. Shuttles could not compensate for the gravity well. The Peep garrison controlled the planet's only functional orbital facility. The garrison was not small.

"What we have," McKeon said, "is an asymmetric advantage in the form of one asset that the garrison is not currently accounting for in their threat calculations."

Everyone looked at Buffy.

She raised one hand slightly. "Hi."

"The question," McKeon continued, "is how to use that advantage most efficiently, which means we need more information about the garrison's current posture than we have. Their patterns have changed in the past three weeks. Regular sweeps have become irregular. Communications traffic we can detect has dropped." He looked at Honor. "Which suggests either they've become more disciplined about emissions or something has changed in their strategic situation."

"Or both," Honor said.

"Or both," he agreed. "What we need is a closer look at their main compound. Close enough to get accurate headcounts and observe their rotation patterns." He paused, which was the pause of a man about to say something that he'd already thought through carefully and had decided was the best available bad option. "Closer than we can get with our current personnel without unacceptable risk of detection."

The silence that followed was not a surprised silence. Most of the people in the room had clearly already followed the same reasoning to the same destination.

"How close?" Buffy asked.

"The outer perimeter is four kilometers from the main compound. The inner perimeter is eight hundred meters. We'd want eyes at approximately two hundred meters to get reliable headcounts through the structure."

"Okay," she said.

"There's a night-vision grid on the inner perimeter. Thermal as well."

"Does the thermal measure above human-baseline body temperature?"

A small pause. McKeon glanced at Fritz Montoya, who looked up from the notes he'd been making throughout the briefing. "Her resting temperature runs approximately half a degree above baseline," Fritz said. "In the field, moving, it could be as much as a full degree. Depending on the sensitivity calibration of their equipment�"

"Is still within the range of natural variation that their system would likely filter out as noise rather than flag as a target," Honor finished. She looked at Buffy. "Probably."

"Probably is better odds than most of my Tuesday nights back home," Buffy said.

Jamie Candless, standing to her left, made a sound that was almost certainly not a laugh and which he immediately converted into a cough.

"We'd want a three-night observation window before we commit to anything," McKeon said. "Pattern analysis. Looking for gaps, rotation timing, any indication of what's changed in their posture." He looked at Buffy steadily. "This would mean three solo nights inside their perimeter."

"Solo's fine. I work better alone."

"That's not�" he started.

"I know it's not what you want to hear," she said. "But it's true and you know it's true. Two people moving through a perimeter is a different calculation than one, and the second person doesn't add anything to my capability that outweighs what they'd cost in detection risk." She looked around the room. "I'm not trying to be a cowboy about it. I'm being accurate."

The room was quiet. McKeon looked at Honor.

Honor looked at Buffy. Through Nimitz she was presumably getting some readout of Buffy's emotional state that Buffy couldn't curate or edit, which she'd decided some time ago to simply accept as the price of being around treecats. Whatever Honor was receiving, her expression settled into something resolved.

"Three nights," Honor said. "Observation only. No engagement with garrison personnel under any circumstances unless your cover is blown and you have no other option."

"Understood."

"And you will have a communication device that you will check in on at regular intervals, and if you miss a check-in we will know within an hour." She met Buffy's eyes. "I am not negotiating that part."

Buffy thought about seventeen-year-old herself, alone in the dark, with no one who would know within an hour whether she'd lived or died. She thought about how that had felt and how it had become ordinary and how she hadn't recognized until right now, in this converted shuttle smelling of jungle and old recycled air, that it had cost something that she hadn't accounted for at the time.

"Okay," she said. "Check-ins. Got it."

Nimitz made a small sound that she was increasingly able to interpret as satisfaction.

The briefing moved on to supply inventory and water purification and the state of the second shuttle's port thruster, which had been giving intermittent warnings since the escape. Buffy listened with partial attention, filing away the information that was directly relevant to her and letting the rest wash past, watching the room instead. The way these people moved around each other. The specific grammar of how they communicated rank and trust and history without saying any of those words directly.

She'd always been able to read rooms. It was a Slayer thing, that physical hyperawareness that included social dynamics as one of its inputs. But this room was doing something she hadn't encountered before.

These people had been through something that had taken pieces of all of them and they had not subsequently fallen apart. They had instead reconfigured around the losses with a kind of disciplined grief that didn't hide itself but also didn't let itself become the loudest thing in the space. She'd seen grief before. She'd lived in it. She knew the version that calcified and the version that leaked constantly and the version that a person carried so long it stopped feeling like grief and started feeling like just the way things were.

This was different. This was grief that had been given its due and then put to work.

She thought about Willow and Xander and Giles and all the losses they'd accumulated over two years of standing next to her and she wondered whether she'd done right by them. Whether she'd given them enough room to process the weight of it or whether she'd needed them so badly as functional parts of her support structure that she'd accidentally treated their capacity for grief as a logistical problem.

Stop. She pushed it down. Not now. File it. Come back to it when there was time to do it properly.

She looked up and found Scotty Tremaine watching her from across the room with that careful expression again, the one she still hadn't entirely decoded. He looked away when she caught him at it, but not guiltily. Like someone who'd been watching a process they found interesting and didn't mind being noticed watching.

She filed that too.

~~~

Carson Clinkscales was in the habit of rising before everyone else. On a ship this had meant the hours before first watch when the corridors were quietest and he could walk them alone with his thoughts. On Hades it meant the jungle before full light, which was a different kind of alone entirely.

He didn't go far. A hundred meters past the camp's outer perimeter, which he could cover and return from quickly enough that he was back before anyone registered his absence. He wasn't supposed to go alone. Ensigns were not supposed to go alone. Carson was aware of this and made a private theological accommodation for it on the grounds that his morning prayers required a degree of solitude that the shuttle interior did not reliably provide.

He prayed for the souls of the people they'd lost. He prayed for guidance. He prayed, in terms considerably more specific than he usually permitted himself, for them to get off this planet alive.

And then, this morning, he prayed about Lady Buffy.

He was aware that praying about a person was something he needed to be careful about. The Church of Humanity Unchained had reasonably specific things to say about the obligations of men toward women and the specific temptations that came packaged with those obligations, and Carson was young enough and honest enough with himself to recognize that what he felt toward Lady Buffy was not entirely composed of reverent theological awe.

She was beautiful. He could notice that without it being a sin. She was also approximately his age, or had been before whatever had happened to her, and that approximation of shared temporal context was something he found himself reaching toward in ways that probably needed examination.

But beyond the beauty and the age and the theological significance was something else, something that he thought about while he knelt on the roots of a tree that had no name in any language he knew and looked up at the edge of the sky between the canopy gaps.

She was lonely in a way he recognized.

Not the loneliness of someone without companions. She had companions now, was acquiring more of them by the day as the survivors of the Prince Adrian worked out their individual relationships with her. It was the loneliness of someone whose actual context was missing. Whose reference points had all been subtracted.

On Grayson, Carson had a grandfather, parents, two sisters, a community of faith, a world that had formed him. He understood himself in relation to all of those things. Take them away, put him somewhere two thousand years removed from everything that had constructed his sense of self, and he would have the same look that lived in the back of Lady Buffy's eyes when she thought no one was watching.

He wasn't arrogant enough to think he could solve that particular problem. But he thought perhaps he could be one of the things she oriented herself toward while she built a new set of reference points. Something steady. Something that saw her clearly and didn't flinch.

He could do that. He was good at steady.

The bell-sound from high in the canopy began its morning sequence, the same rhythm it made every day, and Carson looked up trying again to locate it and failing again. He thought about what Scotty had said this morning about simply liking the sound.

Then he thought about Lady Buffy saying she liked it too.

He was still thinking about that when he heard her.

Not footsteps. There were no footsteps. There was simply a transition from her not being present to her being present, the way certain large predators apparently could move when they chose. He'd read about hexapumas doing it. He hadn't expected to experience it with a seventeen-year-old girl from Old Earth.

"You're going to give Andrew LaFollet a heart attack if you keep leaving the perimeter alone," she said.

She was sitting on the root of the tree nearest his, her knees pulled up, a knife he'd never seen before balanced flat across her palm. Not doing anything with it. Just holding it the way some people held worry stones.

Carson recovered his composure and noted that he was relatively pleased with how quickly the recovery happened. "I could say the same to you."

"I told Jamie where I was going."

"I told no one," he admitted.

"I know. That's why I followed you." She looked at the knife rather than at him. "The perimeter isn't your responsibility. You don't have to work the watch."

"I wasn't working the watch. I was praying."

She was quiet for a moment. "Does it help?"

"Yes." He paused. "Mostly."

She turned the knife over. The blade was the same matte black she'd had since the Tepes, the one that didn't catch light, the one she'd produced from somewhere on her person during the ship's medical bay and that Fritz had been quietly fascinated by ever since. "What do you pray for? Specifically."

It was a direct question. She asked a lot of those. He'd noticed that she became uncomfortable with conversations that worked around the perimeter of what they actually wanted to say. "This morning I was praying about you," he said.

She looked up at that.

"I was asking for guidance," he said, "about what my obligations are. What I'm supposed to do with�" He gestured vaguely, which he immediately recognized as a cowardly substitution for accurate language. "With the fact of you. What you represent. What you might need from me."

"The fact of me," she repeated, something careful in her voice.

"You are the Slayer," he said. "My family has kept that record for a very long time. I am sitting approximately two thousand years after any version of events my family ever contemplated and you are sitting three meters away from me holding a knife and asking me what I pray for." He kept his voice steady. "I am trying to be a person who responds to extraordinary circumstances with clear thinking rather than with either reverence or dismissal, and I will be honest with you that the first is easier than the second in this particular case."

She looked at him for a long moment. Then she looked back at the knife.

"Merrick used to look at me like that," she said. "My first Watcher. Like I was proof of something."

"Was that bad?"

"It was lonely." She tilted the blade slightly and a faint glimmer moved along its edge despite the coating. "Not because he was wrong. More because it was the first thing he saw when he looked at me. Before I was a person I was a proof." She paused. "I got used to it. I don't want to get used to it again."

Carson heard this. He let it sit for a full breath before he answered.

"I cannot unknow what I know about you," he said. "I won't pretend otherwise, that seems dishonest. But I think what I actually want is�" he searched for the word�"to know the person the Slayer is packaged with. If that distinction makes sense."

It was the most he'd articulated that thought to anyone including himself. He felt somewhat exposed by it.

She turned the knife over again. Then she said, "Buffy Summers. Sunnydale, California. I'm a Virgo, I like shoes, I'm afraid of being buried alive, and my favorite food is anything my mother makes, specifically her chicken with the rosemary, and I will never eat it again." She said the last part in the same tone as the rest, which was not quite flat but was the tone of someone who had decided to treat a wound as a fact rather than a wound. "Your turn."

"Carson Clinkscales. I'm from Grayson. I'm the third of four children. I joined the Navy because my grandfather served and because I wanted to do something my sisters couldn't, which in retrospect was not a particularly sophisticated reason but I was sixteen." He paused. "I'm afraid of being responsible for the death of someone under my command."

"Has that happened?"

"Not yet." He let that sit the way she'd let the thing about her mother sit. "I'm aware the 'yet' is doing a lot of work in that sentence."

She nodded slowly. Then she tucked the knife somewhere, one fluid motion with no visible destination, and unfolded from the root in a single movement that shouldn't have been as graceful as it was given how thoroughly her feet were tangled in the ground cover.

"Morning briefing," she said.

"Yes."

They walked back to the camp in a silence that had somewhat less empty space in it than the silence they'd walked out in, though Carson couldn't have said precisely what had changed to account for the difference. The bell-sound from the canopy followed them in its particular rhythm, unanswered and not requiring an answer, simply there.

Something steady, he had thought. He could be that.

He thought maybe she was already doing the same thing for him, and hadn't told him, and probably wouldn't.

Chapter 11: The first night she went out alone.

Not because she hadn't considered taking someone. She'd considered it the way she considered all tactical questions, which was thoroughly and without sentimentality, and the conclusion was the same as the one she'd presented in the briefing. One person moved differently than two. The math didn't change because someone was worried about her.

She'd told Jamie at 2200. He'd looked at her with the expression of a man whose entire professional training was colliding with a situation that training had not anticipated, and then he'd done the Grayson thing, which was to hand her the communicator and tell her the check-in intervals and then stand very still while she went.

She appreciated that. She'd had guardians before who couldn't do the standing still part.

The jungle at night was different from the jungle in the day in ways that went beyond the obvious. It wasn't just the light. The whole sonic landscape shifted, the daytime cacophony of birds and insects and the distant bell-thing replacing itself with something lower and more continuous, a deep background hum that she felt in her sternum more than heard with her ears. The predators that slept through the heat of the afternoon were awake and moving, and she could track them by the pressure-change their movement sent through the understory, the way smaller creatures went quiet in a ripple around anything large enough to be dangerous.

She moved through it like water through water.

The Slayer sense was not precisely vision or hearing or smell. It was all of those and also something that had no name in any language she'd encountered, a direct readout from the world's threat landscape that her brain processed as a combination of knowing and feeling. In the daylight it was useful. In the dark it was something close to supernatural.

She reached the garrison's outer perimeter in forty minutes. In the daylight, moving carefully, it would have taken twice that.

She stopped at the tree line and simply looked.

The compound was larger than the briefing's rough estimates. Four main structures, two smaller outbuildings, a landing pad with two pinnaces and what looked like a modified civilian shuttle that someone had bolted additional equipment onto with more enthusiasm than elegance. The perimeter lights were on a timer rather than a sensor grid, which was either an efficiency measure or a sign of complacency. She filed it.

The night-vision grid McKeon had mentioned was visible once she knew to look for it, the faint ghostly shimmer of the projector arrays mounted at intervals along the inner fence. She watched the shimmer for ten minutes and confirmed the gaps. Four of them, each approximately ninety seconds in duration, cycling in a pattern that was either random or pseudo-random. She'd need a second night to determine which.

She counted personnel. Eighteen visible in the first hour. She suspected the total garrison was somewhere between forty and sixty based on the size of the structures and the number of vehicles, but she'd need more time to see the full rotation.

She checked in at midnight and at two and at four.

At four-thirty she turned back toward camp with information she hadn't had before and the particular quiet satisfaction of a job done cleanly.

She was back in her bunk at five-fifteen. She lay in the dark listening to the camp breathe around her and ran the numbers.

The gaps in the perimeter grid were consistent enough to be exploitable. The rotation timing was regular enough that she could predict position. The question was not whether she could get in and out of the compound. She could. The question was what came after, which was the question that kept her awake until the bell-sound started up in the canopy outside and the camp began to stir.

She thought about what Honor had said. No engagement unless your cover is blown.

She thought about what she knew about herself, which was that she was seventeen years old and had been ending lives since the night a vampire had dropped out of a tree at her in a graveyard and she'd staked him with a branch she'd grabbed on reflex. She'd stopped counting after a while. She'd stopped counting a lot of things after a while.

The people in the garrison compound were not vampires. They were not demons. They were human beings who had done terrible things in service of a regime that valued the terrible things they were willing to do, but they were human and that meant the math worked differently.

She thought about that until the camp was fully awake and then she put it away and went to the morning briefing.

~~~

"Forty-eight to fifty-two," McKeon said, looking at the notes she'd delivered. "That's higher than projected."

"Could be lower. I only got eyes on the rotation, not the full complement. Some of them were probably asleep."

"Still." He looked at Honor. "Our original planning assumed thirty-five to forty."

"Revise it," Honor said. "What's the practical effect on the approach?"

"It means the distraction element needs to be bigger." He looked back at Buffy. "The perimeter gaps."

"Ninety seconds, four gaps, cycling. I need one more night to confirm whether it's random or pseudo-random. If it's pseudo-random I can predict the next cycle. If it's actually random we work with the known gap duration and accept the uncertainty."

"What's your confidence level on the gap duration itself?"

"High. I timed eleven cycles. The variance was under three seconds." She paused. "I want to go back in tomorrow night and get closer."

Silence.

"You said two hundred meters," Honor said.

"I said we'd want eyes at two hundred meters. I was at the tree line last night, which is four hundred. I can do two hundred. I want to see the guard positions at the inner fence up close before we commit to anything."

"And the night after?"

"Depends on what I find."

McKeon looked at Honor again. This was something they did, the two of them, this rapid silent consultation that covered in a glance the same ground that other people needed five minutes of argument to traverse. Buffy watched it with something that was not quite envy and not quite longing. Whatever it was had something to do with the particular kind of trust that only accumulated through shared catastrophe, and she recognized it because she'd had it once with Willow and Xander and she knew what it looked like from the outside.

"Three nights," Honor said. "Then we plan."

~~~

The second night she went to two hundred and fifty meters, which was close enough to see faces.

This was not tactically necessary. She'd told herself it was, that she needed to assess individual guard alertness rather than just rotation patterns, that McKeon's plan required granular information about the personnel themselves. Some of that was true.

Some of it was something else, a need to look at the people she might be asked to help neutralize and make a decision with full information. She owed them that much. She owed herself that much.

The guards were young. Not universally, there were older personnel who moved with the watchful economy of people who'd survived by being careful, but the majority of the faces she could see clearly at two hundred meters with Slayer eyes were young. Mid-twenties. Some of them younger. Kids who'd joined an organization that told them they were the sharp end of history and paid them accordingly and never mentioned that the price was becoming the kind of person who could work a camp like the Tepes without losing sleep.

Or maybe they did lose sleep. She couldn't know that from here.

She watched one of them, a young woman with her hair scraped back under her cap, stand at her post for forty minutes in the humid dark without moving more than was strictly necessary. Professional stillness. The kind you had to learn. The kind that didn't come natural to anyone in their twenties and meant she'd been at this long enough for it to become a habit.

Buffy thought about herself at the same age. The same professional stillness. The graveyard at midnight, perfectly quiet, waiting for something to emerge from a fresh grave so she could put it back.

The comparison was imperfect in every possible way and still landed somewhere uncomfortable.

She checked in at midnight and at two.

At two-thirty she heard the thing that changed the calculation.

It came from one of the smaller outbuildings, the one she'd tagged as a potential storage facility or communications relay. She'd been planning to get closer to it on the third night. She was getting closer to it now because the sound coming out of it was a human voice saying something in the tonal flatness of someone who had run out of ways to scream and had gone somewhere quieter and simpler.

She was at the wall of the outbuilding before she'd made a conscious decision to move.

The window was shuttered but not sealed. She could hear through it clearly enough.

Two voices. One asking questions. One answering in that flat depleted register, a woman's voice, answering in the way that people answered when they'd concluded that answesty or non-answer had the same consequence and they'd simply stopped caring which.

The questions were about the escaped prisoners.

About Honor.

Buffy stood with her back to the wall of the outbuilding and her hands flat against the composite and breathed through the combat calm the way she'd learned to, slow and deliberate, feeling her heartbeat settle rather than race. This was information. This was tactically relevant information about the garrison's knowledge state and the source of their changed posture and the thing she was supposed to do with this information was note it and withdraw and report it.

She stayed for eleven more minutes, until she had everything that was being said, and then she withdrew through the dark without making a sound and ran the numbers back to camp.

~~~

She woke Honor at three in the morning.

Not the way she'd have done it a month ago, which would have been simply appearing next to Honor's bunk without announcement, a habit that had caused three separate incidents involving Honor's sidearm in the first week. She knocked on the shuttle wall and said "it's me" in a normal voice and waited.

Honor appeared in the hatchway thirty seconds later with her hair down and her expression alert in a way that suggested she hadn't been deeply asleep. Nimitz was a dark shape on her shoulder, his eyes catching the ambient light.

"Second night," Honor said.

"Yes. You need to hear this."

They sat in the shuttle with the lights at their lowest setting and Buffy told her what she'd heard.

The summary was this: the garrison knew someone had escaped from the Tepes. They did not know how many. They did not know identities. They had a prisoner who had been on the Tepes and who was being questioned about the same, and the prisoner was not currently in a condition to provide much useful information, but the questioners were not finished.

Honor's face did very little while Buffy talked. This was different from having no reaction. Buffy had spent two years reading faces in crisis and she could see what Honor's face was doing under the stillness, which was processing the tactical situation and the moral one simultaneously and not letting either interfere with the other until she had the full picture.

When Buffy finished, Honor was quiet for a long moment.

"How many guards on the outbuilding?"

"One outside. One inside with the prisoner. Could be more inside that I couldn't locate."

"The prisoner's voice. Could you determine condition?"

"Alive. Aware enough to answer questions. Beyond that I couldn't tell." She paused. "The questioning had been going on for a while. You could hear it in the way they were talking. Both of them. The questioner was tired of asking and the prisoner was tired of the whole situation."

Honor looked at Nimitz. He looked back at her, and whatever passed between them stayed between them.

"I need to think," Honor said. "Can you give me an hour?"

"Yes." Buffy stood. "There's something else."

Honor waited.

"I can get them out," Buffy said. "The prisoner. I know the perimeter gaps. I know the guard positions. I can go back in tomorrow night and get them out before they provide anything useful." She kept her voice level. "I want you to know that's an option before you spend the hour thinking."

"I know it's an option," Honor said. "Go sleep. Come back in an hour."

Buffy went.

She didn't sleep. She sat outside in the dark with her back against the shuttle hull and thought about the woman's voice in the flat exhausted register and thought about the question of what she was for.

She was the Slayer. She was for fighting things that wanted to eat the world. She was for the thing that went bump in the night and the thing that wore a human face over something that had never been human and the specific category of evil that the rest of humanity wasn't equipped to address.

The garrison on Hades were not that category. They were the other kind of evil, the kind that was entirely human, and the conventional wisdom was that the other kind of evil was someone else's problem.

She'd never entirely believed the conventional wisdom.

She thought about Giles telling her that she needed to stay in her lane, that the Slayer's mandate was specific and that she'd burn out trying to solve every version of the problem instead of just the supernatural one. She thought about how she'd argued with him and how she'd lost the arguments and how she'd sometimes done what she thought was right anyway and how the track record on those occasions was sufficiently mixed that she'd learned to at least sit with the question before acting.

Sitting with the question.

She sat with it.

The hour passed.

She knocked on the shuttle wall again. Honor was already awake, or still awake, and had been joined by McKeon, who had the look of a man who'd been briefed while she'd been sitting outside thinking.

"We go in," Honor said. "Four nights from now. The prisoner changes the schedule."

McKeon pulled up the rough map they'd been building. "The distraction element becomes more complex if we're extracting a prisoner who may not be ambulatory."

"I know."

"We're going to need more people on the inside than we originally planned."

"I know that too." Honor looked at Buffy. "Can you take someone with you on the extraction run?"

"One person. Small. Quiet." She thought. "Scotty."

McKeon blinked. Honor didn't.

"He was top of his class in small unit tactics," Honor said, to McKeon rather than Buffy, answering the unasked question. "And he's been watching Buffy move for two weeks."

"I've been watching him move for two weeks," Buffy corrected. "He's quiet enough. And he won't do anything I don't tell him to do."

She wasn't entirely certain that was true. She was certain he'd try.

"Brief him in the morning," Honor said. "Full picture. He needs to understand what he's walking into."

"Yes, ma'am."

She said it without thinking and Honor looked at her with something that might have been surprised and might have been pleased, and Buffy wasn't sure where the instinct had come from either, except that she'd spent enough time watching these people to have started absorbing the grammar of how they spoke to each other and apparently some of it was sticking.

~~~

She told Scotty over breakfast.

Not all of it. She gave him the tactical outline, the perimeter, the gaps, the guard positions, the prisoner. She watched his face while she talked the way she'd learned to watch faces, for the tells that separated the people who could handle what they were being told from the people who thought they could and were wrong.

His face did something she hadn't quite expected, which was that it didn't perform anything. It didn't perform calm or resolve or the anticipatory excitement that some people couldn't keep off their faces when they were handed an operation. It just received the information and processed it and when she finished he asked three questions that were exactly the right three questions in exactly the right order.

"What's the extraction route?"

"What condition do we assume the prisoner is in?"

"What's our abort threshold?"

She answered all three.

He nodded. "Okay."

"That's it?"

"Did you want more?"

She thought about it. "No. That's the right amount."

He drank his coffee. She drank her chocolate. Around them the camp was moving through its morning rhythms, LaFollet doing his perimeter walk, Fritz making notes in the medical bay shuttle, Carson doing something with the water reclamation unit that involved a great deal of careful attention and one carefully controlled expression of frustration.

"Can I ask you something that's not tactical?" Scotty said.

"You can ask."

"Are you scared?"

She thought about giving him the answer that would reassure him. She thought about the habit she'd developed over two years of being the person everyone looked to for the assurance that things were going to be fine, the particular performance of confidence that a Slayer was supposed to project because if the Slayer looked scared then everybody looked scared and that didn't help anybody.

"Of going in?" she said. "No. I know how to do that part."

"Of what comes after?"

She looked at her mug. "The after is where I always had trouble."

He was quiet for a moment. Then: "For what it's worth, I'm good at after. Ships are mostly after. You spend six hours on a combat op and then you spend six months dealing with the paperwork and the inquiry and the people who weren't there asking you to explain your decisions to them in a way that makes sense in a room that smells like furniture polish." He said it lightly, without self-pity. "I'm not saying that's the same thing you mean. I'm saying I have some practice at coming down from things."

She looked at him. He was watching her in that way he had, interested and undemanding.

"I'll keep that in mind," she said.

Chapter 12: The third night she didn't go out.

She'd told Honor she would and then she sat in the shuttle at 2200 and her body wouldn't go. Not fear. She knew what fear felt like and this wasn't it. More like the particular heaviness that settled in after extended operations, the moment when the machine that was the Slayer's body registered the accumulated cost of three weeks of hypervigilance and decided to present the bill.

She sat with it for an hour and then she woke Fritz.

He was remarkably gracious about being woken at ten at night. He appeared in the shuttle hatchway with his hair disordered and his eyes already professional, which she was coming to understand was just how Fritz Montoya was built, professionally curious at all hours and in all conditions.

"Sit," she said, and he sat, and she said, "I need you to tell me what's happening."

"Medically?"

"Yes."

He looked at her the way he'd been looking at her for weeks, with the focused attention of someone cataloguing data and trying not to make it obvious. "Your baseline presentation has been improving steadily since you recovered full cognition. Motor function excellent. Reflexes significantly above human baseline and stable. Sleep quality�" he paused. "Less good."

"I'm not sleeping."

"You're sleeping four to five hours. That's less than your demonstrated need."

"Which is?"

"Seven to eight. Possibly more during periods of high physical demand." He paused. "The healing factor doesn't sleep. It's always running. It needs sleep-cycle biology to do some of its processing, the same way human immune function does. If you're not completing sleep cycles you're running the repair shop on reduced power."

She thought about the dreams. The Girl's memories. The running in the dark that felt more like joy than anything she'd felt in waking hours. "If I sleep more the dreams get longer."

"Are the dreams distressing?"

She considered how to answer that accurately. "No. They're someone else's memories. They're not bad. They're just�a lot. It's like being two people at once and I'm still figuring out where one ends and the other begins."

Fritz was quiet for a moment, making the small note in his personal log that she'd long since stopped trying to talk him out of. "The merging you described. The First Slayer's consciousness in proximity to yours."

"Inside mine. I think. I'm not a neuroscientist."

"Neither am I, technically, though I've done enough fieldwork." He looked at her steadily. "Has her presence affected your decision-making? Your sense of self?"

It was the question she'd been avoiding for two weeks. She sat with it and gave it the answer it deserved. "She's not making decisions. She's more like�a voice in the back. Very old. Very certain about some things. She gets louder when I'm in the field." She paused. "She was very loud the night I heard the prisoner."

"In what way?"

"She wanted me to go in and get them out immediately." She looked at her hands. "She doesn't really do the part where you sit with the question. She identifies a threat to the people she's protecting and she responds to it. The delay for planning is�" She searched for the word. "Uncomfortable. For her."

"But you waited."

"I've been doing this for two years. I've learned that the delay for planning usually saves lives that the immediate response would have spent." She looked up at Fritz. "But I want you to know that it's not purely academic. There's a voice in the back that's pushing. I'm managing it. I thought you should know."

Fritz nodded slowly. "Thank you for telling me." He made another note. "For what it's worth, from the outside you present as entirely coherent and in command of your decision-making. Whatever the internal experience is, it's not visible."

"Good." She stood. "Go back to sleep. Sorry for waking you."

"Don't be." He stood as well, and then said, in the same clinical tone he used for everything, which somehow made it easier to hear: "Lady Buffy. You've survived something that should not have been survivable and you're operating at a level that would be remarkable in a trained adult professional who hadn't been through any of what you've been through. The fact that you're managing it is�" he paused, and she saw him find the word� "not surprising to me, given what you are. But I want you to know that it's still a significant thing."

She looked at him.

"I'm keeping the notes," he said, "because I think someday someone is going to need to understand what happened here. To you. To all of us. And I want there to be a record that's accurate." He paused. "And because you're my patient and I pay attention to my patients."

She thought about doctors she'd known. The ones who'd patched her up in the dark in ways that couldn't be explained in the bright light of an ER. The ones who'd looked at wounds that healed wrong and pretended not to notice.

"Okay," she said. "Thank you."

She went back to her bunk and this time she let herself sleep and the Girl's memories took her running through a darkness so old it had never known a name and it felt like rest.

~~~

Four days later they went in.

The plan was this:

McKeon would lead a five-person team to the outer perimeter as a distraction element. Their job was to draw attention toward the south side of the compound at 0200, enough attention to pull the guard from the outbuilding, without triggering a full response. The method was controlled and deliberate and involved three of the camp's remaining explosive devices deployed at intervals to suggest a larger force than was actually present.

Buffy and Scotty would enter through the north perimeter gap at 0205, reach the outbuilding, extract the prisoner, and be back through the perimeter by 0220.

Fifteen minutes. Everything that happened in those fifteen minutes needed to happen quietly.

"Questions," Honor said, looking around the group.

"What's the signal if the distraction goes wrong?" That was LaFollet, standing at the back, who had not been included in the operational teams and whose feelings about this were visible if you knew how to read Grayson stillness.

"Standard abort. Three clicks on the communicator, everyone withdraws to rally point alpha." Honor's eyes moved to Buffy. "No exceptions."

Buffy didn't argue. She'd been thinking about the abort threshold since Scotty had asked her about it and she'd come to the conclusion that Honor was right, which was not a conclusion that came naturally to her but which she was developing a habit of reaching through a reasoning process that she could mostly follow.

At 0150 she and Scotty moved into position at the tree line north of the compound.

He moved well. She'd told him he was quiet and she'd meant it, but quiet in the camp was different from quiet in the jungle in the dark, where everything had a texture and pressure and moving through it without adding to the sum of its sounds was a specific skill. He was doing it. Not the way she did it, not with the unconscious completeness of two-plus years of training layered over whatever the Slayer sense added to the baseline, but with the deliberate competence of someone who had practiced and internalized and was now executing without the visible effort of execution.

She revised her assessment of him upward.

"Perimeter gap in four minutes," she said, barely voiced. The night-vision image through the gap in the canopy showed the compound in its ambient light, the regular sweep of the perimeter lights on their timer, the guards at their posts.

He nodded. He'd already seen it.

At 0200 the first of McKeon's devices went off, south side, far enough away that the sound arrived as a distant compression rather than a bang. The compound lights snapped to full and she could see the guards moving, turning south, the professional response overriding whatever they'd been thinking about in the quiet before.

The guard outside the outbuilding moved toward the south fence. Not all the way. He stopped at the corner of the nearest main structure and looked south and then looked back at the outbuilding.

She watched him make the decision. Stay or go.

He went.

"Now," she said.

They moved through the gap in a flat run and she was aware of Scotty beside her keeping pace better than she'd expected, and then they were at the wall of the outbuilding and she had her hand on the door and she could hear one voice inside, asking questions at the tired professional pace of someone running through a list they'd already run through many times.

She went through the door.

The questioner was between her and the prisoner, which was tactically convenient. She crossed the distance before the questioner had completed the rotation toward the door and put him down with the specific efficiency of someone who had learned very early that the goal was incapacitation rather than harm and who had trained the distinction into muscle memory. He went down. He would wake up with a headache and questions she did not plan to be present to answer.

The prisoner was a woman, as the voice had suggested. Mid-forties. The kind of face that had been handsome before the past however-long had taken their toll. She looked up at Buffy with the expression of someone who had stopped expecting anything good and was therefore fully available for surprise.

"Can you walk?" Buffy asked.

The woman looked at Scotty. Then back at Buffy. "Who are you?"

"We're the people who are leaving in about nine minutes. Can you walk?"

A beat. "Yes."

"Good. Stay between us. Do what I tell you immediately. If I say down, you go down. If I say run, you run. Questions after."

"Questions after," the woman repeated, in the tone of someone trying to determine whether they were hallucinating. "All right."

Scotty had already been to the door and back, taking in the compound's current state. "Guard's still at the south corner. Two more visible on the fence. No eyes on this building."

"Two minutes to the gap," Buffy said. "Move."

They moved.

The prisoner's walk was the controlled careful walk of someone with damage they were managing rather than recovering from, but it was functional and she kept up and when Buffy said down at the midpoint because one of the fence guards turned the wrong way she went down without argument and got up when Buffy got up without argument and that was exactly the right amount of cooperation.

They were through the gap with forty seconds to spare.

In the tree line she allowed herself one breath.

Scotty was watching the compound. The prisoner was watching Buffy. Nimitz, who had been left at camp over his extremely vocal objections, was not present to tell her what the prisoner was feeling, but Buffy had her own version of that read and what she got from the prisoner was something that was working through the transition from survival-mode to the possibility of actual relief, which was a transition Buffy recognized and which was going to take a while to complete.

"What's your name?" she asked.

"Surgeon-Lieutenant Theodosia Martingale," the woman said. "Grayson Space Navy."

Scotty made a sound. Buffy looked at him.

"I know that name," he said. "She was aboard the Austin Grayson. We listed her as�" he stopped.

"Dead," Martingale said. "I imagine you did." She looked down at her hands. "I've been imagining you did for about four months." She looked up again. "Who's in command?"

"Steadholder Harrington," Buffy said.

Theodosia Martingale closed her eyes.

Buffy watched her do it and recognized the specific quality of that closure, which was not grief but something adjacent to grief, the expression of a person receiving information that recalibrates something fundamental about how they've been organizing the experience of the past four months.

"All right," Martingale said. She opened her eyes. They were steadier. "All right. Let's go."

~~~

Honor met them at the camp perimeter.

She took in Martingale with the rapid assessment of a flag officer cataloguing tactical information, but there was something underneath the assessment that was personal in a way that Honor generally kept well-managed. Buffy had noticed that Honor's emotional life conducted itself in the register below her professional presentation rather than above it, which was unusual in her experience but which she was coming to understand was not suppression so much as architecture. The feelings were there. They were just load-bearing.

"Theodosia," Honor said.

"Milady," Martingale said, and then something happened to her face that was entirely at odds with the controlled voice, and Honor crossed the space between them and put her arms around the woman and Nimitz reached out from Honor's shoulder and laid one true-hand gently on top of Martingale's head and the whole thing was so unaffected and so complete that Buffy looked away.

She found herself looking at Scotty instead. He was watching with an expression she didn't have a word for. Something that acknowledged the weight of the moment without making it about him.

She thought about what he'd said. I'm good at after.

She thought maybe he was.

McKeon's team came in from the south at 0230, all five, with the tight expressions of people who'd run something close and were doing their post-operation inventory of themselves. "Perimeter response was faster than projected," McKeon said to Honor. "They've tightened up since last week."

"We expected that." Honor's arm was still around Martingale. "Debrief at 0800. Everyone get some sleep."

The camp dispersed with the quiet efficiency of people who'd learned to sleep in pieces around the edges of operations. Buffy sat down on the root of the tree outside the shuttle and watched the canopy and listened to the jungle settle back into its nighttime rhythms.

She was aware of Scotty sitting down a careful distance away.

She was aware that she didn't mind.

"Good work," she said.

"You did the work."

"You kept up. That was the work I needed from you."

He was quiet for a moment. "She'd been there four months."

"I know."

"We thought she was dead."

"I know."

The jungle breathed. Somewhere above them the bell-thing was silent for once, and the silence it left was its own kind of sound.

"Does it get easier?" he asked. "The part where you find out what you almost didn't get there in time for."

She thought about an honest answer. The honest answer was complicated, which was true of most honest answers worth giving. "It gets different," she said. "You stop expecting it to be easy and then it stops feeling like failure when it isn't." She looked up at the gap in the canopy where the stars were. Sirius was there. "The part that stays hard is the arithmetic. What you got there in time for versus what you didn't."

"Is the arithmetic ever in your favor?"

She'd thought about that question in various forms for two years. The Slayer was, by nature, a remediation. You arrived after something had already happened, after someone had already decided to open the Hellmouth or raise the Master or invite the demon mayor to ascend on graduation day, and the best possible outcome was that you prevented the completion of a plan that should never have gotten as far as it did. You were always cleaning up. You were never early enough to prevent the conditions that made the cleanup necessary.

"Sometimes," she said. "Tonight."

He looked at her. She looked back.

"Tonight was in your favor," he said.

"Yeah." She looked back at the stars. "I'm going to remember that one."

~~~

Martingale's debrief lasted four hours.

She had the compressed, precise recall of someone who had spent four months organizing information against the possibility of an audience, and she delivered it without editorializing, the dates and names and troop movements and supply estimates falling out of her in the ordered sequence of someone who had kept them alive in her head by treating them as a professional obligation.

Honor asked questions. McKeon asked questions. LaFollet asked questions that were specifically about the garrison's tactical capabilities in ways that suggested he'd already been running planning scenarios before Martingale was fully awake.

Buffy sat at the back and listened to the information she hadn't had before arrange itself into a shape.

The shape was this: the garrison was larger than they'd estimated but less coherent. There had been a shift in command structure approximately six weeks ago, the previous commandant replaced by someone Martingale described as younger, more ideologically committed, and less operationally experienced. The effect had been the tightening of some procedures and the neglect of others, the pattern of someone who'd been trained on theory and was discovering the friction of practice.

"The pinnaces," McKeon said. "You said one of them has been down for maintenance."

"The port landing leg took damage during the last resupply. Their maintenance crew has been working it for three weeks. I don't know whether they've resolved it."

"If it's still down�"

"We'd have one active pinnace as an intercept option if we tried to lift." Honor's voice was level. "One vs. two shuttles."

"Our shuttles aren't armed."

"No." Honor was quiet for a moment. "But one pinnace chasing two shuttles going in opposite directions is a different calculation than one pinnace chasing two shuttles going in the same direction."

Buffy watched the room absorb this. She watched the plan they'd been building adjust itself around the new information, the way a living thing adjusted around an obstacle. These people were very good at this. She'd been watching them do it for weeks and she was still impressed by how quickly they converted new data into revised strategy without the revision feeling like failure. On the Hellmouth, changed plans had always felt like setbacks. Here they felt like evolution.

She thought that was probably a healthier relationship with uncertainty.

McKeon pulled up the rough map again. "If we're going for the pinnace�"

"We may need to," Honor said. "The second shuttle's thruster concerns me. If it fails during the ascent�" She looked at Scotty.

"The variance on the warning signals has increased," he said. "Could be another two weeks. Could be next time we fire it."

"Then we plan for worst case." Honor looked at the map. "We secure the functional pinnace. That means someone has to be inside the compound to do it."

The room was quiet.

Buffy looked at the map. She looked at the compound layout she'd spent three nights learning. She looked at the landing pad and the pinnace and the distance from the outbuilding where she'd found Martingale to the landing pad, which was approximately two hundred meters of open ground.

"I can get to the pinnace," she said. "I need someone who can fly it."

"I can fly it," Scotty said.

She'd known he was going to say that before he said it. She wasn't sure whether that was the Slayer sense or just two weeks of paying attention.

"Of course you can," she said.

Chapter 13: They spent six days planning.

Not because the plan required six days. It probably required four. The extra two were Honor's decision, made explicit on the second morning when McKeon had suggested they were approaching operational readiness and Honor had said, calmly, that they would take the time to make the plan right rather than making it fast, and the room had understood that this was not open for revision.

Buffy used the time.

She ran the perimeter in the mornings, the camp's perimeter rather than the garrison's, a fixed route that Jamie had mapped in the first week that covered approximately eight kilometers and included three significant elevation changes and two water crossings and enough technical terrain to keep the Slayer sense engaged rather than idling. She ran it alone in the early dark while the camp slept and then she showered with the camp's meager water ration and was present for breakfast and looked, she suspected, irritatingly fine.

She spent afternoons with Martingale.

This had not been planned. It had happened on the second day when Martingale had sought her out in the way of someone who had decided to address something directly rather than wait for an opportunity to develop, and sat down across from her with a mug of tea and said, "You went in for me. I'd like to understand what you are."

Buffy had looked at her. "You could ask Carson. He has the family records."

"I did ask Carson. He was very thorough and very reverent and I came away knowing what the Clinkscales family thinks you are. I'd like to know what you think."

That had been a better question.

They talked for three hours that afternoon and two hours the next day and a variable amount on the days after, the conversations ranging widely in the way of conversations between two people who are discovering the shape of each other's minds. Martingale was a surgeon who thought about bodies the way engineers thought about systems, with a preference for precision and an impatience for imprecision that she'd learned to manage professionally if not personally. She was also, underneath the precision, someone who had spent four months alone with her thoughts in a situation that did not invite optimism, and had come through it with a kind of earned clarity about what mattered.

"What kept you going?" Buffy asked, on the fourth afternoon.

Martingale thought about it seriously, which Buffy appreciated. "Habit, mostly. In the early weeks. The habit of being a professional, which means continuing to function regardless of conditions." She looked at her tea. "Later it was something else. Information. I kept cataloguing the information because it seemed like the information would be useful to someone eventually, and that required me to be present to deliver it, so." She paused. "And I prayed. I'm not particularly religious by Grayson standards, but it turns out that when you're in a small room with limited options you discover what you actually believe, and what I actually believe is that random chance alone doesn't account for everything I've seen."

Buffy thought about a universe that had included the Hellmouth and the First Slayer's dreamscape and a portal that had spat her out two thousand years from everything she'd known. "Yeah," she said. "I have some feelings about random chance myself."

On the fifth day she found Carson sitting outside the water reclamation unit at midday with the expression of a person who had finished a task and had not yet found the next one, a state of unscheduled time that appeared to make him mildly anxious.

"Sit down," she said, and sat beside him.

He sat. He had the quality she'd noticed in him from the beginning, which was a steadiness that wasn't passivity. He didn't fill silence with noise. He didn't need to. She'd grown up surrounded by people who needed to fill silence � Willow with words, Xander with humor, Giles with tea-making sounds � and she'd loved them for it, that specific warmth of people whose anxiety expressed itself as sound. Carson's quiet was a different thing. It wasn't anxious. It was occupied.

"Tell me about Grayson," she said.

He looked at her. "What would you like to know?"

"Everything. Start from what it looks like."

He thought for a moment and then he began, and she listened, not to the information specifically but to the quality of his voice when he talked about it. The way it changed when he described the particular angle of the light in the late afternoon, the color of the sky when the storms came in off the northern ocean, the smell of the orchards at harvest. The way he described his grandfather's hands.

She thought about Sunnydale in the early evening, the specific quality of the California light on the palm trees, the sound of her mother's voice from the kitchen. She did not say any of this. She just listened to Carson describe home and let it fill the place in her chest that was shaped like home, not replacing what was there but sitting alongside it.

"What are you thinking?" he asked, after a while.

"That it sounds like a place worth getting back to."

"It is." He paused. "You'll see it."

She looked at him. He said it with a certainty that was not bravado, not the forced positivity of someone trying to encourage themselves as much as her. It was just certainty, the way you'd state a fact.

"You sound sure about that."

"I am." He met her eyes. "We've come this far."

She thought about this. Two thousand years and a prison planet and a trauma she was still cataloguing the full dimensions of and an escape plan that was going to require everything they had and then some. She thought about the Girl running through an ancient dark with nothing pursuing her, just the joy of the run itself, the body doing what it was built for.

"Yeah," she said. "We have."

~~~

On the sixth day Honor called the final briefing.

The plan had three phases.

Phase one: McKeon's team would neutralize the garrison's communications array. Not destroy it. Neutralize it, long enough to prevent them from calling for support during the window of the operation. The array was on the north side of the compound, far enough from the main guard positions to be approachable but not undefended. McKeon had volunteered himself and two others. Honor had looked at him for a long moment and then said yes.

Phase two: simultaneous with phase one, LaFollet would lead a four-person team to create a perimeter engagement on the south and east sides of the compound, enough to draw and hold garrison attention. The objective was confusion and commitment of resources rather than direct engagement.

Phase three: Buffy and Scotty would enter the compound, reach the landing pad, secure the pinnace, and be in a position to lift within eight minutes of phase one executing. The two shuttles would lift simultaneously from the camp, minimum crew, and converge on the pinnace extraction point outside the compound perimeter.

"The timing," McKeon said, which was where he always started when he was about to raise a concern.

"I know," Honor said. "Phase one and phase three have a six-minute overlap. If phase one takes longer than projected�"

"The garrison's response patterns, based on Martingale's information and Buffy's observations, suggest initial response to an external perimeter engagement is approximately two to three minutes before elements are allocated to internal security," LaFollet said. "That gives phase three a window."

"That gives phase three a very precise window," McKeon said.

"Yes." Honor looked at Buffy. "Can you work a precise window?"

Buffy thought about how to answer that. The honest answer was that she'd been working precise windows since she was sixteen, that the Slayer's entire operational existence was built around precise windows because the things she dealt with didn't give you the luxury of imprecise ones. But she'd learned that the way she expressed confidence about her own capabilities required calibration based on her audience, because there was a version of that answer that sounded like arrogance and a version that sounded like information and the difference was mostly delivery.

"Eight minutes from phase one execution to ready-to-lift," she said. "That's my commitment."

Honor nodded. "Lift coordinates?"

Scotty unfolded the map they'd built from Buffy's observations and Martingale's knowledge and three weeks of looking at the available satellite data. "There's a clearing approximately two kilometers northeast of the compound. Large enough for both shuttles and the pinnace with margin."

"That's the rally point," Honor said. "We don't wait. If someone doesn't make the rally point within fifteen minutes of lift�"

"You go," Buffy said. "I'll find my own way up."

The room looked at her.

"The planet isn't the problem," she said. "I can survive down here indefinitely. If something goes wrong and I miss the window, you go. Come back if you can. Don't stay trying to fix a situation that can't be fixed from the ground."

"Buffy�" Scotty started.

"That's the plan," she said. "I'm not negotiating it." She looked at Honor. "You know I'm right."

Honor held her gaze for a moment. "Yes," she said. "I know." She looked around the room. "That's the plan. We execute at 0200 four days from now. Rest, prepare, be ready." She paused. "Questions."

Martingale, who had been cleared for the briefing and who had the expression of someone who had been filing information under 'things that will be useful when I'm out of here' for the past six days, raised her hand slightly. "The pinnace's maintenance status is still unknown."

"Yes," Honor said. "We're assuming it's been resolved. If it hasn't�" she paused for one fraction of a second�"we adapt."

The room absorbed this and moved on, which was, Buffy thought, the right thing to do with unknowns that couldn't be resolved in advance. You named them. You accepted them. You went anyway.

She'd been doing that for two years.

She was getting better at the accepting part.

~~~

Two nights before the operation she had a dream that was hers.

Not the Girl's running memories. Not the ancient dark and the fire and the men who spoke in proto-language. Her own, with her own sky over her own town, the specific amber of Sunnydale streetlights on the sidewalk outside Revello Drive. Her mother in the lit window.

She woke up and the grief was there in the way it always was, present and exact, the specific weight of a specific absence. She'd learned not to fight it. You didn't fight grief, that was the lesson that took the longest to learn, you just let it be what it was and breathed around it and waited for it to become background again.

She lay in the dark and breathed and thought about Joyce Summers who did not know where her daughter was and might never know, who had worried, who had been the one reliable thing in two years of unreliable things.

She thought: I'm okay, Mom. I'm doing something important and I'm okay.

She didn't know if that was true. She thought it was becoming true.

She got up before the camp was awake and sat outside and Nimitz appeared from somewhere, having apparently decided at some point that she warranted the same supervision as Honor, and sat beside her on the root with his tail wrapped around his feet and his eyes steady.

She looked at him. He looked at her.

"Do you actually know what I'm feeling?" she asked. "Or is that a Grayson belief system thing that happens to be correct?"

He tilted his head and made a sound she didn't have words for.

"Right," she said. "Fair."
