She thought about the Girl, who had also not had words for a lot of things, whose world had been prelinguistic in some fundamental way, who had navigated existence through a kind of direct knowing that Buffy had access to in flashes and which the merging had made more consistent if not entirely fluent.

She thought about what it meant to be the Slayer, at this particular remove from everything the Slayer had originally been for. The Hellmouth was gone, or not gone but two thousand years and several light-years in every direction unreachable. The Council was gone. The infrastructure of her calling had been built for a specific context and that context no longer applied.

What remained was the thing itself, under all the context. The capacity. The speed and the strength and the sense and the healing and the two-thousand-year lineage of women who had been exactly this specific kind of instrument.

And the choice about what to do with it.

She'd always had that choice, technically. The Council had spent considerable effort convincing her otherwise. But she'd known it was there, the knowledge you couldn't unknow once you'd seen it, that the Slayer's power was the Slayer's power and not the Council's, and the only authority that could direct it was the one that lived inside her skin.

Here, with the Council gone and the Hellmouth irrelevant and two thousand years of accumulated consequence fallen away, the choice was all that remained.

She thought about what she was choosing.

Nimitz made a sound she thought she could almost interpret as approval.

"Don't read my mind," she said.

His tail tip moved.

"I'm serious."

He put his head under her hand and she scratched between his ears because there was apparently no version of herself that could resist a small warm creature requesting that, and the grief was still there but it was background now, doing the thing grief did when you gave it space, becoming part of the texture of things rather than the loudest thing in the room.

The camp began to wake up.

She felt it before she heard it, the shift in the pressure of occupied space as people moved from sleep to wakefulness, the small sounds of morning routine beginning, someone in the second shuttle heating water for the tea that was running low and would be gone in another week, the specific footstep pattern of LaFollet starting his perimeter walk.

And then, softer than LaFollet and quieter than McKeon and quieter still than Jamie but not quiet enough, the footsteps she'd learned to recognize at four meters.

"You're getting better," she said, without turning around.

Scotty sat down on the other side of Nimitz, who regarded him with green-eyed neutrality. "Or you're still just better than me."

"Both can be true."

He looked at the gap in the canopy where the stars were fading. Morning was coming in shades of gray, the jungle's nighttime sounds giving way to the daytime ones. "Two days," he said.

"Two days."

He was quiet for a moment. Nimitz, apparently satisfied with the situation, settled his weight more firmly against Buffy's side. "Can I ask you something?"

"You keep asking that."

"And you keep letting me ask it."

She considered this. "Yeah. Go ahead."

"What happens after we get off the planet?"

It was the question under the operation, under all the planning and preparation and tactical consideration. The operation had an end state. The end state was getting off Hades. After that was a different kind of question.

"Depends on what Honor decides," she said. "We get back to Manticoran space, there are about a thousand things that have to happen before I figure out what comes next."

"But you have a what-comes-next."

She thought about it. "I have a direction," she said. "That's different from a plan but it's more than I had six weeks ago."

He looked at her. In the early morning light his face was the face of someone who'd been awake for a while and was not trying to hide it. "What's the direction?"

She looked at the fading stars. Sirius, still there, still the brightest thing in that part of the sky. "Something worth fighting for," she said. "I've been fighting to prevent things for two years. That's important, I'm not saying it isn't. But�" she paused, looking for the word. "I want to fight for something. Not just against things."

He was quiet for a moment. Then he said, "That seems like a direction worth following."

She looked at him.

He looked back.

Neither of them said anything else. The morning came in fully and the camp woke up around them and Nimitz eventually extracted himself and went to find Honor, and the day began, two days before the operation, ordinary in all the ways that days were ordinary and extraordinary in the way that all of them were, now, which was that they were still here and still themselves and still choosing.

That, she thought, was enough to work with.

~~~

The night before the operation nobody slept much.

She could tell by the quality of the camp's silence. Not the deep-cycle silence of genuine sleep but the lighter, intermittent silence of people moving in and out of rest, surfacing and submerging, the body's attempt to restore itself running up against the brain's insistence on running the operation forward and backward and sideways.

She didn't try to sleep. She sat in the shuttle with Martingale, who had the operational role of staying with the shuttle as flight crew, and they talked about nothing of consequence for two hours, which was its own kind of preparation.

"You're not scared," Martingale said.

"I'm always scared," Buffy said. "I've just been scared enough times that it doesn't feel like new information."

Martingale looked at her. "You're seventeen."

"I know."

"That's�" Martingale stopped.

"I know," Buffy said again. "It was a lot."

At midnight she checked every piece of equipment she'd be taking in. The knife. The two pieces of cord that had more uses than their appearance suggested. The communicator. The small pen-light she'd never used but which Scotty had pressed on her with the expression of someone who had thought carefully about contingencies. She checked them with the focused attention of ritual, not because she was uncertain about their condition but because the checking was its own kind of preparation, the body doing something concrete in advance of the thing itself.

At 0100 she went to find Honor.

Honor was awake. Of course Honor was awake. She was sitting in the forward section of the lead shuttle with Nimitz asleep across her legs and the operational map open on the display in front of her and the expression of someone who had already run every scenario available to them and was now simply waiting.

Buffy sat down across from her.

"Did you need something?" Honor asked.

"No." She looked at the map. The compound in its rough outline, the perimeter positions, the landing pad with its two pinnaces. "Just didn't feel like sitting alone."

Honor looked at her. Then she looked back at the map. "Tell me about Sunnydale," she said.

Buffy looked at her.

"You've told bits of it. To different people. I've heard pieces." Honor's voice was even. "Tell me the whole thing. From the beginning."

So Buffy told her. From the beginning, from the fire at Hemery and the first Watcher and the first time she'd understood what she was and what it was going to cost her, through the move to Sunnydale and the library and the Scooby Gang and the Master and Angelus and all of it, the whole two years of it, the apocalypses and the losses and the thing with Angel at the end that she hadn't fully told anyone, that she hadn't even fully told herself, and she told it to Honor Harrington on the night before the operation in the forward section of a shuttle on a prison planet two thousand years from everything she'd been talking about.

Honor listened. She asked questions that were exactly the right questions. She didn't flinch at the parts that warranted flinching. She didn't offer comfort she didn't have. She listened the way Buffy imagined good commanders listened when they needed to understand the full picture before they made a decision, with the complete attention of someone who understood that the information being offered was being offered at a cost.

When Buffy finished it was 0145.

Honor was quiet for a moment.

"You've been doing this since you were fifteen," she said.

"Yes."

"Alone, mostly."

"Mostly."

"And you're here."

Buffy looked at her. "I'm here."

Honor held her gaze. Nimitz opened one eye, regarded them both, and closed it again.

"Then we'll do what comes next," Honor said.

Buffy nodded.

It was time.

Chapter 14: At 0155 the camp was already empty in the ways that mattered.

McKeon's team had been in position for twenty minutes, working through the jungle on the route Buffy had mapped over three nights of observation. LaFollet's team had gone south, taking the longer approach that would put them in position at the east and south perimeter faces with enough margin to settle and breathe before the clock ran.

The two shuttles sat in the clearing with their engines in standby mode, Martingale in the lead and a young rating named Tomas Ibanez in the second whose hands Buffy had checked on the controls that afternoon and found steadier than his face suggested. Martingale had looked at Buffy doing this and not said anything, which Buffy appreciated.

Buffy and Scotty were at the tree line north of the compound at 0158.

Two minutes.

The compound sat in its nighttime configuration, the timer lights running their regular sweep, the guards at their posts. She'd watched this configuration for three nights and she knew it the way she knew the rhythms of things she'd lived alongside long enough to internalize. The guard at the northeast corner would complete his turn in forty seconds. The gap in the perimeter grid would open in sixty-three seconds. The guard inside the landing pad fence had a habit of checking his communicator at intervals of approximately twelve minutes and was due for one in about four, which would put his attention down for approximately thirty seconds.

She knew all of this and it was not enough and it was all she had.

"Clock," she said.

"Ninety seconds," Scotty said.

She breathed.

The thing about operations, the thing that nobody who hadn't done them could fully understand, was the quality of the time immediately before. Not the fear, which was present but manageable, not the tactical calculations, which were complete. The quality of the waiting itself. It had a texture unlike any other kind of waiting. It was the moment before the door opened, and what was on the other side of the door was not knowable and had never been knowable and you went through anyway.

She'd been going through anyway since she was fifteen.

"Sixty seconds," Scotty said.

She felt the shift in him beside her, not visible but present, the same way she felt weather fronts before they arrived. The compression of a person gathering themselves.

"Hey," she said.

He looked at her.

"You're going to be fine."

He held her gaze for a moment. "I know," he said. And then, because he was Scotty Tremaine and apparently couldn't help it: "Are you?"

She almost smiled. "I'm always fine."

"Thirty seconds."

McKeon's first device went off on schedule, south side of the compound, the compression reaching them two seconds after the light. The compound lights snapped to full. She watched the guards move, watched the specific choreography of a unit responding to a threat signal, professional and trained and directing themselves toward the south.

The perimeter grid shimmered and cycled and the gap opened.

"Now," she said.

They ran.

The gap was ninety seconds and they were through it in twelve, which left seventy-eight seconds of grace that she didn't use for comfort because the next obstacle was thirty meters ahead and grace ran out without warning. The inner fence was fixed rather than electrified, which she'd confirmed on the second night. She went over it in one movement, felt Scotty land beside her a beat later, kept moving.

The landing pad was visible ahead across one hundred and sixty meters of open ground that was currently unoccupied because everyone capable of occupying it had been pulled toward the south perimeter.

Currently.

"Move," she said, and they moved.

She counted as she ran. Not steps, not time exactly, something more integrated than either, the Slayer sense tracking the compound's attention distribution the way sonar tracked volume in water. The pressure was south. The compound's focus was south. It would not stay south indefinitely.

Eighty meters.

A second device went off, east perimeter, LaFollet's team coming online.

Fifty meters.

The guard inside the landing pad fence turned from the southeast, where the second explosion had drawn his eyes, and turned back toward the pad and saw them.

He was good. She gave him that. He didn't freeze and he didn't shout, he just raised his weapon and she was already past the point where changing course was an option so she committed to the line she was on and covered the last twenty meters faster than she'd been moving before.

The weapon discharged once.

She felt it as a line of heat across her left shoulder, nothing structural, flesh only, the kind of wound that registered as information rather than impairment. She put the guard down with the minimal efficient contact of someone who had learned that the goal was to stop the immediate problem without creating a larger one, and he went down, and the weapon didn't discharge again.

She was at the pinnace.

"Scotty."

He was already at the hatch. The guard's shot had missed him by enough that she could see him registering the near-miss in the way of a person cataloguing an experience for later, when there was time for it. Right now he was focused on the hatch mechanism, his hands moving with the quick certainty of a pilot who knew what he was looking for.

"Thirty seconds," he said.

"We have less than that."

"I know."

She turned and put her back to the pinnace hull and read the compound. The guard she'd put down was out. The shot had been heard, she could tell by the shift in pressure from the south side, someone breaking from the perimeter engagement and turning this direction. Two people. Maybe three.

"Scotty."

"Twenty seconds."

The hatch opened.

She went through it and pulled the guard's body inside with her because leaving it on the pad complicated things and Scotty was already in the pilot's seat and the hatch sealed behind her and the board was lighting up with the speed of someone who had done a preflight in conditions that didn't allow for thoroughness.

"The leg," she said.

"Reads green." His hands were moving. "Either they fixed it or the sensor's lying. We're going to find out."

The compound outside was audible even through the hull, a different kind of activity than before, more directed. Someone had found the empty guard post. Someone would find the guard in a few minutes. She had the gun she'd taken from him and the knife and Scotty's sidearm and eight minutes on the clock had become four.

"Talk to me," she said.

"Engines are up. Navigation online." A pause. "Comms are being jammed. McKeon got the array."

"Can you still coordinate with the shuttles?"

"Short range local, yes. Not going through the garrison system." He looked at the board and then out through the viewport at the landing pad and the two garrison personnel who had just appeared at the fence. "We need to go."

"The shuttles aren't at the rally point yet."

"I know. But if we wait for the shuttles�"

She looked at the fence. The two garrison personnel had been joined by a third. One of them was making the calculation she'd have made in their position, which was that the pinnace was the priority, and moving to the pad access gate with the specific purposeful walk of someone about to prevent a departure.

"Go," she said.

The pinnace lifted on thrusters, the landing leg holding, and she had a half-second of relief about the maintenance status before the compound's second pinnace began powering up across the pad and the calculation changed again.

"The other one," Scotty said.

"I see it."

"If it's combat-capable�"

"Is this pinnace combat-capable?"

"It has defensive systems. Whether they're active and loaded is a different question."

She looked at the board she didn't know how to read. "Tell me which thing to press."

"I need thirty seconds to get us clear of the compound before I can activate anything or we'll be firing into the garrison infrastructure."

"Then get us clear."

He got them clear. The pinnace lifted in the controlled urgent arc of a pilot who was doing this without the safety margins that made the maneuver comfortable and knew it and had decided comfort was not currently the priority. Buffy held the copilot's seat and watched the compound shrink below them and the second pinnace's thrusters light up and tracked the slope of their own climb against the slope of the intercept vector that was forming in her head.

"They're faster," she said.

"We have a head start."

"How much does that matter?"

A brief pause. "Some."

The communicator crackled. Martingale's voice, compressed and efficient: "Pinnace one, this is shuttle lead. We're lifting now. Rally point in four minutes."

"Copy," Scotty said. "We have a trailer."

A pause. "Understood. What's your status?"

"Moving. Defensive systems coming online." His hands moved across the board. "Buffy, the panel to your left. Red cover. Lift it."

She lifted it.

"Two switches. Top one is shields. Bottom is�don't touch the bottom one yet."

She hit the top one.

The board registered something. She didn't know what it registered but Scotty's shoulders dropped approximately two millimeters, which she'd come to understand was his version of visible relief.

"Shields are up," he said. "They won't stop a sustained engagement but they'll buy us time."

The second pinnace was climbing. She could see it through the aft viewport, the garrison vessel running hard on an intercept angle that she didn't have the navigational vocabulary to fully analyze but which her threat sense read clearly enough.

"The bottom switch," she said.

"That's weapons. I need another forty seconds to get target lock. If I fire before lock we're just lighting up the sky."

"They're going to close in forty seconds."

"I know." His voice had the quality she recognized from Honor, the quality of someone working a problem in real time without the performance of working a problem. Just doing it. "I need you to do something."

"Tell me."

"The panel on your right. Bottom row. Three green lights in a sequence. Tell me if the middle one goes amber."

She looked at the panel. Three green lights. "What does amber mean?"

"That their targeting system has a lock on us."

She watched the lights. "Still green."

"Their systems may be older than ours. Or their operator is still calibrating." A pause. "Or they're waiting until they have a clean shot."

"Which of those is worst?"

"The third one."

The middle light went amber.

"Amber," she said.

The pinnace jinked hard left, a movement that did things to her inner ear she hadn't known were possible, and something detonated in the space where they'd been that lit up the viewport in a blue-white wash that faded fast.

"That was a graser," Scotty said, very calmly.

"Is that bad?"

"It's a directed-energy weapon. If it had hit us it would have been very bad." His hands moved. "Target lock in ten seconds."

She watched the amber light. It was cycling, she realized, on and off, the garrison pinnace's operator recalibrating after the evasion.

"The shuttles," she said.

"I see them." The communicator crackled again and she could hear Martingale's voice but not the words clearly. Scotty answered in a clipped exchange she couldn't follow fully but which resulted in a change in his heading that moved them toward the shuttles' position rather than away from it.

"You're using us as cover," she said.

"I'm using the shuttles as a split targeting problem. Two targets is harder than one." He looked at the board. "Lock. Bottom switch."

She hit it.

The pinnace shuddered with the recoil of something she couldn't see, a compression that registered in her chest more than her ears, and the aft viewport lit up in a different color than before.

"Hit?" she asked.

"Hit." A pause. "Non-critical. Their shields are better than ours." He looked at the board. "But now they know we can return fire. That changes their calculation."

It did. She could see it in the way the garrison pinnace's approach vector changed, the flat aggressive line becoming something more cautious, buying range.

"How long until we're out of their effective engagement envelope?" she asked.

"Two minutes. Maybe less if they keep backing off."

The communicator crackled. Martingale's voice, clearer now: "Pinnace one, shuttle two reports thruster variance. Ibanez is compensating but we should be aware."

Scotty and Buffy looked at each other.

"The thruster," he said.

"I know."

"If it fails during the climb�"

"How far are we from the hyper limit?" she asked, which was a question she'd heard enough times in the briefings to know was relevant even without fully understanding what it meant.

"Hours. We're not going for the hyper limit. We're going for minimum safe distance to send an emergency signal and wait for pickup." He paused. "Or we were."

She thought about Ibanez, who had steady hands and a face that didn't match, sitting in the pilot's seat of a shuttle with a thruster that was running variance. She thought about the garrison pinnace behind them, backing off but not gone. She thought about the plan they'd made and the plans that plans became when contact with the actual situation occurred.

"What's the call?" she asked.

He was already making it, she could see it in the way he was working the board, running calculations she didn't have the background to follow. "If we push hard and the thruster holds, we can get enough distance in about forty minutes to be out of their practical interdiction range. If it fails before that�" He stopped.

"Say the rest."

"If it fails before that and they're still in the envelope, we're looking at a difficult conversation."

"Define difficult."

"The kind where Martingale and Ibanez and however many people fit in a crippled shuttle are sitting still while a garrison pinnace decides what to do with them."

She looked at the garrison pinnace, which had stopped backing off and was now holding at range, running its own calculations. She thought about what she knew about the new commandant, younger and more ideologically committed and less operationally experienced. She thought about what more ideological and less experienced meant for the decision to fire on a crippled shuttle versus board it versus let it go.

"They won't fire on a crippled shuttle," she said.

"No?"

"The new commandant wants prisoners. He's been running an interrogation operation for four months. He doesn't want to destroy assets, he wants to exploit them." She watched the garrison pinnace. "If the thruster goes, they'll board."

"And then they have Martingale back. And McKeon's team if they make the shuttles. And everyone else."

"Yes."

"So we need the thruster to hold."

"Or we need them not to have the option to board."

He looked at her. "The second option involves me doing something to a crewed vessel that I am not authorized to do and which would end the career of anyone who did it in a court martial context."

"I know."

"I'm not going to do it."

"I know you're not," she said. "I wasn't suggesting you should." She looked at the garrison pinnace. "I'm suggesting that if it becomes necessary, it won't be your call."

He held her gaze for a moment. Then he looked back at the board. "The thruster needs to hold," he said.

"Yeah," she said. "It does."

It held.

Not cleanly. The variance Ibanez had reported became a periodic fluctuation that put everyone on their respective comms at intervals, and once it became a full dropout that lasted eleven seconds during which the shuttle fell two hundred meters in the climb profile before the thruster caught again and Ibanez's voice on the communicator was the voice of a man who had just experienced something very clarifying and was processing it in real time. But it held.

The garrison pinnace tracked them to the edge of its practical envelope and then peeled off, the calculation having resolved in the direction Buffy had expected. No firing solution that didn't risk the target. No boarding option without coming inside the range of the other pinnace's weapons. A new commandant running his first actual operation, against opponents who had already demonstrated more capability than his briefing material had predicted, making the choice that preserved his remaining assets.

She watched it go.

"They'll report our vector," Scotty said.

"Can they catch us?"

"Not with anything that's currently in this system. If they get a signal out before McKeon's jamming effect lapses, they could have someone at our estimated position in�" he calculated�"sixteen to twenty hours. Depends on what assets are available."

"And McKeon's jamming."

"Bought us another hour, maybe two. After that their comms are back up."

She did the math. "So we need to be somewhere they can't reach in sixteen hours."

"We need to be picked up by someone they can't casually engage in sixteen hours," he corrected. "Which means we need the emergency signal to reach someone useful."

"Honor."

He nodded. "Honor. She knows who to call."

She thought about Honor in the second shuttle, which she hadn't been in when the plan went because the plan had her in the lead shuttle with Martingale and the lead shuttle was in the air and climbing. She pulled up the communicator. "Shuttle lead, this is pinnace one. What's Steadholder Harrington's status?"

Martingale's voice: "Aboard shuttle two with McKeon's team. They made the rally point with two minutes to spare."

She let out a breath she hadn't been tracking.

"Everyone?" she said.

A pause that was slightly too long. "LaFollet's team has one casualty. Non-critical. Ensign Clinkscales took a graze to the right arm during the perimeter withdrawal."

She closed her eyes for one second. Opened them. "Understood. Status of the casualty?"

"Walking, talking, and arguing with Surgeon-Lieutenant Martingale about whether he needs to sit down." A beat. "I'm going to suggest he sits down."

She almost smiled. "Tell him that's an order."

"Aye, pinnace one."

She put the communicator down and looked at the viewport, which showed the planet below them in its nighttime face, the continent they'd been on a dark mass shot through with the faint lights of the garrison compound and the landing pad and the diminishing spark of the garrison pinnace returning to its base.

"Carson took a hit," she said.

"I heard." Scotty's voice was even. "He's all right."

"He's seventeen."

"So are you."

She looked at the planet. "I know. That's why I said it."

He was quiet for a moment. The board hummed its various technical humming. The planet fell away beneath them at the rate of a stressed thruster working harder than its maintenance history recommended.

"First time someone you were responsible for got hit?" he asked.

"No." She watched the planet. "Every time feels like the first time though."

He didn't try to fix that. She appreciated it.

~~~

They found Honor on the communicator twelve minutes later.

Her voice was the same on a crackling short-range signal from a stressed shuttle as it was in a briefing room, which was to say level and precise and carrying its priorities in the order of their importance. "Pinnace one. Good to hear you."

"Good to be heard," Scotty said. "We have a window of approximately ninety minutes before the garrison gets their comms back. After that we're visible."

"Understood. I'm putting together the signal package now. Do you have a preference for who picks it up?"

"Anything Manticoran," Scotty said.

"That may not be the fastest option depending on disposition of forces." A pause that carried the sound of Honor doing several things simultaneously. "I have a contact. She'll be in range to receive inside the window if we boost the signal."

"Who?"

"Someone who will come." The simplicity of it was complete. "Get your people comfortable. We may be waiting a few hours after contact."

"The shuttle's thruster�"

"I know. I heard Ibanez's reports. If it becomes critical we redistribute personnel to the pinnace and the lead shuttle and Ibanez puts the second shuttle in as stable an orbit as it'll hold." Another pause. "How's Scotty?"

Buffy looked at Scotty. He raised an eyebrow. "Tell her I'm fine," he said.

"He says he's fine," Buffy said.

"He would." A warmth in Honor's voice that didn't soften its edges, the way the warmth always operated in her register, foundational rather than surface. "And our other crew member?"

"Also fine," Buffy said. "Left shoulder's already dealt with."

A brief silence. "You were hit."

"Graze. It's been healed for an hour."

"Fritz is going to want to know that."

"Fritz is going to have to get in line." She looked at the planet, now visibly curved at the edges, the atmosphere a thin skin of light at the horizon. "Honor. Your signal contact. She'll actually come?"

"She'll actually come." No hesitation. "Get some rest if you can. I'll call when we have a response."

The channel closed.

Scotty set the pinnace to its minimum maintenance orbit, a long ellipse that kept them moving without requiring active thrust, and then he leaned back in the pilot's seat and looked at the overhead and exhaled.

"Good flying," Buffy said.

"Good shooting."

"I pressed a switch."

"At exactly the right moment." He looked at her. "How's the shoulder actually?"

"Fine actually." She rolled it experimentally. The wound was closed. She could feel the deep tissue still knitting, that faint internal pressure that was the healing factor working through the layers, but the surface was sealed and nothing was compromised. "It'll be fully done in another hour."

He shook his head slowly, not for the first time, in the way of someone who had not yet finished adjusting to the specific reality of her biology. "Does it hurt?"

"While it's healing, a little. Like a bruise." She considered. "Less than it should, probably. I think the pain response is dialed down somehow. I've never been sure whether that's a feature or a design flaw."

"Sounds like a feature to me."

"Unless it means I miss something that's actually serious because it doesn't hurt enough to register." She paused. "That's the argument Fritz makes. That the reduced pain sensitivity is an operational advantage that creates a diagnostic blind spot."

"Is he right?"

"Probably. He usually is." She leaned back in the copilot's seat and looked at the overhead alongside him. The pinnace hummed. Through the viewport the planet turned. "Scotty."

"Mm."

"Thank you. For the after thing you mentioned. For being good at it."

He turned his head to look at her. "We're not in the after yet."

"I know. But I wanted to say it before we get there, in case it's harder to say then."

He held her gaze for a moment with an expression she was getting better at reading, the one that was taking something in without making a production of receiving it. "Okay," he said. "You're welcome."

She looked back at the overhead.

"Buffy."

"Mm."

"For what it's worth." He paused, choosing words with the care of someone who'd thought about how to say something and was now doing the saying. "Whatever comes after this. The Manticore part, the figuring-out-what-you-are part, all of it. You don't have to do that alone either."

She was quiet for a moment.

"That's a significant offer," she said.

"I know."

"You don't fully know what you're offering yet."

"I know that too." His voice was even. "I'm offering anyway."

The planet turned. The pinnace hummed. She thought about the long shape of the thing ahead of them, the after that was building itself on the other side of this operation, all the versions of herself she was going to have to negotiate between on the way to whatever she settled into.

"Okay," she said.

Chapter 15: Honor's contact responded in forty-seven minutes.

The signal came back in the clipped encoded format of a military vessel responding to an emergency beacon, and it was only when Scotty had run it through the pinnace's decoder and stared at the result for a moment that his face did something she hadn't seen it do before, which was a particular species of relief that was also something else she couldn't name.

"What?" she said.

"It's the Prince Adrian's senior surviving officer," he said. "She's been looking for us."

"How long?"

"Three weeks." He looked at the signal readout. "She's been running a search grid." A pause. "There are other ships with her."

The something else she couldn't name on his face resolved itself. She thought it was the specific emotion of finding out that the world had been looking for you when you'd been operating as though the world had moved on.

She took that in without comment and let him have it.

~~~

The pickup took six hours.

During those six hours the garrison got their communications back, as projected, and sent a signal that was received by several parties including the incoming rescue force, which turned out to be three Manticoran vessels operating under a commander who had been conducting an extended search and who received the garrison's claims about escaped prisoners and the destruction of garrison property with the professional neutrality of someone making note of information they planned to address at the appropriate time and place.

The appropriate time and place not being right now.

The shuttle's thruster gave out completely at the four-hour mark, which meant redistributing everyone to the pinnace and the lead shuttle per Honor's contingency plan and leaving the second shuttle in the elliptical orbit that Ibanez had set, Ibanez himself being the last person off and managing this transition with the careful dignity of a man saying goodbye to something he'd been responsible for, which in a sense he had been.

She watched him close the shuttle's hatch from the outside for the last time.

She thought about Sunnydale. About the things you left behind when the contingency plan executed and there was no going back for them.

She went and stood beside him while he was watching the shuttle recede on the cameras.

He was nineteen and from a Grayson town she couldn't pronounce and had never intended to end up on a prison planet, and none of that was visible on his face right now, which was doing what Grayson faces did when Grayson men felt things they'd been taught not to put where others could see them.

She didn't say anything. She just stood next to him while the shuttle drifted.

After a while he said, quietly, "My grandfather's picture was in my kit bag."

"I'm sorry," she said.

"It's just a picture."

"I know. It's still the picture."

He was quiet for a moment. Then he said, "Thank you, Lady Buffy. For getting us out."

She thought about the things she could say in response to that. She thought about the things she actually believed.

"You did most of it," she said. "I just moved faster."

~~~

The rescue ships were called the Wayfarer, the HMS Mordred, and a third vessel whose name she couldn't parse from the signal that turned out to be a converted freighter with a Grayson crew and a name in the Grayson theological tradition that Carson said meant approximately Steadfast in Grace.

She thought that was a good name for a ship that had spent three weeks searching.

They were received aboard the Wayfarer first, the largest of the three, and the process of coming aboard a Manticoran warship for the first time was so thoroughly overwhelming that she simply turned off the parts of her brain that were trying to catalog and contextualize everything and moved through it on instinct, following where she was led, answering the questions she was asked in the brief direct fashion that medical intake procedures seemed to prefer regardless of what century you were in.

Fritz materialized at her elbow at the medical intake point with the focused expression of a man who had been waiting for exactly this moment for several weeks. "Left shoulder," he said, without preamble.

"It's healed."

"I'd like to confirm that independently."

"Fritz."

"Lady Buffy." He held her gaze with the steady professional courtesy that characterized all of his interactions with her, the courtesy that existed in a separate compartment from his curiosity and therefore never felt contaminated by it. "It will take ten minutes. The information is important for your long-term care."

She thought about arguing. She thought about how much she'd appreciated having someone who paid attention to her as a patient rather than as a category.

"Ten minutes," she said.

It took twelve. He confirmed the wound was fully closed and made notes she didn't try to see and then he looked up and said, "You should eat. You've been running high demand for several hours and the caloric debt is real even if the symptoms aren't obvious yet."

She thought about the last time she'd eaten. "When did I eat?"

"Your last meal before the operation was approximately nine hours ago." He said it in the tone of someone who had been tracking this and was unsurprised by her inability to recall it. "I'll arrange something."

"You don't have to�"

"I'm aware I don't have to." He began making the note. "I'm choosing to."

She looked at him. He looked at his notes.

"Fritz," she said.

He looked up.

"Thank you." She meant it in the way she'd meant it with Honor, the way that had weight. "For all of it. The paying attention."

He was quiet for a moment. Then: "You're a remarkable patient. In the clinical sense." A pause. "In the other sense as well, I think, though that's less within my professional purview."

She smiled. She thought it was maybe the first fully unguarded smile she'd produced since arriving in this century. It felt like something returning from a long distance.

He went to arrange food. She went to find the others.

~~~

The reunion happened in a wardroom on deck six.

She stood in the doorway for a moment before entering, watching it unfold, not because she was excluded but because she wasn't yet part of the specific vocabulary of this reunion and she knew it and respected the knowledge.

Honor and the Wayfarer's commander, a woman with iron-gray hair and twenty years of shared history in the way she crossed the wardroom and took Honor's hands. McKeon and the Mordred's captain, who had apparently served together somewhere and were doing the thing where you registered that the other person was alive and intact with the specific thoroughness of people who had been uncertain about it for a while. Carson and Ibanez and two others from LaFollet's team being met by a rating who was very young and very visibly relieved in the way that only people waiting for specific people could be.

Martingale, sitting slightly to the side with a mug of something hot and the expression of someone who was still completing the transition from four months of captivity to this, to a wardroom with carpeting and decent lighting and people who were glad to see them.

LaFollet, standing near the door on the other side from Buffy with his arm in a field dressing and his expression doing the Grayson thing, which was covering whatever was most present with the professional presentation until the professional presentation became its own kind of honesty.

She looked at him. He looked at her.

"Ensign," she said.

"Lady Buffy." He looked at her left shoulder. "You were hit."

"So were you."

"My duty is your protection. Yours is not my protection."

"That's an interesting way to frame a situation where I'm the one with the healing factor." She looked at the dressing on his arm. "How's the arm?"

"Functional."

"Fritz should look at it."

"Surgeon-Lieutenant Martingale already looked at it."

"And?"

"She said it would heal without complication." He paused. "She was considerably more certain about your shoulder's healing timeline than mine."

"Different biology." She looked at him steadily. "Andrew. You did the job tonight. Everyone got out."

Something moved behind his eyes. "Ensign Clinkscales�"

"Has a graze. Which he is going to recover from completely." She held his gaze. "You brought everyone out. That counts."

He was quiet for a moment. Then he did a thing she hadn't seen him do before, which was that the professional presentation relaxed by approximately two degrees, just enough to let her see what was underneath, which was exhaustion and relief in roughly equal measure.

"Yes," he said. "It does."

She went into the wardroom.

Carson found her before she found him, appearing at her side with his arm in a sling and his hair doing something that suggested the operation had not allowed for grooming, and said, "Lady Buffy," in a voice that was doing its level best to be even and not entirely succeeding.

"You got grazed," she said.

"I'm aware."

"Next time you're in a perimeter withdrawal�"

"I know. I was moving and I was watching the wrong angle." He said it with the directness of someone who had already done the self-debrief. "I know what I did wrong."

She looked at him. The arm. The face underneath the arm. The specific quality of a seventeen-year-old boy who had been in a firefight and come out the other side and was now standing in a wardroom on a Manticoran warship doing the inventory of what that meant.

"Carson," she said.

He looked at her.

"You're okay," she said. Not as reassurance. As information.

He took a breath. "Yes," he said. "I am."

She put her hand on his good shoulder briefly, the way you touched someone when words were doing all they could and the rest needed to be something more direct, and then she let go and they stood in the wardroom together while the reunion finished itself around them.

Scotty found her near the end, when the wardroom had mostly sorted itself into smaller conversations and someone had produced food from somewhere that was not a ration pack and the collective emotional temperature had come down from acute to something more sustainable.

He stood beside her with a plate of something she couldn't identify but which smelled considerably better than the past three weeks had smelled.

"Eat," he said.

"Fritz already told me that."

"Fritz is right."

She took the plate. She ate. The thing she couldn't identify turned out to be some variety of protein in a sauce that was not complicated but was genuinely hot and seasoned and her body's response to it was essentially a standing ovation from the parts that had been running on ration packs and adrenaline and healing-factor fuel demands for a month.

"Good?" he asked.

"Really good." She looked at the plate. "What is this?"

"Officer's mess version of a stew. Wayfarer's cook is apparently exceptional by warship standards."

"What are warship standards usually?"

"Functional." He paused. "This is better than functional."

She ate. He ate. Around them the wardroom held the specific warmth of people who had been through something together and were now on the other side of it, in a room with carpeting and decent lighting and food that was better than functional.

She thought about the after.

She thought about what Scotty had said in the pinnace, the offering he'd made that he knew wasn't fully informed and had made anyway. She thought about what it meant to accept something like that and what accepting it would cost and what refusing it would cost and which cost she was more willing to pay.

She thought about two thousand years and the stars and the Slayer's lineage running through her like a current and Carson saying she would see Grayson with a certainty that was not bravado.

She thought about Joyce Summers in the lit window of a house she would never see again and she let that sit and she breathed through it and she let it become background the way she'd learned to let the necessary things become background, present and real and not the loudest thing in the room.

The loudest thing in the room was this. The wardroom and the people in it and the food and the specific quality of the air on a Manticoran warship which was different from any air she'd breathed in weeks and which meant something, the physicality of it, the presence in a new space.

A new space.

She looked around the wardroom. At Honor and the iron-haired commander. At McKeon. At Martingale, who had nearly finished her mug of something and was beginning to look like a person who might sleep tonight. At Carson and Ibanez and LaFollet. At Scotty, who was looking at her without pretending he wasn't.

She thought: this is what after looks like.

It didn't look like any particular thing. It looked like a wardroom and stew and people who were tired and relieved and alive. It looked like the next thing, which was already present even before the current thing was fully finished, the way the next thing always was.

She'd been seventeen for a long time.

She thought it might be time to start the process of becoming eighteen.

"Hey," she said.

Scotty looked at her.

"Tell me about Manticore," she said. "What it looks like. Start from the beginning."

He looked at her for a moment with that expression she'd spent weeks learning to read, the one that received things without making a production of the receiving. Then he settled back in his chair and began.

She listened.

The wardroom held its warmth around them and outside the viewport the stars were all the wrong constellations and entirely present and she was here, in this specific place, in this specific moment, and that was enough.

That was, it turned out, quite a lot.

Chapter 16: Manticore was six weeks away at the speeds available to a convoy that included a converted freighter and a shuttle with a dead thruster being towed on a tractor field.

Six weeks was a significant amount of time.

Buffy had known this intellectually and was now discovering it experientially, which was a different kind of knowing. On Hades the time had been structured by necessity, by the operation and its planning and the daily demands of staying alive in a jungle on a prison planet. On the Wayfarer the structure was different. There was routine � the ship ran on watches and meals and drills regardless of its passengers � but the routine was not hers. She was cargo, in the most benevolent possible sense. She was being delivered somewhere.

The somewhere was still abstract.

She dealt with this the way she dealt with most things, which was to find the parts she could work with and work with those while the parts she couldn't work with yet resolved themselves in the background.

She ran. The Wayfarer had a gym that was significantly more sophisticated than anything she'd trained on for the past two years and significantly more sophisticated than the jungle at dawn, and she used it with an enthusiasm that attracted attention she hadn't intended to attract. After the third morning when she'd had an audience of off-watch crew members standing in the doorway with expressions ranging from fascinated to slightly alarmed, she'd mentioned it to Scotty, who had mentioned it to the Wayfarer's XO, who had quietly suggested that perhaps Lady Buffy might prefer the gym at 0400 when it was unoccupied.

She'd agreed and 0400 had become her time, which she used the way she used all the time that was hers, completely.

She spent mornings with Fritz, who had been given a corner of the Wayfarer's sickbay by the ship's surgeon, a dour man named Petrakis who had looked at Fritz's preliminary notes on Buffy's biology with the expression of someone confronting information that was going to require significant professional reclassification of everything he thought he knew. Fritz and Petrakis had developed a working relationship that Buffy thought of as productive friction, two precise minds with slightly different frameworks arguing toward a shared understanding of the specific puzzle she presented.

She let them argue. She answered their questions when they asked them and ignored the rest.

She spent afternoons with different people depending on the day, which was its own kind of structure. Martingale was recovering with the systematic efficiency of a doctor who knew exactly what her body needed and provided it without negotiation, and on the days when Martingale was up to talking they talked. Honor was often occupied with the kind of work that came from being Honor Harrington returning to Manticoran space with a story to tell that wasn't fully tellable yet, and on the days when she had time Buffy was one of the people she spent it with, though not always the first. McKeon was running the recovered personnel through operational debriefs in preparation for the formal debriefs that would happen when they reached Manticore, and he included Buffy in these with the same professional completeness with which he included everyone else, which she appreciated.

Carson was healing faster than his injury warranted, which Fritz noted with professional interest and Carson accepted with the equanimity of a young man from a religious tradition that had a category for Buffy's existence.

Ibanez had asked, very carefully, whether she would be willing to spar with him.

She'd said yes and then discovered that Ibanez was considerably better at hand-to-hand than his unassuming demeanor had suggested, and that this was because he'd grown up with three older brothers on a working ranch outside Mayhew, the Grayson capital, and had learned to fight before he'd learned to navigate in the polite professional world of the Navy. He lost to her, consistently and by significant margins, but he lost in ways that cost her something and he was learning from each loss rather than from his wins.

She liked that. She liked it enough that the sparring became another regular thing.

LaFollet observed the sparring from the first session with the expression of a man watching something he wanted to join and who had decided that asking would be professionally undignified. On the fourth session she invited him in without making it into anything, and he joined, and was better than Ibanez and worse than Scotty, which she also liked because it meant she had a range, a spectrum of opposition that each asked different things of her.

The Wayfarer's commander, whose name was Commodore Ellen Shaughnessy and whose iron-gray hair came with iron-gray opinions about most things, observed all of this from what she probably thought was a discreet distance and which was not discreet to Buffy's Slayer sense but which she respected because Shaughnessy was being respectful in return and that was the deal.

On the eleventh day Shaughnessy requested a meeting.

Not an order, a request, which told Buffy something about how Shaughnessy had decided to handle her, which was as a person of uncertain but non-trivial status who warranted courtesy rather than an entity to be categorized and managed. She went to the meeting.

Shaughnessy's day cabin was the cabin of a person who had been doing a specific job for a long time and had reached an accommodation with the space it provided, which was to say it was functional and arranged with the precision of established habit and contained on the desk a photograph of two children and a man who looked like he found Shaughnessy as interesting as she clearly found most things, which was quite interesting indeed.

"Lady Buffy," Shaughnessy said. "Please sit down."

She sat.

"I've been reading Fritz Montoya's preliminary notes," Shaughnessy said. "With his permission, and yours, I understand, for the medical content. Not the personal content."

"I said yes to the medical content," Buffy confirmed.

"Yes. I want to be clear that I've not exceeded that permission." Shaughnessy looked at her directly in the way of someone who was comfortable with direct looking. "I want to talk to you about what happens when we reach Manticore."

"I've been thinking about that too."

"I imagine you have." Shaughnessy folded her hands. "The situation is, frankly, unprecedented. In several directions simultaneously." She paused, choosing her next words with visible deliberateness. "You are, as I understand it, a person of Old Earth origin displaced approximately two thousand years from your time of origin. You have no documentation, no citizenship, no legal standing in any jurisdiction I'm aware of, and a biological profile that is�" she paused again.

"Weird," Buffy supplied.

"Significantly outside established human baselines," Shaughnessy said, apparently deciding that was the appropriate register. "When we arrive at Manticore there will be questions. From people with authority to ask them. How you answer those questions will have consequences."

"I know."

"Do you understand the specific political context?"

She thought about what she knew, which was the briefings she'd attended on the Wayfarer and the things Honor had told her in direct conversation and the things she'd pieced together from observation. "Manticore is at war. Honor is coming home after everyone thought she was dead. The political situation around that is complicated. My presence adds a variable that nobody has a ready category for."

Shaughnessy looked at her. "Succinctly, yes."

"What's your advice?"

Shaughnessy seemed to consider whether to give advice. Then she decided. "Let Honor take the lead on how you're introduced. Not because you can't speak for yourself. Because the first introduction establishes the frame, and Honor Harrington has political capital that you haven't had time to accumulate, and how you're framed in the first forty-eight hours will matter for a long time after."

"Is that what you're doing? Framing me?"

"No." Shaughnessy held her gaze. "I'm talking to you so that I understand what I'm dealing with. I'm going to be writing a report. I want the report to be accurate."

Buffy thought about that. "What do you think you're dealing with?"

Shaughnessy was quiet for a moment. "A young woman who has been through a great deal and who is considerably more composed about it than is statistically likely. Who is also, by Fritz Montoya's account and by my own observation, something outside my existing frame of reference." She paused. "And who, I think, is trying to figure out who she is in a context that didn't exist when she was originally herself."

Buffy looked at her.

"That's a reasonably accurate summary," she said.

"I thought it might be." Shaughnessy unfolded her hands. "For what it's worth. You're welcome on this ship. Not because of what you are or what you did on Hades. Because Steadholder Harrington trusts you. That's enough for me."

Buffy thought about what that meant, the specific weight of being trusted by someone whose trust was legible as currency to people like Shaughnessy.

"Thank you," she said.

Shaughnessy nodded once, the nod of someone for whom the relevant business had been concluded. "Is there anything you need?"

She thought about it. "Books," she said. "History. I have two thousand years of catching up to do."

Shaughnessy almost smiled. "I'll arrange access to the ship's library."

~~~

The library was large and she went through it with an appetite that surprised even her, which was saying something given that the past month had recalibrated her sense of what her appetites were capable of. She read history the way she'd read patrol routes, looking for the shape of things, the recurring patterns that let you predict the next iteration.

History, it turned out, was mostly the same several things happening repeatedly with different names and improved technology. There was some comfort in that. The specific texture of human catastrophe had changed but the underlying grammar was consistent. She could read it.

She read for four days straight with breaks only for eating and sleeping and training and Fritz's appointments, and by the end of the four days she had a working outline of two thousand years that was more skeleton than flesh but had enough structure to hang things on.

On the fifth day she went to find Honor.

Honor was in the flag captain's briefing room, which Shaughnessy had made available for her use, with a stack of work that had been forwarded from Manticore through the courier system and which had the general character of a mountain that had arrived while the mountain's usual handler had been presumed dead and had therefore accumulated without maintenance.

She looked up when Buffy came in.

"You've been reading," she said.

"How can you tell?"

"You have the look of someone who has been reading for four days." Honor set down the document she was holding. "What questions do you have?"

Buffy sat down. "All of them," she said. "But I'll start with the Solarian League."

Honor looked at her. Then she set down the rest of her work and sat back. "Start from what you know."

"Big. Old. Technically neutral but structurally not. Internal politics are�" she searched for the word she'd read� "sclerotic."

"That's accurate." Honor's voice had the quality it always had when she was genuinely engaged with a subject, which was slightly different from her usual precision, slightly more alive. "What else?"

"Their Office of Frontier Security is the part I don't understand. It's not military exactly but it has military capacity and it operates in areas outside Solarian direct governance and the relationship between what it's supposed to do and what it actually does is�"

"Exactly as corrupt as it sounds," Honor said. "Yes."

"Which means they're a factor in the war even though they're nominally neutral."

"They're a factor in everything. The question is always how much of a factor and in which direction." She paused. "You've been doing this for four days?"

"I have a lot of catching up to do."

Honor looked at her with the expression that had the layered quality, the one that was several things at once and that Buffy had spent weeks learning to parse. Right now it was something close to what McKeon's face did when he thought something was genuinely impressive and was being professional about it.

"You're going to be formidable," Honor said. "When you have the context."

"I'm already formidable," Buffy said.

"Yes. But right now you're formidable the way a very sharp sword is formidable in a room where nobody knows which end to hold." Honor's voice was entirely even. "Context is the handle."

Buffy thought about that. "Okay. Tell me about the Solarian League's relationship with Haven."

Honor told her.

They talked for three hours and by the end of it Buffy had more skeleton to hang things on and Honor had the look of someone who had also gotten something from the conversation, though what she'd gotten was less clear. Something about the act of explaining, maybe. Something about the particular kind of thinking you did when you had to make your implicit assumptions explicit for someone who didn't share your frame of reference.

"You're useful to talk to," Honor said, which was not the kind of compliment Honor gave carelessly.

"Because I don't know things?"

"Because you ask the questions that people who know things have stopped asking." She paused. "The questions that feel obvious. They're usually the most important ones."

Buffy thought about two years of asking questions that everyone around her had found too basic to take seriously until the answers turned out to matter. "I have a lot of practice being the person in the room who doesn't know things."

"How was that?"

"Annoying. Then useful." She paused. "Mostly annoying."

Honor almost smiled. "It gets less annoying."

"Does it get more useful?"

"Considerably."

~~~

On the twenty-second day out from Hades, Buffy had a dream that was neither hers nor the Girl's.

She was standing in a library that wasn't the Wayfarer's and wasn't the Sunnydale High library either, though it had elements of both, the specific spatial logic of dreams that wasn't spatial logic at all. The books on the shelves were written in a script she couldn't read. The light came from somewhere without a source.

A woman was sitting at the table in the center of the room.

Not the Girl. Not anyone Buffy had seen before, though she had the quality of someone Buffy was supposed to recognize. She was old in the way that wasn't about wrinkles or white hair but about something behind the eyes, the density of accumulated time.

"You're doing well," the woman said.

Buffy looked at her. "Who are you?"

"You don't need my name. You need to know you're doing well."

"That's not a useful answer."

"Most useful answers aren't satisfying. Most satisfying answers aren't useful." The woman looked at the books on the shelves. "You've been reading."

"Apparently I do that in my dreams too now."

"You're trying to understand where you are. That's correct. That's the right response." The woman looked back at her. "Some of them are going to try to make you into a symbol. You'll feel it. The pull toward the thing they want you to represent."

"I know that pull," Buffy said. "I've been the Slayer since I was fifteen."

"This will be different. The Slayer is one kind of symbol. What you're going to be here is another kind." The woman paused. "A symbol of the past, to the people who believe the past means something. A symbol of possibility, to the ones who think it means the future is open." She paused. "You're neither of those things, of course."

"What am I?"

"A seventeen-year-old girl from Sunnydale who is also the Slayer who is also something the merging made you that doesn't have a name yet." The woman's voice was entirely matter-of-fact. "Being three things at once is inconvenient. It's also the only way to do what comes next."

"What comes next?"

"You'll see." She looked at Buffy steadily. "They're going to ask a lot of you. You don't have to give them everything they ask for. You get to decide what this is."

"The Slayer doesn't get to decide what this is."

"This Slayer does." The woman looked at the books again. "Two thousand years of change. The calling is still yours. So is the choice."

Buffy looked at her. "Are you the First?"

A pause. "I'm someone who has been watching for a long time and who wanted to say something before the next part starts." She stood, and the library moved around her the way things moved in dreams, with their own internal logic. "You found people worth fighting for. That's the important part. The rest is navigation."

"I'm not very good at navigation."

"You're better than you think." She paused. "You always were."

The library faded.

Buffy woke up on the Wayfarer at 0330 with the dream already losing its edges and the specific feeling of something settling into place, the way the last piece of a thing sometimes arrived without announcement and simply was there, completed, when a moment before it had been absent.

She lay in the dark and let it settle.

At 0400 she went to the gym and ran, and the running was not joyful the way the Girl's running was joyful, not free in that specific ancient way, but it was good, the body doing what it was built for in a gym on a Manticoran warship in a star system she hadn't known existed a month ago, and good was enough.

Good was considerably more than enough.

She ran until she felt like herself. All three of her selves simultaneously, which was the thing she was apparently going to have to work with.

Then she went and ate an enormous breakfast and watched the stars go by through the mess hall viewport and when Carson came in at 0700 with his arm out of its sling and his hair doing something optimistic, she moved over and made room and he sat down and they watched the wrong constellations together without needing to say anything about them.

The stars were wrong and she was here and Manticore was two weeks away and that was the shape of things.

She could work with that shape.

She was getting quite good at that.

Arc Two: The Long Road Home

Chapter 17: Manticore came up on the sensors fourteen days later as a green light on a board she was learning to read, and by the time it was visible through the viewport as something more than a star it had already become several things simultaneously in her mind, which was a thing she was getting used to.

It was the destination. It was the next problem. It was the place where the after started in earnest.

She stood at the viewport in the mess hall at 0600 with a mug of chocolate and watched it resolve from a point into a disk and thought about Sunnydale coming up on the horizon from the highway, the specific way it looked from the bus the first time, smaller than she'd expected and greener than LA and already wrong in ways she couldn't have named yet.

Every new place was a version of that first look. The moment before you knew what it was going to cost you.

She drank her chocolate.

Scotty appeared beside her with coffee and the look of a man who had also not slept past 0600 and had made his peace with this. He stood next to her and they watched the planet together and after a while he said, "First time seeing it from the outside."

"You grew up here?"

"Sphinx. One of the other planets in the system." He nodded at the disk. "That's Manticore proper. I haven't actually spent that much time there. The capital feels�" he paused. "Like a capital. In ways that Sphinx doesn't."

"What's Sphinx like?"

"Cold. Forested. The treecats are from there originally." He paused. "Quieter than here. The sort of place where you notice when something changes because things don't change that often."

She thought about that. "Is that good?"

"When you're seventeen and restless, no. When you've been away for a while�" He looked at the planet. "I'd like to go back. At some point. When this is finished."

The war, he meant. She'd learned enough in six weeks to understand that the war was the kind of thing that structured everything around it, that when people said when this is finished they meant a thing that might not finish in any timeline she could currently see the edges of.

"I'd like to see it," she said. "Sphinx."

He looked at her. "Yeah?"

"Cold and forested sounds good actually." She looked at the planet. "I grew up in Southern California. I've never really done cold."

"Sphinx has cold you haven't imagined."

"That sounds like a challenge."

"It's a description." But there was something in his voice, something she'd learned to catch, the particular tone that his ordinary statements took on when they were also something else. "You'd need gear. The ambient temperature in the northern regions goes low enough to be medically significant for baseline humans."

"I'm not a baseline human."

"No. But even you would want a coat." He paused. "My family has a place in the northern range. When this is finished."

She looked at him.

He looked at the planet.

"Okay," she said.

He drank his coffee. She drank her chocolate. The planet grew.

~~~

The approach to Manticore took most of a day, the convoy threading through the system's traffic with the careful attention that a system at war paid to everything moving within it. There were warships on the passive sensors at intervals that Buffy couldn't have missed even before the Slayer sense had developed its instinct for the presence of organized force, large purposeful things holding their positions in the geometry of a defended system.

She watched them on the tactical display that Shaughnessy had, apparently on reflection, decided to make available to her without being asked.

Honor came to stand beside her at some point in the middle of the approach and they stood together looking at the disposition of forces in silence for a while.

"Is it what you expected?" Buffy asked.

"More than it was," Honor said. "Six months ago this would have been thinner." She looked at the icons moving on the display. "We've been building."

"Against what?"

"Haven has been building too." She paused. "The question is always who gets to a sufficient advantage first and whether the political situation allows them to use it."

"Does it?"

A pause that was not quite hesitation. "That's what I'm going to spend the next several months finding out." She looked away from the display. "There will be a reception. When we dock. I want to prepare you for it."

"Prepare me how?"

"There are people who will want to see you for reasons that have more to do with what you represent than who you are. Some of them will be important people politically. Some of them will be important people personally." She paused. "Some of them will be both."

"The Queen," Buffy said.

Honor looked at her. "Yes. Queen Elizabeth has been briefed. On the basic facts. She has�expressed interest."

Buffy thought about what that meant, interest from a head of state, and thought about how she'd handled the Mayor of Sunnydale, who had been an actual demon about to ascend to pure demon form, and decided the comparison was probably not usefully predictive. "What does she want?"

"To meet you. To assess you." Honor's voice was even. "She's a remarkable person. I want you to know that before you walk into the room. She is not going to be what you expect."

"What am I going to expect?"

"Ceremony. Formality. The performance of monarchy." Honor paused. "She'll do some of that because it's functionally necessary. Underneath it she is one of the sharpest political minds I've encountered and she will form an opinion of you in approximately ten minutes and that opinion will matter."

"No pressure," Buffy said.

"Considerable pressure," Honor said. "I'm telling you because I think you'd rather know."

She thought about that. "What's your read? On how she'll take me?"

Honor was quiet for a moment. "She's been queen since she was young. She knows what it is to have the weight of an entire civilization's expectations placed on you before you had the vocabulary to refuse them." She paused. "I think she'll recognize something in you."

Buffy looked at the display, at the warships holding their patient geometry in the dark. "And if she doesn't?"

"Then we navigate from there." Honor's voice was entirely certain. "But she will."

~~~

The docking happened in the late afternoon by ship's time, which corresponded to early morning on Manticore's capital continent, which meant that the people who came to meet them had been awake for some time and were doing that specific thing that official people did when they'd been awake before they wanted to be, which was performing alertness with slightly more effort than genuine alertness required.

The docking bay was large and decorated in the way of official spaces, which was with the symbols of the thing being represented more heavily than the symbols of the specific people involved. Manticoran standards and naval flags and the kind of light that was specifically designed to make people look appropriate for the occasion.

There was a crowd, which she'd expected. There were cameras, which she'd also expected. There was a moment, which she had also expected but which arrived with more weight than expectation provided for, when the hatch opened and the sound of the crowd came in and she was standing third in the line behind Honor and McKeon and the reality of the next thing was right there, immediate and irreversible.

She breathed.

The Girl was quiet, which was either a good sign or a sign that the Girl's category system didn't include this particular type of situation. Probably the latter. The First Slayer had not done a lot of press.

She walked out.

The crowd noise changed when she appeared, not dramatically, not the way it changed for Honor, but distinctly, a shift in the quality of the attention. She felt it the way she felt all attention, as pressure, and she walked through it with her chin level and her hands easy at her sides and her face doing the thing she'd learned to make her face do, which was present and composed without being closed.

A man in naval dress uniform appeared at her elbow with the smooth efficiency of someone who had been placed there for this purpose. "Lady Buffy. I'm Commander Hennessy, Admiral Caparelli's aide. I'll be facilitating your transition to temporary quarters and your initial briefings. If you'll follow me."

She followed him.

The temporary quarters were on the naval base adjacent to the docking facility, a suite of rooms that had been assembled for her with the particular kind of care that said someone had thought about what she might need without being entirely certain what that was. There was food. There was a bed that was larger than anything she'd slept in since Sunnydale. There was a window that looked out over a city at dawn, the specific blue-gray quality of early light on stone buildings that predated anything she'd seen in Sunnydale by several centuries.

Commander Hennessy ran through the schedule with the efficiency of someone conveying a great deal of information in the minimum necessary time. Initial medical assessment with the naval medical staff in the morning, which would include Fritz and Petrakis at her request and which she'd been told would be conducted with appropriate discretion. Preliminary debrief with Naval Intelligence in the afternoon. A rest day on the second day. On the third day, a meeting.

He said meeting in the tone that meant it was something more than a meeting.

"With whom?" she asked.

"Her Majesty has requested an audience," Hennessy said, carefully neutral. "At her convenience, which she has indicated is the third day."

She thought about what Honor had said. She thought about a woman who'd been queen since she was young and knew what it was to have the weight of expectations placed on you before you had vocabulary to refuse them.

"Okay," she said. "Third day."

Hennessy left. She stood at the window and watched the city wake up.

It was a real city, the kind she hadn't seen since Sunnydale, though everything about it was different from Sunnydale in ways that went beyond the obvious. The scale was different. The materials were different. The sky was a slightly different shade of blue, Manticore's sun being a slightly different kind of star, and she'd read about this in Fritz's initial orientation materials and knowing about it didn't make looking at it less strange.

But it was a city. People were moving through it with the specific purposeful randomness of people going to their particular version of wherever they were going, and that part was entirely recognizable, that fundamental grammar of human movement through urban space, and something in her chest did a thing she couldn't name precisely but which felt like relief.

Recognizable things. Even if nothing else was familiar, the shape of people moving through a city in the early morning was exactly what it had always been.

She watched until the sun was properly up and then she ate the food that had been left and went to sleep and slept, deeply and without dreams, for eight hours.

~~~

The medical assessment was not as bad as she'd expected, which was a low bar given what she'd expected.

Petrakis had prepared his colleagues with enough precision that none of them were surprised by anything, which she appreciated. Fritz stood slightly to the side through most of it with the expression of a man watching a process he'd largely designed and was monitoring for execution quality. When the two Manticoran physicians conferred in the corner over something she couldn't hear from the biobed she was sitting on, she looked at Fritz.

He gave her a small nod that meant proceeding correctly.

She relaxed fractionally.

The thing about being an unknown biological quantity was that the experience of being assessed depended entirely on the assessors. There was one version of this process that felt like being a specimen, categorized and quantified and reduced to data. There was another version that felt like being a patient, which was a person with a body that a professional was trying to understand in order to take care of.

Fritz had trained her to expect the second version. She'd been getting it consistently, which she attributed entirely to Fritz and partially to something in the culture of these people that she was still identifying but which seemed to include a default assumption of personhood that she found unexpectedly moving.

She hadn't always been given that assumption. The Council, in its less considered moments, had given her the other version. You were the Slayer. The Slayer was the mission. The person it was packaged with was administrative.

She thought about this while the Manticoran physicians did their work, and by the time they were done she had arrived at a thought she wanted to share with Fritz.

"Walk with me," she said, when the physicians had concluded and the room had cleared to the degree that it was going to.

He fell into step beside her with the comfortable ease of someone who had been walking beside her for two months. She went toward the window at the end of the corridor, the one that looked out over the base and a slice of the city beyond it.

"When I was called," she said, without preamble, because Fritz was the kind of person who did better without preamble, "the Council showed up the next day. My first Watcher. And everything changed. Not just what I did. How people looked at me." She paused. "They never stopped looking at me that way, the Council. Like I was the mission wearing a face."

Fritz was quiet, which was his version of active listening.

"These people don't do that," she said. "I've been waiting for it. Six weeks on the Wayfarer, the past two days. I keep expecting the moment when I stop being a person and become a category." She looked at the city. "It hasn't happened."

"No," Fritz said.

"Why?"

He considered this carefully, which was how he considered everything. "I think in part it's the people. Honor. McKeon. Shaughnessy. These are people who have a developed habit of treating people as people regardless of circumstance, and that habit shapes the environment around them." He paused. "In part I think it's your presentation. You've been consistently and clearly a person in ways that make the alternative framing difficult to maintain."

"And in part?"

"In part I think you've been fortunate in your initial context." He looked at her steadily. "That may not hold universally as you move into broader Manticoran society. There will be people who want to use you as a symbol. Honor warned you about that."

"She did."

"The medical community is going to be a particular vector for that. Not intentionally. But you represent something that doesn't fit any existing framework and the desire to build a framework will be strong." He paused. "I'll be writing papers. With your permission. With your review and approval before anything is published. I want to be transparent about that."

She'd known this was coming. She'd thought about it during the long weeks on the Wayfarer, about what it meant to be a documented thing, a thing that went into the record. About the ways that records helped and the ways they constrained.

"Yes," she said. "With review. And there are things that stay out."

"Of course. You determine the limits."

She looked at the city. A tram was moving along a street she could see from here, filled with people going wherever people went in the capital of a star system at war in the morning. "What's the Slayer lineage going to look like in two thousand years of historical record?"
