"Fragmentary," Fritz said. "The Clinkscales family record is the most intact continuous documentation, but even that has significant gaps. The general historical record from your era is�" he paused� "poor, by current standards. The data degradation across two millennia is severe. What survives is mostly institutional records, physical artifacts, some genetic lineages. Personal documentation from your time is largely lost."

She thought about that. "Nobody's going to know who I was."

"Nobody is going to know the details, no. The Clinkscales record establishes the existence of the Slayer lineage but as mythology rather than documented fact, until now." He paused. "You are, in some respects, your own proof of concept."

She turned that over. "Carson said the family kept the records because they believed."

"Yes. Four generations of believing in something that the broader culture categorized as legend." He paused. "They were right."

She thought about Carson with his steady certainty, the young man from a religious tradition that had a category for her existence, who had said she would see Grayson with the confidence of someone stating a navigational fact. "He's going to have a complicated life," she said.

"He already has one," Fritz said. "He seems to be managing it."

She almost smiled. "Yeah. He does."

~~~

The debrief with Naval Intelligence was conducted by two officers who had the specific quality of people whose job was to receive information without contributing their own reactions to the room, which she found professionally impressive and personally exhausting. She told them what she'd told McKeon in the operational debriefs on the Wayfarer, the observation nights and the extraction and the garrison's state and the information Martingale had provided. She answered questions that were precise and that occasionally surprised her with what they were actually after, which was not always the obvious thing.

At the end the senior officer, a captain named Wainwright, looked at her directly for what felt like the first time in three hours despite having been looking at her the whole time. "Lady Buffy. One more question."

"Go ahead."

"The garrison commandant. The new one. Martingale described him as younger and more ideologically committed than his predecessor." He paused. "During your three nights of observation, did you form an assessment of him independent of Martingale's characterization?"

She thought about this. "I never saw him directly. But I saw the garrison's behavior and behavior reflects command culture." She paused. "The rotation tightening was real, you could see it, but it was uneven. Some posts were tighter and some were slacker than before, which is the pattern you get when enforcement is personal rather than systemic. He was riding specific people hard and those people were riding the people below them, but the ones who weren't in his immediate line of sight were taking the space he wasn't watching." She paused. "Ideological commitment tends to be personal. It inspires the people who already believe and it fatigues the people who are just doing the job. His garrison was mixed."

Wainwright made a note. "That matches other data we have." He looked at her. "Thank you."

She went back to her quarters and ate and slept again and woke up in the middle of the night with the Girl's running dreams and lay in the dark of a room in the capital of a star nation listening to a city that was nothing like any city she'd known and was also, in the ways that mattered, exactly like every city she'd known.

She ran through the dark on feet that had never touched a road.

She lay in a bed in Manticore's capital.

She was both of these things at once and she was getting better at holding both without either one displacing the other.

Chapter 18: The rest day was not restful in the way that rest days were supposed to be restful.

It was restful in the way that days without scheduled obligations were restful when you were someone whose baseline operational mode was hypervigilance, which was to say it was slightly less activated than the days with obligations but remained in the register of alert rather than relaxed.

She dealt with this by going for a run.

Not in the gym. Outside, which required a conversation with Commander Hennessy about the security implications of the Slayer going for a morning run in the capital city, a conversation that resolved in her favor when she made the point that her personal security capabilities significantly exceeded anything the naval base could provide and that the run was non-negotiable.

Hennessy had looked at her for a moment and then said he would arrange a route.

The route he arranged was along the waterfront, a kilometer of paved path above a harbor that had the specific character of working water, functional rather than decorative, the kind of harbor where things happened rather than where things were displayed. Ships at the quay, smaller than warships but serious, the kind that moved goods between planets and asked no particular questions about what the goods thought of the arrangement.

She ran.

The city woke up around her as she ran, the early-morning population of people with somewhere to be assembling itself from doorways and transit stops, and she was aware of being looked at in a general way, the way anyone was looked at who was running on a public path, and also in a specific way that said her face was beginning to be recognized.

She'd expected this. The cameras at the docking bay had been there and the information about who she was had been released in some form, she didn't know exactly what form, that was one of the things Honor was managing. She ran through the looking and let it be what it was.

At the far end of the waterfront path she stopped at a railing and looked out over the harbor.

The water was the wrong color. Not dramatically wrong, not alien-wrong, but the specific shade of blue-green that said the mineral content and the biology of the water here were not the same as any water she'd looked at before, and knowing why it was different didn't make the difference less present. She looked at it.

"Lady Buffy."

She turned.

The woman who had come up behind her was about fifty, with close-cropped gray hair and the specific build of someone who had been physically active their entire life and whose body showed the accumulated decisions of that rather than the accumulated decisions of a desk. She was wearing civilian clothes that were the kind of civilian clothes that a person wore when they were trying to look like they weren't being formal about something that was actually formal.

She had a treecat on her shoulder.

The treecat was larger than Nimitz, older-looking, with a scar across one true-hand that had healed to a white line against the cream-colored fur. He looked at Buffy with green eyes that were doing something she couldn't precisely read but which felt like assessment.

The woman looked at Buffy with gray eyes that were doing exactly the same thing.

"Your Majesty," Buffy said.

A beat. Then Queen Elizabeth III of Manticore almost smiled. "Honor said you'd figure it out."

"The treecat is the tell." She glanced at the large cat. "No offense."

The treecat made a sound that was probably not offense.

"This is Ariel," the Queen said. "He was going to be insulted regardless of what you said, so don't trouble yourself." She came to stand at the railing beside Buffy and looked at the harbor. "I wanted to meet you before the audience. I do that sometimes, when I want to see how someone is before they know they're being seen."

"I knew I was being seen," Buffy said.

"You knew someone was there. You didn't know it was me."

"Fair."

The Queen looked at the water. She had the quality that Honor had described, the thing underneath the performance of her role, the sharpness that coexisted with everything the position required of her. Looking at her directly, outside the context of an official audience, it was entirely apparent.

"How are you?" she asked.

Not as ceremony. As the question it was.

"Working on it," Buffy said. Which was the same answer she'd given Honor on Hades and which was still the most accurate one available.

"Good answer." The Queen was quiet for a moment. Ariel's tail moved in a slow pattern that probably meant something. "I've been briefed extensively. I want you to know that. I'm not going to make you go through the whole thing again."

"I appreciate that."

"I have two questions. The rest can wait for the audience or not happen at all." She looked at Buffy directly. "First. What do you want?"

Buffy held her gaze. It was a better question than most people led with. "A place," she said. "Not just somewhere to be. A place that I'm part of. A reason to be in it that's mine rather than assigned." She paused. "Something worth fighting for."

"And the fighting. That's not optional for you."

"No. I've tried the optional version. It doesn't work."

The Queen nodded slowly. "Second question. What are you afraid of?"

She thought about it. She thought about the honest answer, which was complicated, and she thought about the version of the honest answer that was also useful.

"Being made into something I'm not," she said. "I've been the Slayer since I was fifteen and the Slayer is a real thing, I'm not arguing with it, but it's not all of me and there are people who look at it and stop seeing anything else." She paused. "I don't want to be a symbol. I want to be a person who does something real."

The Queen was quiet for a long moment. Ariel was very still.

"I can work with that," she said.

Buffy looked at her. "Most people who say that mean they'll work with it until it becomes inconvenient."

"I know." The Queen looked at the water. "I've been the Queen since I was eighteen. I know the difference between the symbol and the person it's packaged with, because I've been both simultaneously for a very long time." She paused. "What I'm offering is not a promise that I won't need the symbol. I'm going to need the symbol. The political situation requires it and pretending otherwise would be dishonest." She looked back at Buffy. "What I'm offering is that I see both. The symbol and the person. And I won't pretend one doesn't exist to get use out of the other."

Buffy held her gaze for a long moment.

"Okay," she said.

"That's not a yes to anything specific."

"No. It's a yes to the conversation." She paused. "What is it you actually need from me? Specifically."

The Queen almost smiled again. "Honor said you asked the direct question."

"Honor said a lot of things about me, apparently."

"She trusts you. In a way I've rarely seen her trust anyone this quickly." A pause. "That means something."

Buffy thought about Honor on Hades, in the shuttle, asking her to tell her about Sunnydale and listening to the whole thing. About the way Honor's trust worked, the way it was given with precision and held with commitment. "Yeah," she said. "It does."

"What I need from you," the Queen said, "is time. To understand what you are and what you can do and where those things fit into a war that we are working very hard not to lose." She paused. "I need you to be patient with a political process that is slower than you are. And I need you to be honest with me when the political process is asking something of you that you can't give."

"And in exchange?"

"Resources. Support. People who will help you build whatever life you're building here." She paused. "And my personal word that I will not use you as a symbol without telling you I'm doing it."

It was, Buffy thought, an unusually honest offer.

She thought about what Giles had taught her about deals and about reading the person across from you and about the difference between what someone promised and what they actually intended. She thought about what Honor had said: she'll recognize something in you.

"I'll take it," she said.

The Queen nodded. "Good." She looked at the water one more time. "The formal audience is tomorrow. Ceremony. Dress uniform and a room full of people. I wanted this first."

"I know. Thank you."

Ariel made a sound she couldn't interpret but which felt like approval. The Queen glanced at him with the expression of someone receiving information through a channel no one else could access. Then she looked back at Buffy. "He thinks you're all right."

"Tell him I think he's all right too."

"He knows," the Queen said. "That's rather the point."

She walked back along the waterfront path the way she'd come, unhurried, and Buffy watched her go with the specific feeling of having been genuinely assessed and found sufficient, which was different from being found useful and different from being found impressive and was its own category that she didn't have a precise word for.

She turned back to the harbor.

The water was still the wrong color. She looked at it for a while longer, the wrong blue-green of water that had never been touched by her world, and let it be what it was.

Then she ran back.

~~~

The formal audience was what Honor had said it would be, which was ceremony and formality and a room full of people.

She wore the closest thing to appropriate she'd been able to assemble with help from a very efficient woman named Lieutenant Chen who had shown up the previous afternoon with a collection of options and a tape measure and the expression of someone who had been briefed on unusual circumstances and was not going to make them more unusual. The result was something that was technically Manticoran formal civilian dress in a cut that worked for someone with her particular proportions and in a color that Chen had described as appropriate without being presumptuous.

She didn't know enough about Manticoran fashion to evaluate this independently. She trusted Chen's assessment.

The room was the kind of room that had been designed for exactly this function, which was to make the ceremony feel like it meant something through the accumulated weight of architecture and light and formality. It worked. She could feel it working on her even knowing how it worked, which was the thing about well-made ceremonies that she'd learned from watching Giles manage the Council's occasional formal moments.

There were perhaps sixty people in the room. She recognized a portion of them from the Wayfarer's passenger complement. The rest were the kind of people who attended formal royal audiences, which she was learning to read, the specific grammar of rank and relation that expressed itself in Manticoran society through bearing and position and the subtle semaphore of uniform and decoration.

She read it and filed it and walked through it with her chin level and her hands easy.

Honor was three places ahead of her in the receiving line, in full dress uniform with every decoration she was entitled to, which was a significant number. McKeon was behind her. Scotty was somewhere in the room in his own right, she'd seen him earlier, and he'd caught her eye and made a small gesture that she'd parsed as you look correct, which she'd returned with a look she hoped conveyed the same thing because he did.

The audience proceeded with the formal efficiency of a process that had been done many times before and was being done correctly now. Honor received what was coming to her, which included things Buffy didn't have full context for but which the room received with the kind of reaction that said they were significant.

Then it was her turn.

She stepped forward and did the thing Lieutenant Chen had coached her through, the specific Manticoran formal acknowledgment that split the difference between a bow and a curtsey by being neither quite either, and looked up at Queen Elizabeth on the throne that was exactly what a throne was supposed to be.

The Queen was the same person she'd met at the harbor railing. She was also simultaneously not the same person, or rather she was more of the same person, the symbol and the substance both visible at the same volume. She looked at Buffy with the gray eyes that were doing the same assessment they'd been doing at the waterfront, with the additional weight of sixty witnesses and several centuries of ceremony behind them.

"Lady Buffy of Sunnydale," the Queen said. The room was quiet. "On behalf of the Star Kingdom of Manticore and the Grayson Space Navy, we extend our welcome."

Buffy held her gaze. "Thank you, Your Majesty."

"You have come a very long way."

"Yes." A pause. "I intend to make it worth the trip."

The room held a particular kind of silence that she could feel as pressure, the specific silence of sixty people who had just witnessed something they were going to be thinking about for a while.

The Queen's eyes held something that was not quite a smile but was its closest formal approximation.

"We believe you will," she said.

~~~

After the audience there was a reception, because there was always a reception.

She moved through it with the specific skills she'd developed for the school dances and Bronze nights of her pre-Slayer life, the ones she'd been good at before everything changed, the ability to be in a social situation and read it and navigate it without letting the navigation show. It was a different kind of competence than what she'd been using for the past several months and she was surprised by how readily it came back, the social version of muscle memory.

People wanted to talk to her. She talked to them. She deflected the questions that needed deflecting and answered the ones that could be answered and moved through the room with the fluid purposefulness of someone who had a destination even when the destination was just the next conversation.

She found Carson near the food table.

He was wearing Grayson formal dress, which she gathered from his expression was not his preferred environment but which he was managing with the composed dignity of a young man from a culture that prepared you for formal occasions whether you wanted to be prepared or not. His arm was out of its sling. The color in his face was good.

"How's the arm?" she asked.

"Full function." He flexed it slightly to demonstrate. "Surgeon-Lieutenant Martingale is very good."

"She is." She took a piece of something from the table without looking at it, because the reception food had been consistently excellent and she'd stopped being cautious about it. "How are you doing? With all of this."

He looked around the room. The assembled weight of Manticoran formal society doing what formal society did, which was circulating. "It's a great deal," he said.

"Yeah."

"My grandfather is going to�" He stopped, shook his head. "I don't have words for what my grandfather is going to do when I tell him."

She thought about what it meant to have a grandfather who had kept the faith in a lineage for thirty years and was now going to find out that the lineage was literally present and had been through a series of events that made the family records look understated. "Is he going to be all right?"

"He is going to be the most all right he has ever been in his life," Carson said, with a certainty that was affectionate and specific. "He's been carrying those records since he was a boy. He'll cry. He'll pray. He'll probably reread every document in the archive within the week." He paused. "And then he'll want to meet you."

"I want to meet him," she said.

He looked at her. "You mean that."

"He kept the story alive," she said. "That matters." She paused. "In my time the Watchers' Council kept the records. They had the institutional framework and the resources and they still lost things, misinterpreted things, turned things into doctrine that should have stayed as data." She looked at her food. "Your grandfather kept it as something true. Without the institutional framework. That's harder."

He was quiet for a moment. "He would be very moved to hear you say that."

"Tell him." She paused. "When you write. Tell him from me."

He nodded with the gravity of someone accepting a charge. "I will."

She ate her food. He ate his. The reception moved around them with its noise and its circulation and its sixty people sorting themselves into the conversations that were going to matter and the ones that were going to be forgotten by morning.

Scotty found them eventually, with Ibanez in tow and the slightly loosened look of two people who had been at a formal reception long enough that the formality had started to feel comfortable rather than constraining. He caught her eye and she read in the catching a question she answered with a slight nod, because the past two months had given them the vocabulary for that level of nonverbal exchange, and he came over.

"Carson," he said.

"Ensign Tremaine." The formality between them was light, a courtesy rather than a distance.

"How's the arm?"

"People keep asking me that."

"People who were there tend to," Scotty said.

Ibanez had a plate of reception food and the expression of a young man who was deeply grateful for the existence of better-than-functional cooking in a formal setting. He caught Buffy's eye and said, "Lady Buffy," with the particular inflection he'd settled into, which was respectful without being deferential in a way she appreciated.

"Ibanez. You flew clean."

He looked down at his plate. "I had help."

"You had a failing thruster and a garrison pinnace and a good instinct." She paused. "That's not the same as help. That's you."

He looked up. He was eighteen and from a place she couldn't pronounce and had been through more in the past two months than most eighteen-year-olds encountered in a decade, and he was standing in a formal reception at the palace of the Queen of Manticore eating food that was better than functional and processing it all with the careful thoroughness of a person who had decided to take it in properly rather than let it pass over them.

"Thank you," he said.

"You're welcome."

The reception continued. She moved through it and came back and moved through it again, the social muscle memory doing the work while the rest of her ran quieter calculations. The shape of the situation she was in. The deal she'd made with the Queen. The things that were going to be asked of her and the things she was going to ask in return.

The woman in the library dream had said they were going to ask a lot of her.

She thought that was accurate.

She thought she was going to ask a lot in return, which was something she'd never been particularly good at and which she was developing a determined intention to get better at, because this version of her life was not going to be structured around giving everything and not asking what the giving was for.

She was going to ask what the giving was for.

She was going to require an answer.

Chapter 19: Three weeks after their arrival on Manticore, Honor left.

Not permanently. The nature of being Honor Harrington in the middle of a war was that you were where the war needed you, and the war had found something it needed her for that required her to be elsewhere. She told Buffy two days before she went, in a conversation that was direct and brief in the way that Honor's important conversations tended to be.

"I'm not leaving you here alone," she said. "You have Fritz. You have Scotty and Carson while their reassignments process. You have Commodore Shaughnessy, who is staying in the capital for the next month at least. And you have the Queen's office, which will be in contact with you through Lieutenant Chen."

"I know," Buffy said. "I'm not worried about being alone."

"I know you're not." Honor held her gaze. "I'm worried about you being alone."

There was a distinction in there that Buffy worked out after a moment. "I'll be fine."

"I know that too." Honor was quiet for a moment. Nimitz was on her shoulder in the way he always was, the warm living weight of him, the green eyes tracking between them. "This is not a war that ends quickly. What I'm going to be doing for the next several months will determine things that matter for a long time. I need to be doing it."

"I know." She looked at Nimitz. He looked back at her. "Will you tell him something for me?"

"Tell him yourself," Honor said. "He hears you."

She looked at the treecat. "Tell her to come back," she said.

Nimitz reached out and laid his true-hand on her hand briefly. Just for a moment. Then he withdrew it and Honor's expression did the layered thing and she reached out and touched Buffy's shoulder once, a brief firm touch that said more than a longer contact would have.

"I'll come back," Honor said.

She went.

Buffy stood in the corridor outside Honor's quarters for a moment after she left, and then she went to the gym and spent an hour doing things that required her full physical attention and nothing else, because sometimes the body was the right tool for the emotion and sometimes the emotion needed to be temporarily outran.

She outran it.

Then she came back and ate an enormous lunch and went to her afternoon briefing with Naval Intelligence, which had become a regular feature of her Manticore days, and she was present and accurate and useful and when it was over she walked back through the capital city with the late afternoon light on the stone buildings and let the city be a city around her.

She was building context. Not fast enough for her preference, but steadily. The daily briefings were accumulating into something like understanding, the skeleton she'd assembled from six weeks of reading beginning to acquire flesh from direct engagement with people who were inside the situation rather than describing it from the outside.

The war was real in ways it hadn't been real from the Wayfarer's tactical display. It was in the faces of the people she talked to and the specific quality of the decisions being made and the things that weren't being said in the briefings because they didn't need to be said, because everyone in the room already knew them and lived with them.

Haven was real. Not the political entity, which she understood, but the human reality of it, the people who were prosecuting the war on the other side, the ideological machinery that had produced State Security and the Tepes and the garrison on Hades and the questioner in the outbuilding with his professional tired voice. She thought about that machinery with the specific kind of attention she'd learned for thinking about the things she was supposed to fight.

She'd always understood her opponents better by thinking about what they believed rather than what they did, because what they did was downstream of what they believed and if you understood the belief you could predict the action.

Haven believed in the People's Republic. Or the people running it believed in it, or believed in themselves, or believed in the logic of the system they'd built which required certain things to remain coherent and produced the Tepes as a coherent output.

She thought about this for a long time.

She wasn't sure yet what to do with what she concluded. But she filed it with the other things she was filing, the growing archive of this century that was becoming something she could work with.

~~~

Scotty's reassignment came through six weeks after their arrival.

She'd known it was coming. She'd known it from the day they arrived, the way she knew all the things that were going to happen that she didn't want to happen, with the specific anticipatory weight of impending rather than surprise. He was a naval officer in a war. The war had ongoing requirements. The navy was going to put him where it needed him.

He told her the day before it was official.

They were sitting on the roof of the building where her temporary quarters had become her quarters in the way that temporary things became permanent through the accumulation of inhabitation. The roof had a garden, formal by design and slightly wild at the edges, and they'd discovered it two weeks in and used it intermittently since.

The late afternoon was doing what late afternoons in the capital did, the specific quality of the light she was coming to know, not the same as California but its own thing entirely, and she was getting good at letting new things be their own things rather than measuring them against the things they'd replaced.

"HMS Hexapuma," he said. "Commander Terekhov's ship. We're being assigned to the Talbott Cluster."

She'd read about the Talbott Cluster. It was one of the places the war had spread to that had complicated the already complicated political situation in ways that the Naval Intelligence briefings returned to regularly. "That's not the front."

"No. It's something different from the front." He paused. "Possibly more complicated than the front, depending on how the Solarian situation develops."

She thought about what she knew about the Solarian situation, which was more than she'd known six weeks ago and less than she'd have liked. "When?"

"Three weeks."

She looked at the city. The late afternoon light on the stone. "Okay."

He looked at her. "Okay?"

"You're going where the navy needs you. That's what you do. I knew that." She kept her voice even. "It doesn't mean I'm glad about it."

He was quiet for a moment. "I'm not glad about it either."

She looked at him.

He looked back.

They'd been doing this for two months, building the thing they were building, the careful incremental thing that neither of them had named because naming it would change the rate at which it was developing and neither of them had wanted to change the rate. She knew what it was. She was fairly confident he knew what it was. They'd been proceeding at the pace of people who had other things to deal with and also had time, or had believed they had time.

Three weeks was time, but it was a different kind.

"I'll be honest with you," she said.

"Please."

"I don't know how to do this part. The part where someone goes and you don't know when they're coming back." She paused. "I've done it. I've had to do it more times than I wanted. Every time it was something I survived rather than something I was good at."

He was quiet for a moment. "I'll come back."

"Don't make me that promise," she said. "I don't believe that promise. I've heard it too many times and I know what it means when the universe doesn't cooperate."

"Then I'll make you the other one." His voice was steady. "I'll be as careful as the job allows. I'll think about what I'm doing and why before I do it. I won't spend myself on something that doesn't warrant the cost." He paused. "And I'll come back when I can, even if I can't promise the when."

She held his gaze.

"Okay," she said. "That one I'll take."

He reached out and she let him take her hand, and they sat on the roof of the building with the late afternoon going its particular way around them and the city below doing its city things, and she thought about the shape of this, the specific configuration of a person choosing to be present with someone who was going to leave without knowing the date of their return.

She'd always been bad at goodbyes. She'd learned to do them anyway.

She wasn't ready to do this one yet. She had three weeks. She was going to use them well.

"Tell me more about Sphinx," she said.

He looked at her.

"The northern range," she said. "The cold you said I hadn't imagined. Tell me what it looks like."

He told her.

~~~

Carson's reassignment processed two days after Scotty's.

His was different in kind. He was young and he'd been through what he'd been through, and the Navy had decided, with the particular institutional thoughtfulness that the Navy occasionally demonstrated, that what Carson needed was not an immediate combat posting but a period of consolidation. He was being assigned to the Naval Academy on Saganami Island as an instructor's aide, a posting that would keep him in the Manticore system and allow him to finish his recovery from the arm wound and complete certain formalities of his commission.

He told her on the same day she found out about his posting from Lieutenant Chen, which meant they had the same information at the same time and arrived at the conversation equally.

"Saganami Island," she said.

"Yes." He was slightly pink in the way that Grayson men were pink when they were processing emotions they'd been taught to keep well-managed. "It's here. In the system."

"I know." She looked at him. "Is that what you wanted?"

He was quiet for a moment. "I wanted to go back out. To be where things are happening." He paused. "And I also wanted to not go back out yet. And both of those were true at once and I didn't know what to do with that."

"So the Navy decided for you."

"In the best possible way," he said. "I think."

She thought about that. About the specific mercy of having a decision made for you when you were genuinely uncertain, not the relief of having your agency removed but the relief of having your ambivalence resolved by an institution that had thought about it independently and arrived at a reasonable conclusion.

"Saganami Island is not far," she said.

"A short trip by shuttle." He paused. "I could come back to the capital when the schedule allows."

"Yes," she said. "You could."

He looked at her with the steady clear look she'd come to rely on, the one that took things in without flinching and offered steadiness in return. "Are you all right? With all of this. The changing of things."

She thought about the honest answer.

"I'm practicing being all right with it," she said. "Things change. People go places. That's the condition." She paused. "I've spent two years trying to hold things still enough to protect them and things kept changing anyway and the trying to hold them still was some of what cost me." She looked at the window, the city beyond it. "I'm trying a different approach."

"Which is?"

"Letting things be what they are and staying anyway." She paused. "Being the thing that doesn't move when everything else does. Except that I move too, obviously. I just�" She stopped. "I'm still working out what I mean."

He nodded slowly. "I think I understand." He paused. "You're trying to be the anchor rather than the thing being anchored."

She looked at him. "Yeah," she said. "That's it."

"You'd be good at that," he said, with the same certainty with which he said all the things he was certain about. "You already are."

She thought about the Girl running through the ancient dark with the awareness that everything around her was measured relative to where she was. Not the center of things, not the most important thing, but the fixed point that other things could orient by.

"Working on it," she said.

He smiled at her. Young and from Grayson and steady in a way that had nothing to do with age, the earned stillness of someone who had chosen their ground.

"Me too," he said.

~~~

The three weeks before Scotty left were their own kind of weather.

Not dramatic. Not the concentrated urgency of the operation or the sustained compression of the Wayfarer passage. Just weeks, with their particular texture, the daily substance of people building something before they had to stop building for a while and trust that what they'd built would hold.

They explored the city. Not systematically, in the way Buffy had been exploring things since she arrived, with the deliberate intention of building understanding, but the other way, the way you explored when the exploring was the point rather than the product. He knew the capital better than he'd suggested, had spent time here in training and on leave, and he knew the parts of it that weren't on official maps, the specific small places that cities accumulated like sediment, the good ones and the strange ones and the ones that were good because they were strange.

There was a bookshop on a side street off the main commercial district that had been in the same family for four generations and which was organized according to a system that appeared to be entirely personal and which the current proprietor, a woman of about seventy with the look of someone who had been living in a happy world of books for fifty years, could navigate with complete confidence. Buffy spent two hours in it on a Tuesday afternoon and came out with four books and the feeling of having been somewhere that had its own very particular climate.

There was a park that was high enough on the hill that you could see both the harbor and the military base from the same bench, and they sat on that bench on a Thursday evening and ate food from a cart vendor that was not fancy and was very good and watched the two views simultaneously.

There was a concert of some kind on a Saturday that they found by following the sound through three streets and which turned out to be an informal gathering of musicians who played something that was technically traditional Manticoran folk music but which had absorbed enough influences over several centuries to be something that didn't need that description. She listened to it and thought about music she'd loved and music she was going to have to find equivalents for and thought that maybe this was a candidate.

On the last night she made him dinner.

Not in the formal quarters that the Navy had provided, which had a kitchen she'd been using with increasing confidence as the weeks passed, but in a configuration that was as close to her kitchen as she could get it, which was to say she pushed the furniture around and put the table near the window and found candles, which apparently still existed and still did the same thing to the quality of light they'd always done.

She made the rosemary chicken.

She'd found a recipe, something approximating the recipe, in the Manticoran culinary database that Fritz had helped her access when she'd explained what she was looking for. The ingredients were not identical. The herbs here were close but different. The chicken was chicken in the way that everything on Manticore was close but different, the same fundamental biology with two thousand years of evolutionary divergence creating variations that were usually imperceptible and occasionally significant.

She made it anyway.

He arrived at the time she'd told him, dressed for dinner without being formal about it, which was the correct read, and stopped in the doorway when he saw the table and the candles and smelled what was in the oven.

He didn't say anything for a moment.

"Sit down," she said.

He sat.

She cooked and they talked, the particular ease of a conversation that had two months of foundation under it, and she thought about how this was different from the previous versions of this kind of ease she'd had, which had been good and real and had asked less of her than this, or had asked different things. This version asked her to be present all the way down, not the managed version of presence that you deployed in professional contexts or even in friendships but the actual version, the one where the other person could see what was under the management.

She was getting better at the actual version.

The chicken came out of the oven.

He ate it in the concentrated way of someone receiving something seriously. She watched him.

"What is this?" he asked.

"Rosemary chicken. My mother's recipe, or as close as I could get it." She looked at her own plate. "The herbs are different. The actual taste is�not the same. But the shape of it is."

He looked at her.

"I wanted to make something from home," she said. "And this was the thing from home that I could most reconstruct from available materials." She paused. "I'm aware that's a lot to put on a chicken."

"It's not too much," he said. He paused. "It's good."

"It's not the same as hers."

"No." He looked at his plate and then at her. "But it's yours. The version you made here." He paused. "That's not nothing."

She looked at the candles. The light they made, the particular warm quality of it that was the same regardless of what century the candle was in.

"No," she said. "It's not nothing."

They ate. The city was outside the window doing its evening things. She thought about Joyce Summers in her kitchen, the specific way she moved in it, the sounds she made while cooking. She let herself think about it directly rather than sideways, which was the thing she was practicing, looking at the losses directly without letting them become the only thing in the room.

It was still her mother.

It would always be her mother.

She was also here, at a table with candles, with a person she'd found in the worst possible context and who had turned out to be exactly what she'd needed without having been designed that way, which was the only kind that counted.

She was both of those things at once.

She held both.

After dinner they sat at the window and she let him talk about Sphinx until he ran out of things to say about it and then she talked about Sunnydale until she ran out, and by the end the night was properly dark and the city was doing its nighttime configuration and neither of them had said the things that were waiting to be said.

She thought about whether to say them.

She thought about two years of not saying things until it was too late to say them. She thought about the habit of waiting for certainty before speaking, the way the wait had a cost that she'd paid more than once and hadn't accounted for fully until it was already paid.

She looked at him.

He was looking at her.

"I'm not good at this part," she said.

"The last night part?"

"The saying things part." She paused. "I always wait for the right moment and the right moment always passes."

He held her gaze. "What would you say? If the moment was right."

She thought about it. The honest answer was simple, which was usually the sign that it was the true one. "That the past two months have been better because you were in them," she said. "And that I don't know what comes next but I know I'd like you to be in that too. And that I'm aware that's a lot to say to someone who's leaving in twelve hours."

He looked at her for a long moment.

"That's not too much to say," he said. "That's exactly the right amount." He paused. "The past two months have been better because of you. What comes next�" he paused� "I'd like that too."

She looked at him.

He looked at her.

Neither of them said anything else for a while, which was the right amount of not saying anything else.

Outside the city did its nighttime things and the light was the wrong light in the right way and she was here, in this specific place, and it was enough.

It was, it turned out, quite a lot.

~~~

He left in the morning.

She walked with him to the transport that was taking his kit to the naval base, because he was traveling light and his kit was going separately and he was the kind of person who traveled light, which she'd come to understand was characteristic. They walked through the capital in the early morning with the city assembling itself around them and she let it be exactly what it was without trying to make it mean more than it did or less than it did.

At the transport bay she stopped.

He stopped.

"Sphinx," she said. "When this is finished."

"Sphinx," he said. "The northern range. You're going to need a coat."

"You're going to have to help me pick one."

"I can do that."

She looked at him. He looked at her. There were other people around them doing the business of a military transport bay in the early morning and none of it was relevant to this specific moment.

"Come back," she said.

"I'll be as careful as the job allows," he said.

She nodded.

He went.

She stood in the transport bay until the transport was gone and then she walked back through the city in the early morning light and the city was around her in its particular Manticoran way, full of people who were going to their various places for their various reasons, and she was among them.

She stopped at the waterfront on the way back and stood at the railing above the harbor with its working water in its wrong blue-green and she was here, in this place, and the shape of what she was building here was visible from where she stood in a way it hadn't quite been before.

A place. People in it. Something worth fighting for.

She was working on all three.

She turned from the railing and walked back into the city and the city held her the way cities hold you when you've been there long enough to start to belong to them, which is to say impersonally and completely, and she let it.

She was going to be here a while.

She was starting to think that was all right.

Arc Three: Manticore

Chapter 20: Six weeks after Scotty left, something came out of the dark.

Not metaphorically. Or not only metaphorically. The distinction was becoming harder to maintain as she spent more time in this century, which had its own relationship with the dark and what lived in it, and the relationship was more complicated than the previous century's had been even when the previous century had included the Hellmouth.

It started with a dead man in the warehouse district.

She learned about it from Fritz, which was itself a piece of information. Fritz learned about it from Petrakis, who had been called as a consulting physician and who had come away from the consultation with the expression of a man who had encountered something that didn't fit his existing framework and had immediately thought of someone who collected things that didn't fit existing frameworks.

Fritz came to her quarters at seven in the evening and sat down across from her and said, "The police found a body this morning. In the warehouse district. Drained of blood."

She put down the book she'd been reading.

"Drained how?" she said.

"Completely. With no obvious external wound consistent with the volume." He met her eyes. "There were two puncture marks on the neck."

She looked at him.

He looked back at her.

"No," she said. "That's not possible."

"I know." He was quiet for a moment. "And yet."

She thought about two thousand years. She thought about the Hellmouth and the way demon activity clustered around nexus points of mystical energy and the way the specific cluster of activity she'd grown up in had been one of the most active in human history but had never been the only one. She thought about the fact that she was the Slayer and that the Slayer existed because something had always needed slaying, in every era, in every civilization, regardless of whether the civilization had a word for it or not.

She thought about two thousand years of human expansion through the stars and about what might have come with humanity into those stars, patient and quiet and waiting.

"Where's the body?" she said.

"The city morgue. Petrakis has access." Fritz paused. "He doesn't know what to make of it. He's filed the preliminary report with standard unexplained cause of death notation. He's holding the body pending further consultation."

"I need to see it."

"I assumed you would." He paused. "I also need to tell you something else."

She waited.

"This isn't the first one," he said. "I ran a search through the city's medical records after Petrakis told me. The notation pattern is consistent with two other deaths in the past month. Both filed as unexplained. Both in the warehouse district."

Three deaths. A month. A pattern.

She knew what a pattern looked like. She'd been reading patterns since she was fifteen.

"Okay," she said. She stood up. "Let's go."

~~~

The city morgue was a building that did its function with the particular spare efficiency of a place that had been doing the same function for a long time and had optimized everything about itself around that function. It smelled the way morgues smelled, the universal smell that crossed every cultural and temporal boundary she'd encountered, antiseptic over something that was never entirely masked.

Petrakis met them at the entrance with the expression of a man who had been thinking very hard since his conversation with Fritz and who had reached the conclusion that thinking was not going to be sufficient and that someone with a different kind of expertise was required.

He looked at Buffy in the specific way that people looked at her when they were about to ask her for something that they didn't entirely have language for.

"Lady Buffy," he said. "Thank you for coming."

"What can you tell me that's not in the preliminary report?" she said.

He was quiet for a moment, the kind of quiet that assembled itself into an answer. "The exsanguination is complete by every measure I can apply. There is no blood remaining in the circulatory system. The heart shows evidence of continued function after the blood loss began, which is�" he paused� "not consistent with traumatic blood loss of this speed or volume."

"The heart kept going after the blood was gone."

"For some time. Based on the myocardial evidence, yes." He paused. "As if the blood was removed continuously, faster than the body could fail in response to it." He looked at her. "I don't have a mechanism for that."

"I do," she said. "Show me the body."

He showed her.

The body was a man in his late thirties, unremarkable in every way that bodies were unremarkable in death, which was to say completely. The puncture marks on the neck were small and precise and already beginning to close in the way of wounds that had been made by something that contained a coagulant.

She looked at them for a long time.

The Slayer sense was doing something she hadn't felt it do since she arrived in this century, a specific activation that was different from the general hypervigilance of her daily operational state. Something had been in this room and was not here now and the trace of it was still present, the way a scent was still present after the source had gone, faint and specific and unmistakable.

She knew that trace.

She straightened up and looked at Fritz and Petrakis, who were both watching her with the focused attention of professionals encountering something outside their expertise and managing this professionally.

"It's a vampire," she said.

The silence that followed was the silence of two highly trained physicians receiving information that required complete recategorization of their existing model.

Petrakis said, carefully, "The theological literature�"

"This isn't theology," she said. "It's biology. A specific demon subtype that infects humans and converts them into something that requires blood to function and that is technically dead by every standard measure except mobility and intention." She paused. "I've been dealing with them since I was fifteen. These marks are consistent."

Another silence.

"On Earth," Fritz said. Not a challenge. Working out the implications.

"On Earth two thousand years ago, yes." She looked at the body. "The question is how it got here. Which I don't know yet." She paused. "The secondary question is how many. Which based on three deaths in a month is at least one and possibly more."

"If it's been here longer than a month�" Fritz started.

"Then there could be significantly more." She looked at him. "I need to understand the city's unexplained death records for the past year. Longer if you can access it. The pattern will be there if you know what you're looking for."

"What am I looking for?"

"Exsanguination. Any note of neck wounds. Deaths that were attributed to causes that felt wrong to the filing physician but couldn't be explained otherwise." She paused. "And missing persons. Vampires don't always leave bodies where they're found. Sometimes the body becomes another vampire."

Fritz was making notes. Petrakis was making notes. She looked at the dead man on the table and thought about what she owed him, which was the same thing she owed everyone who ended up like this, which was the effort of preventing it from happening to someone else.

"I'm going to need to patrol," she said.

Both physicians looked at her.

"The warehouse district. At night. That's where the bodies are and that's where whatever is doing this is going to be operating." She paused. "I need you to not tell anyone I'm doing it. Not officially. The last thing this needs is an official response that puts uninformed people in the vicinity of a vampire."

"The Queen's office�" Fritz started.

"Lieutenant Chen can know. I'll tell her myself." She paused. "But carefully. Framed correctly." She looked at the body one more time. "This is what I'm for," she said. It came out quieter than she'd intended. "This specific thing. Right here."

Fritz looked at her with the expression he had when he was thinking about her rather than thinking about the data she represented. "Yes," he said. "I know."

She went home and sharpened her stake.

She didn't have many stakes. She'd had more on the Tepes, improvised from the ship's materials, but they'd been left behind in the operational chaos of the escape. She had two that she'd made on the Wayfarer from a piece of equipment housing that had been scheduled for disposal and which she'd requested from the maintenance chief with an explanation that was technically accurate if not complete. She had the black knife, which was not a stake but which she knew how to use in the relevant ways.

She sat on her bed and looked at them and thought about the specific shape of what she was about to do, which was the shape she'd been doing her whole Slayer life, and felt it settle into her like water finding its level.

This was the work.

Everything else�the Queen and the politics and the war and the long project of building a life in this century�all of that was also real and also necessary and she was committed to it. But this was the oldest part, the part that had been true since the first girl in the first dark had been made into the thing that fought back.

She picked up a stake and turned it in her hands and felt the Girl very present in the back of her mind, attentive and still, not pushing, just there.

Ready.

~~~

She went out the first night at midnight.

The warehouse district was three kilometers from her quarters, on the harbor side of the city where the working water that she'd looked at from the railing connected to the infrastructure that served it. At night it had the character that warehouse districts always had, functional and depopulated, the daytime industry having packed itself away and left behind the specific emptiness of purpose-built space between purposes.

She moved through it the way she moved through everything at night, which was with the completeness of the Slayer sense operating at full register. The air here was the air of a working port, overlaid with salt and industry and the specific smell of stored things, and beneath all of that the trace she'd caught in the morgue was present in a diffuse way that said the source of it used this area as territory.

Territory was the right word. She'd learned early that vampires were territorial in the specific way of predators who had a feeding ground and defended it against competition. Understanding the territory was the first step toward understanding the population.

She moved through the district systematically, not rushing, letting the Slayer sense do its mapping work. The territory was large, maybe fifteen city blocks, and had the characteristic signs of established use rather than recent arrival. Certain alleys had more of the trace than others. Certain areas showed less of it, which meant either the territory had boundaries or there was more than one source with different preferred zones.

She filed it all.

She didn't find anything on the first night.

This was not unusual. Finding something on the first night of a new patrol was the exception rather than the rule. The rule was patience and accumulated information and the moment when the pattern became dense enough to act on.

She went back to her quarters at three in the morning and slept and woke up at seven and told Lieutenant Chen.

Chen's response surprised her, which was itself information.

Chen listened to the complete picture, the three deaths and the morgue and the patrol and what she intended to do, with the attentive composure that characterized all of Chen's professional conduct. And then she said, "I'll need to inform Her Majesty."

"I expected that."

"Her Majesty will want to support this operation."

"I expected that too. I'd rather operate alone. Support in the conventional military sense creates more risk than it addresses."

"Her Majesty will understand that," Chen said. "What she'll want to provide is information access and the ability to act on anything you find that requires official response." She paused. "Not control. Resources."

Buffy thought about the Queen at the harbor railing. The offer she'd made, which was resources in exchange for honesty about what she could and couldn't give. "Okay. Information access would help. I want everything the city has on unexplained deaths in the past three years, not just the past month."

"I'll have it by this afternoon."

"And I want a direct channel. If I find something on a given night that needs immediate official response, I need to be able to reach someone who can act without a two-hour approval chain."

"I can arrange that." Chen paused. "Is there anything else?"

She thought about it. "Stakes," she said. "I need more stakes. Wood, hardwood specifically. Something that can be machined to a point."

A pause that was slightly longer than the previous ones. "I'll add that to the list," Chen said, in the tone of someone who was adding something to a list they hadn't anticipated adding.

The information arrived by afternoon. She spent four hours going through it.

The pattern was there.

Going back eighteen months, if you knew what you were looking for. Twelve unexplained deaths with characteristics consistent with exsanguination. Four missing persons who had disappeared from the warehouse district and its adjacent neighborhoods. A cluster in the winter months that suggested either a spike in activity or a period when something had been less careful.

She built a map.

The territory was cleaner than she'd thought. One central zone with two satellite areas, the pattern of a small group rather than a single hunter. Three to five, based on the activity density. The winter cluster suggested something that had been disturbed during that period, a change in the population, either an addition or a departure that had disrupted established patterns before they'd re-stabilized.

She looked at the map for a long time.

Then she went back out.

~~~

She found one on the third night.

Not by seeing it. By the Slayer sense registering its presence in an alley off the main warehouse thoroughfare, the specific signal of something that was functionally human and fundamentally wrong, the two components of the reading that together added up to the only thing they could add up to.

She went into the alley.

It was young. That was the first thing. In vampire terms young meant recently turned, still carrying significant trace of whoever it had been before the turning, the human patterns of movement and expression not yet entirely subsumed into the predator's economy. This one moved like a person who had been a person recently, with the hesitation and the physical uncertainty of the newly made.

It saw her at the same moment she saw it.

For a moment they looked at each other.

She'd had a thousand variations of this moment. The moment before, where everything that came after was implicit but not yet real. She'd learned not to use the moment for anything except reading whatever was readable. Some things could be read in this moment. Some things couldn't.

What she read in this one was confusion and hunger in proportions that said the confusion was losing the ongoing argument with the hunger, and that the hunger hadn't won yet but would, and that the window for anything other than the standard resolution was very small and closing.

"You don't have to do this," she said.

It looked at her. The human face was young, male, maybe twenty before the turning, the residue of someone who had been someone's son and probably someone's friend and had ended up in the warehouse district at night for reasons she would never know and which had led to this.

"What are you?" it said. The voice was the voice of the turning, rough-edged, not yet settled into the register that vampires developed when they'd been vampires for a while.

"The Slayer," she said.

It didn't know what that meant. Of course it didn't. The word was two thousand years old and the specific tradition that had given it meaning was lost to time and the thing in front of her had been human a month ago and had no framework for it.

"I'm the person whose job this is," she said. Which was a more functional answer.

It lunged.

She put it down with the efficiency of someone for whom this was muscle memory across two years and two thousand years simultaneously, the Girl's ancient knowledge and her own modern training arriving at the same conclusion through different paths. The stake was in her hand and then it was in the vampire and then the vampire was dust and she was standing in an alley in a city she'd been in for three months and the dust settled on the harbor-salt air.

She stood there for a moment.

This was always the moment that had a quality to it that nothing else had. Not triumph. Not grief exactly. Something that was its own category, the feeling of a thing having been what it was going to be, the specific completion of an act that was irreversible and necessary and still cost something she couldn't name.

She'd never found a word for it in any language she'd spoken.

She filed it the way she filed all the things she didn't have words for yet and went back into the thoroughfare and continued the patrol.

~~~

She found two more that night.

Not the young one's maker, she could tell by the age of the trace. Something older, more established, the kind of vampire that had been doing this long enough for the human residue to be entirely subsumed and what remained to be purely the predator with no ambiguity about it. And another young one, this one turned longer than the first, maybe three months, who was in the process of feeding on a dockworker who had made the wrong choice about which shortcut to take home.

She pulled it off the dockworker.

The dockworker ran. She didn't watch him go; she was already dealing with the vampire, which was old enough to fight back with a level of capability the younger one hadn't had and which required her full attention and got it.

The vampire went to dust.

She found the dockworker at the end of the block, where he'd stopped running and was standing with his hands on his knees and the specific physiological presentation of someone whose body had just informed him in very direct terms about what adrenaline was for.

She checked the wound on his neck. Shallow. The feeding had been interrupted early enough that the blood loss was minor. He'd have a headache and some muscle soreness from the adrenaline and he'd be fine.

He looked up at her when she crouched beside him.

"What," he said. And then he stopped because apparently what was sufficient.

"You're going to be all right," she said. "The wound is minor. You should go to a medic and have it looked at." She paused. "Don't go through the warehouse district at night for a while."

He looked at her. He looked at the alley where the fight had been. He looked back at her. "What was that thing?"

"Something that shouldn't exist here," she said. "I'm working on that."

He opened his mouth and then closed it again and then said, with the pragmatism of a man who had decided that the direct evidence of his own experience was going to have to be sufficient: "Right. Okay." He straightened up. "Thank you."

"Go get that looked at," she said.

He went.

She stood in the street for a moment and thought about the word shouldn't. It was the right word. Whatever had brought vampires to this planet in this century, it was wrong, and she was going to find out what it was, and she was going to fix it.

That was the job.

She went back to work.

Chapter 21: By the end of the first week she'd found seven.

Seven was not a colony. Seven was a founding population, established and reproducing at a controlled rate, which meant something was managing the reproduction. Vampires left to their own inclinations tended toward exponential growth that inevitably triggered its own collapse through either discovery and elimination or resource depletion, and the ones who survived were the ones who understood carrying capacity and practiced it.

Something here understood carrying capacity.

That was the concerning thing.

She told Fritz after the seventh and she told the Queen through Chen after the seventh and she told no one else, which felt like withholding in a way that was uncomfortable but was also correct. The wrong kind of official response to this would make everything worse.

Fritz received the updated picture with the systematic thoroughness he brought to everything and said, "Management implies leadership. Or at least organization."

"Yes."

"You haven't found the leadership yet."

"No. Everything I've found has been drones, essentially. Following an established pattern. Feeding within defined parameters." She looked at her map. "Whatever set the parameters is older and smarter than anything I've seen so far."

"How do you find it?"

"Follow the drones. They're reporting to something." She paused. "And look for the anomalies. Something this organized is going to have an operation beneath the operation. Some mechanism for sustaining itself that isn't just hunting. Something that explains how it got here."

Fritz was quiet for a moment. "How it got here is the part I keep returning to."

"Me too." She looked at the map. "Vampires don't have interstellar travel capability. They're a supernatural infection of an existing organism. They go where the organism goes." She paused. "Which means either something in the past two thousand years of human expansion brought them here deliberately, or they were carried here accidentally by someone who was already infected and didn't know it."

"Or who knew and didn't care," Fritz said.

She thought about that. "That's a category too," she said. "Yes."

The information access that Chen had arranged proved to be more useful than she'd expected, which was itself useful information about the Queen's intention to treat the offered resources as real rather than nominal. The three-year death record expanded to ten years when she asked for it and expanded again to thirty when she pushed, and across thirty years the pattern was cleaner and older and more structured than the short view had suggested.

Forty years.

The founding population was approximately forty years old. Small and careful in the early years, the death record showing the specific signature of a predator learning its environment and establishing sustainable practice. The current activity level was elevated relative to the historical baseline, which said something had changed in the past year. Something that had increased the population's appetite or reduced its caution.

She sat with this information for a long time.

She was still sitting with it on a Tuesday afternoon when she had a visitor.

The visitor was not announced in the way that visitors to her quarters were usually announced, which was through the building's intercom system. The visitor knocked on the door directly, which meant they were inside the building already, which meant either they lived in the building or someone had let them in.

She opened the door.

The woman on the other side was tall, older than Buffy but younger than middle age, with the kind of face that looked like it had spent a significant amount of time making very careful decisions and had the specific set of lines around the eyes that came from that. She was wearing civilian clothes in the way of someone who was always actually in uniform regardless of what they were wearing.

She had no treecats and no insignia and no identification that was visible.

She looked at Buffy with dark eyes that were doing the assessment thing that Buffy had come to recognize as characteristic of a specific type of professional.

"Lady Buffy," she said. "My name is Usher. I work for the Queen."

"Chen didn't tell me you were coming."

"No. Chen doesn't know I'm coming." She held Buffy's gaze. "May I come in?"

Buffy looked at her. The Slayer sense was not triggering in any of the ways it triggered for actual threat, which didn't mean threat was absent but meant that the threat, if present, was human and contained and not currently active. She stepped back from the door.

Usher came in and looked at the room with the brief comprehensive scan of someone who inventoried spaces by habit and came to the map on the table.

"You've been busy," she said.

"Yes." Buffy let the door close. "Who do you work for specifically?"

"Her Majesty." She looked at the map. "The part of Her Majesty that doesn't go through Lieutenant Chen."

"Intelligence."

"A specific kind." She looked at Buffy. "We've known about the warehouse district situation for approximately two years. We've been monitoring it."

Buffy looked at her. "You've been monitoring it."

"Yes."

"Three deaths in the past month. Twelve over eighteen months. Forty years of what looks like sustained predatory activity in the city of Landing." She kept her voice even. "And you've been monitoring."

"Yes." Usher's voice was equally even, carrying no defensiveness. "Because we don't have a mechanism for addressing it. We know what it is. We don't have the capability to do anything about it." She paused. "Until you arrived."

"How long have you known what it is?"

"Twenty years." She pulled out a chair at Buffy's table and sat in it with the ease of someone comfortable making herself at home in ambiguous circumstances. "There's a historical tradition. Not the Clinkscales tradition, which is theological. A separate line, more operational in character. People who knew about this category of entity and who maintained the knowledge practically rather than liturgically."

"A Watchers' Council," Buffy said.

Usher looked at her. "Nothing so formal. More of a�" she paused� "lineage of awareness. Passed within certain families and certain institutions. What the Church of Humanity Unchained knows as mythology, we know as operational reality." She paused. "With the significant limitation that knowing about something and having the capacity to address it are different problems."

"The Slayer," Buffy said.

"The Slayer," Usher said. "Yes. When you arrived, we took an interest."

Buffy looked at her. "You've been watching me."

"Yes. With Her Majesty's knowledge. Not with the intent to interfere. To understand." She held Buffy's gaze. "The monitoring of the warehouse district is ours. The deaths are real and they trouble us. We've been trying to prevent them without the capability to prevent them and that's a specific kind of failing that�" she stopped. "We've been waiting for something that could actually address this."

Buffy thought about this for a moment. She thought about what Usher represented, which was an institutional awareness of what she was that was neither the Council's model nor the Clinkscales model but a third thing, practical and unsentimental and constrained by capability.

"What do you want from me?" she said.

"What we want," Usher said, "and what we're asking are different things. What we want is for the warehouse district situation to be resolved. What we're asking is whether you'll share information with us while you resolve it, and whether you'll allow us to support you in ways that don't interfere with the operational approach you've already identified."

"What kind of support?"

"Information. We have forty years of records on this population. More detail than the city's medical archive." She paused. "And we have a theory about the leadership question that you're probably already forming yourself."

Buffy looked at her map. "Tell me the theory."

"There's a Solarian merchant vessel that has operated an irregular route through Manticore for the past forty-two years," Usher said. "The Eirene Constantia. Registered out of the Solarian League, ostensibly a luxury goods carrier. Her port visits correlate with the activity pattern in the warehouse district." She paused. "We believe the ship's master is the organizing intelligence you're looking for."

Buffy looked at her.

"A vampire in command of an interstellar merchant vessel," she said.

"Yes."

She thought about what that meant. The scale of it. Not a vampire who had stumbled into the space age through being carried by an infected human. A vampire who had adapted to the space age, who had built an operational infrastructure around the requirement to feed, who was moving through the star systems of the explored galaxy on a regular route with a sustainable practice and what amounted to a franchise operation in at least one port city.

Forty years. At minimum.

"How many systems?" she said.

Usher's expression told her she'd asked the right question. "We don't know. The Eirene Constantia's route takes her through eleven systems on a roughly four-month cycle. We have data on two of them that suggests similar patterns to Landing." She paused. "The others we haven't been able to assess."

"But likely."

"We think it likely."

Buffy looked at her map and the map expanded in her mind to something much larger, eleven star systems connected by a merchant route and at each port city a managed predatory population that fed at a controlled rate and generated a death record that looked like unexplained medical events to people who didn't know what they were looking for.

"When is the Eirene Constantia next in port?" she said.

"She's due in fourteen days."

Fourteen days.

She had fourteen days to understand the local population well enough to address the master when they arrived, which meant she needed to stop mapping and start acting, which meant the approach shifted now from surveillance to operation.

"Okay," she said. "Tell me everything you have."

~~~

Usher's files were meticulous.

The kind of meticulous that came from twenty years of systematic documentation by people who knew what they were looking at and had developed rigorous methods for capturing it. She spent three days going through them alongside her own accumulated information and the picture that emerged was clearer than anything she'd had before.

The Landing population was six. She'd accounted for five. The sixth was the oldest of the local drones, a century-old presence in the city who had been here before the Eirene Constantia's established route and who was therefore either the seed of the local population or a coincidental resident that the ship's master had absorbed into their operation.

The oldest one was going to be the hardest. Older vampires were more capable, more strategic, less likely to be caught in the circumstances that had caught the younger ones. She was going to have to think carefully about the approach.

She thought carefully about it.

On the fourth day she went back out and found the sixth, who was in the warehouse district in the way that an established resident occupied their territory rather than the way a hunter occupied it, more settled, less urgent. It saw her coming because it was old enough to have learned to sense what she was in return, the specific activation of the supernatural awareness that older vampires developed as a survival mechanism.

It ran.

She ran faster.

The chase went through three city blocks and over two rooftops and into a second warehouse that was not in her map because she hadn't found it yet and was clearly a long-established refuge, the kind of space that a careful predator maintained as an emergency option, with secondary exits that she noted even as she went through the primary one.

She caught it.

The fight was the longest she'd had since Hades. Not because she was outmatched but because it was old and strategic and conserved its energy for the moments when expenditure was likely to produce an outcome rather than spending on fights it couldn't win. She respected the intelligence behind that even while she was dealing with it, the specific respect of a professional for another professional's competence applied to a completely different purpose.

It went to dust eventually.

She stood in the warehouse and looked at what remained of the established refuge. A sleeping space. Some containers of supplies. A communication device of a type she didn't recognize.

She picked up the communication device.

She brought it to Usher.

Usher looked at it for a long moment and then looked at Buffy with something that might have been satisfaction and might have been relief. "This is Solarian manufacture," she said. "Commercial encryption with some nonstandard modifications." She paused. "We can work with this."

"I thought you might," Buffy said.

~~~

The communication device yielded, over the next four days, the shape of something she'd suspected and hadn't wanted to confirm.

The Eirene Constantia was not the only ship.

The network was larger. How much larger, the device's contents didn't fully reveal, but the traffic implied regular communication with at minimum three other vessels on routes through different parts of explored space. The master of the Eirene Constantia was not the top of the organization. They were a node.

There was a top.

She sat with this in Usher's office, which was in a building that she'd been to twice now and whose location she'd been asked not to share, which she hadn't, and looked at the map of explored space that Usher had pulled up and thought about what you did when the problem was larger than the immediate operational scope.

"You knew this was a possibility," she said.

"We suspected," Usher said. "The scale of a sustained operation across multiple star systems over multiple decades implied resources and organization that exceeded a single ship's capacity." She paused. "But suspicion isn't knowledge."

"Now it's knowledge."

"A preliminary version of knowledge." Usher looked at the map. "The full scope is still unclear."

Buffy looked at the map. Explored space was enormous. She'd learned this over the past months, built the sense of it through reading and briefings and the accumulated process of understanding a civilization that had been doing something she couldn't have imagined from Sunnydale. Eleven hundred years of interstellar expansion, hundreds of inhabited systems, billions of people spread across a volume of space that made the Hellmouth look like a pothole.

"There are Slayers," she said.

Usher looked at her.

"The lineage," she said. "The Clinkscales records. Carson showed me�there's a tradition that the Slayer is one, always one, but the mythology isn't necessarily accurate about the mechanics." She paused. "I'm not one. I'm the most recent, but the line didn't end with me. Someone else was called when Kendra was called. Someone else was called when Kendra died." She stopped. "That's two thousand years of called Slayers. In a species that has spread across a thousand star systems."

Usher was very still.

"They don't know what they are," Buffy said. "Almost certainly. The institutional knowledge is gone. The Council is gone. There's no infrastructure connecting them to the tradition." She looked at the map. "But the Slayer sense doesn't depend on institutional knowledge. It's operational whether you know it or not." She paused. "There are girls out there who dream of ancient women and who are faster than they should be and stronger than they should be and who have no idea why."
