She thought about fifteen-year-old herself, before Merrick, before the library, before any of it. She thought about what it had been like to have the power without the context and how much harder everything had been because of the missing context.

"We need to find them," she said.

Usher was quiet for a moment. "That's a significant operational undertaking."

"I know." She looked at the map. "Everything significant is." She paused. "But the reason matters. If there's a network operating across explored space, the vampire population in Landing is not an anomaly. It's a data point. And right now the Slayer lineage is scattered across a thousand systems with no connection to each other and no knowledge of what they are." She paused. "That's the strategic problem. The warehouse district is the tactical one."

"You're thinking beyond the tactical," Usher said.

"I always think beyond the tactical," Buffy said. "I just have to deal with the tactical first."

She looked at the communication device. She looked at the map. She thought about fourteen days until the Eirene Constantia arrived in port, and the node she was going to have to address when it did, and the larger network behind the node that was going to require more than she currently had.

She thought about the deal she'd made with the Queen.

Resources, the Queen had said. Support. And honesty about what she needed.

"I need to talk to Her Majesty," she said.

"I'll arrange it," Usher said.

~~~

The Queen received her in the same informal way she had at the waterfront, in a sitting room off the official receiving chambers that had the character of a space actually used rather than a space prepared for use. Ariel was on the back of her chair. Tea was on the table.

Buffy sat down and told her everything.

The Queen listened in the way she'd listened at the waterfront, with the complete attention of someone who was receiving information rather than managing a presentation. She asked three questions, all of them the right questions, and when Buffy finished she was quiet for a long moment.

"Fourteen days," she said.

"Until the Eirene Constantia arrives. Yes."

"And you want to address the ship's master."

"I want to address whoever is on that ship. If the master is the organizing intelligence for the Landing population, then yes, the master. If the master is human and the actual target is something aboard that hasn't shown itself yet�" she paused� "then whatever it is."

"Without triggering the larger network."

"Ideally. If the network realizes its Landing node has been addressed, it will reorganize and we'll lose the intelligence value of what we've found." She paused. "I want to take the node intact, not destroy it."

The Queen's eyebrows moved slightly, which was her version of a significant reaction. "Intact."

"The information it has is more valuable than its elimination in isolation." She paused. "I want to know the scope. I want to know the other nodes. I want to know the top." She met the Queen's eyes. "I've been doing this for two years and I've never had a vampire network this organized or this large. Understanding it fully is worth more than the immediate cleanup."

"What do you need?"

"Support for the boarding. Naval. People who know ship boarding procedure and who won't panic at what they find." She paused. "Scotty's not here."

"No." The Queen looked at her steadily. "I have people. If I can brief them on the situation."

"They'll need to be briefed. Yes." She thought about what she'd just said and what she was asking, which was for the Queen to tell a group of naval personnel that vampires existed and were running an interstellar operation and that the Slayer was going to be leading a boarding action against one of their ships. She thought about how that briefing was going to go. "It's going to be a difficult briefing."

"I've given difficult briefings," the Queen said. "What else?"

"Usher's resources. Full access to what they've found."

"Done."

"And I need someone to start a search," she said. "For girls between fourteen and nineteen who present with the specific physical profile. Across Manticore first and then wherever we can reach." She paused. "I know that's a long-term project. I know it's not the immediate priority. But if the network is as large as I think it is and the Slayer lineage is as scattered as I know it is, those two facts are related and eventually they become one problem."

The Queen held her gaze for a long moment. "You're thinking about building something."

"I'm thinking about what should exist and doesn't." She paused. "The Council kept the tradition alive for a thousand years. Imperfectly. Badly in some ways. But it kept the knowledge connected to the people who needed it." She paused. "Something needs to do that again. Something better than the Council was." She met the Queen's eyes. "I'm not the person to build it alone."

"No," the Queen said. "But you're the person who understands what it needs to be."

Buffy looked at her. "Is that a yes?"

"It's a yes to thinking about it," the Queen said. "Which is the beginning of all the yes that comes after." She poured tea for both of them and handed a cup across. "Let's talk about the boarding first."

Buffy took the cup. "Yes," she said. "Let's."

Chapter 22: The boarding team assembled eight days before the Eirene Constantia's scheduled arrival, which gave them six days to brief and train for a situation none of them had trained for.

There were five of them, selected by Usher with the Queen's approval, and they had in common the specific quality of people who had been selected for difficult things before and had come through those things without losing the capacity for the next difficult thing. They ranged from thirty to fifty, three men and two women, with backgrounds in naval special operations and intelligence work and one, a woman named Reva Castellan, whose specific background Usher described as diverse in a way that Buffy understood to mean things Usher wasn't going to specify.

She met them in a secure room in Usher's building and told them the truth.

She told it the way she'd learned to tell it in the past two months, which was directly and completely and without the hedging that turned necessary information into a negotiation. She told them what vampires were and what they could do and what killed them and what didn't and what the Eirene Constantia likely contained and what the boarding was going to find.

The room was quiet in the specific way of professionals receiving unexpected information and processing it through the framework of their existing competence rather than through the framework of disbelief.

Castellan asked the first question. "The stake. Hardwood specifically?"

"Hardwood works better. Other woods work but less reliably. Composite materials generally don't work at all." She paused. "Decapitation works regardless of material. Sunlight works. Fire works but is operationally messy." She looked around the room. "We're going to be in a ship. Fire is not an option."

"What about energy weapons?" That was one of the men, an older man named Brandis who had the bearing of someone who had spent significant time in zero-gravity environments.

"Depends on the energy level and the delivery. A graser at the levels they use in ship combat would work. A hand stunner probably won't. I've never tested it systematically." She paused. "Stakes are reliable. Decapitation is reliable. Those are the tools we plan around."

"The ship's crew," said the second woman, Osei. "Human or�"

"Likely both." She'd thought about this. "The master's immediate circle is probably vampires. The crew will be a mix. Some human, possibly some turned, possibly some who are aware of what they're working for and some who aren't." She paused. "We treat human crew as non-combatants until they demonstrate otherwise. We don't kill humans."

"Even if they're complicit?"

"Complicit humans are a legal problem for after the operation." She met the questioner's gaze. "We're not judge and jury. We're the boarding team."

The briefing went for three hours. She answered every question they asked and some they were working toward but hadn't formulated yet, and by the end the room had the specific quality that rooms had when people had absorbed something fundamentally reality-altering and had decided to work with it anyway.

Castellan stayed when the others left.

She was forty-two and had the face of someone who made difficult judgments regularly and had made her peace with the cost of that. She looked at Buffy with dark eyes that were doing something different from the assessment they'd been doing during the briefing.

"How old are you?" she said.

"Seventeen." Buffy held her gaze. "Almost eighteen."

Castellan looked at her for a moment. "I've been in this line of work for twenty years," she said. "I've done things in that time that I don't regret but that I know the cost of." She paused. "I want you to know that I understand what you're carrying."

Buffy looked at her. "Do you?"

"Probably not fully," Castellan said. "But more than most people you're going to meet." She paused. "I wanted to say that before the operation. After operations there's less time for it."

Buffy thought about the things people had said to her that had mattered. The ones that had come at the right moment and taken root. She thought about what Castellan was doing, which was offering a specific kind of recognition that was different from the recognition she'd received from Honor and from the Queen and from Scotty, different in kind rather than in quality.

"Thank you," she said.

Castellan nodded once. "I'll see you in six days."

~~~

The six days she spent doing two things simultaneously, which was becoming her characteristic operational mode.

She was addressing the remaining logistics of the boarding, working through the approach with Brandis and Osei, who had the most relevant boarding experience and who were, she found, exactly the kind of professionals she worked best with, which was the kind who said what they thought and did what they said and didn't require management.

She was also running the Slayer sense through the warehouse district every night, following the remaining population and tracking the changes that had started happening since she'd eliminated six of the seven.

The changes were informative.

The remaining drone, the one she hadn't found yet, had gone to ground in a way that said it knew something had changed. Its movement pattern, which Usher's files had documented over years of observation, had become compressed and irregular, no longer the established rounds of a confident predator but the compressed hiding behavior of something that had registered threat and was waiting for additional information.

Something was giving it information.

The communication device she'd taken from the sixth had been transmitting when she found it. She'd interrupted the transmission. Which meant something on the other end had noticed the interruption.

The Eirene Constantia already knew something was wrong in Landing.

She told Usher.

Usher was quiet for a moment and then said, "Do we move up the timeline?"

She thought about it. "No. Moving up the timeline means losing the advantage of the scheduled port visit. If the ship changes course now it goes somewhere we don't control and the intelligence value of the communication device becomes the only thing we have." She paused. "But we need to assume the master is already in a different operational posture than they would have been."

"More alert," Usher said.

"More alert. Which means the boarding approach has to account for that." She thought. "It also means the remaining drone is probably going to receive instructions. If I can find it before it acts on those instructions�"

"You'd know what the instructions were."

"Yes." She looked at her map. "I need to find it tonight."

~~~

She found it at 0100 in a part of the warehouse district she hadn't worked yet, a section closer to the water where the older buildings had been standing long enough to develop the kind of accumulated history in their structure that provided excellent concealment for things that needed concealment.

The drone was old enough to be careful and young enough to have been established here rather than predating the Eirene Constantia's route, which made it a local creation, and it was in communication with something, she could feel the specific quality of purpose that direction gave to a vampire's movement.

She followed it rather than addressing it immediately.

She followed it through four blocks of the warehouse district to a location she hadn't previously mapped, a building that was not a warehouse but a commercial facility of some kind, shuttered and dark, with the specific quality of a space that was being used for something other than its registered purpose.

She watched it go in.

She waited fifteen minutes.

Then she went in.

The building had three levels. The drone was on the second, in a room that had been a storage space and had become something else, and it was not alone.

There were three of them. The drone and two others she hadn't seen before, which meant either they'd been here and she'd missed them or they'd arrived since she'd started working the district. The latter seemed more likely. The Eirene Constantia's network had other nodes. It had been able to send reinforcements.

The three of them were in the middle of a conversation that stopped the moment she entered, which was the moment after her entry rather than during it because she'd entered quietly enough that the moment of awareness was delayed and the delay was enough.

She addressed the drone first because it was the one she had information about and therefore the one whose capabilities she could predict. The other two were faster than the drone which meant older and she worked through them in the specific sequence of fastest to slowest in reverse, the counterintuitive order that let her use the slower ones' movement patterns to constrain the faster ones' options.

It was not the cleanest fight she'd ever had. The confined space was a problem and the second of the older ones had a weapon she hadn't expected, a blade that it used with the specific competence of something that had been using edged weapons since before she was born.

She collected a cut across the left forearm, deep enough to be consequential in the short term and not consequential in the medium term, and dealt with the remaining two in quick succession and stood in the storage room with the dust settling around her and her arm bleeding in a way that would stop in approximately four minutes.

She looked at what remained, which was dust and a room that had been used for something and the specific emotional residue of a place where decisions had been made.

She'd interrupted something.

She didn't know yet what she'd interrupted. But the arrival of reinforcements and the activation of this specific location in the window before the Eirene Constantia's scheduled arrival said that the something was preparation.

The master was not coming to port on a scheduled visit.

The master was coming to find out what had happened in Landing, and possibly to do something about it.

She pulled out her communicator and called Usher.

"The situation has changed," she said. "We need to talk."

~~~

The new plan was simpler and more dangerous than the old plan, which was the direction that plans generally evolved under pressure.

The Eirene Constantia would be met at the port by the boarding team in the guise of a routine inspection, which was something that happened to commercial vessels in a system at war and which would not, by itself, trigger the master's caution before they were already aboard.

Once aboard, they would locate and address the master and whatever the master had brought with it and secure whatever intelligence the ship contained.

Buffy was going in first. This was not negotiable and she didn't negotiate it. She was going in first because the master was going to be old and capable and because the boarding team, however well briefed and however competent, did not have the Slayer sense and she did and in a situation where the immediate threat was going to be faster and stronger and more experienced than anything they'd trained against, the Slayer sense was the operational advantage that everything else depended on.

Brandis argued for a different approach. She listened to the argument completely and then explained clearly why the approach wouldn't work and watched him arrive at the same conclusion.

Castellan didn't argue. She looked at the plan and said, "What do you need from us?"

"Cover the exits," she said. "Everything that comes out of that ship that isn't me and isn't verifiably human crew, you treat as a threat. If I'm not out in twenty minutes, you come in."

"And if what comes out is�"

"If I come out and I'm not acting like myself, treat me as a threat," she said. "I won't be turned. The biology doesn't work that way. But I could be under a degree of coercion." She paused. "Trust the Slayer sense over my verbal instructions if there's any doubt."

Castellan looked at her. "You're asking me to go on gut instinct."

"I'm asking you to trust your training to recognize when something is wrong," she said. "It's different."

A beat. "All right."

She thought about Scotty, who was somewhere in the Talbott Cluster on a ship called the Hexapuma dealing with whatever the Talbott Cluster was dealing with. She thought about Honor, who was somewhere doing the thing the war needed her to do. She thought about Carson at Saganami Island and Ibanez and McKeon and Martingale and all the people who had become her context.

She thought about the Queen, who had offered resources and asked for honesty.

She was going to need to be very honest when this was over, about the scope of what she'd found and what it was going to take to address it.

But first she was going to go aboard the Eirene Constantia and deal with whatever was on it.

That part first.

She spent the night before sharpening her stakes and checking the knife and sleeping six hours and running at four in the morning and eating an enormous breakfast and going to the waterfront and standing at the railing one more time.

The harbor water was still the wrong color. She looked at it.

The Eirene Constantia was due in six hours.

She looked at the water and thought about the thing she'd been thinking about since the communication device and the map of explored space and the scope of what the network implied. She thought about the long shape of what was ahead, which was not just the boarding and not just the Landing population and not just the Manticore system, which was the whole of explored space and the thing that had moved through it and established itself in ways that two thousand years of human expansion had never adequately addressed because nobody had known it was there.

The Slayer lineage was out there.

The network was out there.

Both of those things were true simultaneously and the thing that connected them was her, the fact of her, this specific person at this specific intersection of two thousand years and a portal and the decision she'd made in a library on Hades to let herself become what she was becoming.

She turned from the railing.

Six hours.

She was going to be ready in five.

She walked back into the city and the city held her, already familiar in the way of a place you've inhabited long enough to stop noticing the ways it doesn't fit and start noticing the ways it does. She moved through it and it moved around her and she was here, in this place, at this time, with this purpose.

That was enough.

That was, as it turned out, exactly enough.

Chapter 23: The Eirene Constantia was a beautiful ship.

That was the first thing, arriving before the tactical assessment, the aesthetic fact of the vessel sitting in berth seventeen of Landing's commercial harbor in the early afternoon light with her lines clean and her hull maintained with a care that exceeded what commercial necessity required. Someone took pride in her. That was visible from the quay.

She filed it. Pride of ownership meant the master had a relationship with the ship that went beyond function, which meant the ship was not just a vehicle but an identity, which meant the master would not easily abandon it.

That was useful.

The boarding team assembled at the harbor authority checkpoint in the guise of a routine inspection team, which they looked like because Usher's people had dressed them like one and had provided documentation that would hold up to any check the Eirene Constantia's crew was likely to run. Commercial inspections happened. A system at war inspected commercial vessels. This was normal.

Buffy was not part of the inspection team's official manifest. She went in separately, through the maintenance access that Brandis had identified in the harbor's structural records, a route that was technically a fire egress and practically a way onto the vessel without going through the main boarding point.

She went in ten minutes before the inspection team arrived at the main hatch.

The maintenance access brought her into the ship's lower engineering spaces, which were warm in the way of active drives and smelled of lubricant and the specific recycled-air quality that all ships shared regardless of their age or purpose. She moved through it without turning on lights, not needing lights, the Slayer sense doing what it did in the dark which was more and more precisely than it did in the light.

The ship had eleven people aboard.

She could feel the shape of them, distributed through the vessel's spaces, the pressure of occupied rooms and corridors against the general background hum of the ship's systems. Most were in the forward sections. Two were in the engineering spaces she'd come through, close enough that she'd worked around them on the way in.

She counted again.

Eleven. Of those eleven, four had the quality she was looking for, the specific wrongness that said what she was reading was not entirely human. Three were the distributed kind, spread through the forward sections, old enough to be settled in their operational profile. The fourth was something different.

The fourth was very old.

She'd felt very old before. She'd fought very old. The Master had been very old, and the Master had been the oldest thing she'd encountered in two years of slaying, and the Master had been a presence she could feel from the other side of a warehouse before she saw him.

What she was feeling now from the forward section of the Eirene Constantia was in the same register as the Master.

She adjusted her assessment of the next twenty minutes accordingly.

The inspection team would be at the main hatch in approximately eight minutes. She had eight minutes to understand the ship's layout as it actually was rather than as the harbor's records showed it, which for a vessel with a long-standing irregular operation were likely not the same thing.

She moved forward.

The engineering spaces gave way to crew quarters, a short corridor of doors that were mostly closed, and she went through it without stopping and into the main cargo section, which was large and dim and smelled of the specific mixture of scents that accumulated in spaces where many different things had been stored over many years. The cargo was partially loaded, pallets and containers in configurations she didn't try to read, and she moved through it and found what she'd suspected she would find.

A secondary space. Below the main cargo deck. Accessible through a hatch that was not on any schematic she'd been given but which was exactly where you'd put a secondary space on this class of vessel if you'd had this class of vessel long enough to know where the structural voids were.

She stood over the hatch and felt what was below it.

Very old. And not alone.

She had six minutes.

She didn't go through the hatch.

She moved forward to the main corridor and positioned herself in the intersection that would give her sightlines to the bridge access, the forward compartments, and the cargo deck hatch, and she waited.

At the four-minute mark the main boarding hatch opened and she heard the sounds of the inspection team coming aboard. At the three-minute mark something in the forward section moved.

She felt it before she heard it, the specific quality of something very old deciding to do something. She moved toward it.

The corridor outside the bridge was short and well-lit, and the door at the end of it was closed. She stood in the corridor and waited.

The door opened.

The man who came through it was tall and lean with the look of someone who had been the same age for a very long time, the specific quality of preserved youth that vampires developed after enough centuries. He was wearing clothing that was expensive and understated and that had been fashionable for long enough to have lapped itself and come back around.

He saw her.

She saw him.

The moment before.

He was fast. She'd felt how old he was and she'd recalibrated for it and the recalibration was still insufficient by a margin that she only understood once he was already moving, the kind of speed that came from centuries of practice layered over whatever the base capability had been.

She was faster.

Barely.

The corridor was not a good fighting space. It was narrow and straight and favored whoever had the longer reach, which was him by approximately eight centimeters that mattered significantly in the exchange of the first three seconds. She accepted two impacts that she would have accepted nothing from a younger opponent and used the acceptance of them to get inside his reach and from inside his reach the length advantage was neutralized.

He was strong. She was stronger.

He knew techniques she hadn't encountered before, the accumulated library of every martial tradition it had encountered across centuries. She worked through the unfamiliar patterns by feel rather than recognition, the Slayer sense doing the pattern-analysis work faster than conscious thought could manage.

The Girl was very present.

He went for the wall at one point, trying to create space, and they went through a door that gave way into a small compartment that was a communications room, and she got the stake into position and he saw it and the expression that crossed his face was old enough to be interesting.

Not fear. Something more complex, the expression of something that had survived long enough to have a sophisticated relationship with its own ending.

"You're the Slayer," he said.

"Yes."

"I didn't know one still existed."

"People keep saying that," she said. "It keeps being wrong."

He looked at the stake and then at her. The calculation in his eyes was visible. "You want the network."

"I want information about the network," she said. "There's a difference."

"And if I give you information."

"I don't make deals with vampires," she said. "The information you get from a deal is less reliable than the information you get from the alternative, because the thing making the deal shades what it tells you toward whatever outcome is best for it."

"And the alternative provides accurate information?"

"The alternative provides whatever information is accessible in the operational environment," she said. "Which includes your ship's records and communications logs and whatever your crew knows, which I'm going to have access to regardless of this conversation."

He looked at her.

"You're very young," he said.

"Yes."

"And already this precise about it." Something crossed his face. "How long have you been doing this?"

"Two years." She met his eyes. "You?"

A pause. "Longer than your civilization has existed," he said.

She thought about what that meant.

"Then you know how this ends," she said.

"Yes," he said. Something that was almost regret, too old to be simply regret. "I know how this ends."

He came at her again.

She was ready.

~~~

The dust settled.

She stood in the communications room and breathed and let the category that didn't have a name do what it did when a fight that mattered was finished. Not triumph. Not grief exactly. The completion of a necessary and irreversible act.

She'd gotten something in the last moments. Not a deal. But the very old ones were sometimes legible at the end in ways the younger ones weren't, and what she'd read was a direction.

Not a name. Not coordinates. A direction. The sense of something above the master in the hierarchy that was older still and further away, and that the master had been connected to with a long leash rather than close operational control.

The master had been running this network with significant autonomy.

She filed it.

She picked up the communicator. "Castellan. I'm done forward. Three more in the cargo section. Don't let anything off the ship."

"Copy." A pause. "Are you all right?"

She looked at the dust. "Yes. Give me five minutes on the cargo deck before you bring anyone in."

"Copy. Five minutes."

She went to the cargo deck.

~~~

The three in the cargo section were younger than the master and older than the Landing drones, and they knew she was coming. She dealt with the one positioned over the secondary hatch first, accepting a second impact to her left side, ribs, deep enough to matter in the short term. She accepted it and dealt with the hatch-guardian and then worked through the remaining two.

Three more in the dust.

She stood over the secondary hatch and breathed through the rib pain and opened the hatch.

The space below was a living space, long-established. Furniture from different eras. Books, actual physical books, stacked and shelved with care. Objects she couldn't immediately categorize, some of which were old enough to have been in a museum in her time.

No one in it.

She moved through it carefully.

The books first. Several languages. Dates ranging from the sixteenth century to something within the past fifty years.

Then one thing, on a shelf by itself. A small case of dark material she didn't have a word for, sealed with a mechanism she didn't recognize, that the Slayer sense was registering the way a compass registered north.

Something was in the case.

Something that was not natural.

She looked at it for a long time without touching it.

Then she picked up her communicator and called Fritz.

"I need you at berth seventeen," she said. "Now. And bring whatever you have for imaging something without opening it."

Chapter 24: Fritz arrived forty minutes later.

Castellan had managed the ship and its human crew with the specific competence Buffy had been counting on. Four of the crew had been aware of the master's nature. Three were cooperating. One was not yet cooperating but was being given the time to reconsider.

She stayed with the case and waited for Fritz.

Fritz came in with Petrakis, who had committed fully to the recalibration project and who turned out to be unexpectedly useful because he brought the rigorous external skepticism that prevented her and Fritz from confirming each other's interpretive biases.

Fritz looked at the case and at her.

"You haven't touched it," he said.

"No."

"Tell me what you're feeling from it."

She thought about how to describe what the Slayer sense was doing. "Supernatural. Not demonic. Not vampiric. Something else." She paused. "The closest thing I have in my experience is a nexus point. Like a spot with concentrated mystical energy. Except contained. As if someone took a nexus point and put it in a box."

Fritz and Petrakis ran the scanner over it. The results made Petrakis look at the device and then at the case with the expression of someone whose instrument had just told them something its manufacturers had not anticipated.

"The material is organic in origin," Petrakis said. "Something that was alive at some point and is not alive now." He turned the scanner toward Fritz. "The interior shows two readings. One is a physical object. Small. Roughly cylindrical. The other is—" he paused— "the scanner is reading a second physical presence that I can't localize. As if there's something in the case that the instrument can detect but can't locate."

She looked at the case.

She thought about two thousand years. About mystical energy and nexus points and the Powers That Be and the category of supernatural that she'd never fully understood, that Giles had called the higher order.

She thought about the Girl, who was very present and very still in a way that was different from the usual quality of her presence, more focused, the attention of something ancient recognizing something familiar.

"I'm going to open it," she said.

Fritz and Petrakis stepped back.

She looked at the mechanism. It was not a lock in any conventional sense. It was more like a seal, and the seal's logic responded to her presence with a resonance that she recognized as the same resonance the Slayer sense produced, the same frequency, the same register.

She put her hand on the seal and pressed.

The case opened.

Inside, nested in a material she had no name for, were two things.

The first was a small cylindrical object, dark, approximately the length of her hand, with markings on it that were not writing in any alphabet she knew but which the Slayer sense registered as significant in the specific way of things that had been made deliberately, imbued with intention.

The second was not a physical object.

It was a presence.

A very small presence, compressed and patient in the way of something that had been waiting for a long time and had learned to wait well. She felt it the moment the case opened, felt it orient toward her the way a compass oriented toward north, and the orientation had the quality of recognition.

"Hello," she said.

The presence pressed against the edge of the case.

Not threatening. Not asking anything. Just present, and oriented toward her, and she had the strong impression that it had been waiting specifically for something like her, not her precisely but something with the frequency of what she was, and it had been waiting for a very long time.

She looked at Fritz without moving her hand from the case. "You remember when I said there were things I didn't have a scientific framework for?"

"Yes," Fritz said carefully.

"This is one of those things." She looked back at the case. "I need the Clinkscales archive. Carson." She paused. "And I need to talk to the Queen."

~~~

They spent three days in the archive.

Not the physical archive, which was on Grayson and not immediately accessible, but Carson's memory of it and the scans he'd been making of the most significant documents for the past six months and the digital records the Clinkscales family had maintained since digital records had become available.

The archive had four references to the Keeper's Seal. All terse, all clearly written by people recording received knowledge rather than firsthand experience.

Stripped of interpretive overlays, the primary references said:

That the Seal had been made in the time before the Hellmouth's closing.

That it contained a record of what the Hellmouth had been and what had made it and what preventing another required.

That the record was not text but something else, something the notation's author had described as living knowledge.

And that the Keeper was not a created being but a called one, something that had chosen the function in the way that she'd chosen the Slayer's function, through the intersection of capacity and decision.

She read that last part twice.

"The Keeper chose," she said.

"Yes," Carson said.

"Then it's not a guardian in the sense of a created tool," she said. "It's a person. Or something like a person. Something that decided this was what it was going to do."

She looked at the case. "The Eirene Constantia's master had this. He was keeping it."

"Or he'd taken it from whoever was supposed to have it," Carson said. "We don't know how he came to have it."

She thought about what she'd read in the master in those last moments. The direction, the sense of something above him in the hierarchy that was older still. She thought about the master's long leash and his autonomy.

"He took it," she said. "From whatever is at the top of the network. And he kept it because he understood what it was." She paused. "He was protecting it."

Carson looked at her. "Why would a vampire protect something that belongs to the Slayer line?"

She didn't have an answer to that. She filed the question.

"I need to open the record," she said. "The cylindrical object. I think the Keeper is the key for that. But I need to know what I'm opening first." She looked at Carson. "Anything about the Keeper's Seal. Anything about what the record contains."

They kept reading.

Chapter 25: She opened the record the following morning.

The room she'd chosen was small and had no windows, which she'd asked for without fully explaining why. Fritz and Petrakis were present. Carson was present. Allison was there, sitting at a separate display with the genetic data she'd been working with, because she'd said she wanted to see what happened to the channel in the biological data when contact was made.

Honor was near the door with Nimitz.

Buffy sat across from the case and let herself be still.

The presence in the case oriented toward her immediately. She'd felt it do this every visit and it still had the quality of something fresh about it, the recognition that didn't diminish with repetition.

"I'm going to open the record," she said. "I don't know what that's going to feel like. I don't know how long it's going to take." She paused. "I'm asking you to help me."

The presence pressed against the edge of the case.

She reached out and opened the case.

The cylindrical object was cool in her hand and the markings on it moved under her fingers in a way that was not mechanical, the sense of them changing their configuration without her being able to catch the change.

The presence came out of the case.

Not dramatically. Not visibly. She felt it emerge and orient and settle into a position she couldn't have pointed to physically but which felt like beside rather than inside or above. Beside. Accompanying.

The object opened.

Not literally. But something in it opened in the direction of the sense she was using and the contents became accessible.

The record was not a text.

Living knowledge was the closest description she had, the direct transmission of what someone had known rather than what they'd been able to put into words. She received it the way she'd received the Girl's memories in the dreams, being rather than watching, except that she was still also herself and the anchor of Carson's presence in the room held her in the room while the record did what it did.

What she received was this:

The Hellmouth had not been a natural feature of the Earth. Something had made it. A long time before humanity had developed the vocabulary to describe what a Hellmouth was, something had deliberately introduced a point of dimensional instability into the fabric of a world that had not had one naturally.

The effect of the Hellmouth's presence had been the concentration of supernatural activity around it. The Slayer line had evolved in response, the specific capability calibrated to the specific threat.

The long dark in the archive's notation was not a metaphor.

Something was coming. Not soon by human timescales. But eventually. The thing that had made the Hellmouth had not stopped existing. It was patient in the way of things that measured time in geological epochs.

It was going to try again. Not on a single world. The record was clear: the thing had observed humanity's expansion through the stars and had revised its approach accordingly. Not one Hellmouth. Many. Distributed. Each small enough to be dismissed as local anomaly. Each connected beneath the surface to the others in the way of roots connecting trees that appeared separate above ground.

The Slayer lineage was the specific instrument that could address this.

One Slayer was not sufficient.

She came back to the room slowly. Fritz and Petrakis were exactly where they'd been. Carson was closer than he'd been, not in front of her but near. Honor was still near the door. Nimitz was very still.

She looked at her hands.

"How long?" she said.

"Four hours," Carson said.

She looked at the cylindrical object. It was closed now, the opening she'd felt in it no longer present. The presence was still beside her, its quality different, less patient in the waiting sense and more present in the accompanying sense.

"I know what the work is," she said.

She looked at Fritz. "I need paper. I want to write down what I received before any of it goes the way memories go." She paused. "And then I need to talk to the Queen."

~~~

The meeting with the Queen was the longest conversation she'd had with anyone since arriving in this century.

Four hours, in the informal sitting room with Ariel and tea and the late afternoon light.

She told the Queen everything.

The Eirene Constantia and the network. The case and the Keeper. The record and what the record said. The thing that had made the Hellmouth and what it was doing and the specific shape of the work that needed to exist in response to it.

The Queen listened.

When Buffy finished the Queen was quiet for a long time.

"You're asking me to help you build something," the Queen said. "Not a short-term operation. Not a resource allocation for the current political situation." She paused. "An institution."

"Yes."

"On the basis of information received from an object of uncertain origin via a mechanism I have no framework for, telling you about a threat that won't fully materialize in the lifetime of anyone currently alive."

"Yes." She held the Queen's gaze. "I know how that sounds."

"It sounds like an extraordinary ask." The Queen paused. "It also sounds consistent with everything I know about you and everything Ariel tells me about you." She looked at the treecat. "He thinks you're telling the truth."

"I am telling the truth."

"I know." The Queen looked at her. "Tell me what the institution looks like."

She said: "Connected. The Slayer lineage distributed across human space, which it already is whether we want it to be or not. Instead of isolation and control, support and information. Instead of one Council at the center managing the Slayers as assets, a network that serves the Slayers as people." She paused. "The lineage needs to know what it is. Not to control it. To give it what the knowledge gives, which is context for something that's already happening to them regardless of whether they understand it."

The Queen looked at her. "You're describing something that operates across sovereign systems."

"Yes."

"That's politically complicated."

"I know. Everything significant is." She paused. "But the threat doesn't respect sovereign boundaries. The network we found operates across eleven star systems. Whatever is coming will not stop at the Manticoran border." She paused. "The institution has to match the scale of the problem."

"And Manticore leads it."

"Manticore provides the initial support and infrastructure," she said. "Whether Manticore leads it is a different question. I'd argue the institution needs to be understood as serving the lineage rather than serving any specific political entity. Otherwise it becomes the Council."

The Queen held her gaze for a long moment.

"I'll help you build it," she said. "Not all at once. Not without constraints. There are political realities that apply even to this." She paused. "But I believe you, and I believe the record, and I believe that what you're describing is real and that the work you're proposing is necessary." She met Buffy's eyes. "What do you need first?"

"Resources," she said. "Support. People who will help me build whatever life I'm building here." She paused. "And your personal word that you will not use me as a symbol without telling me you're doing it."

"And in exchange?"

"Honesty," she said. "When the political process is asking something of me that I can't give, I'll tell you."

It was an unusually honest offer on both sides.

"I'll take it," the Queen said.

Chapter 26: Spring came to the capital.

She'd been on Manticore for five months now and she'd accumulated enough seasonal context to notice when it changed, the specific shift in the air and the light that said the angle of the sun relative to the continent had moved enough to matter.

She ran in it and it was the spring air she was learning, the first spring, and the learning had the quality of all first times, direct and total and irreplaceable.

The search for the lineage was three weeks old.

Chen had coordinated with the Queen's office and the result was a quiet inquiry running through the medical records of the Star Kingdom and its allies, looking for young women between fourteen and twenty with the specific physical profile that Fritz had documented.

In three weeks they'd found four candidates in the Manticore system and two on Grayson.

Carson had gone back to Grayson on compassionate leave. His grandfather had wept. Carson had written her a letter that described the weeping with the specific tender precision of a young man who loved someone very much and was not embarrassed to say so. She'd read the letter three times.

The grandfather had also immediately identified two girls in the surrounding community who matched the profile. He'd been watching for them, it emerged, for thirty years. He'd had no way to know what to do when he found them and had said nothing.

She'd written Thomas Clinkscales a letter.

She'd spent a long time on the letter.

The four Manticoran candidates were in various stages of initial contact, delicate work that she was doing mostly personally rather than through intermediaries, because the first conversation was the most important one and she'd been in that conversation from the other end and she knew what it needed to be.

She'd had the first of them the previous week.

The girl was fifteen and from a small city on Manticore's southern continent and had been in three separate incidents in the past two years that her parents described as alarming and which she described with the careful understatement of someone who had been told that alarming was bad and was trying not to be bad.

Buffy had sat across from her and told her what she was.

Not what the Council would have said, which was here is your destiny and here is what you owe it. Not even what Merrick had said to her, which had been more honest than the Council but still filtered through the Council's framework.

What she'd said was: you're not broken. You're not wrong. There is a name for what you are and a reason for it and a history behind it and you are part of something that is larger than this room. And the most important thing I can tell you is that you get to decide what you do with it.

The girl had looked at her for a long moment.

She'd said: are you one too?

Buffy had said: yes.

The girl had said: is it hard?

Buffy had said: yes. And also good. Both of those things at the same time, most of the time.

Walking back to the shuttle she'd thought about Giles, who had done the same thing for her minus the open door, the Council having specific opinions about the doors that got opened. She'd thought about what it would have meant to have the open door from the beginning.

She thought about it now, on the morning run through the pink flowers, and it settled into the category of things that were both still hard and also good.

The Keeper was present in the way it had been present for weeks, the accompanying consciousness that she was developing a relationship with that she didn't have full vocabulary for yet.

She ran until she felt like herself.

Then she went and ate an enormous breakfast and watched the stars go by through the mess hall viewport and when Carson came in at 0700 with his arm out of its sling and his hair doing something optimistic, she moved over and made room and he sat down and they watched the wrong constellations together without needing to say anything about them.

The stars were wrong and she was here and Manticore was home and that was the shape of things.

She could work with that shape.

She was getting quite good at that.

Chapter 27: The second candidate's name was Yeva Ostrova and she was sixteen and she had been waiting for exactly this conversation for approximately two years without knowing what conversation she was waiting for.

That was clear from the moment Buffy sat down across from her in the small front room of the family's apartment, which was the apartment of a family that had done well enough to be comfortable and cared enough about it to show it, books on actual shelves and a rug that had been chosen rather than defaulted to. Yeva's mother had brought tea and then removed herself with the careful discretion of a woman who understood that her daughter needed this conversation without her in it.

Yeva sat across from Buffy with her hands in her lap and her spine straight and her dark eyes doing the thing that eyes did when they were paying complete attention.

She was tall for sixteen, broad-shouldered, with the look of someone whose body had been catching up to itself for the past year. She'd been in four incidents that the school records described as disciplinary issues and that Buffy read, between the lines, as what happened when someone with the physical profile was still developing the judgment to govern it.

She looked, in some specific way, like a version of someone Buffy had been.

"You're the Slayer," Yeva said.

Buffy looked at her. "You know that word."

"My grandmother was Russified," Yeva said, which was not a complete explanation and she seemed to know it wasn't. "She came from a family that kept old stories. The kind of old stories that serious people said were not serious." She paused. "She told me about the Chosen One. When I was small. Before I�" she stopped.

"Before you started noticing things," Buffy said.

"Yes."

"What did you notice first?"

Yeva looked at her hands. "That I was faster." She turned them over, the universal gesture of people looking at the instruments of something they didn't fully understand. "My classmates would react and I would already be done reacting. In gymnastics first. Then in other things." She paused. "I broke someone's wrist once. By accident. I misjudged." She looked up. "I've been very careful since then."

"I know that feeling," Buffy said.

"Are you careful?"

"I've learned to be. It took a while." She paused. "The dreams."

Yeva went still.

"Dark plain," Buffy said. "Women running. Old. Older than anything."

Yeva's expression did something that was relief and fear in proportions that shifted as Buffy watched, the fear decreasing and the relief increasing. "You dream it too."

"Not anymore. I have a�different relationship with the source now." She paused, because that was going to require a lot more conversation to explain. "But yes. I know what you're dreaming."

"What is it?"

"The lineage. The women who came before us. The line goes back a very long way." She held Yeva's gaze. "You're part of it. You were born part of it, the same way I was. It's not optional and it's not an accident." She paused. "What is optional is what you do with it."

"My grandmother said the Chosen One had no choice."

"Your grandmother was working from incomplete information." She kept her voice even. "The tradition your grandmother knew had a version of the truth. The version was�shaped by the people who maintained it. The shaping removed some things." She paused. "Choice was one of the things it removed."

Yeva was quiet for a moment. "What do you need from me?"

"Right now? Nothing." She let that land. "Right now I'm here because you deserve to know what you are. That's all. The knowing is yours. What you do with it is yours." She paused. "I'm not going to ask you to do anything with it. I'm not going to tell you what your obligation is. I'm not going to put conditions on the information."

Yeva looked at her. "That's different from what my grandmother described."

"Yes," Buffy said. "It is."

They talked for two hours. Yeva asked better questions than Buffy had asked at fifteen, which was partly temperament and partly the fact that Yeva had been quietly processing something for two years before the conversation arrived, and the questions that came out of two years of quiet processing were better than the questions that came out of a sudden ambush in a gym.

Buffy answered everything she could and was honest about the things she couldn't.

When she left she gave Yeva a communicator frequency and said she'd write, and she meant it.

Walking back through the spring afternoon she thought about the grandmother keeping old stories. The dispersal of knowledge. Two thousand years of the lineage's infrastructure dissolving into folklore and religious tradition and family memory, the full picture fragmenting into pieces that were each true and each insufficient.

She thought about what it was going to take to reassemble it. Not restore � you couldn't restore something that had been gone this long. Build something new that had learned from what the old version got wrong.

She thought about that all the way back to her quarters.

~~~

The Keeper, she was discovering, had opinions.

Not expressed as language. The Keeper didn't use language, or hadn't yet, and she'd stopped expecting it to and started paying attention to the other register it used, which was something between emotion and direction. It was how the Girl communicated when the Girl communicated directly rather than through the dreams, a mode that she'd been receiving without fully recognizing it for months.

The Keeper's opinions expressed themselves as a kind of pressure. Not insistent. More like the pressure of weather, the sense of something coming from a direction, which she'd learned to read as opinion about what she was doing.

It had opinions about Yeva. Positive ones.

It had opinions about the pace of the network search, which it found simultaneously appropriate and insufficient, a combination she'd come to understand as the Keeper registering the tension between the urgency of the underlying situation and the operational reality of what was possible.

It had opinions about the letter she'd written Carson that was still unsent on her desk, the one she'd been revising for four days. She wasn't entirely sure what its opinion was on that one. She suspected it was something in the vicinity of send it.

She sent it.

The letter said what it said, which was that she was thinking about the long work and thinking about what came after the long work and thinking about the shape of a life that was also the shape of a purpose, and that thinking about all of those things kept arriving at the same place, which was that the shape was better when it included the people who were already part of it, and that she wanted to say that explicitly rather than leave it as an implication.

She sealed it and sent it before she could revise it again.

The Keeper's pressure eased slightly in the way of something satisfied, which she found both reassuring and mildly annoying.

"You're not my Watcher," she said.

The accompanying presence did something she'd come to interpret as amusement.

"Don't," she said.

It persisted in the amusement.

She went to bed.

~~~

Three things happened in the same week in the seventh month.

The first was that Fritz's preliminary paper produced a response from a physicist at the Manticore Royal University named Dr. Haverstock who had been working on what he called dimensional interface theory for fifteen years and who had a framework for the scanner readings that Fritz had not anticipated and which required an urgent meeting.

The second was that the Grayson node of the vampire network moved. She'd been tracking it through the communication intelligence from the Eirene Constantia and through Usher's contacts on Grayson, and the movement said the node knew something had happened in Landing and was redistributing in response.

The third was a letter from Honor.

She read Honor's letter first because that was the order in which they arrived and also because it was Honor.

The letter was not long. Honor was not given to long letters when short ones said what needed saying, which Buffy had come to understand was characteristic. The relevant content was in three sentences in the middle.

The first said that things were developing in ways that she couldn't specify in a letter but which she expected would require her to be back in the Manticore system within the next two months.

The second said that she hoped Buffy was well and that Nimitz sent his regards, which was not the kind of thing Nimitz sent as a formality.

The third said that she had been thinking, over the months since Hades, about the conversation in the shuttle the night before the operation when Buffy had told her everything, and that what she'd been thinking was that she was glad Buffy had.

She read the third sentence three times.

Then she set the letter aside and went to meet Dr. Haverstock.

~~~

Dr. Haverstock was sixty and small and had the kind of energy that people had when they'd been working on something for fifteen years and had just received information that reorganized the entire project. He shook her hand with both of his and said, "The readings Fritz sent me are consistent with a stable dimensional interface of limited aperture, which I have theorized the existence of for fifteen years but have never had instrumental evidence for."

She looked at him. "Explain dimensional interface to me like I don't have a physics background."

He explained it. It took twenty minutes and several diagrams on the whiteboard behind his desk and two cups of tea, and what she came away with was this: the physics of this universe, the physics of two thousand years of scientific development in human civilization, had arrived at a framework that explained what the Keeper was in terms that didn't require the word supernatural.

The Keeper was a stable self-sustaining pattern of organized energy at a dimensional interface. The dimensional interface was not a metaphor. It was a real physical boundary between the experienced universe and a contiguous dimension that existed alongside it and that human physics had been theorizing about for decades without instrumental confirmation.

The case was a containment device for the interface. The Keeper was the pattern at the interface.

The pattern could, in theory, interact with physical reality at the interface boundary in ways consistent with what she'd been experiencing.

"It's not magic," she said, when he finished.

"It's physics we don't fully understand yet," he said, which was the kind of answer she'd been getting about the supernatural her entire Slayer career from people who were trying to be accurate.

"Can you talk to it?" she said.

He looked at her. "It?"

"The pattern. The Keeper." She paused. "The interface."

He looked at his whiteboard. "The interface itself, no. The pattern at the interface�that's a more interesting question." He paused. "The pattern has structure. Structure implies information organization. If the information organization can be addressed�" He stopped. "I would need significantly more data."

"You can have access," she said. "Within limits I'll specify." She paused. "Fritz is the point of contact. He's been working the biological angle and you'll need to coordinate." She looked at the whiteboard. "I want to understand it. What it is and how it works and what it can do." She paused. "It's been beside me for three months and I still don't fully know what I'm working with."

"Neither do I," he said. "But I'm very interested in finding out."

She left the meeting with the specific feeling of a thing having been placed in the hands of someone who was going to do something useful with it, the relief of delegation to competence.

The Grayson node was more immediately pressing.

~~~

The Grayson node's redistribution was not random. She'd spent two days with the communication intelligence mapping the movement and what she saw was a pattern consistent with a node that was contracting rather than fleeing, pulling itself into a smaller and more defensible configuration. Something had told it that the Landing operation had been targeted rather than accidental, and it was responding the way a professional operation responded to targeted pressure, not by dissolving but by consolidating.

Consolidation was both better and worse than dissolution. Better because a consolidated target was a locatable target. Worse because a consolidated target had resources pooled in one place.

She told Usher.

Usher had Grayson contacts that were the product of the Alliance relationship and years of intelligence cooperation, and the contacts produced, within forty-eight hours, a preliminary location for the consolidated node and a population estimate that was higher than she'd have preferred.

Eight to twelve.

That was not a one-person operation.

She sat with this for a day and then she called Carson.

He was still on Grayson. He'd extended his leave by two weeks to spend more time with his grandfather and the two newly identified lineage candidates, which was the right use of the time and which she'd told him so. He answered her call with the expression of someone who had been expecting it and had been waiting to see what form it took.

"I need help on Grayson," she said. "Not the lineage work. The other kind."

He looked at her steadily. "How many?"

"Eight to twelve. Consolidated. I can't do eight to twelve alone in a consolidated position."

"No," he agreed. He was quiet for a moment. "I can't do it either. Not with current capability."

"I know. I'm not asking you to fight them. I need your help with the approach." She paused. "You know Grayson. I don't. I need to understand the geography and the access and the political sensitivity of operating on Grayson soil without it becoming an official Grayson operation." She paused. "And I need to know if Castellan can come."

He thought about it. "The political sensitivity is significant. The Church of Humanity Unchained has specific positions on demonic entities and the methods of addressing them that may or may not be consistent with how this operation has to run." He paused. "My grandfather could help with that. He has relationships with the Sacristy that are� he's trusted. On the subject of the lineage specifically."

"Would he want to?"

"He would consider it a profound religious obligation and one of the great opportunities of his life," Carson said. "So yes."

She thought about Thomas Clinkscales, whom she'd still never met, who had kept the faith for thirty years and identified candidates the system hadn't known to look for and wept when his grandson came home from a prison planet with news that the lineage was real and present and in this century.

"Okay," she said. "Set up the meeting with your grandfather. I'm coming to Grayson."

His expression shifted slightly in the way it did when he was receiving something significant and not making a production of it. "When?"

"Five days," she said. "I need to coordinate with Usher and arrange Castellan and brief the Queen's office." She paused. "And I need to eat a very large dinner because the healing factor has opinions about operating on insufficient caloric reserves."

He almost smiled. "I'll have my mother cook."

"What's your mother's cooking like?"

"Better than mine," he said. "Which is not the comparison you want. She does a lamb dish that�" he paused. "You'll like it."

"I'll be there in five days," she said. "Tell her I eat a lot."

"I'll tell her," he said, with the specific quality of someone who had been telling people this for months and had stopped needing to explain why.

Chapter 28: Grayson was not what she'd expected.

She'd expected what Carson had described, cold and forested, the northern range and the particular quality of the light he'd described, but she arrived at the capital first, Mayhew, which was different from the capital of Manticore in ways that went beyond the obvious and which she needed several hours of direct experience to begin to characterize.

It was serious, was the first word she found. Not grim. Serious. The architecture and the people moving through it and the specific quality of the attention they paid to each other, all of it had the weight of a civilization that had survived something genuinely difficult and had organized itself around that survival in ways that were still structurally present even generations after.

She'd read the history. She knew about the near-extinction and the recovery and the specific shape of the Grayson character that had emerged from that history. Knowing was different from being in the middle of it.

The other thing she noticed, immediately and then continuously, was how people looked at her.

Not the way they'd looked at her in Manticore, which was the recognition look, the I know who you are from the news coverage look. This was something older. People on the street didn't know her face but some of them � older people, people from families with deep roots, people with the specific look of the more committed believers � registered something when they saw her that she couldn't quite read.

"They feel it," Carson said, walking beside her. "Some of them. The older Graysons especially. The awareness that was in the original tradition." He paused. "They don't know what they're feeling. But they feel something."

She thought about what that meant, an entire civilization with a vestigial awareness of the lineage built into its religious tradition. "Is that going to be a problem?"

"For the operation, no. For everything else�" he paused� "we'll manage."

The Clinkscales family home was outside the capital proper, which in Grayson terms meant twenty minutes in an air car and in physical terms meant a landscape transition from urban to something that was not quite rural and not quite suburban, a specific Grayson configuration that she didn't have a category for. Large properties. Old trees that were the Grayson version of old trees, different from the Manticoran ones in the specific ways that parallel evolution produced. A quality of space between structures that said people here had decided space mattered.

The house was large in the way of houses that had accumulated additions rather than been designed at full size, each addition visible in the architecture as a layer of decision. It had a garden that was clearly a serious garden rather than a decorative one, planted with intention and tended with care, and it smelled of things she had no names for that were the Grayson equivalent of the smells she associated with gardening, close enough to be recognizable.

Carson's mother came out to meet them.

She was a small woman with dark hair going gray and the quality of someone who had been managing a complicated household for decades and had arrived at a state of competence so complete that it had become ease. She looked at Buffy with the frank assessment of a Grayson woman who had decided that the situation required directness and said, "You're thinner than Carson described."

"I've been eating more," Buffy said.

"Not enough more." She looked at her son. "You didn't tell me she was this young."

"I told you she was seventeen, Mother."

"Telling me isn't the same as seeing." She turned back to Buffy and did something that Buffy hadn't expected, which was reach out and briefly touch her face in the way of someone confirming the reality of something they'd heard about for a long time. "Come in," she said. "There's food."

There was food. There was a significant amount of food, distributed across a kitchen that was the heart of the house in the way that kitchens in houses that were actually lived in always were, with the accumulated evidence of regular serious cooking in every surface and implement.

The lamb dish Carson had described was on the stove.

She ate three portions while Carson's mother watched with the expression of someone filing information for future use and Carson watched with the expression of someone who had been watching this for months and was not surprised but was still fond of watching it.

Thomas Clinkscales came in during the third portion.

He was eighty-three and moved with the specific deliberateness of someone whose body required more management than it had used to and who had made peace with this without being resigned to it. He was tall and had been taller, the compression of years visible in the way he carried himself, and his face was the face of someone who had spent a very long time thinking about something specific and had the lines to show it.

He stopped in the doorway and looked at her.

She set down her fork and looked at him.

The room was quiet.

He crossed it with his deliberate careful walk and stood in front of her and looked at her for a long moment with eyes that were very dark and very clear, and then he did what Carson had said he would do, which was that his face did the thing that it did, the specific configuration of a man who had been right about something for thirty years and was now standing in the direct presence of that rightness.

He didn't weep immediately. He held himself together with the controlled dignity that Carson also had and that she was coming to understand was a Clinkscales quality rather than a Grayson one specifically, and he said, "Lady Buffy."

"Mr. Clinkscales," she said.

He sat down. His wife brought him tea that he didn't look at. He looked at Buffy.

"My father told me," he said. "When I was a boy. He sat me down the way his father had sat him down, and he said: the family has a responsibility. There is a lineage. It is real. We keep the record and we watch for the signs and when the time comes we will know it." He paused. "I believed him. I've believed it every day since. There were days when believing was�" he stopped. "When the thing you believe has been dormant for long enough, believing becomes a practice rather than a response. You practice it every day regardless of whether it responds." He paused. "It responded."

She looked at him. She thought about what she wanted to say, which was what she actually wanted to say rather than what was appropriate to say, and she said it. "I read the record you kept. Carson showed me." She paused. "It's the most complete documentation I've encountered. In this century or the last one." She paused again. "You did something that was necessary and that didn't look necessary for thirty years. That's the hardest kind of work. I wanted you to know that I know that."

He held her gaze for a moment.

Then he wept.

Not dramatically. Quietly, in the way of someone who had been holding something for a long time and had simply been given permission to set it down, and she sat with it and didn't try to address it because it didn't need to be addressed, it needed to be witnessed, and she witnessed it.

Carson's mother put her hand on her husband's shoulder.

Carson sat quietly to Buffy's left.

After a while Thomas Clinkscales took a breath and looked at her again with the clear dark eyes, clearer now. "The girls Carson told me about," he said. "You've met them?"

"I have an appointment tomorrow," she said. "Both of them. You'll be there, Carson said?"

"If you'll allow it." He paused. "I've been watching for them for years. I didn't know what to say when I found them."

"I'll say the first things," she said. "You can say the things that come after. The lineage's history. The record." She paused. "The context I didn't have when I was called. The thing that would have helped me most." She looked at him. "You know the record better than anyone alive. That matters."

He straightened slightly. Not performatively. The straightening of someone receiving a task they recognize as theirs. "Yes," he said. "All right."

~~~

The Grayson candidates were different from each other in most of the specific ways that people were different from each other and shared the things that the lineage shared, the physical profile and the dreams and the quality of someone navigating a gap between what they knew about themselves and what they were.

The first was fourteen, which was young, the youngest she'd encountered, and sat in the Clinkscales sitting room with Thomas beside her and her own family present and did the thing that fourteen-year-olds did in serious situations that adults had organized around them, which was be more present and more alert than the adults expected and ask questions they hadn't prepared answers for.

The question she asked that no one had prepared an answer for was: will it hurt?

Buffy looked at her and thought about the honest answer, which was yes, some of it, and the context the honest answer needed, which was considerable.

She said: some of it will hurt. I'm not going to tell you otherwise. But the hurt is not the point of it and it's not all of it, and you'll have people with you, which I didn't always have, and that makes the hurt different.

The girl looked at Thomas, who was beside her with the dignity of thirty years of faith made present, and then back at Buffy.

She said: what's the good part?

Buffy thought about running in the dark. About the operation on Hades and the boarding of the Eirene Constantia and Yeva's face when the explanation arrived. About Scotty's letter from the Talbott Cluster that had arrived two days ago and that she'd read twice.

She said: you're enough for something that matters. That's the good part.

The girl sat with this.

Then she said: okay. Tell me the rest.

The second candidate was seventeen and had the controlled watchful quality of someone who had been managing something alone for long enough that alone had become her default. She listened to Buffy with a stillness that said she was receiving and filing rather than performing reception, and when Buffy finished she said: I knew something was different about me. I thought I was broken.

You're not broken, Buffy said. I know that's what I'm supposed to say and I'm saying it because it's true.

The girl looked at her. How do you know?

Because I thought I was too, Buffy said. For a long time. And I was wrong.

She held the girl's gaze.

I was wrong, she said again.

The girl looked away and then back. She said: what do I do now?

We figure it out, Buffy said. Together.

Thomas Clinkscales wept again on the way home, quietly, in the air car, and Carson sat beside him and Buffy sat across and watched the Grayson landscape go past the windows, the old trees and the space between properties and the sky that was slightly the wrong shade of blue in the specific way that all skies were slightly the wrong shade.

She thought about the fourteen-year-old asking what the good part was.

She thought about Yeva and the four Manticoran candidates and the two on Grayson and the ones she hadn't found yet, across Grayson and Manticore and the Alliance and beyond that, everywhere the lineage had scattered in two thousand years of forgetting.

She thought: this is the thing that matters.

Not instead of the other things. Alongside them. The work that was going to be present regardless of what else was happening, the work that would still be there when the war ended and when the political situation resolved and when all the immediate things became the things that had happened.

The Keeper was present.

She felt it orient toward the landscape around her the way it oriented toward her when she was focused on something relevant, the specific quality of attention it brought to the work rather than to her specifically.

She looked out the window.

The trees were the wrong trees and the sky was the wrong sky and she was here, in this air car, in this century, on this planet, doing this specific thing.

She was here.

~~~

The Grayson operation took four days to plan and one night to execute.

Castellan arrived on the second day, which was also the day Buffy met with Usher's Grayson contacts, two people who worked in a structure that had no official name and whose relationship to the official Grayson security apparatus was lateral rather than integrated, the kind of parallel institutional awareness that was apparently a feature of civilizations that had been dealing with things they couldn't fully categorize for long enough to develop informal infrastructure for it.

They knew about the node. They'd known about it for longer than the Landing situation, which told her something about the node's age and establishment. They'd been managing it in the same way the Landing operation had been managed, which was documentation and monitoring and the specific frustration of an awareness without a capability.

She told them what she was going to do.

They gave her everything they had, which was more than she'd expected and better organized than she'd hoped, the specific gift of people who had spent years preparing information for exactly this kind of transfer.

Castellan spent a day reviewing the site information and came back with observations and one significant recommendation about the approach that Buffy incorporated because Castellan was right and being right mattered more than whose idea it was.

On the third day she did what she'd learned to do before operations, which was to sleep and train and eat and be present with the people around her rather than the operation ahead of her, because the operation ahead of her was as prepared as it was going to be and the people around her were what they were.

She ate dinner with the Clinkscales family that night.

Thomas was there and Carson's mother and two of Carson's sisters who had returned home for reasons related to the news that something significant was happening, which in a family like this moved through the structure quickly even without explicit communication. The sisters were different from each other, the elder one with a quality like Thomas and the younger one with a quality like Carson's mother, and they looked at Buffy with the combined assessment of two women who loved their brother and wanted to know if the situation was what it appeared to be.

She was apparently what it appeared to be, because by the end of dinner the elder sister had asked if she wanted to learn the Grayson card game that was apparently a family tradition and the younger one had found and played a piece of music on an instrument Buffy had no name for that was the Grayson equivalent of a piano in that it was large and keyboard-based and produced a sound that was close to what a piano produced in the specific way of parallel development toward the same function.

It was a good dinner.

She thought about her mother's kitchen and let the thought be present without being displaced by it, and the Clinkscales kitchen held what it held around her, warm and inhabited and full of the evidence of a family that had been a family for long enough to have a character that was the sum of all its accumulated decisions.

She went to sleep at ten and woke at one and went to work.

~~~
