The node's consolidated position was a facility in an industrial area outside Mayhew's eastern limits, a storage and logistics facility that had been in legitimate use until approximately eight years ago when a transaction had transferred it to an ownership entity that Usher's contacts had spent considerable effort trying to trace and had traced as far as a Solarian legal construct that stopped being traceable at that point.

The facility had three structures and a perimeter that was functional rather than decorative and which had been augmented in ways that were consistent with a population that was being careful.

Being careful meant, among other things, that they knew she was coming.

She'd assumed this. A network that had been warned by the Landing situation and had consolidated would not be surprised by the arrival of the thing that had addressed Landing. The element of surprise was not available. What was available was being faster than their prepared response and better than their consolidated capability.

She went in from the east, which was the direction that was hardest to approach and therefore the direction the prepared response would weight least.

The first two were outside. Old enough to have some capability and young enough to have overestimated it, which was the combination she'd been hoping for when she'd made the eastern approach choice. She addressed them with the speed the situation required and moved to the first structure.

The first structure was empty of vampires and full of the evidence of recent occupancy, sleeping areas and the stored provisions that vampires who had humans operating for them sometimes kept for those humans, and she moved through it and went to the second.

The second structure was where eight of the remaining ten were, which was more than she'd faced in a consolidated position before but which was also the specific concentration that made the Keeper's advice � she'd started thinking of its pressure as advice � useful in a way she was still developing the practice of.

The advice, insofar as she could read it, was about sequence.

The Keeper understood something about the population's internal hierarchy that she couldn't read directly but could receive through the pressure, and the pressure said oldest first, and oldest first was counterintuitive from a conventional tactical standpoint because it committed you to the hardest engagement immediately before dealing with the remainder.

She trusted the advice.

The oldest one in the second structure was not the node's oldest � that was in the third structure, she could feel it, and it felt like something that had been here for a very long time. This one was old enough to be the oldest one she'd faced other than the Eirene Constantia's master and she went at it directly and with everything she had in the first three seconds because the first three seconds were when the tactical initiative was available and the time after that was when you were responding.

She won the first three seconds.

The remainder of the second structure took twelve minutes and cost her a cut to the right shoulder and a bruised rib she wouldn't fully assess until later and produced seven more instances of dust.

She went to the third structure.

The node's oldest had been here, she thought, since before the Eirene Constantia's established route. It was older than the master of the Eirene Constantia, which she hadn't expected. It was in its own space in the third structure, a space that bore the accumulated evidence of very long habitation in the way that the master's quarters on the ship had, the furniture from different eras and the objects that had been valuable for different reasons at different times.

It looked at her with the kind of eyes that had been looking at things for long enough to have the specific quality of accumulated looking, not tiredness exactly, something more like the weight of volume. A great deal had passed through those eyes.

It said: you're young.

She said: I know.

It said: I've been here since before your civilization reached the stars.

She held its gaze. I know that too.

It looked at her. How many of the others?

Nine, she said.

It was quiet for a moment. The weight of the accumulated looking doing whatever it did internally.

Then it said: I've been waiting for someone to come.

She looked at it. Why haven't you asked someone to?

A pause. Because what I am makes that impossible. In the way that what you are makes certain things impossible.

She thought about that.

Is there something you want me to know? she said.

It looked at her.

It said: the one above us. In the hierarchy. It's very old. Older than me. It doesn't know about the Keeper's Seal yet. If it did, what you found would already be gone.

She filed that.

It said: it's on Beowulf.

She said: why are you telling me this?

A long pause. Because I've been here long enough to be tired of what I am. And because you're the only one who could use the information.

She looked at it for a moment.

I'm going to give you a choice, she said, which surprised her as she was saying it and which the Keeper's accompanying presence received with something she could only call attention. You can keep existing. I know how to contain rather than eliminate. It would be �constrained. You wouldn't be able to feed. But you'd exist.

It looked at her.

No, it said. Thank you.

She thought about the master of the Eirene Constantia and the complicated last moments, the regret too old to be simple regret. She thought about what it was to have been something for long enough that not being it was more coherent than continuing.

She finished the work.

She stood in the third structure afterward and breathed through the rib and shoulder assessments and thought about Beowulf, which was a system she knew from the briefings, important, League-adjacent, the kind of place where something very old could be very established with significant resources available.

The Keeper's accompanying presence was very still in the way of something that had just received important information and was organizing its response.

She looked at the wall of the structure and thought about the scope of the thing ahead and thought about what she had and what she needed and the distance between those two.

She called Usher.

"The node is addressed," she said. "I have something else. When can you be here?"

Chapter 29: The something else took three weeks to become actionable intelligence.

Usher came to Grayson and received the information and went back to Manticore and ran it through the apparatus she ran things through, and what came back was a picture of Beowulf that was more complex than Buffy had been given in the standard briefings and that Usher had access to because of the specific kind of work Usher did.

Beowulf was the oldest inhabited system after the Sol system itself. It had a medical research tradition that was unparalleled in human space. It had a relationship with the Solarian League that was ambiguous in the specific ways that old and powerful things had ambiguous relationships with other old and powerful things. It had the kind of accumulated institutional complexity that came from being eleven hundred years old as a civilization and having made a great many consequential decisions in that time.

It was also, according to what Usher assembled, home to something that had been established there long enough to be effectively invisible in the accumulated complexity, hiding not through active concealment but through the specific camouflage of things that had been present so long that they were part of the background.

She studied the picture for three days.

She studied it alongside Fritz, who was now operating as something between her chief medical officer and her research director, a role that had accumulated to him without either of them planning it. And she studied it alongside Carson, who was back on Manticore now with his leave extended further, something his Academy posting was accommodating because the Academy had been briefed that Carson's duties in the current situation were considered by the Queen's office to be of operational significance.

The picture said several things.

It said that the thing on Beowulf was not a vampire. The activity signature was different from the distributed predatory pattern she'd been reading. Whatever was on Beowulf was not operating a feeding network. It was doing something else with the infrastructure it had built over eleven hundred years on the oldest inhabited system in human space.

That was more worrying than the vampire network, not less.

She brought this to the Queen.

The Queen read the picture that Usher's people had assembled and was quiet for a longer time than she was usually quiet, which was itself information.

"Beowulf is not Manticoran territory," she said finally.

"No."

"Operating in Beowulf requires Beowulfan cooperation or significant discretion." She paused. "At the current political moment, Beowulfan cooperation is�complicated."

"I know." She'd read the political context carefully before this conversation. "What I'm asking for is access to the relationship. Not an operation. Not yet." She paused. "I need to understand what I'm dealing with before I know what dealing with it requires."

"And you think Beowulf will cooperate with that."

"I think Beowulf has its own reasons to want to know what's been hiding in its institutions for eleven hundred years," she said. "I think when that's framed correctly, the cooperation is available."

The Queen looked at her. "You want to go to Beowulf."

"I want to go to Beowulf," she agreed. "Not immediately. There are things to do here first." She paused. "And I'm waiting for something."

The Queen waited.

"Honor," she said. "You said two months in her letter. It's been seven weeks."

"Yes," the Queen said. "She's coming." A pause that had something behind it that the Queen was choosing not to specify. "She has things to tell you that I'll let her tell you."

Buffy looked at her.

"Good things?" she said.

The Queen's expression did the thing it did when she was not going to be drawn. "Let Honor tell you," she said.

She let Honor tell her, which required three more weeks of patience and the specific patience of waiting for something you've been told is coming without knowing its exact shape.

~~~

Honor arrived on a Tuesday.

She didn't come through the main docking facility this time, no ceremony and no cameras, which was itself information. She came through the naval annex and she came directly to Buffy's quarters with Nimitz on her shoulder and a look that was the same as always, level and precise and carrying its priorities, with something underneath it that was different from the last time.

Buffy looked at her.

She looked at Buffy.

Nimitz made a sound that was not a greeting sound exactly and was not an assessment sound exactly and was something in between.

"You look good," Honor said.

"You look like you've been doing things that cost something," Buffy said.

"Yes." Honor came in and sat down, and Nimitz flowed from her shoulder to Buffy's in the specific way that treecats moved when they'd decided to be somewhere, which was decisively and without negotiation. He arranged himself across her shoulders and put his head against her cheek briefly and withdrew it.

"Hi," she said to him.

He made the sound.

"The Queen said you had things to tell me," Buffy said.

"She has no patience," Honor said, without heat. "Yes. I have things to tell you." She paused. "The war is changing. I can't give you details yet because some of them aren't mine to give. But the shape of the next several months is going to be different from what it's been." She paused. "Better, I think. But different."

Buffy waited.

"Personal things," Honor said. "Some of what I've been doing touches things that are�" she paused. "I've been thinking about what comes after the war. In a way I haven't let myself think before, because the before part needed all of it." She looked at Buffy. "I thought you should know that."

"What comes after?" Buffy said.

"I don't know yet," Honor said. "That's new for me." She paused. "I used to know. Or I used to think I knew, which is different but felt the same." She looked at her hands, which was something Honor rarely did. "I know what the war needs from me. After is less clear."

Buffy thought about what she was hearing.

"You're thinking about what you actually want," she said. "Rather than what's required."

Honor looked at her. "That's it exactly."

"That's a good thing to be thinking about."

"It's disconcerting," Honor said. "I'm not accustomed to not knowing."

"I'm accustomed to it," Buffy said. "It gets easier." She paused. "Not knowing becomes information eventually. You start to read the shape of what you want from where you find yourself looking."

Honor looked at her for a moment with the expression that was several things at once. "When did you get wise?"

"I'm not wise," Buffy said. "I'm just chronologically confused in a way that gives me perspective."

Nimitz made the sound that she'd come to recognize as laughter.

"Don't encourage him," she said.

Honor almost smiled. Then she looked at the desk, at the map and the files and the accumulated evidence of the work. "Tell me what you've been doing."

She told her.

All of it, again, the way she'd told her everything in the shuttle the night before the operation. The Eirene Constantia and the case and the record and Yeva and the Grayson candidates and Thomas Clinkscales and the Grayson node and what the oldest one on Grayson had told her before the end.

Honor listened the way she always listened, with complete and unhurried attention, and when Buffy finished she was quiet for a moment and then she said: "Beowulf."

"Yes."

"That's a complicated conversation."

"I know."

"The people I know on Beowulf," Honor said slowly, "the ones I trust and who have the kind of reach this would require�" she paused. "There's a woman. Dr. Allison Chou Harrington."

Buffy looked at her.

"She's my mother," Honor said. "She practices medicine on Beowulf. She has relationships there that I don't have, that go back decades." She paused. "She's going to want to meet you regardless." She paused again. "She's been wanting to meet you since the moment she heard about you."

"Your mother," Buffy said.

"My mother," Honor confirmed. "Who is not a conventional person in any way and who is going to have approximately forty-seven thousand questions and who is going to want to run tests that Fritz hasn't thought of yet and who will also feed you extensively and without mercy."

Buffy thought about all of this.

"I want to meet her," she said.

"I knew you would." Something settled in Honor's expression, something that had been slightly unresolved coming to rest. "I'll arrange it." She paused. "It means going to Beowulf."

"I know." She looked at the map. "That's where I need to go anyway." She looked at Honor. "Will you come?"

Honor looked at her.

"When my current obligations allow," she said. "Yes."

Nimitz made a sound on Buffy's shoulder that she felt as much as heard, a low satisfied rumble that was the treecat equivalent of something working out the way it was supposed to.

She reached up and scratched behind his ears.

"Don't be smug," she said.

He was smug anyway.

She looked at the map and at Honor and at the window where the city was doing its late afternoon things and thought about the shape of what was building.

Beowulf. Honor's mother. The thing on Beowulf that had been established for eleven hundred years in the oldest civilization in human space.

And somewhere in the Talbott Cluster a ship called the Hexapuma with a letter from her in its communications queue that she hadn't gotten a response to yet because the Talbott Cluster was far and couriers were slower than she wanted them to be.

She thought about Scotty telling her about Sphinx. The northern range. The cold she hadn't imagined.

She thought: come back when you can.

She thought: I have things to tell you.

She looked at the city and the light and the wrong-colored harbor and the sky that had been the wrong sky for seven months and had become, in the accumulated way of things you inhabited long enough, simply the sky.

Her sky.

She was here.

She was building something.

She was, she thought, all right.

She was, she thought, considerably more than all right.

She turned from the window.

There was work to do.

Chapter 30: Scotty's response to her letter arrived nine days after Honor did.

She was in Dr. Haverstock's office at the university when it came through, sitting across from him and a colleague he'd brought in, a woman named Dr. Prieto who worked in information theory and who had developed what she described as a protocol for addressing organized patterns at dimensional interfaces, which was the most precise language anyone had yet produced for what the Keeper was.

She felt the message arrive on her communicator the way you felt things you were waiting for, the specific alertness of attention that had been positioned toward something finally having something to land on. She let it sit for twenty minutes until the meeting reached a natural pause and then she read it.

It was long.

Scotty's letters were not usually long. His letters were the letters of someone who wrote precisely, who said what he meant in the minimum words that accomplished that, and who understood that courier time made length a different calculation than it was in real-time conversation. This letter was long for him, which meant it was about six hundred words, and she read it twice in the twenty-minute break while Haverstock and Prieto debated something at the whiteboard.

The letter said:

That the Talbott Cluster was a situation that he couldn't fully describe in a letter but that was developing in ways he hadn't expected when he'd arrived and that required the kind of attention that didn't leave much room for anything else.

That he'd read her letter three times.

That the thing she was building � the lineage search, the institutional structure, the long work � was exactly the thing she'd said she wanted, which was something worth fighting for, and that reading about it in her letters over the past months had given him something he hadn't had words for until he'd tried to write this letter, which was the word proud, which he recognized was not his pride to feel exactly but which was what he felt and he was done with the habit of having feelings and not stating them directly.

That Commander Terekhov's current operation was moving toward a resolution he couldn't specify but which was going to change the shape of things in the Talbott Cluster and by extension in the broader political situation, and that when the resolution arrived he expected to have a clearer sense of what his next assignment would be.

That he was thinking about after in a way he hadn't let himself think before.

That the northern range in autumn was specifically the kind of cold she hadn't imagined and he wanted her to experience it in autumn rather than winter for the first time because autumn was the spectacular version and winter was the version you had to have been prepared for by autumn to properly appreciate.

That he missed her.

That was the last sentence, stated plainly and without the hedging that earlier versions of the same sentiment might have required.

She read that last sentence twice.

Then she put the communicator in her pocket and went back to the meeting and was present for it with the part of her that was always present for work regardless of what else was happening, which was most of her, because the Slayer's focus was not optional and she'd stopped fighting it years ago and started working with it.

After the meeting she sat in the university garden, which was a formal garden in the Manticoran style with the specific quality of a space maintained for thinking, and wrote back.

Her letter was longer than his. It had been getting longer. She'd noticed this and had decided it was fine.

She told him that she missed him too, which was true and which she'd been circling in the letters for a month without landing on directly, and that stating it plainly felt like the right response to his plain statement.

She told him about Honor being back and about Beowulf and about the thing the oldest Grayson vampire had told her before the end, which she'd been holding back from the letters because she was still processing it and she'd found that the things she was still processing came out better on the second or third try.

She told him that the Keeper had been present while she wrote this letter and that its accompanying quality was something she'd describe as approval, for what that was worth.

She told him about autumn.

She said she wanted to see it. The autumn version. Before the winter version. She'd take his word for the sequencing.

She sealed it and sent it and sat in the garden for a while longer with the afternoon doing what it did and the Keeper present in its accompanying way and thought about the geometry of the situation she was in.

Scotty in the Talbott Cluster coming toward a resolution.

Honor back in Manticore coming toward whatever the war's changing shape was going to require of her.

Beowulf ahead, with the thing that had been established there for eleven hundred years and Honor's mother who had forty-seven thousand questions and relationships that reached into the places she needed to reach.

The lineage candidates building their understanding with the support of the people around them. Yeva who had written her two letters already, one asking about the practical management of the physical profile in a school gymnastics context and one asking about the dreams in a way that was more personal than the first letter and said she was starting to trust the channel.

The institution that didn't exist yet and was beginning to exist in the preliminary ways, the conversations and the documented decisions and the slow accumulation of the agreements that were the substrate of real things before they became visible.

She sat in the garden and let all of it be present at once, which was a thing she was getting better at, the capacity to hold the multiple simultaneous shapes of a life without any of them displacing the others.

Then she went back to work.

~~~

Two weeks after Honor's return, something happened in the war.

She learned about it in pieces, the way you learned about large things that were happening through the secondary effects of how people around you responded to them rather than through the primary event itself. Lieutenant Chen's manner changed. Usher was unreachable for thirty-six hours. The Queen's office cancelled two non-urgent appointments.

Then Honor came to her quarters at seven in the evening and sat down and told her.

She wasn't going to be able to write it in a letter to Scotty. That was the first thing she thought, which was itself information about where her mind went when she received significant information. She thought: I need to tell Scotty this and I can't yet.

What Honor told her was this: a significant Havenite naval engagement had resulted in an outcome that was changing the strategic situation in ways that were still being assessed but that the initial assessment said were good, from the Alliance's perspective, in ways that good hadn't been available for a significant portion of the war.

Not over. Honor was specific about this, not because she thought Buffy would misread it but because Honor was specific about everything. Not over. But different.

"What does different mean for you?" Buffy said.

Honor was quiet for a moment. "Less defined than it was," she said. "The next several months are going to be politically complex in ways I can't fully predict. There are things that need to happen in Manticore. Things that need to happen in Grayson." She paused. "And there's Beowulf."

"Yes."

"My mother is expecting us," Honor said. "When I told her about you she was�" she paused, and something in her voice did the warmth thing that her voice did when she talked about her parents, a specific register that existed in her alongside everything else� "enthusiastic is not sufficient. She's been making preparations for three weeks."

"What kind of preparations?"

"I genuinely don't know. With my mother the preparations could range from a research protocol to a dinner party to both simultaneously." Honor paused. "She's remarkable. I want to be clear about that before you meet her, because she can be a great deal in person and I want you to know what it is."

"You said that before."

"I'm saying it again because I mean it twice." A pause. "She's also going to see things about you that I haven't seen. She does that. She pays a specific kind of attention that produces information people don't realize they've given."

Buffy thought about the Queen, who did something similar. She thought about what it said about Honor that the people who mattered to her tended to have this quality. "Okay," she said. "I can work with a great deal."

"I know you can." Honor looked at her with the layered expression. "When can you leave?"

She thought about the current status of everything. Yeva and the other candidates were in sufficient contact that a gap of a few weeks was manageable. Haverstock and Prieto had enough material to work with for months. The Beowulf matter was the thing that was actively pending.

"A week," she said. "I need a week to arrange things."

"I need ten days," Honor said. "There are things here first."

"Ten days," she agreed.

Honor stood to leave and then stopped. "Nimitz wants to know if you're all right," she said. "He says something is different."

Buffy thought about what was different, which was the letter in her pocket and the last sentence of it. "Tell him I'm good," she said. "Better than good."

Honor looked at her with the expression that wasn't quite reading but was close to it. "All right," she said.

She left.

Buffy sat for a moment in her quarters and then she went to the window and looked at the city and thought about ten days.

She could do a great deal in ten days.

~~~

She spent the ten days doing several things.

She spent two of them on Grayson, not for an operation but for the second round of meetings with the lineage candidates and Thomas Clinkscales and the formal establishment of something that Thomas called the Keeper's Archive and she called the beginning of the thing she was building. They signed a document that was largely ceremonial and entirely real, the kind of paper that said an agreement existed that people were going to honor.

Thomas cried again. She was beginning to think this was going to be a feature of her relationship with him and she was making her peace with it, the peace of someone who understood that some people expressed significant things in tears and that there was no undignified way to mean something.

She spent three days on administrative work for the institution, which had not yet been named and which she was deliberately not naming yet because names had weight and weight came with constraint and the thing was still finding its shape. She worked with Chen on the formal agreements with the Queen's office and with Usher on the intelligence-sharing protocols that connected the institution to the existing infrastructure and with Fritz on the research framework that would feed information about the lineage candidates into the understanding of what the lineage was and what it needed.

She spent one day with Haverstock and Prieto, who had made progress on the Keeper question that was genuinely surprising and that required her to sit for two hours while Prieto ran what she described as an interface mapping procedure and which was the most peculiar experience Buffy had had in this century, including the record, because the procedure produced a map of the Keeper's presence that was three-dimensional and dynamic and which both physicists stared at with the expression of people who had just received the experimental confirmation of fifteen years of theory.

The Keeper's response to being mapped was something she read as bemused.

She spent the remaining days writing. Not letters, though she wrote those too. Writing down what she'd received from the record, the things she'd put on paper the night after the opening and which she'd been refining since, working them into something that had the structure of a document rather than the structure of immediate notes. She wasn't a scholar. She hadn't been trained to write this way. She did it anyway because the information needed to exist in a form that could be transmitted and used and because the doing of it was a way of understanding what she'd received more completely.

Carson read the draft.

He sent back comments that were the comments of someone who had been living in primary documents for months and had developed opinions about how information was most precisely expressed. The comments were good. She incorporated most of them.

On the ninth day a letter arrived from Scotty.

He couldn't say much. The Talbott Cluster situation was at the point where what he could commit to paper was limited by operational security. But he said that the resolution he'd anticipated was moving faster than expected and that the shape of what came after it was becoming visible and that he was thinking about leave.

The last line said: I looked up the transit time from the Talbott Cluster to Manticore. It's manageable.

She read that line twice.

Then she packed her bag for Beowulf.

Chapter 31: Beowulf was old in a way that Manticore was not old and Grayson was not old, an oldness that was qualitatively different from either, the specific character of a civilization that had been a civilization since before the concept of interstellar civilization had been fully invented.

She'd read about it extensively and the reading had not prepared her for what direct experience produced, which was the same thing she'd felt on Manticore but older and stranger and more layered, the specific weight of eleven hundred years of continuous human habitation doing something to the fabric of the place that you couldn't measure but could feel.

The Keeper felt it too. She knew because the accompanying quality changed when they entered the system, a heightened attention that was different from what it brought to Manticore or Grayson, more alert, more directed. She'd come to trust the Keeper's reactions as information even when she couldn't fully interpret them and this one she interpreted as: something here is relevant.

She'd already known that. But now she knew it in the way you knew things you felt rather than reasoned.

Honor's mother met them at the docking facility.

Dr. Allison Chou Harrington was not what she'd built from Honor's description, which was itself information about the limits of Honor's capacity to describe her own mother. She was small, which the description had included, and had dark hair going silver at the temples and eyes that were doing the assessment thing the moment they found Buffy's face and didn't stop doing it.

She was also, immediately and without preamble, warm in the specific way of someone who had decided that warmth was not a performance but a mode of operation, the kind of warmth that was its own kind of precision.

She took both of Buffy's hands when they met, which was not a Manticoran greeting custom and was apparently a Beowulfan one, or possibly just an Allison one. "You're younger than the pictures," she said.

"The pictures are accurate," Buffy said. "I'm actually this young."

"I know. That's what I said." She looked at Buffy's face with the specific kind of looking that Fritz did, the medical version of seeing, except that Allison's had more layers. "You're also tired. Not the way someone is tired who hasn't slept. The deeper kind."

"I've been busy."

"I know. Honor told me." She released her hands. "We'll fix the tired. Come with me."

Honor's expression behind Allison's shoulder was the expression of a daughter watching someone she loves do something completely characteristic and finding it exactly right.

Allison's home was the home of someone who had been somewhere long enough to make it exactly what they wanted it to be, not large but complete, with the specific quality of a space where serious work and serious living happened in the same rooms without conflict. Books and instruments and the tools of medical research were present alongside the evidence of a life that was also a life, art on the walls and plants that were alive and a kitchen that Buffy could smell from the front door.

"Kitchen first," Allison said. "Then I'll show you the rest."

The kitchen produced, over the next ninety minutes, the most significant meal Buffy had eaten since her mother's kitchen two and a half years and two thousand years ago, which was a comparison she made in the middle of the third course and then held quietly because it was hers and didn't need to be said.

Allison watched her eat with the frank pleasure of someone who cooked seriously and was watching someone receive it seriously. "More," she said, twice, when Buffy's plate was empty, and when Buffy said I eat a lot she said I know, Honor told me, which was what people said to Buffy regularly now.

It never got old.

Over dinner Allison talked about Beowulf the way people talked about places they'd lived long enough to have a relationship with rather than just a location in. She talked about its medical research tradition, about the Beowulf Code which governed genetic research and which she had opinions about that were complex and which she expressed without self-consciousness. She talked about the political situation, the League relationship, the things happening in the Solarian system that she followed carefully and that connected to the war in ways she traced with the specific intelligence of someone who paid attention to more than her immediate specialty.

And she asked questions.

Not the forty-seven thousand. The ones that got to the point.

"The record you opened," she said. "The information in it. Fritz's preliminary notes describe it as received rather than read." She looked at Buffy. "What's the difference?"

"Reading is sequential," Buffy said. "Information in a linear structure that you move through from one end to the other. What I received was�" she looked for the word� "simultaneous. Like the information was complete and I received the whole thing at once and then spent time afterward sorting what I'd received."

"Like a gestalt."

"Yes. Except with content that was specific rather than impressionistic."

Allison looked at her with the layered seeing. "Was it painful?"

"No." She thought about it. "It was disorienting. There was a period after when I wasn't completely sure where I ended and the content began." She paused. "Carson pulled me back. Having someone in the room who knew me helped."

"It would." Allison looked at the table and then back up. "The research I've been doing since Honor told me you were coming. The dimensional interface theory, which I'd read because Haverstock published preliminary notes six months ago and I know Michael Haverstock and I follow his work. And the Slayer lineage documentation, which Carson Clinkscales sent me copies of." She paused. "I have a hypothesis."

Buffy looked at her.

"The healing factor," Allison said. "Fritz has characterized it as anomalous but hasn't identified a mechanism. The enhanced physical capabilities � same. The sensory processing. The connection to the lineage through the dream-space." She paused. "These aren't supernatural in the sense of being outside physics. They're physics we don't have a complete framework for yet. But I think the framework exists."

"The dimensional interface," Buffy said.

"The dimensional interface. And specifically what happens at the interface to an organism that has a stable, heritable connection to it." She held Buffy's gaze. "The Slayer line is what it is because something at the interface created a stable heritable modification in an ancestral individual. The modification has been expressing itself in every generation since." She paused. "Which means it's in the genome. Which means I can find it."

Buffy held her gaze for a moment.

"You want to study me," she said.

"I want to study the modification," Allison said. "You are the most accessible and thoroughly documented example of its full expression. But the modification is what I'm after, not you specifically." She paused. "With your permission. Everything with your permission."

"Fritz has been saying that for months."

"Fritz is correct." She paused. "I have equipment here that Fritz doesn't have and methods he hasn't considered and thirty years of experience in exactly this category of problem." She paused. "I can help you understand what you are."

Buffy thought about this. She thought about the Council, which had studied the Slayer as an asset to be optimized rather than a person to be understood. She thought about the difference between those two things and how she read it in the way Allison was asking.

"Yes," she said. "With Fritz coordinating and me reviewing everything."

"Of course." Allison looked at her with something that was satisfaction and something else, something more personal. "Thank you."

"Don't thank me yet," Buffy said, without thinking.

Allison almost smiled. "You sound like the Queen."

"She says that too," Buffy said. "I've been spending time with her."

"I know," Allison said. "Honor told me."

Honor, across the table, had the expression of someone who had been listening to this conversation with complete attention and was now feeling something she wasn't going to put into words, and Nimitz on her shoulder was looking at Buffy and Allison with the green-eyed satisfaction of something that had assessed a situation and found it exactly right.

~~~

Beowulf had three days of preliminary work before the other thing.

The three days were Allison and Fritz, who had arrived on a later ship, in Allison's research facility doing the things that Allison and Fritz did in a research context, which was proceed with rigorous systematic precision and argue about methodology in ways that were professionally respectful and entirely serious. Haverstock was present by remote link because Haverstock had opinions about the interface question that he wanted to contribute directly. Petrakis had sent notes.

She was a patient, which was a role she'd accepted and which she was getting better at accepting, the specific discipline of holding still while people who knew things she didn't used those things on her behalf.

She was also doing her own work in the evenings, walking the parts of Beowulf that were walkable in the specific way she walked new places, with the Slayer sense at its mapping register and the Keeper's attention directed in the way that said relevant and she was building a picture.

The picture was what she'd expected and more detailed than she'd expected.

Something very old was in the infrastructure of this civilization. Not obviously. Not in any way that would be visible to the ordinary processes of an eleven-hundred-year-old society. But present in the specific way of things that had adapted to institutional structures long enough to become structural themselves, not parasitic exactly, more like symbiotic in a relationship that served one party considerably more than the other.

The something was not a vampire.

She kept returning to this because it was the thing she had the least framework for. The active supernatural threats she'd encountered in her career had been in categories she had names for: vampires, demons, hellgods, various arcane entities with specific operating principles she could research in the Watchers' journals. This was in a category she didn't have a name for. The Keeper registered it in a way that was different from how it registered vampires or the residue of the Hellmouth or the mystical energy in the lineage itself, a different register entirely.

On the third evening she told Honor.

They were on the terrace of Allison's home in the specific quality of Beowulf's late evening, which was different from Manticore's late evening in ways she was cataloguing. Honor had a glass of something and Nimitz was on the railing and the city was doing its evening configuration below them and Buffy sat across from Honor and told her what the Keeper was registering and what she was reading from the walks.

Honor listened.

"Not a vampire," she said, when Buffy finished.

"No."

"Then what?"

"I don't know yet." She paused. "The Keeper knows. Or knows something I don't have access to yet. I can feel it knowing." She paused. "Which is frustrating."

"Can you ask it directly?"

"I can try. The communication isn't�" she stopped. "It's like trying to ask someone a question in a language you're still learning. I can feel it respond but I can't always read the response with precision." She paused. "Haverstock and Prieto are working on the interface mapping. If the map gets more detailed maybe the communication gets more precise."

Honor looked at the city. "Should we be concerned about the current approach? If something here is aware of what you are�"

"I've been thinking about that," Buffy said. "I don't think it knows I'm here yet. Whatever it is, it's not reading the way the older vampires read, which was active awareness of the Slayer sense. This registers as�" she tried to find the word� "less present. Like something that doesn't need to actively monitor because it's been undisturbed long enough to have stopped expecting disturbance."

"Complacent," Honor said.

"As much as something eleven hundred years old can be complacent." She looked at the city. "I don't want to disturb it before I understand it. If it leaves Beowulf because I showed up, I lose the chance to understand what it is and what it's doing here."

"So we're in the observation phase."

"We're in the observation phase." She paused. "Allison is helping with that too. She knows this system. She has relationships I can use without those relationships being connected to me directly."

Honor looked at her. "You've been planning this."

"I've been thinking about it since the Grayson operation," she said. "Thinking is planning with lower commitment." She paused. "Allison is�" she tried to find the right word� "she's exactly what I needed here. I didn't know that until I was sitting across from her at dinner."

"She has that effect," Honor said, in the tone she used when she was talking about her mother, the layered warmth that was both personal and precise.

"How did you become who you are," Buffy said, "when she's this much?"

Honor looked at her. Something moved in her face. "Slowly," she said. "And she made it possible rather than impossible. There's a difference." She paused. "She never asked me to be her. She asked me to be whatever I was going to be and then she was interested in it." She paused. "That's the harder version of love. Interested rather than invested. She's always been interested."

Buffy held that.

She thought about Joyce Summers in the lit window of a house she would never see again, who had been scared of the Slayer part and who had been her mother anyway, who had been both of those things simultaneously without pretending one of them away.

"Mine too," she said.

Honor looked at her.

"She was scared of it," Buffy said. "My mom. She didn't want it to be true. She spent about a year trying to pretend it wasn't and then she stopped and she was just�there. Scared and there at the same time." She paused. "I think that was harder than what Allison did."

"Probably," Honor said. "Both are love. Different shapes."

"Different shapes," Buffy agreed.

They sat in the Beowulf evening and the city did its things and Nimitz was on the railing being present in the way treecats were present, completely and without apology.

After a while Honor said, "She would be proud of you."

Buffy looked at her.

"Your mother," Honor said. "What you're doing. What you're building." She held Buffy's gaze. "She would be proud."

Buffy sat with this.

It landed the way certain true things landed, not with the impact of surprise but with the weight of something she'd known and needed to hear confirmed. She sat with it and let it settle and when it had settled it was just present, warm and specific and hers.

"Yeah," she said. "I think so too."

~~~

On the fourth day Allison's contact produced the thing they'd been waiting for.

The contact was a woman named Dr. Ingrid Tomas who held a position in Beowulf's Biological Survey Institute that was her official function and also, as Allison had established over fifteen years of professional relationship, a secondary function that involved the kind of awareness that Usher's people had on Manticore and that the Grayson contacts had on Grayson. The informal infrastructure.

Tomas came to Allison's home on the fourth afternoon and sat across from Buffy with the expression of someone who had prepared for this meeting and was now discovering that preparation was insufficient.

She said, "I've been aware of the anomaly in our institutional structure for eleven years. We call it the Constant. It's been in the record under various names for as long as there are records." She paused. "What it is, we have never determined. What it does�" she stopped.

"Tell me what you think it does," Buffy said.

"We think it maintains certain conditions," Tomas said carefully. "Not actively. Not in ways that are visible as action. More like�" she paused� "like a standing arrangement. Conditions that favor certain outcomes over others. A bias in the probability distribution." She looked at Buffy. "Eleven hundred years of subtle influence on the oldest research civilization in human space."

Buffy thought about what that meant. Beowulf's medical research tradition, which had shaped the development of every inhabited system in human space. The Beowulf Code, which governed genetic research across the League and beyond. The specific distribution of knowledge and capability that had accumulated in this system over eleven centuries.

"What conditions?" she said.

"We don't fully know," Tomas said. "That's the honest answer. We know the Constant exists and we know it's old and we know it's present in ways we can trace in the historical record but we don't know its objective." She paused. "Until now possibly."

Buffy looked at her.

"You're connected to something that understands it," Tomas said. "The Keeper, as you call it. Allison told me." She paused. "Does it know what the Constant is?"

Buffy checked the Keeper's accompanying quality.

It was different from usual. More directed, more focused, the attention not of something encountering something familiar but of something encountering something it had specific knowledge of and had been waiting for the moment to convey.

"Yes," she said. "It knows." She paused. "I don't yet. We're working on the communication." She looked at Tomas. "Give me what you have. All of it. Every year of the record. I'll cross-reference it against what the Keeper is giving me and we'll figure it out together."

Tomas looked at Allison.

Allison said: "Give her everything."

Tomas gave her everything.

~~~

She spent two days in Allison's study with the files and the Keeper's attention and the specific kind of work that was receiving and comparing and building toward a picture. She didn't leave the house much. Allison brought food and didn't ask how it was going until the evening of the second day when she asked once and accepted the answer we're not there yet without pushing.

She was getting there.

The Keeper's communication was not becoming language. But it was becoming more readable, the way something you'd been listening to for months in a foreign language started producing comprehension not of words but of intent. She couldn't have transcribed what the Keeper was telling her. She could map it against the historical record and find where the map corresponded and where it didn't.

What she assembled was this:

The Constant was old enough to predate Beowulf's colonization. It had been here before humans arrived in this system, established in the dimensional interface in the way the Keeper was established at a different interface, a presence at the boundary between dimensions that was organized and intentional and that had been here when the first humans arrived and had been present ever since.

It was not malevolent. The Keeper's quality when she tried to read the Constant's nature was not the quality it brought to the vampire network, the specific register of threat. It was something different. Something that the Keeper seemed to recognize not as an enemy but as a different category of thing entirely.

Old. Waiting. Patient in a different way than the Keeper's patience.

The Constant had been influencing Beowulf's research direction, she was fairly confident of that from the historical record cross-reference. The influence was subtle and long-term and aimed at something she could see the direction of without seeing the destination.

The direction was the dimensional interface.

Beowulf's research tradition, over eleven centuries, had been slowly moving toward a complete understanding of the interface between dimensions. The work Haverstock was doing was the most recent iteration of a line that went back centuries, each generation building on the previous one in ways that had the character of directed progress rather than random exploration.

Something had been guiding Beowulf toward understanding the interface.

She held this for a long time.

She went out on the third morning and walked and thought about it and let the Keeper's attention move with her through the city and sat at a waterfront that was the Beowulf version of what waterfronts were and looked at the water.

Then she called Haverstock on the communicator.

"Your interface theory," she said. "The fifteen years of work. Does it feel like you developed it or discovered it?"

A pause. "That's an unusual question."

"I know. Answer it anyway."

Another pause, longer. "Discovered," he said finally. "Always discovered. Like the framework was already there and I was finding it." He paused. "I've always assumed that was the nature of physics. Physics is what it is regardless of whether we understand it."

"Yes," she said. "That's true." She paused. "What if it was also true that the framework was already there and something was making it easier to find?"

A silence.

"That's a significant implication," he said.

"I know." She looked at the water. "Michael. I need you on Beowulf."

Chapter 32: Haverstock arrived five days later.

The intervening five days were the days when the picture she'd assembled acquired detail and the detail changed what she thought she understood, which was the process by which working knowledge became actual knowledge, slow and not always comfortable.

She told Honor on the second day.

Honor listened with the same complete attention she always brought to things Buffy told her, and when Buffy finished she was quiet for a moment and then she said: "Something has been guiding Beowulf's research toward understanding the dimensional interface. Something that predates human habitation of this system."

"Yes."

"And the purpose is not yet clear."

"The direction is clear. The destination I don't have yet."

Honor considered this. "It's not the same thing as the network we found. The vampire network."

"No. Different in kind." She paused. "The Keeper doesn't register it as a threat. But I don't know yet whether the Keeper's read is complete."

"What does your read say?"

She thought about this. "That it's old and it's been patient and it's been doing something specific for a very long time without requiring acknowledgment for doing it." She paused. "That's not a threat profile. But it's also not something I can categorize as benign without understanding it better."

"And Haverstock."

"Haverstock is the person who can help me understand the interface better. Which is where the Constant lives. If I can understand the interface better I can understand the Constant better." She paused. "And possibly communicate with it."

Honor held her gaze. "Communicate."

"The Keeper is a consciousness at an interface. If the Constant is something at an interface � and I believe it is � then communication might be possible." She paused. "Might."

"What does the Keeper say about that?"

She checked the accompanying quality. The Keeper was attentive in the way it was attentive when something was directly relevant, the heightened focus it brought to things that connected to its function. "It doesn't say no," she said.

"That's not yes."

"No. But it's the closest thing to yes I have right now." She paused. "I'm not going to try to communicate before Haverstock has the interface mapping at a level that gives me a better read on what I'd be communicating with." She held Honor's gaze. "I'm doing the research first. I've learned to do the research first."

Honor nodded. "Good."

Allison, when she told her, had a different reaction.

Allison went still in the way of someone receiving information that reorganizes a large amount of previously organized information and who is watching the reorganization happen in real time. She was quiet for longer than she was usually quiet, which with Allison was significant because she was not a person who was usually quiet.

Then she said: "The Biological Survey Institute's founding documents. Eleven hundred years ago. There's a phrase in them that nobody has ever been able to attribute to a source or explain in context. It's been annotated and debated and no one has ever found where it came from."

She looked at Buffy.

"What phrase?" Buffy said.

Allison said: toward the light between.

Buffy held that.

She looked at the Keeper.

The Keeper's quality was the quality of something that had been waiting for exactly this moment, not in the way of the dramatic revelation but in the patient way of something that had known where the path was going and had been walking it steadily.

"Tell me about the founding documents," she said.

~~~

The founding documents of the Biological Survey Institute were eleven hundred years old and had been digitized and archived with the care that eleven hundred year old founding documents received in a civilization that understood what it had. She read them in Allison's study over an evening with Tomas present and Allison present and the Keeper's full attention accompanying every page.

Toward the light between appeared in the preamble. Its source was annotated as unknown, which in a document this carefully attributed meant it had appeared in the earliest drafts without any record of who had introduced it. The annotations across eleven centuries of scholarship speculated: a reference to enlightenment, to interdisciplinary collaboration, to the literal light spectrum in medical research.

Nobody had suggested: a reference to the dimensional interface, because nobody doing the annotation had the framework for that suggestion.

She had the framework.

She read the phrase and felt the Keeper orient toward it in the specific way of recognition.

She looked at Tomas. "The people who drafted this document eleven hundred years ago. Do you know who they were?"

"The founding council is well-documented," Tomas said. "Sixteen individuals. Their biographies are in the archive."

"Were any of them�" she paused, working out how to ask this� "unusual? In ways that the archive notes without fully explaining?"

Tomas looked at Allison. Allison looked at Buffy.

"One," Allison said. She had a look on her face that said she'd been thinking about this since the first day and had been waiting for the conversation to arrive at it. "A woman named Yuen Sharra. She's in the founding documents as the primary author of the research methodology section. The biographies note that she was � the language is careful but consistent � remarkably insightful. Insights that preceded the research that would have produced them." Allison held Buffy's gaze. "Insights that subsequent centuries proved accurate."

"She knew where the research was going," Buffy said.

"She knew," Allison said. "More than she should have been able to know."

Buffy checked the Keeper.

The Keeper's response to the name Yuen Sharra was not registering. But the Keeper's response to the description � someone who knew more than they should have been able to know, eleven hundred years ago, in the first generation of Beowulf's civilization � was the quality of something recognizing a category.

Not the Constant.

Something the Constant had used as a channel.

"She was a conduit," Buffy said. "The Constant communicated through her. The founding documents don't just record what Beowulf's founders wanted the Institute to be. They record what the Constant wanted the Institute to become." She paused. "Over eleven hundred years."

The room was quiet.

Tomas said, after a moment: "Toward the light between."

"Toward understanding the interface between dimensions," Buffy said. "Yes."

Allison looked at the ceiling for a moment in the specific way of someone organizing a very large amount of information into a structure that could contain it. Then she looked at Buffy. "Why?"

Buffy looked at the Keeper.

The Keeper's response was the most complex thing it had communicated since the record. She sat with it for a long moment, receiving it in the way she'd learned to receive the Keeper's communications, not as language but as direction and quality.

Then she said, slowly: "Because the thing that made the Hellmouth is also patient. And it's been preparing. And what it's preparing for requires understanding the interface to counter." She paused. "The Constant has been preparing Beowulf to be the civilization that develops that understanding." She paused again. "Not for now. For later. For when it becomes necessary."

"How much later?" Allison said.

"The same timeline as the record," Buffy said. "Centuries. Not our lifetimes."

Allison was quiet for a long moment.

Then she said, very calmly: "Then we have work to do."

It was, Buffy thought, exactly the right response. The practical response. The response of someone who received information about a very long problem and began immediately to think about what came next.

"Yes," she said. "We do."

~~~

Haverstock arrived and the work shifted into the register of active research rather than the register of investigation, which was its own kind of shift, the specific quality of a thing that had been building toward action beginning to act.

She spent time every day in Allison's facility and time every evening with Haverstock in his temporary workspace in the university's physics department and the two lines of work were beginning to converge in ways she hadn't fully anticipated when she'd started both of them.

Allison's work on the lineage modification was producing a picture.

The modification was in the genome, as Allison had hypothesized, but not in the way that genetic modifications usually operated. It wasn't a sequence that coded for a protein or a regulatory element that affected expression in the conventional way. It was something Allison described, in the careful language of someone operating at the edge of her framework, as a standing pattern that mediated the organism's relationship with the dimensional interface.

Every person in the lineage had it. To different degrees, the expression varying, most of them expressing it subclinically � faster reflexes, slightly elevated sensory processing, the dreams � and a small fraction of each generation expressing it fully, which was what the calling was, the full expression finding a host.

She was the full expression.

But the modification was in all of them.

"This changes the scale of the thing you're building," Allison told her, on the morning she presented the full finding. "You said the institution was for the fully expressed. The called ones. But if everyone with the lineage modification is part of the line�"

"Then the line is much larger than I thought," Buffy said.

"Much larger. The modification has been maintained through two thousand years of population expansion across dozens of star systems. The number of people carrying it is�" Allison paused. "We don't have the data to project it with precision. But it's not hundreds. It's not thousands." She paused. "It's more."

Buffy held this.

She thought about the institution she was building and the scale it needed to be at and the scale that much larger implied and the gap between those two things.

She thought about the record. The thing that was coming, not in their lifetimes but in the lifetime of this civilization. The preparation that was necessary.

She thought about what Allison had said: then we have work to do.

"Okay," she said.

"Okay?" Allison looked at her.

"Okay means I understand what the work is and I'm not running from it." She held Allison's gaze. "It's bigger than I thought. It's also not starting from zero. The institution is already beginning. The lineage search is already underway. This makes the scope larger but the direction is the same." She paused. "We scale up."

Allison looked at her for a long moment.

"You're remarkable," she said. "In the clinical sense." She paused. "And in the other sense."

"You sound like Fritz," Buffy said.

"Fritz is a good doctor," Allison said, without self-consciousness. "Now. Do you want to know what else I found?"

There was more.

Of course there was more.

There was always more, and she was getting very good at receiving more.

She said: tell me.

Allison told her.

The modification had a component she hadn't characterized yet, a sequence that was present in all carriers but that didn't fit the pattern of the rest of it, that seemed to be doing something separate from the interface mediation. Allison had been looking at it for a week and had developed a hypothesis that she described as preliminary but that she thought was significant.

The sequence, she said, looked like a communication channel.

Not metaphorically. In the literal sense of a biological structure designed to receive and transmit information through the interface in a targeted rather than ambient way.

"In the fully expressed," Buffy said slowly, "the channel is active."

"Yes," Allison said. "In the subclinical carriers it's present but inactive. In you�" she paused. "The channel is active and it's been active for longer than you've been in contact with the Keeper." She paused. "It was active before. You just didn't know what you were receiving."

Buffy thought about two years of Watcher dreams and Slayer dreams and the First Slayer and the sense of the lineage that ran through her like current. She thought about all the things she'd been receiving without a framework for receiving them.

"The dreams," she said.

"The dreams," Allison confirmed.

She sat with this for a long time.

Then she said: "The Constant. On Beowulf. Has it been using this channel?"

Allison looked at her. "I think that's the question," she said. "And I think Haverstock is the person who can help us answer it."

~~~

The evening she told Haverstock about the channel was the evening that the interface mapping reached sufficient resolution to be useful as a communication framework, which was timing she didn't think was accidental and which the Keeper's accompanying quality confirmed was not.

Haverstock looked at the genetic sequence Allison had isolated and the interface map he'd built and spent forty minutes in silence that she recognized as the silence of someone doing very fast and very large thinking, and then he said: "The channel is tuned to the Constant's frequency."

She'd thought this might be true. Hearing it stated was different from thinking it.

"Yuen Sharra," she said.

"Probably," he said. "If Sharra was a carrier with an activated channel, the Constant could have communicated through her in exactly the way you described. Using the biological interface the modification provides." He paused. "Which means�"

"Which means it can communicate with me," she said. "Directly. If I let it."

"Yes." He held her gaze. "Do you know enough to do that safely?"

She looked at the Keeper.

The Keeper was still. Not the stillness of withholding but the stillness of something that had arrived at a moment it had been working toward and was holding it with the care the moment required.

"No," she said. "Not yet." She paused. "But I'm closer than I was yesterday."

He nodded. "Then tomorrow."

"Tomorrow," she agreed.

She went back to Allison's house and ate the dinner that was waiting and sat on the terrace and looked at the city and thought about all of it.

A letter arrived from Scotty.

It was short. Three sentences.

The first sentence said that the Talbott Cluster situation had resolved and that the resolution was significant and that he would be able to explain in person sooner than she'd expected.

The second sentence said that he was requesting leave and that it had been approved.

The third sentence said: I'm coming.

She read the three sentences and let the reading of them settle in the way of something that had been expected and was still, in the moment of arrival, more than expected.

She looked at the city.

She looked at the stars beyond it, which were wrong and were hers.

She looked at the Keeper's accompanying presence and thought about tomorrow and the interface and the Constant and all the work ahead that was large and specific and hers.

She thought: I'm here.

She thought: I know what I'm doing.

She thought: he's coming.

She held all three of those things at the same time and they held.

The city did its nighttime things.

She sat on the terrace of Allison Harrington's home on Beowulf, the oldest inhabited system in human space, with the wrong stars above her and the work ahead of her and the people in her and she was, she thought, entirely where she was supposed to be.

That was new.

That was, as it turned out, enough.

Chapter 33: She didn't sleep well the night before.

Not anxiety exactly. She knew the difference between the pre-operation alertness that was anxiety and the pre-operation alertness that was the Slayer sense doing its preparation work, and this was the second kind, the quiet activation of something that knew something significant was coming and was getting ready for it. She lay in the dark of Allison's guest room and let her body do the preparation and looked at the ceiling and thought about tomorrow.

The Keeper was very present.

More present than it had been since the night after the record, the night she'd sat on the roof of her building in Manticore with the city below her and the shape of the work visible for the first time. That night the Keeper had been present in the quality of something that had waited and was now watching the thing it had waited for begin to happen.

Tonight it was present in the quality of something that was also preparing.

She looked at the ceiling and said, quietly, "Is it going to work?"

The Keeper's response was not language. It was the quality of a particular kind of certainty, not the certainty of a guaranteed outcome but the certainty of a correct direction, the sense of something that knew the path even if it didn't know every step on it.

She'd learned to take that.

"Okay," she said.

She slept at two and woke at six and ran through the Beowulf morning, which was doing the thing mornings did, the specific assembly of a day from its component parts of light and air and the sounds of inhabited space coming awake. She ran and the Keeper ran with her, which was a thing she thought about as she ran, the quality of an ancient consciousness accompanying a seventeen-year-old girl through the streets of the oldest city in human space.

Eighteen, she corrected herself.

She'd turned eighteen three weeks ago on a planet that didn't know the word January. Carson had sent flowers. Fritz had sent a formal medical assessment with a handwritten note at the bottom that said happy birthday in the careful script of someone who didn't write by hand often but had decided this warranted it. The Queen had sent a letter that was brief and precise and warm in the way the Queen's warmth operated.

Scotty had sent a letter that she'd read four times.

She'd thought: eighteen.

She'd thought: I made it.

She ran through the morning and came back and ate an enormous breakfast that Allison watched her eat with the frank scientific interest of someone who had been studying the caloric math of the healing factor for weeks and was still revising her estimates upward, and then she went to the university.

~~~

The room Haverstock had arranged was in the physics department, a research space that had been cleared of its usual equipment and replaced with the interface mapping array that he'd spent three weeks assembling and reconfiguring and that produced, when active, the three-dimensional dynamic map that he and Prieto had developed.

The map was currently active.

She stood in front of it and looked at the Constant's signature in the map, which Haverstock had isolated over the past week by a process of elimination that he'd described as subtracting everything he knew until what remained was what he didn't know. The Constant appeared in the map as a stable pattern at the interface that was distinct from the Keeper's pattern, which she'd learned to recognize, and distinct from the ambient interface activity that the mapping revealed throughout the dimensional boundary.

It had the quality of something organized. Deliberate. Old in the way that organized things were old, not like geology was old but like intention was old, the specific age of a purpose that had been maintained across time rather than merely endured it.

Haverstock was at his instruments. Prieto was present by remote link, her face on a display in the corner with the focused expression of someone watching something they'd been preparing for. Fritz was here, standing slightly to the side in the position he took when he was monitoring rather than acting. Allison was here, sitting at a separate display with the genetic data she'd been working with, because she'd said she wanted to see what happened to the channel in the biological data when contact was made.

Honor was here.

She was standing near the door with Nimitz on her shoulder and the expression she had when something significant was happening and she was present for it as witness rather than participant, the specific quality of Honor being a witness, which was complete.

Buffy stood in front of the map and checked the Keeper.

The Keeper was oriented toward the Constant's signature with the attention of something that was about to do something it had been building toward and knew how to do.

"Okay," she said. "Walk me through it."

Haverstock said: "The channel Allison identified in the genetic sequence has an interface expression. I can see it on the map. It's quiescent right now, which matches the baseline readings from before. What we're looking for is activation." He paused. "Activation means the channel opens. What goes through it is�" he stopped. "Unknown. That's your part."

"And the Constant?"

"The Constant's signature is stable. It's not approaching. It's not reacting to our presence here, as far as I can tell." He paused. "But the map's resolution has limits. Things that happen below a certain threshold I may not be reading accurately."

She nodded.

She looked at the map. She looked at the Constant's signature, steady and old and patient in the way of things that had been patient for eleven hundred years and could afford to remain patient for whatever it took.

She thought about what she was doing.

She was going to open the channel. The biological channel in the lineage modification. She was going to open it and she was going to direct it toward the Constant's interface presence and she was going to see what the Constant said.

She'd communicated through the channel before without knowing that's what she was doing. The dreams were the channel. The Keeper's communication was through the channel. The lineage's connection had always run through the channel.

Opening it deliberately was different from receiving what came through it. She didn't have a complete framework for the difference. She was going to find out what the difference was.

The Keeper's quality shifted slightly, the shift of something that was moving from readiness into action.

She breathed.

She let the Slayer sense do the thing she'd been asking it to do for the past week, the specific focusing of the non-visual non-auditory read toward the dimensional interface, not diffuse in the way it normally operated but directed, a conscious direction of something that was usually reflexive. It was like the difference between peripheral vision and looking directly at something, the shift from ambient awareness to focused attention.

The channel opened.

She felt it as a change in the quality of her own perception rather than as a sensation in the conventional sense. Not quite like the record, which had been receiving. Not quite like the Keeper's communication, which was beside. Something different from both. A direction, a vector, an opening toward.

The Constant was there.

Not immediately. Not with the sudden presence of a thing arriving. More like the way a room became apparent when your eyes adjusted to its light, gradual and then complete, the Constant's presence assembling itself from what had been background into what was foreground over the course of several seconds.

It was very old.

She'd thought she'd understood very old from the vampires. She'd thought the Eirene Constantia's master had been a lesson in the specific weight of very old. This was older than that in a way that the vampires hadn't prepared her for, not because the vampires weren't old enough but because the Constant wasn't old in the same register. The vampires' age was the age of accumulated experience, of memory layered on memory for centuries. The Constant's age was something different, the age of a presence that had existed in relation to time rather than in time, that had been what it was when the first humans had arrived in this system and had been what it was before and would be what it was after.

She held her ground.

Not because she was afraid. Because the weight of what she was encountering required her to be present for it rather than to be flattened by it, and she'd spent two years and then seven months in this century developing the practice of being present for things that were larger than her. She stood in the room and breathed and stayed.

Something in the Constant's presence shifted.

She felt it the way she felt the Keeper's communications, as quality rather than language, and the quality was recognition. Not the recognition of something familiar. The recognition of something expected. The sense of something that had been waiting for this specific contact and was now receiving it.

She didn't try to speak. Not yet. She received first, the way she'd learned from the Keeper's communications that receiving produced better information than asking.

What came through the channel was not the simultaneous gestalt of the record. It was slower and more sequential, the accommodation of something very old learning to communicate with something very young in the way the young thing could receive. She felt that accommodation happening in real time, the Constant adjusting its output to the channel's bandwidth and her channel's specific character.

What came through was this:

Old. Older than humanity's presence on this world. Older than this world's star in its current configuration. A presence at the dimensional interface that had been here since before.

Watching.

Not passively. Not indifferently. The watching of something with an investment in what it was watching, the specific attention of something that cared about the direction of events without being willing or able to intervene in them directly.

The lineage.

The modification had not been accidental. The Constant had been present when the first Slayer was made, had been present at the making, had not made it but had been there when it was made and had understood what it was and had understood what it meant.

The thing that made the Hellmouth was known to the Constant.

Very old and very patient and working on a timeline that was geological. Not hostile in the way of something that wanted destruction. Hostile in the way of something that wanted replacement, the conversion of the experienced universe into a state that served its purposes and not the purposes of anything that currently occupied it.

The Constant had been watching this since before humanity existed.

It had been watching it since humanity appeared, with the specific interest of something that had seen this kind of conflict before and knew the shape of it.

The lineage was not an accident. The Constant had not made the lineage. But the Constant had understood what the lineage was when it appeared and had been, in the eleven hundred years since humanity reached Beowulf, doing what it could within its constraints to ensure that the lineage survived long enough to be ready.

The constraints were real and significant. The Constant could not intervene directly. Could not make choices for the people whose choices were the operative variable in the long conflict. Could only create conditions. Favor probabilities. Place a phrase in a founding document.

Toward the light between.

She stood in the room with the interface map and the Keeper beside her and the Constant in the channel and held all of it.

The question she'd been building toward arrived fully formed.

She asked it through the channel.

She didn't ask it in language. She asked it the way the channel worked, in the quality of the question rather than its words, directed toward the Constant's presence with the specific focus of something that wanted to know and was ready to receive.

What do you need from me?

The Constant's response was long.

Not in duration exactly but in density, the kind of communication that had been compressed into the available bandwidth and expanded in the receiving. She held it for a long time after, standing in the room with everyone watching her and not saying anything, because the holding was necessary before the articulation was possible.

Then she opened her eyes, which she hadn't known she'd closed.

Haverstock was at his instruments with the expression of someone watching something his instruments were recording that he didn't have a framework for yet. Allison was at her display with the expression of someone watching the biological data do something that was going to require several papers to adequately describe. Fritz was steady in the corner, watching her directly rather than his instruments.

Honor was still near the door. Nimitz was very still on her shoulder.

She said: "It needs two things."

The room was quiet.

She said: "It needs the lineage connected. Not just informed. Not just supported. Actively networked, in contact with each other, operating as a coherent whole rather than isolated individuals. That's the first thing." She paused. "The second thing is the interface research. Haverstock's work. Allison's work. It needs to continue and accelerate and it needs to produce, eventually, a complete understanding of the interface." She paused.
