"Because the thing that made the Hellmouth works through the interface. Understanding the interface is the only way to understand the mechanism it uses. And understanding the mechanism is the only way to counter it."

She looked at Haverstock.

He held her gaze. "Fifteen years," he said. "That's how long I've been working toward that."

"I know," she said. "You're closer than you were. We're going to help you get there."

She looked at Allison.

Allison was looking at the biological data display with the expression of someone who had just received the confirmation of something she'd suspected and was now thinking about what came next with the focused energy of someone who found next things interesting rather than daunting. She looked at Buffy. "The channel," she said. "In the subclinical carriers. If we could activate it�"

"Not all at once," Buffy said. "Not without the infrastructure to support what activation means." She held Allison's gaze. "That's the institution. That's why it has to exist before we can do the next part."

Allison nodded. "Then we build it faster."

"Yes," Buffy said. "We build it faster."

She looked at the interface map, at the Constant's signature still present and patient and old in the way it was old. She looked at the Keeper's signature beside it, the two patterns at the interface that were different from each other and both relevant to the same long work.

She thought about the dream she'd had weeks ago, the woman in a library who had said she'd been watching for a long time. She thought about the quality of the Constant's presence, which was not identical to the woman in the dream but was in the same register, the same kind of old, the same kind of watching.

She had a question she hadn't asked through the channel. She held it and looked at the map and decided it could wait.

Some questions needed time before they needed answers.

"Okay," she said. "Let's get to work."

~~~

The work that followed was three kinds simultaneously.

The interface research, which was Haverstock and Allison and Prieto and the apparatus they were running, producing data that was unprecedented in volume and specificity. She contributed to this in the specific ways she could contribute, which was as a subject of the biological study and as a channel to the Constant when specific questions required that channel, which happened twice in the two weeks that followed with results that Haverstock described, both times, as revolutionary.

The lineage network, which was the institution she'd been building and which was now building faster. The Queen's office was fully engaged. Carson was coordinating from Manticore, his Academy posting having been formally converted to a role in what the official documentation was calling the Survey of Lineage Continuity, which was accurate and which was also exactly the kind of name that attracted minimal attention from people who didn't know what it meant.

The Constant work, which was the ongoing process of understanding what she'd received in the channel and what it meant for the shape of everything she was doing.

She spent the evenings writing. She'd started a document that was not the record notes, not the institutional frameworks, something different. Something that was part personal account and part operational record and part the kind of document that was written by someone who knew they were in the middle of something that would need to be understood by people who came after.

The Keeper, when she wrote, was very present.

She noticed this. She mentioned it to Fritz, who mentioned it to Haverstock, who developed a hypothesis about the channel remaining partially activated after direct contact with the Constant and the Keeper using the activation to be more present than it had previously been.

This seemed right. The Keeper was more communicative since the contact. Not with language even now, but with the quality of its communications becoming clearer, more specific, more accessible to interpretation. She was starting to be able to distinguish between its responses to different things, the specific texture of approval versus concern versus the quality that meant pay attention to this.

It expressed pay attention to this a lot when she was writing.

She took that as encouragement and kept writing.

~~~

Scotty arrived on a Thursday.

She knew he was coming on Thursday because he'd sent a message three days earlier from a transit point with the arrival time and because she'd been aware of the approaching Thursday with the specific peripheral awareness of something that was not the Slayer sense and not the channel and was simply the ordinary human awareness of waiting for something.

She met him at the docking facility.

Not with a plan about how the meeting would go. She'd learned that plans for the meetings that mattered were less useful than simply being present for them, and she was present, standing in the docking facility at the time the ship came in.

He came through the hatch with his kit bag and the look of someone who had been on a ship for long enough that the idea of being somewhere specific was still recalibrating. He saw her and his face did the thing it did when he saw her, which she'd learned over the months of their correspondence was an accurate read and which she was not tired of reading.

He said: "Hi."

She said: "Hi."

They stood in the docking facility for a moment.

Then she said, "Allison's cooking tonight."

He picked up his kit bag. "Let's go."

She fell into step beside him and they walked through the docking facility and out into the Beowulf afternoon, which was doing the Beowulf version of afternoon, different from Manticore and from Grayson and like itself.

"How was it?" she said.

"Long," he said. "Significant." He paused. "I'll tell you properly later. When I've had time to organize it." He looked at her sideways. "You look different."

"Different how?"

"More�" he paused, looking for the word. "Settled."

She thought about this. "The Constant," she said. "The contact. Something about making contact with something that old and that certain about what it's doing settles you. The scale of it. Everything small stops feeling small and everything big stops feeling overwhelming." She paused. "Both just become the work."

He looked at her.

"Both just become the work," he repeated.

"Yes."

"I've been away for four months," he said. "In four months you made contact with a consciousness that predates human civilization."

"Among other things," she said.

He was quiet for a moment. Then he said, "Tell me everything. From the beginning."

She told him everything, from the morning she'd first arrived in Beowulf's atmosphere to the contact to what the Constant had communicated to the research work and the institution and all of it, the whole four months in the version she could tell while walking through the afternoon of an alien city.

He listened the way he always listened, with the complete attention that he brought to things that mattered and the specific quality of his listening that received information without needing to interrupt it, and when she finished they were at Allison's building and the afternoon had become early evening.

He was quiet for a moment.

Then he said, "What do you need?"

She looked at him.

"From me," he said. "In this. What do you need?"

She thought about what she actually needed, the honest answer rather than the manageable one. "Someone who sees the whole of it," she said. "Not just the operational piece or the research piece or the political piece. All of it, together, as one thing." She paused. "Someone who doesn't need me to explain the context every time. Who already has the context and can think with me rather than needing me to think at them."

He held her gaze. "I can do that."

"I know," she said. "That's why I said it."

He almost smiled.

She almost smiled back.

They went up.

~~~

Allison's reaction to Scotty was immediate and characteristic.

She took one look at him in the doorway and said, "You're the one from the letters," and then did not wait for confirmation before directing him to the kitchen where dinner was in its later stages and which required his assistance with something, she said, that he did not need a culinary background for, only hands and willingness.

He followed her without visible surprise, which told Buffy something about his capacity for Allison.

Honor was already there, Nimitz flowing between Honor's shoulder and the back of the kitchen chair in the way he did when he was comfortable and was choosing his own positions, and McKeon was there, which was unexpected. McKeon had arrived two days ago on the same ship as Scotty but earlier than Scotty because the same ship had two separate arrival times for its passengers depending on their internal transit point, which was a navigational detail she was still not fully fluent in.

McKeon looked at Scotty in the way of two people who had known each other long enough that greetings were a formality and said, "Terekhov sends his regards."

"I'll return them in writing," Scotty said, and accepted a role in the dinner preparation that Allison assigned him without preamble.

Fritz was there, in the corner with his notes in the way of Fritz in any social situation, present and documenting and content. Haverstock had declined dinner because Haverstock frequently declined dinner when data was current and the data was always current, but he'd sent a message of the specific kind that said he was working and happy rather than working and depriving himself, which she read as him being fine.

The dinner was large in the way of Allison's dinners when there were people who needed to eat and people she wanted to feed, which in Allison's framework were not always the same people and sometimes were, and the table held all of them in the specific configuration of a group that was not a family and was something that the word family gestured toward without containing.

She sat at the table and ate and listened to the conversation move and thought about something.

She'd been in this century for nine months.

Nine months ago she'd been on Hades, coming back to herself from a state she didn't like to examine too closely, in a jungle on a prison planet with a small group of people who were the only people in the universe who knew where she was.

Now she was at a dinner table on the oldest inhabited planet in human space with those people and the people they'd connected her to and the work that had found her and the shape of a life that was, in its broad outlines, recognizable as a life. Not finished. Not settled in the way of something that didn't move anymore. Settled in the way of something that had found its ground and was growing from it.

She thought: this is how it works.

She thought: the after doesn't look like anything in advance. It looks like this when you're in it.

Scotty was across the table, listening to something McKeon was saying with the expression he had when he was filing information he was going to use later. He looked up and caught her looking and his expression shifted in the small way it shifted when it was just her.

She looked back.

Nimitz made a sound on Honor's shoulder that she'd come to recognize as the treecat's version of are you going to do something about that.

"Mind your own business," she said.

Honor, without looking up from her conversation with Allison, said: "He never does."

Nimitz was entirely unrepentant.

She looked back at Scotty.

She thought: later. Not yet. Soon.

The dinner continued.

Chapter 34: The something she'd been building toward with Scotty happened three days after his arrival, which was not planned but which had the quality of things that happened when the conditions were right rather than when a decision was made.

They were on the terrace. Late, after dinner, which had been a smaller dinner than the first one, just the two of them because Allison had gone to bed early for Allison reasons that were probably accurate and possibly diplomatic, and Honor was doing something she'd described as correspondence with people she needed to correspond with and which Buffy suspected involved more of the war's changing situation than correspondence usually implied.

The city was in its nighttime configuration. Beowulf at night had a quality that was different from Manticore's nighttime and Grayson's nighttime and was its own thing, and she was adding it to the catalog of things that were their own things rather than comparisons to things she'd lost.

Scotty had been telling her about the Talbott Cluster, the full version now that he had context and time to organize it, and she'd been listening in the complete way of someone who was interested in both the information and the person telling it.

He finished and they were quiet for a moment with the city below them.

Then he said: "Buffy."

She looked at him.

He looked at her.

He said: "I've been thinking about what comes after the current situation. The next posting. The way things are developing with the war, the next several months are going to look different. There are going to be choices about where I go and what I do that I haven't had before." He paused. "I've been thinking about those choices with you as a variable."

"A variable," she said.

"A significant variable," he said. "The most significant one, currently." He held her gaze. "Is that � do you want to be that?"

She thought about the letters. The four months of letters that had built a structure she couldn't have named at the beginning and could name now. She thought about him in the docking facility saying hi with his face doing the thing it did. She thought about Allison saying you're the one from the letters and what that confirmed.

"Yes," she said. "I want to be that."

He breathed out.

She looked at him and he looked at her and the terrace held the Beowulf night around them and neither of them said anything for a moment because the moment was doing what it needed to do without additional input.

Then she said: "Autumn."

He looked at her.

"You said autumn in Sphinx," she said. "The spectacular version. You said I should see it before the winter."

"I did," he said.

"Is it still on offer?"

He held her gaze. Something settled in his expression, the same settling she'd felt in herself when the Constant's communication had organized what she'd known into what she understood. "Yes," he said. "Whenever you can get there."

"The work here is going to take another few weeks," she said. "Maybe a month. And then Manticore, to report in properly. And then�" she paused.

"And then Sphinx," he said.

"And then Sphinx," she agreed.

The city did its things. The stars were the wrong stars and they were hers and he was here, on the terrace, and she was here, and this was the after.

She thought about Joyce Summers who had asked her, once, when she was fourteen and the asking was hypothetical, what she wanted her life to look like.

She hadn't known then.

She thought she knew now.

Not the details. The shape.

This. The work and the people and the something worth fighting for. The wrong stars that were hers. The accumulated weight of a life being built in a place she hadn't planned to be and couldn't imagine not being now.

This was the shape.

She looked at it and it held.

~~~

The final week on Beowulf had the quality of things that were ending and things that were beginning simultaneously, the specific texture of a transition rather than a stop.

Haverstock's interface research had reached the point of sufficiency for the next phase, which was bringing it back to Manticore and integrating it with the institutional framework and the lineage network that was growing. He was coming to Manticore for the next phase. He seemed, she thought, genuinely happy about this, the specific happiness of someone whose work had found its context.

Allison's biological research had reached the point of the first major paper, which she'd shown Buffy in draft and which Buffy had reviewed and approved with specific redactions and which was going to be, Allison said, the most significant publication in her specialty in the past several decades.

She'd said: I'll believe you.

Allison had said: you should.

The Constant was quiet in the specific way of something that had communicated what it needed to communicate and was returning to its patient background position. She'd made one more contact, brief, not to receive new information but to confirm what she'd received and to establish that the channel was open and available when it was needed.

The Constant had confirmed both things with the quality of something that had been waiting a very long time for exactly this and was now, in whatever the Constant's equivalent of relief was, able to return to its patient watching from a different position.

She thought about that on the last night. Sitting on the terrace again, alone this time, with the city and the wrong stars and the Keeper's presence and the open channel that was quieter now but present.

She thought about the long shape of what the Constant had been doing for eleven hundred years and about the long shape of what the lineage had been doing for two thousand years and about her specifically, the specific person at the specific intersection, the seventeenth-now-eighteenth-year-old girl from Sunnydale who had been the most recent in a long line of called ones and who had ended up here, in this century, at this place, at this particular moment when the various long threads were converging.

She didn't believe in accidents.

She'd stopped believing in accidents around the time she'd stopped being surprised by the specific way that the universe placed the right people in proximity to each other at the right moments, not through design in the sense of a plan that had been made and was being executed, but through the accumulated probability of things that wanted to be in contact eventually finding each other.

She'd found the people she was supposed to find.

She was doing the work she was supposed to do.

The displacement and the trauma and the terrible months and the recovery and all of it had been real and costly and she wasn't going to pretend otherwise. But the shape of what it had aimed her at was visible now and the shape was right.

The Girl ran through the ancient dark at the edge of her awareness, present in the way she was always present, the ancient lineage doing what it did, which was simply continuing. Forward through time as it always had. Patient and specific and uninterested in the distinction between what was expected and what was not, which was the oldest thing about it, that it had never required expectation to continue.

She let herself be that for a moment. Just the continuation. The oldest part of herself, the part that was in the lineage and the channel and the open connection to the interface, just present in the flow of it.

Then she was also the girl on the terrace. The one who was eighteen and had a bag to pack and a ship to catch in the morning and a life that was large and specific and hers.

Both of those things at once.

She looked at the city.

She looked at the stars.

She thought: I'm ready for what comes next.

She thought it with the full weight of what that meant, not the bravado of someone who was trying to convince herself but the grounded knowledge of someone who had done the preparation and was now at the point where the preparation gave way to the doing.

I'm ready.

She got up.

She had a bag to pack.

Chapter 35: The ship back to Manticore was the same class as the Wayfarer, which she noticed and then let go, because the Wayfarer was the Wayfarer and this was a different ship and the comparison served nothing.

It served nothing and she made it anyway because that was still how her mind worked, the constant triangulation against previous experience that was the specific mode of someone who had lost a frame of reference and was building a new one piece by piece.

The new frame of reference was more complete than it had been.

She sat at the viewport on the first day out of Beowulf and watched the system recede and thought about what she was carrying back.

The Constant's communication, which she'd written down in the document and which was going to be the most significant addition to the Clinkscales archive in its history.

Allison's research, which was going to change the understanding of the lineage on a biological level.

Haverstock's interface data, which was going to change the understanding of what the supernatural in this universe actually was.

The open channel, which was available now in a way it hadn't been before the contact, a resource she hadn't had and now did.

And the shape of what she was building, which was clearer than it had ever been. Not a finished plan. She'd learned not to wait for finished plans. A direction, clear and specific, the kind that let you navigate even when the exact path wasn't visible yet.

Scotty was at the seat beside her.

He had his own work, the debrief materials from the Talbott Cluster that needed organizing into the formal reports that the navy required, and he was doing it with the focused efficiency of someone who had learned to work in transit. She watched him work for a while and then went back to her own.

They worked in the same space in the comfortable silence of people who had the same relationship to silence, which was that it was a form of company rather than an absence.

She wrote in the document.

She wrote about Beowulf and the Constant and what she'd received and what it meant. She wrote about Yuen Sharra and toward the light between and the founding documents. She wrote about Allison's research and the channel and the biological substrate of the lineage.

She wrote about the thing that had made the Hellmouth and the long timeline and what the preparation required.

She wrote carefully and completely because the document was going to be read by people who came after her and those people needed the full picture.

The Keeper was present while she wrote, attending in the way it attended to the document work, and she wrote with that attention alongside her and thought about the document becoming part of the archive alongside the Clinkscales records and what the combined picture looked like for the people who would eventually need it.

She wrote until she ran out of what she could articulate for that day and then she put the document aside and looked at the stars, which were the transit stars, the specific quality of stars in motion, different from the fixed configuration that ports produced.

She thought about Manticore and the work waiting there and then she thought about Sphinx after Manticore, the autumn version, the cold she hadn't imagined.

She was looking forward to the cold she hadn't imagined.

She was looking forward to a great many things.

~~~

Manticore from approach was different from Manticore the first time. The first time it had been the destination, which had made it enormous with the weight of what it represented, the new place, the new life, all of it arriving at once.

This time it was home.

She noticed this as the planet resolved from point to disk to the specific configuration she'd spent ten months learning. Home. Not in the sense of Sunnydale, not in the sense of her mother's house and her mother's kitchen and the specific smell of rosemary chicken. In the sense of the place she returned to. The place with the shape of her life in it.

Both could be true.

She'd learned that both could be true.

The docking and the transition back to her quarters and the immediate meeting with Chen and then with Usher were efficient and she moved through them with the accumulated competence of someone who knew this system and how to operate in it.

The Queen met with her on the second day.

The informal sitting room again. Ariel on the chair back. Tea. The afternoon light that she knew now, the specific Manticoran quality of it.

She told the Queen everything. The complete version, not the summary. Two hours of it, detailed and organized, the way she'd learned to brief people who needed the full picture rather than the navigable excerpt.

The Queen listened.

When Buffy finished the Queen was quiet for a moment and then she said: "The timeline."

"Centuries," Buffy said. "Well outside anyone's lifetime currently."

"But within the lifetime of the civilization."

"Yes."

"Then what I build in the next decade matters for something that will happen in three hundred years." The Queen held her gaze. "That's an unusual way to think about governance."

"I know." Buffy held her gaze back. "But you've been governing with an eye toward what your civilization will be in fifty years. A hundred years. The extension is the same logic."

"It is," the Queen said. She looked at Ariel, who looked back at her with whatever he was telling her through their link. "The institution."

"Yes."

"I've been thinking about what it needs to be officially," the Queen said. "The Survey of Lineage Continuity is accurate but insufficient. What you're describing is more than a survey." She paused. "It needs authority. Legal standing. The ability to operate across sovereign systems by agreement rather than by tolerance." She paused again. "That's a negotiation with every member of the Alliance at minimum and ideally with Haven and the League eventually."

"I know it's not immediate," Buffy said.

"No. But it's the direction." She looked at Buffy. "I'll take it to the Alliance Council. With your full briefing. What you received from the Constant, the timeline, the nature of the threat. All of it." She paused. "Some of them are going to have difficulty with the framework."

"I know."

"Not me," the Queen said. "Ariel hasn't been wrong about you yet."

Buffy looked at the treecat. He looked back at her with green eyes that were doing the thing they always did, which was see clearly and without apology.

"Tell him he hasn't been wrong about you either," she said.

The Queen conveyed it. Ariel's tail tip moved.

"Good," Buffy said.

~~~

Three days after the meeting with the Queen she sat down with Carson in her quarters, the two of them across from each other at her desk with the accumulated evidence of the work around them.

He had the Survey's current status. Twelve confirmed lineage candidates across the Alliance. Seven in contact. Five with some form of ongoing support structure around them. The Grayson candidates integrating well. Yeva sending letters at increasing frequency that had shifted from questions about the physical profile to questions about the history to questions about the institution and whether she could be part of building it, which was a development Buffy had not expected at the timeline it arrived on but which she received as exactly right.

She told Carson what she'd received from the Constant.

Not the summary version. The complete version.

He listened with the complete attention he always brought to things she told him, and when she finished he was quiet in the way of someone whose entire frame of reference for a thing they'd spent their life committed to had just been confirmed and expanded in ways that required time to hold properly.

"The founding," he said finally. "My great-grandfather's grandfather. The man who started keeping the archive." He paused. "He said he started because something told him to."

She looked at him.

"He wrote it in the first entry," Carson said. "My grandfather showed me when I was eleven. He said: something told me this was necessary and I could not say no to the telling." He paused. "We thought it was a metaphor. A way of describing conviction." He paused. "It wasn't."

"No," she said. "It wasn't."

He was quiet for another moment. Then he said, with the specific steady quality of someone who had committed to something thirty years ago by inheritance and was now recommitting to it by direct knowledge: "Tell me what the institution needs and I'll help you build it."

She looked at him.

She thought about Sunnydale and the Council and Giles, who had given her everything he could within the constraints of what he was and had wanted to give her more and hadn't always known how. She thought about what it would have meant to have what Carson was offering, the direct specific commitment of someone who saw the whole thing and was in it.

"I know," she said. "That's why I'm here."

He nodded once.

They got to work.

~~~

She wrote to Scotty that evening from her desk with the city outside the window and the work spread across the surface and the Keeper present in the specific way it was present when she was doing something it considered correct.

The letter was shorter than usual. She'd said the significant things in person and the shorter letters were, she'd found, sometimes the more complete ones.

She said: Manticore is good. The meeting with the Queen was good. Carson and I are moving forward on the institutional structure and it's beginning to have the shape of something real rather than something planned.

She said: I've been thinking about what you said on the terrace. About choices and significant variables. I wanted to say again that I'm glad you said it the way you said it. Directly. I've been learning that direct is better and I'm glad you already know that.

She said: how are the leave arrangements looking. I want to know when we're going to Sphinx.

She sealed it and sent it.

Then she turned back to her desk and the work and thought about the shape of the days ahead.

Full. Specific. The long work and the immediate work and the people who were part of both and the city outside the window that was home in the sense she'd found for home in this century.

She'd built something.

Not finished. Not settled in the sense of done. Settled in the sense of having found its footing.

She'd found hers.

She looked at the desk and the files and the map with its nodes and Carson's letters and the copy of the founding document of the Biological Survey Institute with toward the light between underlined in her own handwriting, and she thought about all of it as one thing, the accumulated specific whole of nine months in a century she hadn't expected.

The Keeper's quality was the quality of satisfaction.

Not completion. The work was too large and too long for completion to be a present-tense experience.

But satisfaction. The specific satisfaction of something that had been waiting and was now watching the thing it had waited for doing what it was built to do.

She felt that.

She let herself feel it.

Then she turned back to the work, because the work was what she was for and the work was also, in the deepest sense, what she wanted, and those two things being the same thing was the best version of what a life could be.

She worked until midnight.

Then she went to sleep and the Girl ran through the ancient dark and the running was joy, pure and uncomplicating, the body doing what it was built for, the lineage continuing forward as it had always continued, patient and specific and without requiring expectation to go on.

Forward.

Always forward.

She ran and the dark held her and she was entirely herself, all three versions of herself at once, and the running felt like beginning rather than ending, the specific quality of a threshold crossed.

She ran.

Tomorrow there was work.

Tonight there was this, the ancient joy of the body and the dark and the forward motion, and it was enough.

It was, as it turned out, exactly enough.

Arc Four: The Vigil

Chapter 36: Sphinx in autumn was exactly what Scotty had said it was, which was the spectacular version, and the spectacular version was not a figure of speech.

She stood at the edge of the property in the northern range on the first morning and looked at what the light was doing to the trees and understood why he'd used that word specifically. Not beautiful, which would have been accurate but insufficient. Spectacular, which was beauty that demanded something from you in return for being received.

The trees were not the same trees as any trees she'd known. They were the Sphinx version, old and enormous in the way of things that grew in cold climates where growing was slow and what grew did so with the accumulated weight of many careful seasons. Their leaves were the wrong colors for autumn in any frame she had, not the red and orange of California's inland hills or the New England colors she'd seen in photographs, but something stranger and more saturated, a range of colors that the pigment systems of Sphinx's specific biology produced and that had no precedent in anything she'd looked at before.

She looked at them for a long time.

Scotty came to stand beside her with coffee and she didn't say anything and he didn't say anything and the morning held them in the specific way of mornings in places where you were not a visitor.

"Okay," she said eventually.

"Yes," he said.

"The winter version has to earn this," she said.

"It does," he said. "In different ways."

She thought about different ways and about the word earn and about the specific project of coming to know a place through the different versions of itself that it offered across time.

She thought: I want to know this place through its different versions.

She thought: I want to come back.

She held both of those things and they were both true.

~~~

The house was the Tremaine family's property in the way of properties that had been family properties for long enough to have the accumulated character of that, the layers of different generations' decisions visible in the architecture and the garden and the arrangement of the spaces inside.

His parents were not in residence. They were on Manticore for the next month, which she'd been told was a coincidence and which she suspected was something closer to the considerate arrangement of considerate people who had heard about her and decided that the first visit to the place should not also be the first meeting with the family.

She filed this with appreciation and didn't mention it.

The property had a person who managed it, a woman named Sascha who was seventyish and had the specific quality of someone who had been managing something for long enough that the management had become indistinguishable from care. She'd received Buffy and Scotty with the comfortable authority of someone in their own domain and had produced dinner that first night that was the Sphinx version of what Allison's cooking was the Beowulf version of, which was to say serious and specific and made with the understanding that food was how you told people things you wanted them to know.

Buffy had eaten an enormous amount. Sascha had watched and said nothing and produced more, which was the correct response.

She slept better than she'd slept in months.

The Sphinx air was different from Manticore's air and different from Beowulf's and had the quality that Scotty had described as something you'd know when you breathed it, a specific character she didn't have a word for. Cold, obviously, but cold with a composition, the specific mixture of what Sphinx's atmosphere produced in autumn at this elevation making something that tasted like clarity when she breathed it.

She breathed it and let it be what it was.

The Keeper was present in the way it was always present and was also, she thought, doing something that she could only describe as appreciating the air. Which was a strange thing to think about a consciousness at a dimensional interface. And which she thought was probably accurate.

She spent the first three days learning the property.

Not systematically. She wasn't building a patrol route or mapping a territory for operational purposes. She was walking it the way she'd walked the waterfronts of Manticore and Beowulf, with the ambient version of the Slayer sense that was simply awareness rather than assessment, learning what a place felt like to be in.

The property felt like something that had been inhabited with care for long enough that the care had become structural. The garden that Sascha tended and which was between its season of growth and its season of rest, the specific beautiful stage of things winding down that had its own character distinct from the wound-down version that winter would produce. The outbuildings that had the purpose-built quality of things that had been added when the purpose became clear rather than in anticipation of it. The paths between things that had been worn by the repetition of actual use rather than designed.

Scotty walked with her on some of it and let her walk alone on the rest of it, which was the correct calibration and which she didn't have to explain.

On the fourth day she asked him about the treecats.

She'd known they were from Sphinx from Honor's description and from the briefings she'd read months ago, but knowing from description and being in the place were different things. She'd seen the signs of them in the edge of the property, the specific evidence of things that moved through the canopy with deliberate purpose.

"Are they here?" she said. "On the property?"

"The range that uses this area. Yes." He looked toward the tree line. "They generally avoid close contact with people they don't know."

"Generally."

"There's been one who comes to the edge of the garden sometimes." He paused. "Sascha leaves fruit. The treecat takes it." He paused. "Sascha says it watches the house."

She looked at the tree line.

The Keeper's quality was the quality it brought to things that were relevant in the lineage register rather than the operational one, the sense of something touching the channel in the way the dreams touched it.

"It's watching me," she said.

Scotty looked at her. "The treecat?"

"Yes." She kept looking at the tree line. "The Keeper's doing something I associate with � contact. Not the Constant's kind of contact. Something more like when Nimitz is paying attention to me." She paused. "Treecats feel things through their empathic sense. The Keeper operates through the channel. They're not the same thing but they might be�adjacent."

"Adjacent," he said.

"In the same register," she said. "Both of them reading something at the interface level." She looked at the tree line. "The treecat on this property has been reading me since I arrived."

Scotty was quiet for a moment. "Is that a problem?"

"No," she said. "I think it's�" she stopped. "I think it's curious. And I think if Nimitz's read on me is any guide, being read clearly by a treecat is a good thing rather than a concerning one." She paused. "I want to meet it."

"Treecats choose when they meet people."

"I know." She looked at the tree line. "I'm going to sit out here tomorrow morning and see what happens."

What happened was that she sat on the low wall at the edge of the garden at six in the morning with the autumn doing its morning version around her and the wrong stars fading in the specific Sphinx way, and at six-forty-two something moved in the canopy and a treecat came to the edge of the lower branches and looked at her.

It was not Nimitz's size. Slightly smaller, younger-looking, with coloring that was the Sphinx autumn colors, the camouflage of something that had evolved in exactly this landscape. Its eyes were the same green as Nimitz's eyes and they were doing the same thing Nimitz's eyes always did, which was see clearly and without apology.

She looked back.

The Keeper was very present.

The treecat looked at her for a long time. Then it made a sound she hadn't heard Nimitz make, something low and questioning, and she didn't know what the question was but she answered it as honestly as she could, which was to simply be present and readable, offering whatever the channel and the Slayer sense and the accumulated weight of who she was produced in the register the treecat was reading.

The treecat came closer.

Not all the way down from the canopy. But closer, close enough that she could see its whiskers and the small deliberate way it held its true-hands when it was alert.

It made another sound.

She said, very quietly: "Hi."

It looked at her.

Then it went back up into the canopy and disappeared.

She sat on the wall for another twenty minutes and thought about what had just happened, which was that a native Sphinx treecat had made a preliminary assessment of her and had not left immediately and had said something she didn't have a word for and had done so in the register that the Keeper was present in.

She was going to write to Nimitz's previous studies about this.

She was going to write to Honor.

She was going to tell Haverstock, who was going to have a theory.

She did none of those things immediately. She sat on the wall and let the morning happen around her and thought about the specific and wonderful strangeness of the universe she was in and let it be wonderful and strange rather than asking it to be otherwise.

She thought: this is a remarkable place.

She thought: I want to come back.

She went inside and ate an enormous breakfast and Sascha watched and said nothing and produced more.

~~~

The week in Sphinx had the specific quality of a week that was also a different kind of time, the time you were inside rather than moving through, where the days had texture rather than direction. She'd been operating in directional time for months, the time of someone who knew where they were going and was going there. This was different. Restorative in a way she hadn't known she needed until she was in the middle of it.

She and Scotty talked. The real versions of the conversations they'd been having in letters for four months, the conversations that the letter form could approach but couldn't fully contain. About the war and where it was going and what that meant for him. About the institution and where it was going and what that meant for her. About Sphinx and Manticore and Beowulf and all the places that were becoming the geography of a life in this century.

About after. The shape of it.

They talked about the after with the specific care of people who were building something they couldn't fully see yet and knew that was true and were continuing anyway, which was the only way you built anything real.

She told him things she hadn't fully told anyone else. Not because she'd been hiding them but because they required the specific kind of conversation that required being somewhere you weren't operational, somewhere you could be still without the stillness being a resource you were directing at something.

She told him about the loneliness. The specific texture of it in this century, which was different from the loneliness she'd known in the previous one because there had been the Council and the Scoobies and the sense of a world she knew even when she was alone in it, and this loneliness had been the loneliness of not knowing the world, of learning to be a self in a context that had no landmarks from the previous version.

She told him about building the new self from available materials, which was what she'd been doing, and how that process was the same as the physical healing in some ways, the body doing what it needed to do regardless of whether the conscious mind was on board with the timeline.

He listened.

He didn't try to fix it. He received it with the quality of attention that she'd come to understand was one of the most specific things about him, the capacity to receive something difficult without either resolving it prematurely or being flattened by it.

He told her things too.

About the Talbott Cluster and what had happened there and the specific kind of cost that those months had produced, not the physical cost which had been minor but the other kind, the cost of being in situations that required decisions faster than certainty was available and living with the decisions afterward.

She listened the same way he had.

They were, she thought, good at this. At the actual version of each other, not the managed presentation.

On the last night she said: "I want to tell you something."

He looked at her.

"I've been bad at this," she said. "The letting people in part. I've been better at it here than I was in the previous century, which is an indictment of the previous century as much as a credit to me, but I want to say it directly rather than leave it implied." She held his gaze. "You're in. If you want to be."

He held her gaze for a moment.

"I've been in," he said. "Since the terrace at Allison's."

"I know," she said. "I wanted to say it in words."

"Thank you for saying it in words."

She looked at him. He looked at her.

Then she leaned across the space between them and kissed him, which was the other way of saying it in words and which she'd been working toward with the same care she brought to things she'd decided mattered.

He kissed her back.

The Sphinx night was outside the window, cold in the way of the cold she hadn't imagined, the spectacular autumn doing its last act before it gave way to winter, and they were here, in the house, and this was the after.

Not everything she'd lost. Not a replacement for anything she'd lost. Its own thing, made of its own materials, with its own specific shape.

She thought: this is mine.

She thought: yes.

Chapter 37: She went back to Manticore with the autumn colors still in her memory and the cold in her bones in the good way and the Keeper very present in the way it was present when something significant had been settled.

The work was there.

It was always there and she'd stopped resenting it for being there, which was the change that Sphinx had produced in her, the specific reset that restorative time produced in people who needed it and had been running without it for too long.

She went back to it with the energy of something that had been restored.

The institutional framework had advanced while she was away. Carson had been working with Chen and the legal team the Queen's office had assigned and the structure that had been beginning to look real before she left looked more real now, the way things looked when someone was actively paying attention to them.

She had a name for it.

She'd been thinking about the name since Beowulf and she'd landed on it in Sphinx, in the quiet of the property and the morning run and the space to think that the Tremaine house provided.

She told Carson first.

He looked at the name and was quiet for a moment and then said, "The Watchers never called themselves that."

"No," she said. "That's part of the point."

He held her gaze. "The Vigil," he said. "As in�"

"As in what the Clinkscales family has been doing for four generations," she said. "And what the Constant has been doing for eleven hundred years. And what the lineage itself has been doing for two thousand years without knowing it." She paused. "It's not a Council. It's not a hierarchy. It's people keeping watch on the long thing, together, without requiring a center to organize around."

"The center is the lineage," he said.

"The lineage connects it," she said. "But it's not the center. There's no center. That was the Council's mistake." She held his gaze. "The Vigil is all of us. Everyone with a role in the long work, in whatever way that role expresses itself." She paused. "Haverstock. Allison. Tomas on Beowulf. Usher's people. The lineage candidates. The Clinkscales archive." She paused. "You."

He looked at her.

"Me," he said.

"You've been the Vigil since before you knew the word," she said. "Your grandfather was the Vigil before you. It's just a name for what already exists."

He was quiet for a longer moment than she'd expected.

Then he said, with the specific quality of someone receiving something they've been unknowingly carrying for a long time and finally being given the right container for it: "Yes. All right."

She told the Queen the same day.

The Queen's response was the Queen's version of Carson's response, more political in register but the same in substance, which was the recognition that the name made the thing more real in the specific way that names made things real, the word arriving to describe what had already been forming.

"The Vigil," the Queen said. She said it with the quality of someone testing its weight. "It'll need a formal charter."

"I know. Chen's team is working on it."

"It'll need Alliance recognition eventually."

"I know that too." She paused. "Not yet. The Alliance has enough to deal with and the Vigil needs to be solid before it's recognized. Recognition before solidity creates the wrong incentives." She held the Queen's gaze. "It needs to be real before it needs to be official."

The Queen looked at her. "You've thought about this."

"I've been thinking about it since Beowulf." She paused. "I've also been thinking about what the Council got wrong. Official recognition was the thing that gave the Council the wrong shape. It had authority before it had wisdom and the authority set the shape and the wisdom that came after had to fit the shape that was already there." She paused. "I want to build the wisdom first."

The Queen held her gaze for a long moment.

Then she said: "You're eighteen."

"I know."

"You've been in this century for ten months."

"I know."

"And you're describing institutional design with the perspective of someone who has been thinking about it for decades."

"I've been thinking about what the Council was since I was fifteen," she said. "That's three years of thinking about it from the inside. And I've had ten months to think about what it should have been instead, from the outside." She paused. "The outside is where you can see the shape of something. The inside is where you live with it."

The Queen was quiet.

"Build it right," she said. "Take the time it takes."

"Yes," Buffy said. "That's exactly what I'm going to do."

~~~

Yeva came to Manticore two months later.

Not for the Vigil, or not only for the Vigil, though the Vigil was the framework that made the visit possible. She was enrolled in a program at the Manticore Royal University that was technically a gap-year program in biological sciences and was actually the first formal educational track that the Vigil was offering to lineage candidates who wanted more than the information she gave them in initial contact.

She arrived with a bag that was packed with the efficiency of someone who had been preparing and the expression of someone who had been waiting for exactly this.

Buffy met her at the transit station.

Yeva was taller than she'd been at the initial meeting, which was what seven months of the Slayer physiology finishing its expression did. She'd had her hair cut shorter and she moved differently than she'd moved before, the unconscious recalibration that happened when people started to inhabit what they were rather than navigating around it.

She looked at Buffy and said: "I've been practicing."

"I know," Buffy said. "I read your letters."

"I want to show you."

"We'll get you settled first," Buffy said. "Then you can show me."

They got her settled in the residence that the Vigil was developing adjacent to the university, a building that had been acquired through a process Buffy had not been involved in directly but which Chen had managed with the specific efficiency of the Queen's office when it was fully engaged. The residence was the beginning of what would eventually be a training facility, though it didn't look like one yet, still having the character of a building being repurposed rather than a building built for purpose.

She'd learned that most things started as repurposed rather than built for purpose and that was fine.

She showed Yeva the building and introduced her to the two people already in residence, a young man named Decker who was from Trevor's Star and who had presented subclinically until six months ago when the expression had shifted to full, and a woman named Priya who was from Grayson and who was the second of the Grayson candidates and who had the specific quality of someone who had been working toward this moment systematically since the day of her initial contact.

Yeva and Priya looked at each other.

She watched the moment of recognition between two people who were in the same category of experience, the specific thing that happened when someone who had been navigating something unusual met someone else who was navigating the same unusual thing.

She remembered that moment. The first time she'd met another Slayer.

That memory had weight. She let it have its weight.

"I'll leave you to settle in," she said. "We'll start tomorrow."

~~~

Starting tomorrow meant something specific.

She'd been thinking about curriculum since Beowulf, since the Constant's communication had made clear what the long work required and therefore what the preparation required. The Vigil wasn't training Slayers in the way the Council had trained them, which was as weapons for a specific operational context. The Vigil was developing people who could operate in the long work in whatever way their specific capabilities and situations allowed.

For the fully expressed, that meant the physical training, yes, because the physical capability was there whether you used it or not and operating in it well was better than operating in it badly. But it also meant the history, the biology as Allison was mapping it, the dimensional interface research as Haverstock was developing it, the lineage network and how to work within it.

For the subclinical carriers, it meant something different. The channel was active enough for the dreams and the low-level sensory enhancement but not for the full operational profile, and what those people needed was not the same as what Yeva and Decker and Priya needed.

She was developing both tracks simultaneously.

It was a lot.

She did it anyway because a lot had always been the operating condition and she'd stopped waiting for it to become less.

The training with Yeva and Decker and Priya was the immediate thing, the piece that was hands-on and present rather than institutional and systemic. She worked with them in the way she'd wanted someone to work with her, which was as people first and capabilities second, building the physical foundation not through trial and combat but through understood principles.

She told them what she knew about the healing factor and how to work with it rather than around it.

She told them about the Slayer sense and how to listen to it rather than be controlled by it.

She told them about the dreams and the lineage and the ancient knowledge that ran through all of them like current and how to use it as a resource rather than an imposition.

She told them about fear. That it was present and didn't need to not be present. That the goal wasn't fearlessness but competence in the presence of fear, the capacity to do the thing while the fear was happening rather than after it was resolved.

Yeva was the best student she'd had yet, which was not entirely because Yeva was the most talented, though she was that. Yeva was the best student because she asked the questions that were actually questions rather than the questions that were checking whether she was getting the right answers. She challenged what she didn't understand and accepted what she did and had the specific quality of someone who was going to be excellent at this and knew it without arrogance.

She was going to be remarkable.

Buffy told her this directly one afternoon after a session that had pushed both of them and had produced something she hadn't expected, which was Yeva adapting a technique faster than Buffy had developed it.

Yeva looked at her. "Is that a compliment or a warning?"

"Both," Buffy said. "Being remarkable means people are going to want things from you. I want you to be ready for that."

"What kind of things?"

"Depends on who's wanting them." She looked at Yeva. "Some of it will be good things. People who need the capability you have and who will be better for having it available." She paused. "Some of it will be the other kind. People who see the capability before they see the person." She held Yeva's gaze. "You get to decide which asks you answer."

Yeva held her gaze back. "You got to decide that?"

She thought about the Council. She thought about what it had taken to get to the deciding. "Eventually," she said. "I'm trying to give you the eventually earlier."

Yeva nodded slowly. She looked at the training space, which was a room in the residence that had been cleared and equipped with the basics. "Can I ask you something that's not training?"

"Yes."

"The war," Yeva said. "The Vigil's work. The thing that's coming in three hundred years." She paused. "How do you hold all of it at the same time?"

She thought about the honest answer.

"You don't hold it all at the same time," she said. "You hold the piece that's in front of you and you trust that the other pieces are being held by the other people who are holding them." She paused. "That's the thing the Vigil is for. Distributing the holding."

Yeva looked at her. "That's new for you," she said. "You didn't always think that."

She looked at the seventeen-year-old who had been paying attention. "No," she said. "I didn't always think that." She paused. "I learned it."

Chapter 38: Honor came back in the spring.

Not for good. Nothing in the shape of the war allowed for back for good. But she came to Manticore with the changed quality that the past several months had produced in her, the same quality Buffy had seen beginning when she'd returned from wherever the war had sent her in the autumn, but further along now, more settled.

She came to Buffy's quarters on the second evening and they sat in the way they sat and Nimitz went where Nimitz went and they talked.

Honor said: "The next several months are going to be significant. The strategic situation is�" she paused, choosing words. "Moving toward a resolution. Not a fast one. But directional."

Buffy looked at her. "How long?"

"Years," Honor said. "But the direction is clearer than it was." She paused. "I've been offered something. I'm going to take it." She paused. "I wanted to tell you before it becomes official."

"Tell me."

Honor told her.

She listened and when Honor finished she said: "That's what comes after."

"Part of it," Honor said. "The part that's mine to take." She held Buffy's gaze. "There's going to be a political restructuring. Not immediately. But the shape of what's coming in the next few years is going to change things." She paused. "The Vigil's formal recognition, when you're ready for it, is going to be possible in a way it might not have been in a different shape." She paused. "I wanted you to know."

Buffy thought about the institutional timeline. The Vigil in its current state was a year old and solid enough to be real and not yet solid enough for recognition, which was where she'd intended it to be. The timeline for recognition had been a variable she couldn't predict.

"Good," she said.

"Good?" Honor looked at her. "That's all?"

"That's all," she said. "I know where we are and I know what we need and I know the direction you're describing is the direction that makes it possible. That's what I needed to know." She held Honor's gaze. "The rest is work."

Honor looked at her for a moment with the layered expression.

"You've changed," she said.

"I've been here over a year," Buffy said. "Things were going to change."

"Not that way," Honor said. "Or not only that way." She paused. "When I met you on Hades you were�" she stopped. "You know what you were."

"Yes."

"You're not that now."

She thought about Hades and what she'd been and the specific distance between that and this. "No," she said. "I'm not."

"You're�" Honor paused. "Settled."

Scotty had used that word in Sphinx. She thought it was the right word. "Yes," she said.

"Settled in what?" Honor said. Not challenging. Genuinely asking.

She thought about it. "In what I'm doing and why," she said. "In where I am. In who's in it." She paused. "I spent two years in the previous century not quite settled in any of those things, doing the work anyway because the work was necessary whether I was settled or not." She paused. "Being settled makes it better."

"It makes the work better?"

"It makes the work and everything else better," she said. "I can't separate them anymore. The work is part of it and the people are part of it and the place is part of it." She paused. "I used to think you had to choose. The mission or the life." She looked at Nimitz, who was on Honor's shoulder with his green eyes doing what they did. "You don't have to choose. You have to build them together. The one makes the other possible."

Honor was quiet for a moment.

"I've been learning that too," she said. "In my own way."

Nimitz made a sound.

"Tell him I know," Buffy said.

Honor told him. His tail tip moved.

They sat for a while longer in the good silence of two people who had each other's context and didn't need to fill the space with anything it didn't require.

Buffy thought about the shape of the thing.

Not the immediate thing. The whole thing, the arc of it from the portal and Hades to here, from the feral clarity of the jungle to the trained and deliberate clarity of now. She thought about what it had taken to get from there to here and she let herself see it fully, the cost and the gift of it both.

She was here.

That was not nothing.

That was, as it kept turning out, quite a lot.

~~~

The Vigil's first formal gathering happened on a Thursday in late spring.

Not a ceremony. She'd been specific about not a ceremony. It was a working meeting, the kind of meeting that produced documents and decisions and assignments rather than the kind that produced photographs and speeches. Ceremonies came from institutions that had existed long enough to need them. The Vigil was not there yet and she intended to make it to that point before letting it become what it needed to become.

The people in the room were the people the Vigil currently was.

Carson, who had become its institutional memory and its archivist in the way that was consistent with the Clinkscales tradition and also something new, something that went beyond archiving into the kind of active coordination that the network required.

Fritz, who was its medical director and the most complete picture of the lineage's biology available anywhere.

Haverstock, who had come to Manticore six months ago and had not gone back to the university because the work here was better and because Prieto had followed him and together they were producing research that was already changing the theoretical framework in three separate scientific disciplines.

Usher, who was its intelligence arm and who had by this point absorbed the Vigil's requirements into her operational infrastructure so thoroughly that it was not clear whether the Vigil was using Usher's apparatus or Usher's apparatus was using the Vigil as a framework and she'd decided it didn't matter.

Castellan, who had taken the specific role of operational coordination in ways that her background made her precisely suited for and which no one else in the room could have filled.

Thomas Clinkscales, who had come to Manticore for the gathering with the expression of someone who had been waiting his entire adult life for exactly this room and was receiving it with the careful fullness of someone determined to receive it properly.

Yeva, Decker, and Priya, who were the first generation of the Vigil's trained lineage members and who were in the room as full participants, not observers, because she'd been specific about that too.

Dr. Tomas, from Beowulf, by remote link.

Lieutenant Chen, representing the Queen's office.

And Scotty, who was between postings and whose role in the Vigil was the role of someone who saw the whole of it and thought alongside her, which was not a formal role and was also the most important role in the room.

She stood at the front of the room and looked at all of them and thought about what she was looking at.

She'd built this.

Not alone. Not through any one decision or any one conversation. Through the accumulated specific work of a year and a half in a century she hadn't planned to be in, finding the people and the resources and the direction and doing the next piece every day until the pieces were a whole.

She looked at the room.

The Keeper was very present.

She said: "The Vigil exists to serve the lineage and through the lineage to serve the long work. The long work is not ours to complete. It is ours to advance." She paused. "We do not control the people in the lineage. We support them. We do not direct the research. We resource it. We do not manage the threat. We prepare for it." She paused. "The purpose of this meeting is to make sure that what we're doing is consistent with those principles and that we know what each of us is responsible for and that we have what we need to do it."

She looked around the room.

"Then we get back to work," she said. "Because the work is what we're for."

The meeting lasted three hours and produced what it was supposed to produce, which was clarity and assignments and the specific quality of people who know what they're doing and why moving forward with it.

Thomas Clinkscales cried once, briefly, during a discussion of the archive's integration into the Vigil's formal record-keeping. Yeva noticed and reached over and put her hand on his briefly without making anything of it.

Buffy noticed that too.

She filed it in the category of things that said the Vigil was becoming what it was supposed to be.

After the meeting she sat with Scotty in the room after everyone else had gone, the two of them at the table with the evidence of the working meeting around them.

He said: "Well."

She said: "Yes."

He said: "You did that."

She said: "We did that."

He held her gaze. "You did it first," he said. "The rest of us followed."

She thought about following and leading and the specific question of what you were doing when you were doing both at once. "I did the part I could do," she said. "Everyone else did the part they could do." She looked at the table. "That's how it's supposed to work."

He reached across the table and took her hand.

She looked at his hand holding hers and thought about the airport in Los Angeles when she'd been fourteen, before any of it, before the calling and the Hellmouth and the weight of the mission that had become her life. She'd held her mother's hand in the airport when they were going somewhere, she couldn't remember where. She'd been fourteen and distracted and had held the hand without noticing she was holding it.

She noticed now. She noticed the hand she was holding and who it was and where she was and what she'd built and what was ahead.

She noticed everything.

"Hey," she said.

He looked at her.

"Thank you," she said. "For being part of this."

He looked at her for a moment. "I'm not going anywhere," he said.

"I know," she said. "Thank you for that too."

He squeezed her hand.

She looked at the table and the room and thought about everything the room had held today and everything it was going to hold in the days ahead, all the work and all the people doing it and the long shape of the thing they were all part of.

She thought: this is mine.

She thought: yes.

She held his hand and let the room be quiet around them and the spring evening came in through the window with its Manticoran quality and she was here, in this place, in this life, and it was good.

It was very good.

Chapter 39: The letter from Allison arrived in summer.

It was long, which was characteristic of Allison in epistolary mode, and it was full of research updates that required concentration to follow and personal observations that required no concentration at all because they arrived with the directness that was Allison's mode in all registers.

The research update was this: the genetic analysis had reached the point of a complete map. Not just the modification but the full lineage genome, the specific sequence that every carrier shared and that varied only in its expression level. The map was the most detailed picture of what the lineage was, biologically, that had ever existed.

Allison's assessment of the map included a paragraph that Buffy read three times.

The paragraph said: the modification is not stable at the interface level in isolation. It requires something to maintain its coherence across generations. I have been working on what that maintenance requires and I believe the answer is the network. The connections between lineage members are not merely social or informational. There is a biological component. The channel functions better, maintains its calibration more precisely, when lineage members are in contact with each other. This is why the Vigil's work is not merely logistically useful. It is biologically necessary. The lineage maintains itself through connection.

She read that paragraph to Carson.

He was quiet for a long time.

Then he said: "That's why it survived."

"Yes," she said. "Not the archive. Not the Watchers' Council. The lineage itself. The connections between people who didn't know they were connected." She paused. "It maintained its calibration through contact that wasn't understood as contact."

"And now we're making the contact deliberate."

"And now we're making the contact deliberate," she said. "Which means the calibration gets better." She held his gaze. "Which means the fully expressed get better. And the channel in the subclinical carriers gets�" she paused, looking for the word.

"Clearer," he said.

"Clearer," she said. "Yes."

She wrote back to Allison immediately.

The letter she wrote back was full of questions that Allison was going to love and a question at the end that was different from the others.

The different question was: do you want to come to Manticore?

Not for a visit. The letter was specific about that. To stay. To be the Vigil's chief medical researcher, the person who continued the work in the place where the network was strongest because that was where the Vigil was.

She sent the letter and told Fritz what she'd asked and watched his face do the thing it did when he received news that required significant reorganization of his existing priorities and which he was going to reorganize around because the reorganization was correct.

"She'll say yes," Fritz said.

"I think so too," Buffy said.

"And Honor's mother coming to Manticore is going to�" he paused.

"Yes," she said. "Honor is going to have feelings about that."

"Good feelings," Fritz said, with the certainty of someone who had been paying close attention to the relevant people for long enough to have reliable data.

"Good feelings," she agreed.

~~~

She went back to Sphinx in the winter.

Not as a visit. She'd been thinking about what Scotty had said months ago, about the property and the northern range, and she'd been thinking about what the Vigil needed in the way of a place that was not the capital, not the institutional space of the Manticore system, but somewhere with the quality of the Tremaine property in autumn, the specific restorative quality of a place built for inhabitation rather than function.

The conversation about it happened on a Tuesday evening in her quarters with Scotty across the table and the city outside the window and the Keeper present.

She said: "I want to talk about the property."

He looked at her.

She said: "Not just for visits. For the work. Some of it." She paused. "Training. The physical and the other kind. The lineage candidates need a place that isn't an institution. A place that feels like somewhere they actually are rather than somewhere they're being processed." She paused. "Your family's property feels like that."

He was quiet for a moment, looking at her.

"That's the practical framing," he said.

"Yes," she said.

"What's the other framing?"

She held his gaze. "I want to be somewhere that's ours," she said. "Not the capital. Not the institutional spaces. Somewhere we chose because we chose it." She paused. "I want to plant a garden."

He held her gaze for a long time.

"Talk to my parents," he said. "When you're ready."

"Are you ready?"

"I've been ready," he said. "I was waiting for you to be."

She thought about the specific kind of patience that was not waiting-out but waiting-toward, the kind that was not passive but was the deliberate holding of a space for something to arrive in its own time.

She said: "I'm ready."

He said: "Then let's go to Sphinx."

~~~

The winter version of the Tremaine property was not the spectacular version. He'd warned her about this and she'd believed him and the believing had not prepared her for the specific quality of Sphinx in winter, which was a different kind of overwhelming.

The trees that had been spectacular in autumn were bare now, stripped down to their structural reality, the bones of things that had been full of color and were now the pure shape of themselves, and there was something in that she found beautiful in the way she hadn't expected, the beauty of things revealing what they were without the elaboration.

Snow. She hadn't been in snow before. Southern California and then this century's capital cities had not provided snow, and the snow that Sphinx produced in the northern range in winter was the kind that was not metaphorical, was not decorative, was the real thing in full weight.

She stood in it the first morning at six and let it fall on her and looked at the property under it and thought: yes. This.

She ran in it.

The running in snow was not the same as the running on the waterfront or the running in the gym or the ancient running of the Girl's dream-memories, but it was its own specific thing, the body working differently in a different medium, finding the adaptation that the adaptation required. She ran until she was warm from the inside in the specific way that cold-weather running produced and then she came back and her footprints were in the snow behind her and the property was the property and she was here.

She was here.

She ran her hands through a branch of the bare tree at the garden's edge and looked at the structure of it, the specific articulation of bark and smaller branch and where the growth had happened and where it hadn't, the whole decision-history of the tree visible in its winter architecture.

The treecat was in the canopy.

She'd known it was there before she saw it, the Keeper's orientation toward it the signal that had become reliable over the months since her first visit. It was looking down at her from a branch that gave it a clear angle and she looked up at it.

"Hi," she said.

It made the sound. The low questioning one that it had made the first time.

She answered it the same way she had before, by being present and readable.

It came lower.

Not all the way down. But lower, and when she turned back toward the house it came with her, moving through the canopy in the parallel way that treecats moved when they were accompanying rather than following.

She let it accompany her.

Scotty was at the kitchen window and he watched her come across the snowy garden with the treecat in the canopy above her and his face did something she didn't have a name for exactly.

She came in.

He said: "It's coming closer."

"I know," she said. "I'm not pushing it."

"I know you're not." He handed her coffee that she turned into hot chocolate by the process she'd developed which involved a packet from the supply she'd brought from the capital. "Sascha thinks it's been watching the property for a year."

"It has," she said. "Since the first visit."

"And it's coming closer."

"Yes." She looked out the window at the canopy. "I don't know what that means yet. I'm letting it tell me."

He looked at her with the expression he had when he was thinking about her rather than about the subject of the conversation. "Okay," he said.

She drank her chocolate and looked at the winter and let it be what it was.

Full. Specific. The bones of the trees and the snow and the treecat in the canopy and the kitchen and the man beside her and the work ahead and the life they were building in this place from available materials.

This was the life.

Not the one she'd planned. Not the one she'd lost. The one she'd built from what was here when she arrived, in this century, in this place, with these people.

The Keeper was present.

The Girl ran somewhere at the edge of awareness, patient and ancient and simply continuing as it had always continued.

She was here.

She thought: good.

She drank her chocolate and watched the winter and let it be good.

Chapter 40: The treecat's name, when it chose to have one, was something she couldn't hear directly.

Treecats named themselves in their own language, the complex combination of sound and empathic communication that humans couldn't fully perceive, and what humans called the naming was the approximation, the nearest thing in human sound to whatever the treecat was expressing about itself. Nimitz's name was the human word that came closest to whatever Nimitz communicated about himself, and over the months of her relationship with the treecat in the Tremaine property's canopy she'd asked enough people who knew enough about treecats to have a working sense of the process.

She asked Honor on a Tuesday in late winter when Honor was briefly on Manticore for reasons the war still required.

Honor said: "They choose. The human approximation of the name is usually something the treecat finds acceptable. The communication goes both directions." She paused. "What are you getting from it?"

Buffy thought about the quality the treecat had when it was most directly communicating, which was more frequent now that the approach had become a regular feature of the Sphinx visits. "Something that's�" she looked for the word� "like watching. But not passive watching. Watching that's also participating." She paused. "Like a vigil."

Honor looked at her.

Buffy looked back.

"Vigil," she said again, testing the weight of it as a name.

Nimitz, from Honor's shoulder, made a sound that was not his usual sound, something different, a note she didn't have a name for.

"What is that?" she said.

"He's�" Honor paused. "He says that's right."

She thought about the treecat in the canopy who had been watching her property and watching her for a year, who had followed her across the snowy garden, who came closer each visit and received her reading each time she offered it, and she thought about the word vigil and the thing she'd built and the quality the treecat expressed when it was most directly present.

"Vigil," she said. To the canopy and to the room and to the Keeper and to the sense of the lineage running through her.

The treecat in her memory of the most recent morning came through the channel at the specific angle of recognition.

"Yes," she said. "Okay. Vigil."

Honor looked at her with the layered expression. "You've been bonded," she said.

"I don't think that's what's happening."

"I don't think you get to decide what's happening," Honor said. Not unkindly. With the precision of someone who had direct experience. "The treecat decides."

She thought about this for a long moment. She thought about what it meant, the specific relationship she'd read about in the briefings she'd done on her first weeks on Manticore, the bond that treecats chose and that was understood in Sphinx culture as one of the most significant things that could happen between a person and a treecat.

She thought about the Keeper, which was a consciousness that accompanied her through the channel. She thought about Nimitz, who was Honor's and whose bond was visible in the quality of everything Honor did.
