Honor wrote back from Manticore, two days later because the courier lag was what it was, a letter that said: I've already been thinking about it. Tolman arrived. We've been talking. There are things you need to know when you get back.

She thought about what things and thought about the patience required for when you get back and kept reading.

~~~

Manticore from approach was still home.

She thought she'd stop noticing this at some point, the specific quality of that recognition, and she hadn't. Two years and it was still there, the approach to the system producing the feeling of return that she'd been working toward since she'd first arrived in this century.

She didn't try to make it stop. She let it be what it was.

Scotty was at the docking facility.

She'd known he would be. He'd said he would be in his last letter and Scotty said what he meant. But the knowing hadn't prepared her for the specific quality of seeing him there, the way the knowing and the seeing were different things, the way return was its own experience regardless of how thoroughly you'd prepared for it.

She walked through the hatch and he was there.

They held on to each other for a moment in the middle of the docking facility with the movement of it around them and she thought about three weeks and about the camp on Hades and about all the distances and how this specific moment was the accumulation of all of them.

Then he pulled back and looked at her.

He said: "Tell me you're all right."

She said: "I'm all right."

He held her gaze. He was doing the read, the same read she did on the people she was responsible for, the specific assessment of actually rather than conventionally all right.

He said: "Yes. You are."

"I said I was," she said.

"I know," he said. "I like confirming it."

She looked at him.

She thought about the very old vampire in the communications room on the Eirene Constantia who had said you're very young and meant it as something more than a comment about age. She thought about what she'd been when she came through the portal and what she was now, standing in the Manticore docking facility being held by someone who had confirmed she was all right.

She was all right.

She was considerably more than all right.

"Come on," she said. "You said you had things to tell me."

"The things can wait until we're home," he said.

"They can," she said. "But tell me on the way."

He told her on the way.

The things were several things, some operational and some personal and some in the category of things that were both simultaneously.

The operational things were about Honor's conversations with Tolman and what Tolman had, which was considerably more than Buffy had understood from the brief exchange in Larise's facility. Not just Havenite naval infrastructure knowledge. Relationships inside Haven's intelligence apparatus that went back fifteen years. A network that was the Havenite equivalent of what Usher ran on the Manticoran side, oriented toward the same general direction of things-that-existed-outside-the-official-framework.

Honor had been talking to Tolman for two weeks and what she'd assembled from those conversations was a picture of a parallel institutional awareness on the Haven side that had been operating in isolation from the Manticoran equivalent for the full duration of the war.

Two sides of the same work, unable to coordinate because the politics that surrounded them had made coordination impossible.

The war ending was going to change that.

The personal things were about the Tremaine property.

His parents had been back. She'd known they were going to be back eventually and had been in a steady state of mild awareness that the meeting with his family was coming, not dreading it but knowing it was there in the way of knowing that something significant was in your near future.

She said: "Tell me about the visit."

He said: "They were on the property for two weeks. Sascha had briefed them, I think, more than she told me she had, because they knew things about the garden that I hadn't mentioned." He paused. "My mother asked to see your letters."

She looked at him. "My letters."

"I said no," he said. "Obviously. But the asking told me something about where she was."

"Where was she?"

"Wanting to know who you are," he said. "Which the letters would have told her but which is not how that question gets answered." He paused. "My father asked me if I was sure."

"What did you say?"

"I said yes," he said. "Without qualification. He didn't ask anything else."

She held this.

She thought about what sure meant in the context and what the yes meant and what the without qualification said about both of them.

"Okay," she said.

"They want to meet you," he said. "Not here. The property. They want to meet you on the property."

She thought about that. "That's the right place."

"I thought so too."

They were almost home, the city around them in its familiar configuration, the streets she knew by now, the waterfront visible from the transit route with its harbor water. She looked at it as they passed.

"When?" she said.

"Whenever you're ready," he said. "They're not putting a deadline on it."

She thought about ready and what that meant.

She said: "Autumn."

He looked at her.

"The spectacular version," she said. "I want to meet them when the property is at its best." She paused. "Also I need a month to be back before I think about the next significant thing."

"A month," he said.

"Minimum," she said. "I've been away for six weeks including transit. The Vigil needs my attention. Honor has things to tell me. Allison has research updates. Haverstock has results from the expanded mapping array." She paused. "I want a month of here before I think about the next thing that requires me to be fully present for something that isn't the immediate work."

He looked at her with the expression he had when he was thinking about her rather than what she was saying. "You've been doing that," he said. "The month. The space before the next significant thing."

"Learning to," she said. "It's better for everything."

"Yes," he said. "It is."

They were home.

She looked at the building that was home and thought: yes. Here.

She went up.

~~~

The month was the month she'd needed it to be.

She went back to the Vigil's daily work with the specific energy of someone who had been away long enough to appreciate return and not so long that the return required adjustment. She worked with Yeva and the new candidates and the institutional infrastructure and the diplomatic work that Honor was running and all of it.

She met Eris Vandamme properly, by communicator first and then in person when Eris made the transit to Manticore, which she did with the specific quality of someone who had been in the same place for eleven months and found the transit easy by comparison.

Eris was different in person from what the brief meeting on Barnett had conveyed. Calmer. The residue of eleven months of intensive work still on her but the acute version of it faded, replaced by something more settled, the look of someone who had found the thing they were supposed to be doing and was continuing to do it in conditions that allowed doing rather than surviving.

The accompanying presence was with her in the way Vigil was with Buffy, the bond's ambient quality. She felt it immediately and it felt, through the Keeper's read, like the Keeper felt when it encountered something in the same register.

She said to Eris: "Ask it what it would like to be called. For our reference."

Eris communicated this. She was quiet for a moment and then she said: "It says it doesn't use names the way we do. It says if you need a reference it accepts Witness."

She held that.

Witness and Keeper. She thought about the words and what they described and what the described things had in common, which was the long watching that the Constant and the Vigil and apparently others like them had been doing in their various ways across the explored systems.

She said: "Tell Witness we're glad it came."

Eris communicated this. She was quiet for a moment and then she said: "It says it was going to come eventually. Your Keeper told it to be patient."

She looked at where the Keeper was present.

The Keeper's quality was entirely unapologetic.

"I see," she said.

Allison, who was present because Allison had requested to be present for this meeting with the thoroughness of someone who was going to want to study whatever it produced, said: "Did your Keeper know about Witness before Barnett?"

She checked.

The Keeper's quality was the quality of something that had known and had been waiting for the right moment.

"Yes," she said.

"And it didn't tell you because�"

"Because I didn't ask the right question yet," she said. "And because the Keeper's communication is improving but it's not complete." She paused. "And probably because the timing of finding out mattered."

Allison made a note.

Eris said: "Witness says that's correct. The timing mattered."

She held this.

She thought about the Constant and the message through the founding documents and the eleven hundred years of slow work and about patience as a mode of operation for things that existed at a different timescale than the things they were working with. She thought about what patience looked like from the inside of that timescale versus from the inside of a human lifetime.

She thought: they see us the way we see mayflies. Not dismissively. With care, the way you cared about things that were brief and real.

She thought: we see them the way mayflies might see a river. Something too large to fully hold, that we move through in the portion of it available to us.

She thought: the long work requires both. The patience of the river and the urgency of the mayfly. That's why it needs both of us.

She said to Eris: "Tell Witness I want it to be part of the Vigil. Not formally. Not institutionally. But in communication. The same way the Constant is." She paused. "If it's willing."

Eris communicated this.

She said: "It says yes. It says it has information about three other presences that are doing similar work. It's willing to facilitate introductions."

She held that.

Three others. Which meant at least five total, counting the Constant and Witness. And the network effect of five presences at the dimensional interface communicating and coordinating, the specific power of that, what it could mean for the Vigil's ability to understand what was coming and prepare for it.

She thought: we are so much less alone than we thought.

She thought: that changes things.

She said: "Yes. Please."

Chapter 46: The expanded mapping array, when Haverstock presented his full results, produced a picture that required several hours of presentation and several more hours of processing before she could hold it.

The room for the presentation was the Vigil's largest working space, which had been the original residence room and had expanded with the Vigil's expansion and which now had the quality of a place that serious things happened in regularly, the accumulated texture of work.

Everyone relevant was there. Fritz and Allison and Prieto and Carson and Honor and Scotty and Castellan and Yeva, who had become the most consistent presence in the Vigil's daily work besides Buffy herself.

Haverstock presented for two hours.

The mapping array, expanded across the systems it could now reach, was showing a picture of the dimensional interface that was unprecedented in its resolution and scope. The patterns she'd learned to recognize, the Constant's signature and Witness's signature and the two others that Witness had introduced her to since the Barnett meeting, were visible in the map as distinct stable presences at the interface, each with its own character and its own location.

Five, confirmed. More that the array hadn't yet reached, suggested by the communication patterns between the known five.

Not all of them were working through channels the way the Constant had worked through Yuen Sharra. Not all of them were working alongside a human the way Witness worked alongside Eris. Each had its own approach, consistent with what Eris had said about Witness's description of itself: more mobile, different method, same direction.

What Haverstock had found in addition to the presence signatures was something that took longer to explain.

The interface itself was not static.

She'd understood it as a fixed boundary, a stable dimensional edge that the presences inhabited and worked through. What the expanded mapping was showing was that the interface had its own activity, independent of the presences, a slow and large-scale movement that was happening across the full reach of the array.

Not threatening movement. Not the kind of activity that the thing that made the Hellmouth had produced in the geological record she'd found in the Constant's knowledge. Something different.

She said: "What kind of movement?"

Haverstock said: "The closest analogy I have is tidal. Something is causing a periodic variation in the interface's boundary position across a very large area." He paused. "The period is long enough that human observation wouldn't have detected it without the specific instruments we're now running." He paused. "And the direction of the variation is�interesting."

He put the map on the display.

The variation was toward human space.

Not in the threatening sense of something pressing in. In the sense of something that was moving toward, the way weather moved, the way ocean currents moved, large and slow and present regardless of the intentions of anything it encountered.

She sat with this.

She said: "Is it natural?"

Haverstock said: "I don't know yet. We don't have a baseline long enough to know whether this is part of the interface's natural dynamic or something else." He paused. "The presences know about it. I asked Witness through Eris." He paused. "Witness says it's not natural. It's being caused."

The room was quiet in a specific way.

She said: "By the thing that made the Hellmouth."

Haverstock said: "That's what Witness says. It doesn't call it that. The name it uses I can't render in phonetics. But yes. The cause is the long-patient thing that the record describes."

She held this.

The geological timescale thing was doing something to the interface. Not the activation she'd been reading as the eventual threat in the record, not the main event. Something preparatory. The way you prepared ground before you built something in it.

She thought about the record and the timeline and what several centuries meant in the timescale she was working with.

She said: "How long has this variation been happening?"

Witness, through Eris who was present by communicator, said approximately forty years.

She thought about the vampire network and the Eirene Constantia's forty-year established route and about what established something in a system forty years ago and what they'd been doing since.

She said: "The network. The vampires. The infrastructure they've been building."

Honor, who had been quiet for the presentation, said: "The variation in the interface makes certain things easier."

She looked at her.

Honor said: "If the interface is moving toward human space, if the boundary is becoming thinner in some meaningful way, then the things that work through the interface�" she paused� "would find it easier to establish and maintain operations."

"Yes," Buffy said. "That's it." She looked at the map. "The network isn't incidental. It's preparation. The forty years of it are forty years of establishing infrastructure that will be more effective as the interface variation progresses."

She looked at the map.

She thought about geological patience and about forty years and about what several centuries looked like as a preparation timeline when you had the kind of patience the long-patient thing had.

She said: "How long until the variation reaches the level where the interface is thin enough to matter?"

Haverstock and Witness, working through Eris, conferred via the communicator display in a way that was entirely strange and entirely practical, the physicist and the presence at the interface doing math together through a human intermediary.

The answer took seven minutes.

Haverstock said: "At the current rate? Approximately ninety years."

Ninety years.

Not several centuries. Not the comfortable long distance of the record's timeline.

Ninety years.

The room processed this.

She held it.

Ninety years was still outside the lifetimes of most of the people currently in the room. But it was in the lifetimes of the candidates she was training. In the childhood of people alive right now. A thing that was going to happen within the span of known and predictable futures rather than the abstract long distant future of the record.

She said: "The timeline has changed."

Nobody in the room said anything that wasn't true.

Honor said: "What do we do?"

She held her gaze for a moment.

She said: "The same things we were doing. Faster."

~~~

The month she'd asked for became two weeks because the timeline changed what was urgent, and the urgent things needed her specifically rather than the delegation she'd been developing.

She worked without stopping for two weeks.

Not the way she'd worked before she'd learned to pace herself, the burning kind of work that consumed everything in range. The better version, the sustainable version, the work that used what was available without exceeding it, that trusted the people around her to carry what they could carry.

She worked and the Vigil worked with her.

The operational pace of the network search increased. The contact rate for lineage candidates went from the careful measured pace of the first two years to something faster, the expansion of the search into Haven's systems moving ahead of the diplomatic timeline on the grounds that the candidates were there regardless of whether the diplomacy had caught up.

Tolman's network on the Havenite side began to function in coordination with Usher's network on the Manticoran side in the specific way of two professional operations that had been working toward the same things in parallel and were now exchanging what they'd developed independently.

The results were significant.

The Havenite network had found things that Usher hadn't found. The Manticoran network had found things the Havenite network hadn't found. The combination produced a picture of the distributed vampire infrastructure across human space that was considerably more complete than either side had assembled alone.

She and Honor and Usher spent a full day with the combined picture.

She came out of that day with a list of seventeen active nodes across explored space.

Seventeen.

She was two and a half years into the Vigil's operational work. She'd addressed seven nodes. There were seventeen more.

She held the list.

She thought about the ninety years.

She thought about the pace of what was possible with the Vigil's current operational capacity versus the pace of what was necessary given the timeline.

She thought about Yeva.

She went to find Yeva.

Yeva was in the training space with two of the newer candidates, the specific focused quality of someone who had become good enough at something to teach it without losing their own edge in the teaching. She looked up when Buffy came in and something in her expression changed, the read she'd developed on Buffy's quality when she arrived somewhere.

She said to the candidates: "Take a break."

They took a break.

She looked at Buffy.

Buffy said: "I need to tell you something."

Yeva said: "Tell me."

She told her.

The timeline. The seventeen nodes. The operational pace required versus the operational capacity current. The math of what she had and what the math needed.

Yeva listened.

When Buffy finished Yeva was quiet for a moment.

She said: "You're going to need more of me."

Buffy held her gaze. "Yes."

She said: "Define more."

Buffy said: "Operational lead. Not supporting. Leading. With your own team, with Castellan's support structure, working nodes that I assign based on assessment of your capability and the node's character." She paused. "Not the highest-complexity nodes yet. The ones that are in the tier below. While I continue to address the highest-complexity ones."

Yeva held her gaze.

She said: "You said yet."

"I said yet," Buffy confirmed. "Your ceiling is not visible to me from where you currently are. I'm not putting a ceiling on this conversation."

Yeva was quiet for a longer moment.

She said: "Two years ago you sat across from me in my family's apartment and told me you weren't going to ask me to do anything with what I am."

"Yes," Buffy said.

"And now you're asking."

"Yes," Buffy said. "The situation has changed. The timeline has changed." She held Yeva's gaze. "I'm asking. Not requiring. Not obligating. Asking. You can say no. You can say yes with conditions. You can say yes." She paused. "I need your answer to be yours."

Yeva looked at her for a long moment with the quality she had when she was thinking about something completely, taking it in at every level simultaneously.

She said: "Can I have a day?"

"Yes," Buffy said.

"Then I'll have an answer tomorrow."

"Okay," Buffy said.

She left the training space and went back to her own work and the day continued around her with its pace and its requirements and its specific texture of being in the middle of something that was always moving.

Vigil, she felt through the bond, was present in the way it was present when she was working hard, the specific accompanying quality of something that was alongside rather than assisting, the companionship of a consciousness that didn't do the work but was there while the work was done.

She thought about Nimitz and Honor, about what Honor had described as the thing the bond provided, which was the knowledge of being fully seen and not alone in the seeing.

She thought: yes. That's it exactly.

She worked until eight in the evening and then she went home and Scotty was there and dinner was there and the Sphinx autumn was coming in two weeks for the meeting with his parents and all of it was the shape of the life.

She ate and she told him about the timeline and the list and Yeva and he listened and when she finished he said: "Ninety years."

"Yes."

"Not comfortable."

"No," she said. "But workable. If we do the work."

He held her gaze. "You'll do the work."

"We'll do the work," she said. "All of us."

He held her gaze.

He said: "I've been thinking about my next posting."

She looked at him.

He said: "The Vigil's operational work is going to expand significantly. The pace you're describing is going to require coordination and logistics and support capacity that the current structure doesn't fully have." He paused. "I've been thinking that what I can contribute in that direction is more useful than another ship deployment right now." He paused. "If you'd use it."

She held his gaze.

She thought about what he was offering, which was specific and generous and which she needed and which she'd been aware of needing without knowing how to ask for.

She said: "You'd need to talk to the navy."

"I've been talking to the navy for a month," he said. "The framework exists. I wanted to know if you wanted it before I made the request formal."

She looked at him.

She thought about two and a half years and the Vigil and the work and the people in it and him specifically, the person who saw the whole of it and thought alongside her.

She said: "Yes. I want it."

He said: "Then I'll make it formal."

She looked at the dinner and the table and the city outside the window and the life that was here and being built and continuing to build.

She said: "Thank you."

He said: "I'm not going anywhere."

She looked at him.

She thought: he keeps saying that and it keeps being true and I keep being glad of it.

She thought: that's the shape of it. That's what the after looks like.

She ate her dinner.

Tomorrow there was Yeva's answer and the expanded network coordination and the operational planning for the accelerated timeline and all the work that the work was.

Tonight there was this.

She held both and they held.

Yeva's answer came in the morning, not by letter or communicator but in person, Yeva appearing at her office door at seven with the specific quality of someone who had done the thinking and arrived at the other side of it.

She said: "Yes. With one condition."

Buffy said: "Tell me the condition."

Yeva said: "The newer candidates. The ones I've been working with. I want to keep that work. Not instead of the operational role. Alongside it." She held Buffy's gaze. "I don't want to become someone who only operates. I want to be someone who also builds what comes after."

Buffy held her gaze for a moment.

She thought about herself at seventeen, before the Council, before Merrick, before any of it, and she thought about what she'd needed and hadn't had.

She thought about what she'd said to Yeva two years ago: I'm not going to ask you to do anything with what you are.

She thought about the condition Yeva was setting and what it said about who Yeva was becoming.

She said: "That's not a condition. That's required." She held Yeva's gaze. "The Vigil builds and operates simultaneously. Anyone who does one and not the other is doing half the work." She paused. "I should have been clearer about that when I asked."

Yeva looked at her.

She said: "Okay. Yes."

Buffy said: "Yes."

They held each other's gaze for a moment in the specific way of something being settled between two people who both understood what was being settled.

Then Yeva said: "When do we start?"

Buffy said: "Now."

They started.

Chapter 47: The Sphinx autumn came.

She was on the property when it did, which meant she saw the transition from late summer to the beginning of the spectacular version, the specific days when the trees were starting to decide and the decision was visible in them before it was complete, the first hints of color in the higher canopy while the lower leaves were still the summer green.

She was in the garden on one of those days when Scotty's parents arrived.

Not dramatically. An air car came down in the field adjacent to the property that served as a landing area and two people got out of it with the ease of people returning to somewhere they knew well, and then they saw her in the garden and stopped.

She stopped too.

They looked at her and she looked at them across the space of the garden in the early autumn light.

His mother was small, dark-haired going gray, with a look that was specific and kind in the way of people whose kindness was a practice rather than a mood. His father was taller, with the bearing of someone who had served and whose service was in how they held themselves rather than on display, and with eyes that were doing the same calibration that Scotty's eyes did when they were assessing something they cared about.

She crossed the garden.

His mother said, when she was close enough: "You're Buffy."

She said: "Yes."

His mother looked at her for a moment.

Then she said: "Carson told us about you."

She looked at her. "He's talked to you."

"He came to Sphinx two months ago," Scotty's father said. "He came to tell us about the Vigil. About what it is and what it does and what you've built." He held her gaze. "He didn't ask us to think well of you. He just told us the truth about you and let us reach our own conclusions."

She thought about Carson, who had said that seeing someone clearly and honestly was how you helped them be known. She thought about what it meant that he'd done this for her without telling her he was going to.

She was going to have to write him a letter about this.

She looked at Scotty's parents.

She said: "What conclusions did you reach?"

His father held her gaze for a moment.

He said: "That our son chose well."

She held this.

It arrived with the specific weight of something that had been waiting for delivery, the thing that parents of people you loved could give that nobody else could give, the specific recognition that was theirs to offer.

She said: "Thank you."

His mother said: "Come inside. I want to see what you've done with the garden before the light changes."

She came inside.

His mother walked the garden with the specific attention of a person who knew gardens and was reading this one, the decisions in it, the things planted for function and the things planted for other reasons and the way the two intermingled.

She stopped at the rosemary equivalent.

She said: "This isn't Sphinx."

"No," Buffy said. "It's a Grayson variety. The closest equivalent I could find to something I grew up with."

His mother looked at it. She touched one of the stems and rubbed the leaves between her fingers and smelled it.

She said: "It's close."

"Close enough," Buffy said.

His mother looked at her. Something in her face was doing the assessment that the Vigil made Buffy think of as the slow kind, the kind that came from living alongside someone rather than looking at them directly.

She said: "Scott told us about the work. What it is. The long work." She paused. "He explained it well. I want you to know that I understand what you're doing and why and that I think it matters."

Buffy held her gaze.

"Thank you," she said.

"Don't thank me," his mother said. "It's just accurate." She looked at the garden. "What else are you planting?"

They talked about the garden for an hour.

Not about the Vigil or the timeline or the operational work or any of the things that were the visible content of her days. About the garden, which was the other content, the underneath content, the soil of a life rather than its superstructure.

His mother had opinions about companion planting. She had opinions about the specific Sphinx winter challenges that came with the elevation they were at. She had the particular directness of someone who had been making things grow in difficult conditions for decades and who found the conversation about it more interesting than most other conversations.

Buffy found it interesting too.

She thought: this is what the after looks like. This specific thing, in this garden, with this person who loves someone I love, talking about what grows.

She thought: yes.

The afternoon moved through its stages and became evening and the spectacular version was continuing to begin in the canopy around them, the decision spreading from tree to tree, and Vigil was present in the canopy in the way Vigil was always present when she was on the property, the bond's ambient quality, the knowledge of accompanying.

Scotty came out of the house at some point and stood at the garden's edge watching her and his mother and not interrupting, and when she caught his eye he had the expression she had no precise name for, the one that was several good things at once.

She held that.

She held all of it.

The garden in the autumn and the wrong stars that were hers and the treecat in the canopy and the man at the garden's edge and his mother telling her something useful about the soil composition at this elevation and the Keeper present at the channel and the Vigil in its collected distributed reality doing the work that was theirs to do.

She was here.

She was building something.

She was, as it had kept turning out every time she let herself look at it directly, exactly where she was supposed to be.

Not supposed to in the sense of destiny or the sense of design, not the Council's sense or the sense of any authority external to herself. Supposed to in the sense of having made the choices that brought her here and finding that the choices were right. Supposed to in the sense of this specific fit between who she was and what she was doing.

She'd spent a long time not having that fit.

She had it now.

She let herself have it.

She stood in the garden in the Sphinx autumn and let herself have the life she'd built and the people in it and the work and the wrong stars and the treecat and all of it, fully and without the reservation she'd learned to hold things with, the protective distance that had kept her from being too disappointed if they went away.

Some things went away.

Some things stayed.

This was staying.

She let it stay.

She turned back to the conversation about soil composition and was present for it and the evening came in around them and it was the Sphinx autumn and it was home and it was good.

It was very, very good.

Chapter 48: The war ended on a Thursday.

Not with drama, not from her vantage point. She was in the Vigil's working space with Yeva going over the operational plan for the next node when the message arrived and Scotty appeared in the doorway with the expression that said something significant had happened and he wanted to tell her in person rather than send it through the communicator.

She looked at him.

He said: "The armistice."

She said: "When?"

He said: "Two hours ago. The signing was at noon."

She held this.

Yeva, beside her, was quiet in the way of someone processing news that had been expected and that still arrived with its own weight regardless of how long you'd been expecting it.

She thought about the war and what it had been and what it had cost and what the ending of it meant, not for the Vigil specifically but in the full human accounting of what wars were and what ending them was.

She thought about Honor, who had been part of what produced the ending and who was going to have feelings about this that were complicated in the specific way of people who had given significant portions of themselves to something and were now on the other side of it.

She said to Scotty: "Where's Honor?"

He said: "On her way here."

"Good," she said.

To Yeva: "We'll pick this up tomorrow."

Yeva said: "Go. I've got it."

She went.

Honor arrived an hour later.

Not with the quality of someone who had been celebrating. With the quality of someone who had been doing many things simultaneously for several hours and had needed to get to this specific place to stop doing them.

She scratched between his ears.

He went back to Honor.

She looked at Honor.

She said: "How are you?"

Before the Prince Adrian. The whole shape of what I was doing was aimed at this." She paused. "Now it's here and I don't know yet what comes next because the next hasn't arrived yet and I'm in the space between."

"The in-between is real," Buffy said. "You don't have to know what it is yet."

Nimitz made a sound.

They sat in the quiet of the room with the city outside doing whatever the city did on the day a war ended, which was probably complicated, various people processing the same news in the various ways that news was processed, some of it relief and some of it grief and some of it the numb in-between that came before you knew what you felt.

Buffy held her gaze.

Honor said: "I know."

The kind of institution that served the work rather than the work serving the institution." She paused. "You understand that distinction. You've been living on the wrong side of it for three years and you know the difference." She paused. "That's what the Vigil needs. People who know the difference from the inside."

Honor held her gaze.

She said: "Thank you."

Buffy said: "Don't�"
