She held this.

She let it be received.

Nimitz made the sound he made when something was what it should be.

She looked at him.

She thought: yes.

~~~

The weeks after the armistice were not quiet but they were different.

The work was the same work and the pace was the accelerated pace and the timeline was what it was. But the political context around the work shifted in ways that changed what was possible.

Haven's system was no longer the other side of a war.

Haven's reform movement, which had been the connection through Tolman and Larise, was moving into a position of political relevance that the armistice had created. The people who had been trying to change Haven from within were now in the process of actually changing Haven from within, with the specific challenges and opportunities of doing that in the immediate post-war period.

Honor's diplomatic work became more complex and more productive simultaneously, which was the specific character of post-war political environments that she hadn't experienced before but that she read in the quality of Honor's working days, more dense but with more traction.

The Vigil's formal recognition was still Manticoran. The bilateral recognition, the cross-systemic version that she'd been thinking about since the early institutional conversations, was now possible in a way it hadn't been while the war was ongoing.

She had a conversation with the Queen about this on a Tuesday afternoon with the spring outside the window and the Vigil's second year well underway.

She said: "The Vigil needs to be seen as neither Manticoran nor Havenite. Not affiliated with any political structure that was part of the war."

The Queen said: "The Vigil was established in Manticore and I signed its charter."

"I know," she said. "That's the institutional reality and it's not going away. But the Vigil's mandate extends across every system the lineage has reached, which includes Haven and the League and every sovereign entity in explored space." She held the Queen's gaze. "The institutional structure needs to reflect that eventually. A board that includes Haven. A charter revision that makes the political neutrality explicit." She paused. "Not immediately. The relationships need to exist before the structure can reflect them." She paused. "But direction matters."

The Queen held her gaze.

She said: "You're asking me to build something that doesn't report to me."

"I'm asking you to build something that serves something larger than either of us," she said. "Which is what you've been doing since you agreed to help with this." She held the Queen's gaze. "You've been building toward this from the beginning. This is just the next step."

The Queen was quiet for a long moment.

Ariel was very still.

She said: "All right." She paused. "I'll have the charter revision drafted. And I'll open the conversation with the Haven contacts through Honor." She paused. "The League is going to be more difficult."

"The League is going to be more difficult," Buffy agreed. "The League is always more difficult. We'll get there when we get there."

The Queen almost smiled.

"You've become very patient," she said.

"I've been learning from things that measure time in geological epochs," she said. "Some of it rubs off."

The Queen's expression did the thing it did.

She said: "Good."

~~~

Thomas Clinkscales came to Manticore in the summer.

Not for an occasion. His health had been the reason Carson had given in the careful way that Carson gave reasons for things he didn't want to frame as urgency, but she'd read the frame and had written back to say she'd make the time and had meant it.

He was eighty-six now and had the quality that some eighty-six-year-olds had, the specific compression of long life into a presence that was not diminished but was concentrated, as though the years had burned away everything that wasn't essential and what remained was the essential in full.

He came to her quarters on the second day with Carson beside him and sat down across from her and looked at her with the clear dark eyes.

She looked back.

He said: "I have something for you."

Carson reached into the bag he was carrying and took out a case that she recognized as the archival kind, the hard-sided kind that protected what was inside from the physical processes of time.

She looked at it.

She said: "What's in it?"

Thomas said: "The first document. The one my great-grandfather's grandfather wrote. The one that began the archive." He paused. "I want you to have it."

She held his gaze.

She said: "This should stay in the Clinkscales archive."

He said: "The Clinkscales archive is the Vigil's archive now. It belongs to the same record." He held her gaze. "And the first document belongs with the person who made the record true." He paused. "Carson made a copy. The copy will be in the archive. The original is yours."

She looked at the case.

She said: "Why?"

He said: "Because you are the proof." He said it without the weight of revelation, simply as fact. "The first entry says: I keep this record because something told me it was necessary and I could not say no to the telling. Four generations of my family kept it without knowing what they were keeping it for." He held her gaze. "We kept it for you. It should be with you."

She held this for a long moment.

She thought about the lineage and the record and the four generations of Clinkscaleses keeping faith with something they couldn't see the full shape of, the specific quality of that faith, which was not blind but was patient in a way that was its own kind of sight.

She reached out and took the case.

She said: "Thank you."

He said: "Don't thank me yet."

She looked at him.

He almost smiled, the specific quality of the expression.

She said: "You sound like the Queen."

He said: "The Queen is a wise person." He paused. "Thank me when you've passed it on to the next person it belongs with."

She held the case.

She held his gaze.

She thought about passing things on and what that meant and who the next person would be and what the passing would look like.

She thought: I'll know when I see them.

She said: "I will."

He nodded once.

They sat in the room for a while longer and she asked him about the archive and what he thought it needed that it didn't have, and he told her with the precision of someone who had been thinking about a specific question for thirty years and had the accumulated specific answer. She took notes. She was going to do something with the notes.

When he left she held the case in her hands for a long time.

Then she put it on the desk alongside the document she'd been building, the record that was growing, and the founding document of the Biological Survey Institute with toward the light between underlined, and the Clinkscales archive copies that Carson had been providing.

The desk held the full shape of it.

She looked at it.

She thought: this is what the work looks like. Not the operations. Not the diplomatic conversations. This. The records and the documents and the lineage of people who kept the knowledge and passed it forward and did the piece they could do so the next piece was possible.

She thought about the document she was writing and the people who would eventually read it and what she wanted them to have that the previous versions of this record hadn't provided.

She wrote:

The record you are reading exists because people kept it when there was no guarantee anyone would read it. That is the first thing I want you to know. Not because it is heroic, though it is, but because it is the shape of the work. The work happens before anyone can see why it mattered. You do it anyway. The mattering comes later, and when it comes, it is in full.

She looked at what she'd written.

She wrote more.

She wrote for three hours and the day moved outside the window and the Keeper was present and Vigil was somewhere in Sphinx doing what treecats did in the summer, and the record accumulated forward.

Chapter 49: The third year of the Vigil had a different quality from the first two.

The first year had been building. The second year had been expanding. The third year had the quality of something that was running, not in the sense of running away but in the sense of a thing in its proper motion, doing what it was built to do at the pace it was built to run.

She noticed this about the way she noticed the change of seasons on Sphinx, by living in it rather than by watching from outside.

The lineage network stood at forty-seven confirmed candidates across the Alliance and eight in Haven's systems, with Tolman's network having expanded the search in ways that were producing candidates faster than she'd projected. Seventeen of the confirmed candidates were in regular contact with the Vigil's support structure. Nine were in active training at various stages. Three were operational.

Yeva had led six node operations.

All successful. Two of them in complexity ranges that Buffy wouldn't have considered assigning a year ago and which Yeva had handled with the specific capability of someone whose ceiling was still not visible.

Decker had been deployed twice solo, which was a threshold she marked because solo operations were the ones where structural soundness was the only thing between you and what the operation produced, and Decker's structural soundness had proved in both cases to be exactly what she'd assessed it was.

Priya was in a different track, the research track, working with Allison and Haverstock on the channel activation question that had begun with Allison's discovery in the second year. The work was producing results that Allison described as unprecedented in a way that said she meant the word literally rather than as professional enthusiasm.

The channel in subclinical carriers was activating in a measurable pattern that correlated with network density, time in the network, and something else that Allison described as resonance quality, a metric that she'd developed to capture the specific character of the connection between lineage members and which didn't have a precise physical analog but which the mapping array confirmed as real.

The resonance quality increased when lineage members were in meaningful contact rather than superficial contact. Not geographic proximity. Quality of connection. Depth of shared understanding.

She thought about that.

She thought about the Council and its isolation model and the specific way that isolation had not just been unkind but had been biologically counterproductive, had actually suppressed the lineage's capability by removing the connection that the biology required.

She said to Allison: "The Council was making us weaker."

Allison said: "The Council was making the Slayers weaker, yes. The isolation suppressed the channel activation not just in the called ones but across the network. Every Slayer who was isolated was less capable than she would have been in connection." She paused. "The compounding effect across two thousand years of institutional isolation is�" she paused. "Significant."

She held this.

She thought about what the lineage had been capable of before the Council's model had fully calcified and what it had been capable of under the Council's model and what it was becoming now that the model was different.

She thought about Yeva and Decker and Priya and the candidates in the network, all of them operating at the edge of what the lineage had been capable of in isolation and beginning to exceed it.

She thought: we've been running at reduced capacity for two thousand years.

She thought: what does full capacity look like?

She thought: we're going to find out.

She said to Allison: "Document everything. All of it. The before and the after and the mechanism and the compounding effect." She held Allison's gaze. "This is the thing that explains why the Vigil exists. Not the threats. The biology. The lineage is more of what it is when it's connected. That's the foundation of everything."

Allison said: "I've been writing it for six months."

She said: "Write it for six more. And then we publish it."

Allison looked at her.

She said: "All of it?"

"All of it that can be published without compromising the operational security," she said. "The world�this century's world�needs to understand what the lineage is. Not mythology. Biology. Physics." She paused. "If people understand it they can help it. And the scale of the work is going to require people who understand it and want to help it."

Allison held her gaze for a long moment.

She said: "That's a significant decision."

"I know," she said. "I've been working toward it for a year." She held Allison's gaze. "The Vigil can't stay behind closed doors indefinitely. The work is too large. We need more people than we can build in secrecy." She paused. "And the lineage candidates deserve to live in a world that knows what they are. Not as something to be afraid of. As something real."

Allison was quiet.

She said: "There will be people who are afraid."

"There always are," she said. "There were people who were afraid of me from the beginning. I'm still here."

Allison held her gaze for a long moment.

Then she said: "All right." She paused. "I'll need three months to prepare the full publication."

"Take four," she said. "Do it right."

~~~

The publication was the thing that the third year produced that changed the shape of what came after.

Not immediately. Not in the dramatic way of revelations that restructured everything overnight. In the slow and specific way of accurate information entering a field and beginning to shift the frame.

The paper, when it came out, was in two simultaneous publications: a major peer-reviewed medical journal and a public-facing explanatory document that Allison and Fritz had worked on for the last month of the preparation period with the specific goal of being accurate without being inaccessible.

She'd reviewed everything before it went out.

She'd written the foreword to the public document.

The foreword said:

What follows is a description of something that has existed alongside human civilization for as long as there has been human civilization. It has not been secret by design. It has been unknown because the knowledge was lost and because the people who carried the trait were told they were broken rather than told what they were. This paper is the beginning of the telling. It is not complete. The science is ongoing and the full picture will take generations to develop. But the beginning is overdue and we offer it now, with the acknowledgment that some of what follows will be challenging to receive and with the confidence that accuracy is kinder than silence regardless of the challenge.

She'd written it in an afternoon.

She'd read it three times before she sent it to Allison.

Allison had written back: this is right.

The response to the publication was what she'd told Allison it would be: some of it relief and some of it fear and most of it the beginning of a conversation that had needed to start.

The lineage candidates in the network, when they heard about the publication, had responses that ranged from Yeva's direct and immediate yes, this is what I wanted to Decker's quiet acknowledgment to the newer candidates' something that looked like being seen for the first time and recognizing that they'd been waiting to be seen.

The response from the broader medical and scientific community was more complicated and more extended and she let Honor and Allison handle most of it because they were better positioned for the specific kind of complicated it was.

The response from the political community was Honor's domain and she left it there with trust that had become deep enough to be structural.

The response from the people who were afraid was what she'd expected and she handled it with the patience she'd been developing, the specific patience of someone who had been afraid herself and knew what fear needed before it could become something else.

She talked to people when it was useful and didn't when it wasn't and kept doing the work while the world adjusted to the new information.

The world adjusted.

Not all at once. Not without friction. But it adjusted, the way it always adjusted when accurate information replaced inaccurate silence, slowly and then in pieces and then all at once when enough pieces had shifted that the new configuration was simply what was true.

~~~

In the fourth month after the publication, a woman came to the Vigil's residence who hadn't been brought there by any of the standard channels.

She came on her own, which was itself information. She'd found the address through the public-facing information the publication had made available, which was carefully designed to be findable by the people who needed to find it and minimally findable by people who were looking for different kinds of trouble.

She was fifty-three. She had gray hair that was the gray of someone who had decided to let it be gray a decade ago. She had the specific quality of someone who had been navigating something unusual for a very long time without a framework for it.

She said, at the residence door, to the person who answered: "I read the paper. I think I have the thing it describes. I have had it for thirty years." She paused. "I want to talk to someone who knows what they're talking about."

The person who answered the door brought her to Buffy.

She came in with the quality of someone who had been in the subclinical category long enough that it had become the background of her entire adult life, the thing she'd been managing and adjusting to and navigating around for three decades without a name for it.

She sat down and looked at Buffy.

She said: "Thirty years."

Buffy said: "I know."

She said: "I thought I was�I didn't know what I thought I was. I thought I was strange. I thought I was too fast and too strong and I spent thirty years not trusting it because I didn't understand it." She paused. "I'm fifty-three years old and I've been�" she stopped.

Buffy said: "You've been managing it alone."

She said: "Yes."

Buffy held her gaze.

She said: "You're not broken. There's a name for what you are. You're part of something that is larger than you knew." She paused. "And you didn't find us until now because we didn't exist until now. That's not your failure. That's ours." She held the woman's gaze. "We should have been here thirty years ago. We weren't. We're here now."

The woman held her gaze.

She said: "What does it mean? For me now. At fifty-three."

She thought about it.

She said: "It means you understand what you are. It means you have people who know what you know. It means the thirty years of managing it alone were real and the cost of them was real and we can't give them back." She paused. "What we can do is the rest." She held the woman's gaze. "There's a lot of rest left. If you want it."

The woman was quiet for a long time.

She said: "The paper said the network. That being in the network changes the capability."

"Yes," Buffy said.

She said: "For someone who's been subclinical for thirty years."

"Allison's research suggests the activation responds to network quality regardless of how long the isolation has been," she said. "There are other people in the network who came in after significant subclinical periods. The activation follows the connection." She paused. "You would be studied. Fritz and Allison would want to document your case. You would have control over that documentation." She paused. "And you would have people."

The woman held her gaze for a long moment.

She said: "I'm fifty-three."

"I know," Buffy said.

She said: "The paper talks about things that are happening in this generation and the next generation and the generation after that."

"Yes," Buffy said.

She said: "Am I too late?"

Buffy held her gaze.

She thought about what too late meant and what thirty years of managing something alone had cost and what the rest looked like and what the long work needed.

She said: "No. The long work is long. It needs every generation and every person in every generation. What you have is the thirty years of knowing this specific thing in your body without a framework for it, which produces a specific kind of knowledge that the people who came in with a framework early don't have." She paused. "The Vigil needs that knowledge." She held the woman's gaze. "You're not too late. You're exactly when you arrived."

The woman looked at her for a long moment.

Then something in her face did what faces did when they received something they'd needed for a long time and had stopped expecting.

She said: "All right."

She said: "Yes."

Buffy said: "Good. Let me introduce you to people."

She introduced her to people.

Chapter 50: The fourth year.

She turned twenty-one in January, which felt significant and which she let feel significant rather than managing it down to something more comfortable. Twenty-one in the century she'd come from had been a specific cultural marker, the age of full adulthood in the legal sense, and while Manticore's legal framework had its own metrics that didn't map precisely onto the one she'd grown up in, she held the personal marker anyway.

She'd been twenty-one for three hours when Scotty came into the kitchen of their quarters, which was the capital quarters, the Manticore one, not the Sphinx property, and put a mug of hot chocolate in front of her.

She looked at it.

He said: "Happy birthday."

She said: "You remembered."

He said: "I've been remembering it for three weeks."

She looked at the mug.

She thought about the birthdays she'd had in the previous century and the ones she'd had in this one and about January 19th being the date that ran through all of them, the specific continuity of a date that the calendar held regardless of what century the calendar was operating in.

She said: "I'm twenty-one."

He said: "Yes."

She said: "When I was fifteen I genuinely wasn't certain I'd make twenty-one."

He sat down across from her. He said: "And now?"

She thought about it.

She said: "Now I'm thinking about thirty." She paused. "What it's going to look like. What the Vigil will look like at thirty. What Yeva will be capable of at twenty-five." She paused. "What we'll be building in Sphinx." She held his gaze. "What we'll have built."

He held her gaze.

He said: "Big thinking for six in the morning."

She said: "I always think big at six in the morning. You've been watching me do it for two years."

He said: "I have." He paused. "What do you see? At thirty."

She thought about it seriously, the way she thought about things she wanted to answer accurately.

She said: "The Vigil will be something different from what it is now. Bigger. More complex. The formal structure will include Haven. Probably the League by then, at least in preliminary form." She paused. "Yeva will be�" she stopped. "I don't have complete language for what Yeva is going to be. But it's significant." She paused. "The research will have produced things we can't fully predict because the research is going somewhere that hasn't been mapped." She paused. "The network will be larger than I can currently picture." She paused.

He said: "What else?"

She held his gaze.

She said: "This." She gestured at the kitchen and the city outside and the whole of the specific shape of what they were in. "More of this. Better versions of this." She paused. "The garden will be established. The property will be�fully what it's becoming." She held his gaze. "The things that are growing will have had time to grow."

He held her gaze.

He reached across and took her hand.

He said: "Yes. That's what I see too."

She looked at their hands.

She thought about fifteen and twenty-one and about the distance between and about what thirty looked like from here, visible and specific and good.

She thought about what the Vigil would look like. About Yeva leading operations she wasn't on. About candidates she hadn't met yet who were going to be essential. About Allison's research producing the breakthrough she could feel was coming, the specific sense of something building toward its own confirmation. About the interface mapping revealing something new, always something new.

She thought about all of it and it was large and it was specific and it was hers and it was the work and the work was what she was for.

She drank her chocolate.

She thought: twenty-one.

She thought: I made it.

She thought: I'm going to keep making it.

The morning assembled itself around them in its Manticoran way and she was here, in the kitchen, in the life, with the work ahead and the people in it and the thing she'd built and was still building and would be building at thirty and after that, for as long as the work required it and she was here to do it.

The Keeper was present.

Vigil was present through the bond.

All of it was present.

She was twenty-one and alive and in this century and building something that mattered and loved and loving and here.

She was here.

She thought: good.

She thought: yes.

She drank her chocolate and the morning came in and the fourth year began.

~~~

The fourth year was the year the war's end became visible in the political landscape in ways that the armistice had promised and that the months after it had been building toward.

Haven's reform government, which had been forming since the armistice, held its first free elections in the spring. The election results were complicated and contested in some quarters and genuine in others and produced a government that was neither what Haven had been nor what the Alliance would have chosen for it but was Haven choosing for itself, which was the only version that was going to be stable.

Tolman's network, which had become part of the Vigil's extended apparatus, was a connection into that government that she hadn't had a year ago and which Honor was carefully developing into something more formal.

The charter revision she'd asked the Queen for was in its third draft, the one that was finally close enough to right that she was going to be able to sign it without reservations. The board that would govern the Vigil under the revised charter would include Haven and, in a preliminary capacity, two League worlds whose governments had been in conversation with Honor about the Vigil's mandate and whose interest was genuine if not yet fully committed.

She signed the third draft on a Thursday.

The signing happened in the same room as the original signing, with the Queen and with most of the same people. The room felt different. More certain of itself. The quality of a thing that had been established long enough to know what it was.

Ariel was present. Vigil was present, which had required some logistical creativity involving a shuttle to Sphinx and back, because she'd wanted Vigil there and the Sphinx autumn was not a time she wanted to take Vigil far from its territory, and Vigil had communicated its own opinion about the matter which was the opinion of a treecat who had decided that certain things were non-negotiable.

She'd arranged the shuttle.

The signing produced what the original signing had produced but with more weight, the weight of three and a half years of building adding itself to the moment, and she held the document afterward and thought about the first document, the one from the Clinkscales archive in its case on her desk, and thought about the line between them.

Four generations of faith and three and a half years of work and the thing that had been kept alive through both of them, the long watch maintained across two thousand years of loss and recovery.

She held the document.

She said, very quietly, to the Keeper and to the document and to the room: "We're real. Formally and functionally and in the biology. We're real."

The Keeper's quality was the quality of something that had waited a very long time for exactly this.

She held the document.

She thought about the woman who had come to the residence at fifty-three. She thought about Yeva at the beginning. She thought about the fourteen-year-old on Grayson who had asked what the good part was.

You're enough for something that matters.

She looked at the signed document.

She thought: yes.

~~~

Autumn came again to Sphinx.

She was there for it in the way she'd been there for every autumn since the first one, the spectacular version arriving and her standing in it with the same quality of reception that she'd brought the first time and that had not diminished.

Vigil was in the canopy.

The garden was what it had been growing toward for three autumns, established and specific, the things she'd planted finding what they were capable of in the Sphinx soil, the wrong things becoming their own things, the rosemary equivalent in its third year now with the specific settled character of something that had found its ground.

She stood in it in the early morning with the autumn doing its thing around her and thought about the shape of the work.

Seventeen nodes on the original list. Ten addressed. Seven remaining, plus the new ones the expanded network intelligence was identifying as the mapping extended.

The timeline was ninety years from the point of measurement and the measurement was two years ago.

The lineage network stood at sixty-three confirmed candidates. Twenty-two in active engagement with the Vigil. Eleven operational at various levels.

Allison's research was in its fourth year of unprecedented production and showed no signs of reaching diminishing returns.

Haverstock's mapping array had expanded to cover sixty percent of the explored systems and was finding things in the interface data that he described as requiring a new framework, which was the highest-level description he used for results that had outgrown the existing theoretical structure.

The presences, as she'd come to think of the Constant and Witness and the others, were in regular communication through the channels they'd established, a distributed network of ancient watches doing the long work from their various positions, each providing information that the others didn't have.

The Vigil was real and growing and doing what it was built to do.

She stood in the garden and held all of it.

She thought about twenty-one and what thirty looked like from here and what it was going to look like from there when she was in it, how the picture would have changed by then in the specific ways that pictures changed when you lived in them rather than looking at them from outside.

She thought: forward.

She thought: always forward.

The Girl ran at the edge of her awareness, ancient and patient, the lineage continuing as it always continued, not requiring anything of her except what she was already giving, which was everything she had and everything she was becoming.

The Keeper was present.

Vigil was in the canopy above her.

The spectacular version was all around her, the trees doing what they were built to do in the autumn, the wrong colors that were right, the specific beautiful gift of a planet expressing itself in the season when it was most fully itself.

She stood in it.

She was here.

She thought: this is what after looks like.

She thought: this is what the work looks like.

She thought: this is what I am.

All three at once, holding, none of them displacing the others.

She breathed the Sphinx autumn.

She thought about the document she was writing and the record growing and the things she was going to need to tell the people who came after, the things she was still learning and would keep learning and that needed to be in the record while she was the one who knew them.

She thought: I'll keep writing.

She thought: the work will keep going.

She thought: I'll be here for it.

All of it was true.

She turned from the garden.

There was work to do.

She was looking forward to it.

Always.

Forward.

Chapter 51: It started with Vigil.

She was in the garden on a morning in the third year's winter, the kind of morning that Sphinx produced in January that was cold in the way she'd learned to dress for and still found bracing in a way that felt like a gift. The garden was under its winter cover, the established plants doing what established plants did in the cold, which was rest with the specific patience of things that knew spring was coming and had done this enough times to trust it.

She was just standing in it.

Not working. Not thinking about the work. One of the mornings where she'd come outside because the inside had been too much and the outside was what she needed, the cold air and the bare winter architecture of the trees and the specific quality of Sphinx at rest.

Vigil was in the canopy.

She felt this through the bond in the ambient way she always felt it, the low steady awareness of the other's presence that had become as much a part of her baseline as the Keeper's accompanying quality. Both of them present, both of them constant, the specific texture of not being alone that she'd been building toward since Hades.

Then Vigil did something.

Not dramatically. Treecats didn't do things dramatically when they were doing them on purpose. They did them with the specific deliberateness of something that had decided to communicate and was choosing its method carefully.

Vigil came down from the canopy to the low branch, the approach branch, the one it had used since the beginning, and it looked at her.

She looked back.

The bond's quality shifted.

Not the ambient warmth she was used to. Something more directed, the specific quality of the bond when Vigil was actively communicating rather than simply present. She'd felt this before, usually about things in her immediate environment, things Vigil had noticed that she hadn't.

This was different.

The quality was internal. Directed not at something around her but at something in her, the specific quality of the bond when Vigil was reading something she was carrying and responding to the reading.

She held still.

She felt what Vigil was reading.

It was not a clear picture. The bond didn't work in pictures. It worked in the quality of things, the emotional register of what was present, and what Vigil was reading in her and reflecting back was something she recognized the moment she felt it reflected.

Grief.

Not the grief she knew she carried, the managed version, the grief for her mother and Sunnydale and the world she'd lost that she'd processed and integrated and that was part of her without being the loudest thing in her. That grief she knew. She'd made her peace with it in the way you made peace with permanent losses, not by not feeling them but by feeling them in their proper proportion.

This was different grief.

This was the grief she hadn't known was there.

It didn't have a face. It didn't have a name. It had the quality of something that had been underground long enough to lose its surface features, to become simply a weight she'd incorporated into how she moved through the world without knowing it was additional weight.

Vigil held the reflection for a moment.

She breathed.

She felt the shape of what was being shown to her.

Not the losses themselves. Not the people and the place and the world she'd come from. Something underneath the losses. A question she hadn't let herself ask because asking it made the losses louder and she needed the losses to be manageable and asking the question was not compatible with the losses being manageable.

The question was: is the door still there?

Not open. She wasn't asking if it was open. She wasn't asking to go through it. She was asking whether it existed, whether the mechanism that had displaced her was a one-way event or whether there was a possibility, theoretical, however remote, of a direction back.

She had not asked this question.

She had not asked this question since the first months when asking it had been too raw and then the question had gone underground and she had stopped being aware that she wasn't asking it.

Vigil's true-hand came off the branch and settled on her shoulder briefly, the specific communication of the touch, and then withdrew.

She stood in the garden for a long time.

The winter held her in its quiet.

She thought: I haven't asked.

She thought: I need to ask.

She went inside.

~~~

She didn't ask immediately.

That was the honest account of what happened. She went inside and she ate breakfast and she worked and the day was the day and she carried the awareness that she needed to ask alongside all the other things she carried, and she worked with it the way she worked with things that needed time before she could approach them directly.

She told Scotty that evening.

Not everything. Not yet. But the outline of what Vigil had shown her, the thing she'd been not-thinking about, the question she hadn't asked.

He listened.

When she finished he said: "How long?"

She thought about it. "Since the beginning," she said. "I think. I think I stopped asking in the first weeks when asking was too much and I never started again." She paused. "I didn't know I wasn't asking. I thought I'd settled it."

He was quiet for a moment.

He said: "You settled the grief. You didn't settle the question."

She held this.

She said: "Yes. That's right."

He held her gaze. "Is it a question you want to ask?"

She thought about what wanting meant in this context, the specific character of wanting something that was also frightening, the particular complexity of wanting to know an answer that could be many different things.

She said: "I need to ask it. Whether I want to or not." She held his gaze. "I've been building here. What I'm building is real. What we're building is real." She held his gaze steadily. "Knowing whether the door exists doesn't change any of that. But not knowing�" she paused� "not knowing is a weight I didn't know I was carrying."

He held her gaze for a long moment.

He said: "Then ask."

She said: "Yes."

She said: "I'm going to need to go through the Constant."

He said: "When?"

She said: "Tomorrow."

He said: "I'll be here when you get back."

She looked at him.

She thought about what he'd offered, the specific quality of it, the steadiness that didn't require her to manage how she was before or after or to protect him from the size of the question.

She said: "Thank you."

He said: "Ask first. Thank me after."

She did.

~~~

She contacted the Constant the next morning.

Not the channel's full activation, not the kind of contact she'd initiated on Beowulf. The lighter version, the ambient contact that the channel allowed without full opening, the way you could hear through a door without opening it.

She asked the question.

She asked it directly and without hedging, because the Constant received direct questions better than indirect ones and because she'd learned that the quality of what came back through the channel corresponded to the quality of what went in.

The question was: is there a way back?

The Constant's response was long.

Not in the dramatic sense. Not the kind of response that arrived with weight and restructured everything. The careful response of something very old that was being asked about something very specific and was taking the question seriously.

What came back through the channel was this:

The portal that had displaced her was not a unique mechanism. It was a specific application of something that existed in the interface's natural physics, a point of instability that had been held open by the ritual and that had closed when the ritual's energy was expended.

Closed was not the same as gone.

The interface retained the record of what had passed through it in the way that water retained the record of what had moved through it, not visibly, not permanently, but as a residual configuration in the local interface structure at the point of passage.

The point of passage was Earth.

Specifically, the location in Sunnydale, California that had been the Hellmouth, which was also the location where the portal had opened, which was also the location that had the highest concentration of interface activity in the geological record of that region of space.

The Hellmouth was not a random location. She'd known this from the record. Something had made it a Hellmouth deliberately, introducing the instability that the portal had exploited.

The instability at that location was not closed. It was dormant.

The interface's physics, at a location with that history, did not return to neutral. It retained the configuration, the way a channel in soil retained its shape after the water had passed. The configuration was not active. It was not open. But it was there.

Whether it was activatable from this end, from this century, without the ritual that had originally opened it � the Constant did not know. The physics was Haverstock's question rather than the Constant's.

She came back from the contact.

She sat in the room for a long time.

She thought about what she'd received.

The door was not gone. The door was dormant. Whether the door could be opened from this side, from this century, across two thousand years of time and however many light-years of space, was a question she didn't have the answer to yet.

She wrote to Haverstock.

She wrote carefully, because the question she was asking had implications that went in several directions and she needed to be precise about which direction she was asking about.

She wrote: the portal that displaced me was a specific application of interface instability at a location on Old Earth that has the highest concentration of historical interface activity in the geological record of that region. The Constant tells me the instability is dormant rather than closed. I want to know whether it is theoretically activatable from this side, from this century. Not whether we should. Whether it's physically possible.

She sent it.

She sat at her desk and looked at the case with the first Clinkscales document in it and thought about what she'd asked and what the answer might be and what the answer meant for the shape of things.

The Keeper was very present.

She said, to the Keeper: "You knew about this."

The Keeper's quality was the quality it had when it had known about something and had been waiting for the right moment.

"The right moment was Vigil," she said.

The Keeper's quality did not deny this.

She thought about treecats and the bond and the specific gift of something that read what you were carrying and reflected it back at the moment when reflecting it back was what you needed.

She thought about what she owed Vigil for this.

She thought: carrots. Sascha had mentioned that Vigil had a fondness for a specific variety of Sphinx root vegetable that was in the same general category as carrots. She thought: a great many carrots.

She held the thought alongside the larger thought and let them both be present simultaneously, which was a thing she'd gotten better at, the capacity to hold the small human reality and the large consequential reality in the same moment without one displacing the other.

Then she went to find Scotty and tell him what the Constant had said.

~~~

Haverstock's response took four days.

Four days was fast for Haverstock, who thought at the pace of someone whose thinking was thorough by nature and who had learned that incomplete thorough thinking was less useful than complete thorough thinking regardless of the timeline.

The response was long.

She read it twice.

The theoretical answer was: yes. With significant qualification.

The interface instability at the Hellmouth location was real and retained in the interface's structure in the way the Constant had described. The physics of the channel that the lineage modification provided had, in theory, the capacity to interact with a dormant instability at sufficient range if the interaction was mediated by a sufficiently powerful interface presence.

The sufficiently powerful interface presence was the question.

The channel as it currently operated, even at full activation through the lineage network, was not sufficient to reach across two thousand years of time and however many light-years to interact with a dormant instability on Old Earth.

The interface variation, however, was relevant.

The interface variation that the mapping array had been tracking, the slow movement of the interface boundary toward human space over the past forty years, was reducing the effective distance between the current interface structure and the historical one. Not quickly. Not on a human timescale. But measurably.

At the current rate of variation, the effective distance would reach the threshold at which a sufficiently powered interface interaction might be possible in approximately � Haverstock's estimate was careful and ranged � forty to sixty years.

Not in her lifetime.

Unless.

The unless was the part that required her to sit with for a long time.

Unless the interface variation was not the only variable. The variation was being caused by something, which she knew. The thing that had made the Hellmouth was doing the causing. The interface was being moved for its own purposes, which were not her purposes, and the movement was producing a side effect that was potentially useful to her in a way the mover hadn't intended.

But the presences. The Constant and Witness and the others. They had their own capacity for interface interaction, independent of the variation. If they were coordinating, if they were applying their combined capacity in the same direction at the same moment�

Haverstock's estimate for the combined scenario was different.

He wrote: if the presences can coordinate their interface interaction toward the Hellmouth location's dormant instability, the effective threshold might be reachable within a decade. The uncertainty in this estimate is very high because I don't have good data on the presences' individual interface capacity and the combination effect is theoretical. But the physics doesn't prohibit it.

She read that paragraph three times.

A decade.

She was twenty-one.

She was going to be thirty-one in a decade.

She sat with this for a long time.

Then she went to the Vigil's working space and found Yeva, because whatever she was going to do with this information she wasn't going to do it without Yeva in the room, and Yeva needed to know.

Not because the question was Yeva's to answer. It wasn't. The question was hers. It had always been hers.

But the Vigil was not something she did alone and the things she was working through were not things she worked through alone, and Yeva had become, over three years, the person inside the Vigil who understood the full shape of what Buffy was in a way that nobody else in the room quite did in the same way.

She said: "I need to tell you something."

Yeva said: "Tell me."

She told her.

All of it. The Vigil showing her the submerged grief. The question she hadn't asked. The Constant's response. Haverstock's physics. The decade estimate.

Yeva listened with the complete attention she always brought and when Buffy finished she was quiet for a long time.

She said: "What are you going to do with it?"

Buffy said: "I don't know yet."

Yeva held her gaze.

She said: "You've been building here for three years. Everything you've built is real." She paused. "The question of whether you go back is separate from whether what you've built is real."

"I know," Buffy said.

Yeva said: "Do you?"

She held Yeva's gaze.

She thought about the specific worry she'd had that looking at the door would make what she'd built feel provisional. She thought about whether that worry had been prescient or protective or both.

She said: "I'm working on knowing it."

Yeva said: "That's honest."

She said: "Yes."

They held each other's gaze.

Yeva said: "Whatever you decide. The Vigil exists. I exist. What you've built doesn't go away if you go back. It goes forward. Without you for a while, maybe." She held Buffy's gaze. "But what you built was always meant to go forward without you eventually. That's why you built it the way you built it."

Buffy held this.

She thought about the Council and its model, the one Slayer always at the center, everything dependent on the one. She thought about the Vigil and the deliberate building of something that distributed the carrying rather than concentrating it.

She thought: I built it so it didn't need me.

She thought: that was always the point.

She thought: I didn't know until this moment that was also preparation for this.

She said: "I need to think."

Yeva said: "Take the time."

She said: "The work continues."

Yeva said: "The work always continues." She held Buffy's gaze. "That's what you taught us."

She looked at Yeva for a long moment.

She thought about who Yeva was becoming and about what twenty-five looked like on her and about the ceiling that was still not visible.

She thought: yes. She'll be fine. Better than fine.

She said: "Thank you."

Yeva said: "You're welcome." She paused. "Now go think."

She went.

Chapter 52: She thought for three weeks.

Not only thought. She worked, because the work was always there and the work continuing regardless of personal circumstances was both necessary and, in this case, stabilizing. She worked and she thought and she let the question exist alongside everything else without forcing it to resolve before it was ready.

She told Scotty the full version on the second evening.

Not the outline she'd given him before but the complete picture, Haverstock's physics and the decade estimate and what it meant and what she didn't know yet.

He listened the way he always listened.

When she finished he was quiet for a longer moment than usual.

He said: "A decade."

She said: "Approximately. If the presences can coordinate. If the physics holds at Haverstock's estimate. If�" she stopped. "There are a lot of ifs."

He said: "But it's possible."

She said: "The physics says it's possible."

He held her gaze.

He said: "What would you find? If you went back."

She'd been thinking about this for three weeks. She had an answer that was the honest answer, which was the only kind she was willing to give him.

She said: "Not what I left. Two thousand years have passed. Sunnydale�" she paused. "Whatever Sunnydale became in two thousand years, it's not what I remember. The people I knew are two thousand years gone." She paused. "The Hellmouth might not exist anymore. The Earth might be very different. The supernatural landscape on Earth in this century is something I have no information about." She paused. "Going back would not be going home. It would be going to a different version of the place I'm from."

He held her gaze. "Then what's there?"

She thought about it.

She said: "The place itself. Old Earth. The first place." She paused. "The lineage's origin. The Hellmouth's history." She paused. "Whatever is there now, whoever is there now, they're part of the same long story." She paused. "And the thing that made the Hellmouth has been doing something with that location for two thousand years. Whatever it's been doing there matters for what we're working toward here." She held his gaze. "The intelligence value alone is�" she stopped. "That's not the only reason."

He said: "I know it's not."

She held his gaze.

She said: "I want to see it. I want to see what it became." She paused. "I want to know if anything of what I knew is still there. Not the people. The place." She paused. "And I want to close something. I don't know exactly what. But there's something that's been open since the portal and I want to close it." She paused. "Or at least look at it."

He was quiet.

He said: "And then?"

She held his gaze.

She said: "Then I come back."

He held her gaze for a long moment.

She said: "This is where my life is. The Vigil. The lineage. The work." She held his gaze. "You." She paused. "Going back doesn't mean staying back. Going back is�" she looked for the word� "completing something. And then returning." She paused. "If the physics allows a return."

He said: "Does it?"

She said: "Haverstock's estimate covers the going. The return is a different calculation that he's working on." She paused. "The physics that allows the going should, in theory, allow the coming back. The instability is bidirectional. But the same factors apply � the variation, the presences, the threshold." She paused. "He thinks yes. With the same level of confidence he has about the going."

He held her gaze.

He said: "So you'd go. You'd return. The time differential�"

She said: "Is the question I don't have a full answer to yet. The physics of time displacement through the interface is something Haverstock is still working out." She paused. "In the original event, going through the portal displaced me temporally as well as spatially. The return might do the same thing." She paused. "Or it might not. The original event was an unstable crisis point. What we're describing is a controlled, coordinated interaction with a dormant instability. The physics is different."

He said: "Might and might not."

She said: "Yes."

He was quiet for a long time.

She watched him hold the weight of this and she didn't try to make it smaller or easier than it was, because it wasn't small or easy and he deserved the full version.

He said: "When would you know? Whether you're going."

She said: "When the physics is sufficiently understood and the presences confirm they can coordinate and the threshold is reachable. That's what the decade estimate means � that's when those conditions align." She paused. "I'm not deciding now. I don't have enough to decide now." She held his gaze. "But I'm asking the question. Out loud. For the first time." She paused. "I wanted you to know I was asking."

He held her gaze.

He said: "Thank you for telling me."

She said: "It's the kind of thing you tell someone."

He said: "Yes. It is."

They held each other's gaze in the specific quiet of the room.

Then he said: "The decade. Whatever we build in the decade matters. Regardless of what you decide."

She said: "Yes."

He said: "Then we build."

She said: "Yes."

He reached across and took her hand.

She looked at his hand holding hers and thought about the decade and what it was going to contain and what she was going to know at the end of it that she didn't know now and what would be the same.

She thought: forward.

She thought: both directions.

She held his hand and let the question exist and didn't make it resolve before it was ready and the evening came in around them and it was, despite the weight of the question, still good.

Still very good.

She told the Constant that she was asking.

Not the full conversation. Just the acknowledgment. Through the channel at its ambient level, not the full contact, just the awareness directed toward the Constant's presence and the question she was asking it to hold with her.

The Constant's response was the quality of something that had been waiting for exactly this and was now doing what it did, which was the long patient work of preparation.

She told Witness through Eris, who was now on Manticore and who had become part of the Vigil's research structure in the specific way of someone whose accompanying presence added a dimension that the purely human researchers didn't have.

Witness's response, through Eris: it has been aware. It has been factoring it into the interface coordination work.

She held this.

She thought: they knew before I did.

She thought: of course they did.

She thought about what it meant that the presences had been factoring this into their work before she'd consciously asked the question, and she thought about the Keeper's knowing about Witness before Barnett and about the specific quality of very old things that were very patient having a longer view than she had.

She was going to spend the rest of her life being gently startled by this.

She thought: that's fine. That's the shape of it.

She sent a long message to Haverstock that was equal parts appreciation for his response and additional questions that his response had generated, because Haverstock's responses always generated additional questions and he was the kind of person who found this energizing rather than exhausting.

He wrote back within a day with the specific enthusiasm of someone who had been waiting for the additional questions.

They began the work of understanding the physics properly.

Chapter 53: The decade didn't arrive as a decade.

It arrived as days, as it always did, the accumulation of specific days with specific work and specific people and the ongoing texture of a life that was full enough to be present in rather than merely waited through.

She turned twenty-two.

She turned twenty-three.

The Vigil's network reached a hundred confirmed candidates. Fifty-three in active engagement. Twenty-three operational at various levels.

Yeva led the highest-complexity node operation the Vigil had attempted, in a Solarian system that had required six months of preparation and three weeks of careful diplomatic groundwork by Honor before the operational approach was possible. She executed it with a team of four, two of whom were candidates who had been in the Vigil's operational track for less than a year.

The team performed exactly as Yeva had assessed they would.

Buffy received the operational report and read it and thought: there it is.

The ceiling had been visible for some time. Now it was visible to everyone.

She told Yeva: "The next highest-complexity operation is yours if you want it."

Yeva said: "What's the next highest-complexity operation?"

She said: "The thing in the Solarian League that Honor has been building toward for two years."

Yeva held her gaze.

She said: "That's not mine yet."

She said: "No. But it will be." She held Yeva's gaze. "I'm telling you it's coming so you can prepare for the kind of operation it's going to be."

Yeva said: "How long?"

She said: "A year. Maybe less."

Yeva said: "All right."

She thought about herself at seventeen being told things she wasn't ready for yet and how the telling had shaped the readying and how she was doing the same thing for Yeva that nobody had done for her in quite that way.

She thought: this is what the Vigil is for. Among other things.

She turned twenty-four.

Allison's publication had been out for two years and the response in the scientific community had moved through the stages that significant publications moved through, from initial skepticism to cautious engagement to the beginning of active research by people outside the Vigil who were interested in the lineage modification as a biological phenomenon.

She'd been careful about the relationship with the outside researchers. Not closed. The Vigil wasn't a secret anymore and the lineage wasn't a secret and the research was better with more people contributing to it. But the candidates' privacy was protected and the operational work was not discussed and the balance was managed with the careful hand of someone who had thought about it from both sides.

Fritz had published four papers. Allison had published seven. Haverstock and Prieto had published eleven in four years, each one moving the interface framework a step further along the path that the Constant had been pointing toward since before Beowulf's civilization existed.

The mapping array now covered eighty percent of explored space.

The interface variation was measurable with a precision that two years ago hadn't been possible, and the precision was revealing detail that Haverstock described in his letters as beautiful in the specific way that physicists found things beautiful, which was precise and inevitable and pointing toward something large.

She read his letters and wrote back and thought about beautiful and inevitable and what those words meant for the shape of what was coming.

The presences were coordinating in ways that were new. She felt it through the channel, the specific quality of multiple very old things working in concert, the slow and deliberate alignment of capacities that had been operating independently for centuries.

Witness communicated through Eris that the coordination was progressing.

The Constant communicated through the channel that the threshold was moving.

She didn't ask for timelines. She'd learned not to ask the presences for timelines, which were a human category that the presences accommodated politely and which they found imprecise in ways they didn't fully articulate.

She turned twenty-five.

Scotty's operational role in the Vigil had become what she'd known it was going to become when he'd made it formal, which was essential in the way of things that had been missing from the structure until they arrived and then were impossible to imagine the structure without. He coordinated the operational logistics with the specific capability of someone who had spent a career coordinating complex things under pressure and who brought that capability to a context that used it fully.

He was also, in the Sphinx garden in the spring of her twenty-fifth year, the person who handed her a cup of hot chocolate and said: "Look at that."

She looked.

The rosemary equivalent was in its fourth year. What it had become in four years was not what it had been in the first year, not the cautious tentative growth of something finding its ground, but the established thing, the thing that had found its ground and was doing what it was built to do with the confidence of roots that went deep enough.

She looked at it for a long time.

She said: "Mom would have liked this."

He said: "Yes."

She said: "She would have had opinions about the companion planting."

He said: "Your mother gardened?"

She said: "She tried. She was better at cooking than gardening. But she tried." She looked at the plant. "She would have wanted to know about Sphinx. The climate. What grows here." She paused. "She would have asked your mother a lot of questions."

He held her gaze.

She said: "I think about that sometimes. What she would have made of all of this. Not in the grieving way. Just�" she looked for the word� "imagining."

He said: "What do you imagine?"

She thought about Joyce Summers. She thought about her mother's specific quality, the combination of fear and love that had been Joyce's relationship to the Slayer and how that relationship had evolved in the time they'd had and what it would have become in the time they'd been denied.

She said: "She would have been proud. And scared. And she would have made friends with your mother at some point and the two of them together would have been� a great deal." She paused. "She would have loved the Vigil. Not for the work specifically. For what the work meant. That her daughter had found something worth doing and people worth doing it with." She paused. "She was always more interested in the people than in the mission."

He was quiet.

She said: "I think she and Allison would have understood each other."

He said: "Both of them feeding people constantly."

The spring was doing what spring did and the garden was doing what established gardens did and she was here, in it, and the missing and the present were both true simultaneously and they held.

She turned twenty-six.

The operation in the Solarian League that Honor had been building toward for two years was ready.

Yeva led it.

She was there as backup, present in the operational sense of someone who was available and not required, and what Yeva produced was the confirmation of what she'd seen coming.

She led the team through the forty-eight hours with a quality that Buffy recognized from the inside, the quality of full engagement that was also full presence, the thing that made the difference between someone who was doing the work and someone who was the work.

The debrief afterward.

Haverstock's letter arrived in the autumn of her twenty-seventh year.

It was the letter she'd been expecting and not expecting simultaneously, the letter that arrived when the physics had caught up to the theory and the theory had proved out and the estimate had become something more solid than an estimate.

He wrote: I have been running the calculations with input from the presences for six months. The threshold is reachable. Not in forty years and not in twenty. The combination of the interface variation's progression and the presences' coordinated capacity has compressed the timeline significantly from my original estimate. My current best estimate for the threshold being reachable is three to five years. The uncertainty remains, but the direction is consistent across every model I've run.

Three to five years.

She was twenty-seven.

She would be thirty to thirty-two when the threshold was reachable.

Forward.

Always forward.

Both directions.

That was enough.

That was, as it always turned out when she looked at it directly, exactly enough.

Chapter 54: The three to five years arrived as three years, which was the faster end of the estimate and which Haverstock attributed to two things: the interface variation accelerating slightly beyond his projected rate in the second year, and the presences' coordinated capacity proving, in practice, to be greater than his theoretical estimate had allowed for.

She was thirty when he sent the letter that said: the threshold is reachable. Not in three months and not tomorrow. In approximately six months, during a window that the variation and the presences' alignment will produce. The window will last between seventy-two and ninety-six hours based on current models. After the window closes, the next comparable alignment is approximately eleven years away.

She read the letter sitting at her desk in the Manticore capital quarters with the city outside the window doing its morning things and the Keeper very present and Vigil through the bond somewhere in Sphinx and the first Clinkscales document in its case on the desk where it had been for three years.

She read it twice.

Then she put it down and looked at the window for a long time.

She was thirty.

Haverstock had said thirty to thirty-two. He'd been accurate.

She thought about the three years. What they had been and what they had contained. She thought about Yeva, who had been in the operational lead for two and a half of those years and who had done with it what she'd known Yeva would do. She thought about the Vigil, which was now something she could look at from the outside and see as a thing that existed rather than a thing she was continuously producing by existing.

She thought about the garden in Sphinx, which was in its fourth season of establishment and which had become what established gardens became, which was itself, fully, without apology.

She thought about Scotty.

She put the letter in her pocket and went to find him.

He was in the kitchen of the capital quarters, which was where he often was in the mornings, and he looked at her face when she came in and he knew before she said anything.

She sat across from him.

She said: "Haverstock's letter."

He said: "Tell me."

She told him.

Six months. Seventy-two to ninety-six hours. Eleven years to the next window.

He held this.

He said: "Six months."

She said: "Yes."

He said: "That's the decision timeline."

She said: "Yes."

He was quiet for a long moment. Not the quiet of someone processing alone. The quiet of someone who had been holding this possibility for three years and was now holding the specific version of it that was real rather than theoretical.

She held the quiet with him.

He said: "What are you thinking?"

She said: "That I need to finish thinking. That six months is enough time to finish thinking if I use it." She held his gaze. "And that I want to spend some of those six months not thinking about it at all. Just in the life. Before the decision."

He held her gaze.

He said: "How much time not thinking about it?"

She said: "Most of it."

He said: "Okay."

She said: "And then the last month thinking about it properly."

He said: "All right."

She held his gaze.

She said: "I'm going to ask you something and I want the honest answer."

He said: "You always get the honest answer."

She said: "What do you want me to do?"

He was quiet.

He said: "I want you to come back. Whatever you decide about going." He held her gaze. "If you go I want you to come back. That's what I want." He paused. "What I think you should do is�" he stopped. "That's your question. Not mine."

She held his gaze.

She said: "I know it's my question. I'm asking what you want. Those are different questions."

He held her gaze for a long time.

He said: "I want you to be who you are. All the way." He paused. "The person I've been building this with has the door as part of her. The not-asking was costing her something. The asking has been different." He paused. "I want you to be the version that has closed what needs closing. Even if that means going." He held her gaze. "And then I want you to come back and be here with me."

She held this.

She said: "That's the most honest thing you've said."

He said: "I've been practicing for three years."

She held his gaze.

She said: "I love you."

She hadn't said it in those words before. There had been other words and other ways and the whole of what they'd built together said it continuously in a language that didn't require those specific words. But the specific words had their own weight and the moment warranted the weight.

He held her gaze.

He said: "I know." He paused. "I love you too."

She held this.

She let it be fully present, the specific simple weight of it.

She said: "Six months."

He said: "Six months."

She said: "We build."

He said: "We build."

She reached across and took his hand.

~~~

The six months were the fullest she'd known.

Not because she was performing fullness or trying to store it against a possible absence. She was incapable of that kind of strategic emotion and she knew it and didn't try. The fullness was because she was present in a way she recognized as the deepest version of herself being present, the Vigil and the question and the life and the people all held simultaneously, and the simultaneous holding produced something she didn't have a precise word for.

Presence, as a practice rather than a performance.

She was present for the Vigil's work in the way she'd always been present for it, fully and without the management that divided attention. She was present for the candidates in the network, the ones she met directly and the ones she knew through the reports and the ones the bond's ambient quality brought to her attention through channels she couldn't entirely articulate.

She was present for Yeva, who was leading the operational track with a capability that was exceeding even what Buffy had known was coming, the ceiling still not visible, the thing Yeva was becoming still in the process of becoming.

She sat with Carson in the third month and talked for four hours about the archive and what it needed to be and what she wanted the document she was writing to become after she'd finished it and after that after.

Carson listened and then said: "You're writing it to be passed on."

She said: "Everything I've built is to be passed on."

He said: "Yes. But the document is specifically�" he paused. "It's a Watcher's journal."

She held this.

She said: "I always thought I'd have hated being a Watcher."

He said: "You'd have been a terrible Watcher in the Council sense." He held her gaze. "You're an excellent Watcher in the sense of someone who watches over the lineage and keeps the record and passes the knowledge forward."

She looked at him.

She said: "That's what the Vigil is."

He said: "Yes." He held her gaze. "It's what you always were. You just had to build the version that wasn't terrible."

She held this for a long time.

Then she said: "Write me into the archive."

He held her gaze.

She said: "The complete version. Not the operational record and not the research record. The whole one. Everything in the document and everything that's not in the document yet and whatever I tell you in the next six months that needs to be in it." She held his gaze. "When this is finished I want the archive to have the full picture of what happened in this generation."

He said: "I've been keeping that record since Hades."

She said: "I know. I want you to finish it."

He held her gaze.

He said: "All right."

She said: "Thank you."

He said: "Don't�"

She said: "I know. Thank you anyway."

He almost smiled.

She almost smiled back.

~~~

She talked to Honor in the fourth month.

Not because she hadn't been talking to Honor continuously throughout the six months. Because the specific conversation that needed to happen hadn't happened yet and the fourth month was when it needed to happen.

Honor came to the capital quarters on a Thursday evening with Nimitz and they sat in the way they sat, and before Buffy said anything Nimitz came to her shoulder and did the thing, the brief touch of his true-hand, the communication of what he communicated.

She scratched between his ears.

He stayed.

She said: "I want to talk about what comes after."

Honor said: "After you go."

She said: "After I go or after I decide not to. Either way." She held Honor's gaze. "The Vigil needs a different kind of leadership than I've been giving it for the past year. "The long-work side, the interface research and the presences coordination and the thread that runs from the Constant through everything�" she paused. "That needs someone."

Honor held her gaze.

She said: "You're not describing that role for me."

She said: "No. You're doing what you're built for in the diplomatic work." She paused. "I'm describing Eris."

Honor held her gaze.

She said: "Yes.
