Honor was quiet for a moment.

She said: "You've been thinking about succession."

She said: "I've been building toward succession since the beginning. I just wasn't calling it that." She held Honor's gaze. "The Vigil was always supposed to outlast me. I've been trying to build it that way." She paused. "The succession is just the formalization of something that's been the structure all along."

Honor held her gaze.

She said: "And if you come back?"

She said: "If I come back I come back into whatever the Vigil has become without me. Not to take it back. To be part of it in the way that's right then." She paused. "The same way I'm part of it now but not the same role." She held Honor's gaze. "The Vigil is bigger than any one role. That was always the design."

Honor was quiet for a long time.

Nimitz on her shoulder was very still.

She said: "You're not afraid."

She said: "I'm afraid of several things." She held Honor's gaze. "The time displacement. Whether the return physics holds. What I'll find on Old Earth." She paused. "Whether I'll make the right decision." She paused. "I'm afraid of the right things. I'm not afraid of the wrong things."

Honor looked at her.

She said: "What are the wrong things?"

She said: "Whether the Vigil can survive without me. Whether the people here will be all right." She paused. "I know the answers to those. I built the answers in." She held Honor's gaze. "You and Yeva and Eris and Carson and Fritz and Allison and Scotty. The presences. The network." She paused. "The wrong fears are fears about things I've already addressed. I know they're addressed. So I'm not afraid of them."

Honor held her gaze for a long moment.

She said: "When did you become wise?"

She said: "I'm not wise. I've just had time."

Honor said: "You've had less than four years."

She said: "I've had thirty years plus four." She held Honor's gaze. "The thirty didn't count for most things when I arrived. They count for this."

Honor was quiet.

Then she said: "Come back."

She said: "That's the plan."

Honor said: "I know it's the plan." She held her gaze. "I'm not saying it as a request. I'm saying it because�" she stopped. She paused. "I came very close to not surviving Hades. And you were there, and the person you've become is partly because of what happened there and partly because of who you already were." She paused. "I want that person to come back."

She held Honor's gaze.

She said: "I will."

She said it with the same quality she'd heard Scotty use, the directness that wasn't bravado and wasn't performance. The directness of someone who meant the thing they were saying.

Honor held her gaze.

She said: "All right."

Nimitz made the sound.

She thought: yes. That's it exactly.

Chapter 55: The fifth month she spent on Sphinx.

Not the whole month. Most of it. The specific decision to be on the property and in the garden and in the life that had been built there for most of the last month before the last month, when she'd said she was going to think about it properly.

She ran in the mornings through the Sphinx landscape, which was the landscape she knew best now after four years of running through it in every season. She ran and Vigil ran with her in the canopy, the bond's ambient quality a constant, and the morning air was the Sphinx morning air that she would know anywhere, that had become so specific to her body's knowledge that she thought she could find it in the dark.

She was in the garden one afternoon when the treecat did something unexpected.

Vigil came down. Not to the low branch, the approach branch. All the way down. To the ground, which treecats didn't do casually, the specific deliberateness of something that had decided the communication required a different proximity.

She held still.

Vigil crossed the garden.

It came to where she was standing and sat down in front of her and looked up at her with the green eyes that saw clearly and without apology.

She looked back.

The bond's quality was the quality it had when Vigil was communicating something significant, more directed than the ambient warmth, more specific in its character.

What she felt through the bond was not what she'd expected.

She'd been expecting something about the question. About the door and the decision and what Vigil had felt in her when it had surfaced the grief three years ago.

What she felt instead was simpler than that.

Vigil was showing her what Vigil felt about her.

Not as an assessment or a communication about the question. Just�what was there, in the bond, from the treecat's side of it. The specific quality of a consciousness that had been alongside her for three years and had accumulated what that time had produced.

It was a large thing.

She held it.

She thought about Honor and Nimitz and what Honor had described about the bond, the knowledge of being fully seen and not alone in the seeing.

She thought about what it meant to be fully seen by something that didn't have the capacity to see you other than truly, that had no investment in the version of you that was easier to see and therefore only ever saw the version that was there.

She crouched down so she was at Vigil's level.

Vigil looked at her.

She said: "I know."

She said it the way you said things you meant to the people who couldn't misunderstand you.

Vigil put its true-hand on her knee briefly. The specific communication of the touch. Then withdrew it.

She stayed crouched in the garden for a long time after Vigil had gone back up into the canopy. The afternoon was doing what Sphinx afternoons did in the late autumn, the specific low quality of the light, and the garden was around her in its established quiet and she was here, in it.

She thought about Vigil showing her what was there from the bond's other side.

She thought about what she felt in return, from her side, the thing that had been building since the winter morning three years ago when Vigil had come to the low branch in the snow.

She thought: this is the other thing I'm afraid of.

She thought it honestly, without the management.

Vigil. The bond. The specific irreplaceable quality of what had built itself between them, the thing that didn't translate, that was itself and only itself, that she would not have when she went through the portal.

The bond would not work across the displacement. The physics of what the bond was and the physics of what the portal did were not compatible. She'd thought about this and hadn't said it directly to anyone because saying it directly required acknowledging what it cost and acknowledging what it cost required being with the cost rather than around it.

She was being with the cost.

It was the largest single cost in the decision.

Not Scotty, who had Scotty's quality of groundedness, who would hold what they had while she was gone in the specific way that Scotty held things. Not the Vigil, which she'd built to hold itself. Not the life on Manticore and in Sphinx, which was real and established and would be there.

Vigil, and what was in the bond, and the fact that the bond was with this specific treecat and this specific place and what the portal would do to that.

She sat with the cost.

She didn't try to manage it or minimize it or place it in context against the larger considerations. She just sat with it and let it be the size it was.

Then she looked up at the canopy.

She said: "I'm not going forever."

Vigil's presence through the bond shifted slightly.

She said: "I'm coming back. Whatever it costs to come back, I'm doing it." She held the presence. "I need you to know that."

Vigil made the sound she'd come to associate with it receiving something and holding it, the low note that was its own kind of acknowledgment.

She said: "I don't know what the bond does while I'm displaced. I don't know whether it stays or whether it�" she stopped. "I don't know. But I know I'm coming back to where you are. Whatever else happens, that's true."

The bond's quality shifted again.

Not to the ambient warmth. To something different. Something she didn't have a precise word for but that had the quality of the treecat making a decision about something it had been considering for a while.

She waited.

Vigil came back down from the canopy.

All the way down again.

It came to her and this time it didn't stop at the distance it had stopped at before. It came closer, the specific closer of something that had decided the distance was no longer the right distance, and it settled against her side with the warm weight of a creature that had made up its mind about where it was going to be.

She held very still.

The bond's quality was something she didn't have language for.

She put her hand on Vigil's side, carefully, and Vigil allowed it and the bond was full in the specific way of things that were complete rather than full in the way of things that were overflowing.

She sat in the garden with Vigil against her side and the autumn doing its last things and the wrong stars coming in through the canopy gaps and the whole of what was here around her.

She thought: this is worth coming back for.

She thought: all of it is worth coming back for.

She thought about what she was going to find on Old Earth. She thought about what two thousand years had made of the place she came from. She thought about the Hellmouth, dormant in the interface, and what looking at it directly would do, what closing what needed closing meant.

She thought about her mother's house on Revello Drive. She thought about whether it still existed and what existed where it had been and what that was going to feel like to see.

She thought about the Vigil's work in this century and about what the long work needed and about what intelligence the other end of the displacement might produce, the view of the interface's history from the source rather than from its effects two thousand years downstream.

She thought about coming back.

She thought about Vigil, against her side, warm and real, the bond's quality doing what it was doing.

She thought: I'm choosing to come back.

Not because the alternative was impossible. Not because the other end of the portal was certainly worse. Because this was where she was choosing to be. Because the life and the people and the work and the treecat against her side were where she was choosing, and choice was the thing the Vigil had been built on from the beginning, the thing the old Council had taken away, and she was not going to make the decision unconsciously.

She was choosing.

She was going through, and she was choosing to come back, and the choosing was the thing that made the going something other than flight.

The autumn evening settled around her and Vigil was warm against her side and she sat in the garden of the property she'd been building for four years and let the decision arrive, not all at once, but the way things arrived when they were ready, gradually and then complete.

She thought: yes.

She thought: this is what I'm going to do.

She thought: I'm ready.

She scratched gently behind Vigil's ears and Vigil made the low sound and the garden held them both.

~~~

The last month.

She spent it exactly as she'd said she would, thinking about it properly.

Not to the exclusion of everything else. The work continued. The Vigil continued. The life continued. But alongside all of that, the deliberate thinking, the working through of the question from every direction until she'd seen every side of it and there was nothing left to see.

She talked to Haverstock about the physics.

The physics was solid. The window was confirmed. The presences had communicated their readiness through the channels, the Constant's quality shifting in a way she'd learned to read as fully prepared, Witness similarly, the others she'd met in the intervening years all aligned.

She talked to Fritz about the biology.

The healing factor, the channel, the modification�all of them would make the transit, he believed, in the same way they'd survived the original displacement. The biology was hers and it went where she went.

She talked to Allison about what she might find on the biological side at the other end.

Allison said: "Two thousand years of the lineage on Earth. In isolation, potentially, or potentially not�we don't know what institutional memory survived in two thousand years of human civilization." She paused. "But the modification persisted here. It should have persisted there. The people who carry it on Earth exist." She paused. "They've been existing without us for two thousand years."

She thought about that. The lineage on Old Earth, the origin point, the place where the first Slayer had been made and from which everything had spread.

She said: "If I can make contact. If the Vigil's knowledge can reach them�"

Allison said: "That's the operational value." She held Buffy's gaze. "But you know what the operational value is. I think the other value is what you're actually going to Old Earth for."

She held Allison's gaze.

She said: "Closing what needs closing."

Allison said: "Yes." She paused. "What needs closing?"

She thought about it.

She said: "I left in the middle of something. Not just the battle. The�" she looked for the word� "the arc of my life there. I was seventeen and I'd been the Slayer for two years and everything I'd built was there and I was in the middle of it and then I wasn't." She paused. "I've built a new life here and it's real and it's mine. But the arc of the other life is still�" she stopped. "Interrupted. I want to see the end of the interruption. I want to see what became of the place." She paused. "And I want to put something there. For them. The lineage on Earth. Whatever's left of them." She paused. "The knowledge that the Vigil exists. That there are people in this part of space who know what they are and are doing the long work." She paused. "I want to give them that before I come back."

Allison held her gaze for a long time.

She said: "You're going to be remarkable when you're forty."

She said: "I intend to find out."

Allison almost smiled.

She talked to Carson.

They sat in the archive room that the Vigil had developed, the proper one with the physical documents and the digital archive and the Clinkscales records and everything that had been collected and organized over four years of institutional work, and they talked for a long time about what the archive needed to be.

He said: "I want you to know that the record is complete. Everything you've told me is in it. The document you've been writing will be integrated. The first Clinkscales entry and the most recent entry are the same arc." He held her gaze. "Whoever reads this in a hundred years or two hundred years is going to have the full picture."

She said: "That's what I needed to know."

He said: "What do you need from me. For the going."

She said: "Be here when I come back."

He held her gaze.

He said: "That's a long tradition. The Clinkscales family keeping faith with the lineage." He paused. "I'll be here."

She held his gaze.

She said: "Thirty years from now, when the next generation is running this and you're explaining to them what happened in the founding period�" she paused� "tell them the truth about what it cost and what it was worth." She held his gaze. "Both parts."

He said: "I will."

She said: "Thank you. For everything." She held his gaze. "The thirty years before me and the three years with me and whatever comes after."

He said: "Don't�"

She said: "I know." She paused. "Carson. Thank you. I'm saying it."

He held her gaze.

He said: "You're welcome." He paused. "Come back safely."

She said: "Yes."

She talked to Yeva on the second-to-last day.

Not a long conversation. The long conversations had already happened. This one was brief and direct in the way of Yeva, who had become someone who said what needed saying in the words it needed and stopped.

Yeva said: "The Vigil is ready."

She said: "I know."

Yeva said: "I'm not going to make it into something you wouldn't recognize."

She said: "I know that too."

Yeva said: "I'm going to make it into something better."

She held Yeva's gaze.

She said: "Yes."

Not much. The portal physics didn't favor encumbrance and she'd thought carefully about what she was bringing. The knife, which was always with her. She packed these things.

She sat in the capital quarters for a while with the city outside the window.

She thought about who she'd been then.

She thought about who she was now.

She thought: this is what after looks like.

She thought: I know who I am.

She thought: let's go find out what's there.

Chapter 56: The window opened on a Tuesday.

Haverstock had given her the date three weeks before and she'd held it in the way she held things she was preparing for, with the specific quality of not-yet-using so the energy was available when it was needed.

Tuesday. The portal would be theoretically accessible from the current location of the Hellmouth's interface residue. The presences would be aligned. The window would be approximately eighty hours based on the final models.

She spent Monday on Sphinx.

Not doing anything. Being there. In the garden in the morning and on the property through the afternoon and with Scotty in the evening. She ran in the morning and Vigil was in the canopy and the bond was the bond and she let it be fully present, the weight of it, the specific fullness of something she was going to carry the memory of through whatever came next.

Vigil came down in the afternoon.

All the way down again, the ground distance, and it settled against her side and she put her arm around it and they stayed that way for a long time in the garden with the Sphinx autumn doing what it did.

She said: "I'll come back."

The bond's quality was the quality of something that had received and held this and was holding it forward.

She thought: yes. Hold it. I'm coming back.

That evening Scotty cooked, the real cooking, the kind he did when he was being present with his hands because his hands were how he was present when words weren't sufficient. She watched him cook and ate what he made and they talked about things that weren't the portal and weren't the decision and weren't the goodbye, just the things that people who have been building a life together talked about, the small specific things that were the texture of the life.

She thought: I'm going to remember this. This specific evening.

She thought: I'm going to come back to this.

She went to sleep in the Sphinx property and woke up at four and ran and Vigil was in the canopy and the bond was there and she said goodbye to the property in the way she said goodbye to places, which was to simply be in it for a moment with full attention and then go.

She went.

~~~

The activation point was on Old Earth.

She was not going to Old Earth physically. The physics of the portal activation was not a physical transit to the location of the Hellmouth. The activation worked through the channel, through the coordinated capacity of the presences, creating an interface event that could reach across the distance and the time to the dormant instability at the source location.

The effect, on her end, would be the portal opening around her wherever she was.

She was going to be in Haverstock's interface research facility on Manticore when it happened.

The facility had been prepared for this. Not dramatically. Haverstock didn't do dramatic. He did thorough. The mapping array was at full resolution. Every instrument he had was running. The presences were in their coordinated configuration, which the array showed as a pattern she'd never seen before in three years of looking at the maps, five distinct signatures in alignment that produced something the sum of whose parts was larger than any of them individually.

The channel in her was fully open.

Not something she'd done deliberately. The channel's response to what the presences were doing with the interface was automatic, the biology responding to the interface activity the way the eyes responded to light, the opening of something that was built to be open in exactly these conditions.

The Keeper was present in the most complete way she'd felt it since Beowulf.

Not beside her. With her. The distinction was real and she felt it clearly, the Keeper's consciousness in closer proximity than it had been since the opening of the record, the specific quality of something very old that was going to do something significant and was fully engaged with the doing of it.

Everyone was there.

Haverstock and Prieto at the instruments. Fritz, because Fritz was always present for significant biological events. Allison, because she'd asked. Carson, because she'd asked. Honor, with Nimitz. Yeva. Eris.

Scotty was there.

He was standing to her left in the specific position she'd come to associate with him, not close enough to be in the way and not far enough to be not present.

She looked at him.

He looked at her.

He said, very quietly: "Come back."

She said: "Yes."

She looked at the room. At all of them, the people who were the Vigil and the people who were her life and the people who were both, and she held the looking for a long moment.

She thought: this is what I'm coming back to.

She thought: I know exactly what I'm coming back to.

She turned to face the interface map.

The presences' alignment was complete, visible on the map as something she'd never seen before, five ancient signatures in a configuration that the map registered as unprecedented.

The Constant communicated through the channel: ready.

Witness communicated through the channel: ready.

The others, in sequence: ready.

The Keeper communicated: ready.

She took a breath.

She said: "Yes."

The window opened.

~~~

It did not open dramatically.

There was no blinding light and no sound like a thunderclap and no particular wind. What there was was a shift in the quality of the room, the specific quality of the air changing in a way that was not atmospheric and not entirely sensory, a change in what the channel was reading that was so complete and so present that it was its own kind of overwhelming without being overwhelming in a way she couldn't stand in.

The Slayer sense lit up.

Not in the threat register. In the register she associated with things that were enormous and real and present, the register of the first contact with the Constant, of the record opening, of the bond with Vigil in its first full moment.

The register of things that were exactly as big as they were.

The portal was there.

She couldn't see it the way you saw a door. It was present the way she felt the presences, in the channel, in the Slayer sense, as a quality in the space rather than a visible object in it. The quality was the quality of an opening, a direction, a vector toward something very far away in time and space that had been placed within reach by the coordinated work of the presences and the channel and the interface's dormant memory.

Old Earth.

The Hellmouth's residue.

Two thousand years.

She felt it.

She turned and looked at the room one more time.

Scotty's face.

She held it.

She said: "I'll see you soon."

He said: "I'll be here."

She looked at each of them in turn, the brief specific looking of someone fixing things in memory, and then she turned back to the opening.

She walked into it.

~~~

The transit was not instantaneous.

It was not long either, in any sense she could have measured with instruments. But it was experienced, which the original displacement had not been, or had not been remembered. The original displacement had taken her unconscious and deposited her on the Tepes without the experience of the passage.

This was different.

She was in the channel fully, the opening she'd walked into, and what the channel was doing in the transit was the specific thing it did at full activation, which was the simultaneous reception of everything available through it. The lineage running backward and forward. The presences in their positions, all five of them maintaining the alignment that held the window open. The Keeper, traveling with her through the Keeper's own relationship with the interface, which was not the same as her channel but was compatible with it and was present.

The Girl was there.

Very present. The most present she'd been since the merge on Hades. Running through the ancient dark at the edge of what the transit was, and the running was not the joyful running of the dreams. The running was purposeful. Directed. The Girl running toward something rather than simply running.

She felt the Girl's direction and understood it.

She was going home.

Not her home. The Girl's home. The ancient place where the first Slayer had been made, the original ground, the place before the Hellmouth was a Hellmouth and before there was a word for Slayer, when there was only the dark and the thing in the dark and the girl who ran toward it instead of away.

Old Earth.

The transit ended.

She arrived in darkness.

Not the jungle darkness of Hades or the city darkness of Manticore's early mornings. Absolute darkness, which resolved as her eyes adjusted into the darkness of enclosed space, underground, the specific quality of air that had not been circulated recently.

She was standing on stone.

The Slayer sense was active in every register simultaneously, the specific activation of something that was in a place of significance, and the significance was the oldest significance she'd ever felt, the interface reading at a density that made Beowulf's interface activity look ambient.

The Hellmouth.

Not open. Not dormant exactly either. Present in the way of something that had been there a very long time and had accumulated meaning the way stone accumulated age, the visible evidence of what it was.

She was underground. Below what had been Sunnydale, California. Below what Sunnydale had become in two thousand years, which she didn't know yet and was going to find out when she went up.

She stood in the dark and breathed.

She was here.

Old Earth.

She felt the Keeper, present and oriented with the specific quality of something that had returned to the place where it had been closest to its origin. Not made here. But connected to what was made here, the same thread running through both of them.

She felt the Girl, very present, the ancient consciousness of the first Slayer recognizing the ground it had come from.

She felt the lineage, running through this place in the residual way of things that had their origin somewhere and retained the connection across distance and time.

She stood in the Hellmouth's underground space and let herself feel all of it.

Then she pulled out the light she'd brought and turned it on and looked at what was there.

The space was old. Not the library basement that she remembered. Whatever structures had been built over and destroyed and built over again in two thousand years had done their work on the surface above. But the cave system beneath, the natural structure that the Hellmouth had occupied, had persisted in the way that geology persisted, patient and unchanged relative to the speed of human decision-making.

The interface was here.

She could feel it the way she felt the presences, as a quality in the space rather than a visible thing. The dormant instability that Haverstock had identified in the data. Present and quiescent and very old and the specific density of something that had accumulated two thousand years of history.

She looked at it for a long time.

She thought about Acathla. About the night she'd sent Angel through the portal that was meant to swallow the world, the worst night of her life that she'd been carrying the weight of ever since. She thought: that was the interrupted thing.

She thought: this is where the interruption happened.

She stood in the Hellmouth's space and looked at the interface and thought about closing what needed closing, and what she found when she looked directly at it was that closing it was not quite the right word for what needed to happen.

What needed to happen was acknowledgment.

Not ceremony. Not ritual. The specific human act of looking at a thing directly and saying: this happened. This is real. I was here and I was changed by here and the changing was not the end of me.

She said, out loud, in the dark: "I was here."

She said: "I was seventeen and I was the Slayer and this place was the whole world and it cost me everything I had and I survived it."

She said: "I came back."

The interface's quality shifted slightly.

Not in a threatening way. In the way of something very old that had been present for everything that had happened in this place and was acknowledging the acknowledgment.

She breathed.

She thought about her mother.

She took the letter out of her pocket.

She'd written it to Joyce Summers, who was two thousand years gone and for whom the letter served no practical purpose and every personal purpose, the letter she'd needed to write since the morning she'd woken up in the sickbay of the Tepes and understood where and when she was.

She said: "I found a life." She said it to the darkness and to the place and to the letter in her hands. "It's a good one. I did something that mattered. I found people worth being with." She paused. "I became someone. Not just the Slayer. Someone."

She held the letter.

She said: "I don't know if you could have imagined this. What I found. I don't think I could have imagined it from where I was." She paused. "But you would have been glad. I know that." She paused. "You would have made friends with everyone and fed them all and asked too many questions and you would have been glad."

She set the letter down on the stone of the Hellmouth's floor.

She left it there.

She thought: this is what I came to do.

She thought: this and the other thing.

She pulled out the data chip.

The other thing was the reason she'd come that the Vigil needed regardless of what the personal reason was. The intelligence value, the thing that going to Old Earth could provide that nothing else could. And the other thing she'd told Allison she wanted to give: the knowledge that the Vigil existed, for whoever was here, whoever carried the lineage on Old Earth in this century.

She had no way of knowing whether there was anyone to give it to. Two thousand years of the lineage on Old Earth without the Council's framework, without any connection to the interstellar civilization that had been building for eleven hundred years above them�she didn't know what that looked like. She didn't know whether the lineage had maintained itself or fragmented or produced what it had always produced, a series of called ones who didn't know what they were and navigated it alone.

She didn't know.

But the data chip went here regardless. Whatever happened to it, the information in it was in this place, the origin place, and if the channel had the qualities Allison's research suggested it had, if the lineage on Earth was connected to the residual interface at this location in any way�

She set the data chip beside the letter.

She thought: I was here. The Vigil exists. You're not alone.

She thought: find it.

She breathed.

She looked at the Hellmouth's interface one more time, the ancient dormant instability, and she understood in the looking that what she'd been carrying toward this moment was not grief exactly and not guilt exactly but something that was its own category, the specific weight of a thing left unfinished.

It was finished now.

Not the work, which was never finished while the work was ongoing. This. This specific interrupted thing. She'd come back to where the interruption happened and she'd looked at it directly and she'd said what needed saying and she'd brought what the place needed.

The door she'd been not-looking at was now a door she'd walked through.

She was ready to go back.

She oriented toward the channel and toward the presences, whose alignment she could feel from here, still holding, the window still open, the eighty hours not yet expired.

She thought about Scotty saying I'll be here.

She thought about Vigil in the Sphinx canopy.

She thought about the capital city's morning light on the harbor water.

She thought: home.

Not this place, which had been home and was now the origin.

The other place. The one she'd built.

She activated the channel at full opening and directed it toward the alignment and said, to the Keeper and to the presences and to the opening:

Now.

The transit home was faster than the transit out.

Not instantaneous. But faster, the sense of direction being clearer in one sense than the other, the way water flowed faster downhill. She moved through the channel with the lineage running around her and the Keeper present and the Girl present and the ancient darkness becoming the specific quality of the research facility's air�

And she was there.

She was back.

Standing in the same position she'd been standing in when she'd left, in Haverstock's facility, with the mapping array running and the instruments recording and everyone who had been there when she'd gone still there, because the physics of the window meant that what felt like hours to her had been a specific and measured interval from their side.

Four hours.

She'd been gone four hours.

The room was exactly as she'd left it.

She stood in it and breathed the Manticore air and felt the channel closing back to its ambient state and felt the presences releasing their alignment and felt the Keeper settle back into its accompanying rather than with-her quality.

She was back.

She looked at the room.

She looked at Scotty.

His face did the thing.

He said: "Yes." He said it in the specific voice of someone receiving the thing they'd been holding space for. "Yes. You are."

She looked at the room.

Everyone who had been there when she'd left was still there. The same faces, the same people, the same specific collection that was the Vigil and her life and the work, all of it present and immediate and real.

Nimitz on Honor's shoulder made the sound.

She looked at him.

She thought: Vigil.

The bond was there.

The bond's quality was the quality of something that had never considered not waiting.

She thought: yes.

Buffy Summers. The Slayer. The Vigil's founder. The person who had been displaced two thousand years and had built something in the displacement that mattered.

Home.

She thought: yes.

Chapter 58: Spring came.

The document she'd been writing was finished in the spring of her thirty-first year.

Not finished in the sense of complete. No document was complete that was also living. But finished in the sense that the record she'd set out to build was built, the full arc of it from the beginning to the going and the coming back and the landing, the thing that the people who came after would need to understand what had happened in this generation.

She read it from the beginning.

It took two days.

She read it the way she read things she wanted to understand rather than the way she read things she already knew, which meant she encountered it slightly differently than she'd encountered it in the writing, the completed thing having a different quality than the thing in process.

What she found when she read it was this:

It was a Watcher's journal. Carson had been right.

Not the Council's version, not the institutional document designed to serve the institution's interests. The other version. The one that was written for the people in it rather than about them. The one that said: this happened and here is what it meant and here is what I learned and here is what I wish I'd known and here is what you need to know when you're in the middle of your own version of this.

She read it and thought: yes. This is the right thing.

She told Carson.

He came to the capital and they sat in the archive room and she gave him the document and he read for four hours while she worked on other things and when he was done he was quiet for a long time.

He said: "It's the best record we have."

He said: "The archive documents what happened. This explains why it mattered." He held her gaze. "That's different. And necessary."

She held his gaze.

She said: "Integrate it."

He said: "Yes."

He held her gaze.

He held her gaze for a long moment.

She thought about all the accounts that were going to go into the archive over the next decades, the full distributed picture that no one person could provide and that required many people writing many versions of the same event from their own positions. She thought: they'll know what we were doing and why.

She thought: they'll have what we didn't have. The full picture.

She thought: that was the point.

She looked at the archive room, at the accumulated record of four years of work and thirty years before that and eleven hundred years before that and two thousand years before that, all of it converging in this room in this specific form.

She thought: we kept it. We kept it and we're passing it forward.

She thought: forward.

Always forward.

Chapter 59: She was thirty-one when she wrote the last entry in the document.

She wrote it in the Sphinx kitchen on a morning in late spring, with the garden outside the window doing what established gardens did in late spring, which was the full expression of what they'd been building toward through the winter and the early spring, everything that had been holding itself in reserve now present all at once.

She wrote:

This is the last entry I'm going to write in this form. Not the last thing I'll write � the record continues and I'll continue contributing to it for as long as I'm here to contribute. But this specific document, the one that started as notes on what I'd received from the record and became the account of what happened in this generation, is complete.

I want to say a few things to whoever reads this.

First: you are not alone. Whatever you're carrying, whatever you're navigating, whatever the specific version of this work looks like in your time � you are part of something that has been held by many people across a very long time, and those people held it because they believed it mattered and because they couldn't not hold it. You are in that tradition. The tradition is in you whether you know it or not. Now you know it.

Second: the work is large. Larger than any one person or any one generation. This is not a warning. It is the condition of the work, and the condition is what makes it possible to give everything you have to the part that is yours without requiring yourself to carry the whole of it. Your part is yours. Someone before you held the part before yours. Someone after you will hold the part after yours. Trust the distribution. It is what makes the long work possible.

Third: the Slayer is not the Vigil and the Vigil is not the Slayer. The Slayer is what the lineage produces when the channel opens fully in a specific person. The Vigil is what the lineage produces when it is connected and supported and given the infrastructure it needs to be what it is. Both are necessary. Neither is sufficient alone. If you are reading this and you are a fully expressed Slayer, you need the Vigil as much as the Vigil needs you. If you are reading this and you are a subclinical carrier doing the research or the diplomacy or the archival work or any of the other forms the Vigil takes, you are as essential as the Slayer. Remember that.

Fourth: be afraid of the right things and not the wrong things. The right fears are the ones that orient you toward caution and preparation. The wrong fears are the ones that tell you what you've built is not real or that the people in it cannot carry it without you. I spent time with both kinds of fears before I learned to tell them apart. The telling apart is the work.

Fifth: the Girl is always there. The channel carries her. She is the lineage's accumulated consciousness and she is in every person who carries the modification, fully expressed or subclinical, and when you feel something at the edge of perception that you cannot fully articulate, that is her, and she is trying to tell you something, and it is worth listening to.

Sixth: love the people in your life. Not instead of the work. Alongside it. The work and the life are not in competition. The one makes the other possible and the other makes the one sustainable. I learned this slowly and imperfectly and I am still learning it and it is the truest thing I know about how to do this.

Seventh and last: the door is not always closed. What seems like an ending is sometimes an interrupted thing waiting to be completed. Look at the doors you've been not-looking at. They usually lead somewhere important. The looking is the first step.

She stopped writing.

She read what she'd written.

She thought: yes. That's what I wanted to say.

She thought: that's the thing I would have needed to read.

She closed the document.

She looked out the window at the garden.

The rosemary equivalent was in its fifth spring and it was doing what things in their fifth spring did, which was to be entirely itself, no longer tentative, no longer finding its ground, simply present in the ground it had found, and the smell of it came through the window on the morning air and it was not her mother's rosemary and it was the rosemary she had here and it was good.

Scotty was somewhere in the house, the ambient quality of him present the way the Keeper's quality was present, the specific warmth of knowing someone was there.

Vigil was in the canopy.

She felt the bond.

She thought: thirty-one.

She thought: here.

She got up from the table.

She went to the door and out into the garden and the late spring morning was doing what it was doing and she walked into it and it held her in the specific way of mornings in places you have lived long enough to know.

The garden was around her. The trees were above her. The Sphinx air was the Sphinx air. The bond was the bond. The Keeper was present. The work was ahead.

She was here.

She was completely, entirely, specifically here.

She thought: this is what after looks like.

Not a single moment of arrival that announced itself. Not a destination. The ongoing specific quality of a life that was happening, that was in its middle rather than its ending, that had the work and the people and the wrong stars that were hers.

She thought about all the versions of herself that had existed before this one. The fifteen-year-old in Hemery who hadn't known yet. The seventeen-year-old in Sunnydale who had known too much too fast. The seventeen-year-old on the Tepes who had been something the Slayer reduced to its most ancient form. The girl who had come back to herself in the jungle and the girl who had walked into Manticore for the first time and the girl who had sat across from the Queen at the harbor railing and the girl who had stood in the Hellmouth's underground space and set a letter on the stone floor.

All of them were her. All of them had been building toward this, the specific version of herself that was standing in the established garden of a property she'd built on a planet that had been new to her and was now home.

She thought about the record and what she'd written and the seven things and the document that was going to be in the archive and would be read by people she'd never meet in a future she couldn't picture.

She thought: they'll have this. They'll know what we were doing.

She thought: forward. Always forward.

And then she stopped thinking about the future and was simply present in the morning, the late spring morning of her thirty-first year on a planet she hadn't known existed when she was seventeen, with a treecat in the canopy and a garden established and the work ahead and the life here.

She breathed.

She let it be what it was.

It was very good.
