Epilogue

She was thirty-nine when the second window opened.

Not exactly thirty-nine. Thirty-nine years and four months, which was close enough that she thought of it as thirty-nine. The calculation had proved approximately correct: eleven years from the first window, the presences' alignment producing the second opportunity to reach the Hellmouth's dormant interface.

This time she did not go alone.

Yeva went with her.

Not because she needed Yeva operationally. She'd been through the transit once and knew what it was. But the Vigil was eight years further along and Yeva was its operational lead and the intelligence value of two people with two perspectives at the other end was double the intelligence value of one, and more than that, the second visit to Old Earth should have more than one set of eyes and one set of hands.

She went with Yeva.

The transit was the same and different. The same in its mechanics, the channel at full activation, the presences in their alignment, the Keeper present. Different in the quality of the passage, the Girl's presence not the directed urgency of the first transit but something more settled, the consciousness that had been home once and knew the way.

They arrived underground in the dark and turned on their lights.

The space was the same.

She'd wondered if it would be the same. Eight years was nothing on the geological timescale but it was enough for things to change in human terms. The space was the same. The letter she'd left was gone, which was expected, decomposed into whatever eight years had done to it. The data chip was still there, which was not expected, the specific material of it having proved more durable than she'd assumed.

She picked it up.

She said to Yeva: "We left this the first time."

Yeva looked at it. "And it's still here."

She said: "Which means either no one found it or someone found it and put it back."

Yeva said: "Or someone found it and couldn't take it and put it back."

She held the data chip.

She said: "Someone found it."

Not certainty. The Slayer sense wasn't giving her certainty. But something in the quality of the space, the interface reading differently than it had the first time, a specific way in which the dormant instability had been�touched was the word she arrived at, the sense of something having interacted with the interface at this location since her last visit.

She said: "Someone with the channel."

Yeva looked at her.

She said: "Someone came here. Someone who could feel the interface at this location. Someone who found the chip and couldn't interpret it and put it back." She held the chip. "Someone was here."

The lineage on Earth.

She said: "We leave more this time. We leave something that doesn't require technology to receive."

Yeva said: "What does that look like?"

She said: "Writing. Physical." She looked at Yeva. "Did you bring the�"

Yeva was already reaching into her kit. She'd thought of it before the transit, which was Yeva, who thought of the things you needed before you knew you needed them.

She handed Buffy the materials.

Buffy wrote.

Not the full record. The compressed version, the essential things, in the clearest language she could produce. She wrote on the material Yeva had brought and she wrote: the Vigil exists. You are not alone. The lineage is connected across many worlds. If you feel the thing you cannot name in the dark, it is the Girl, and she is real, and the lineage is real, and we are looking for you. We will come back. Leave a mark if you can.

She left it beside the data chip.

She stood up.

She looked at the Hellmouth's interface.

She said, to the space: "We're coming back. Keep the faith."

She thought about Thomas Clinkscales saying those words across four generations, the specific faith kept without confirmation.

She thought about what it would mean to whoever read what she'd written to know that somewhere out there the faith was being kept in the other direction.

She thought: they'll know.

She turned to Yeva.

She said: "Ready?"

Yeva said: "Ready."

They activated the channel and the presences held the window and they came home.

~~~

She was fifty-one when the Vigil made first contact with the Old Earth lineage.

Not through the window. Not through the portal. Through the data chip, which had been found and which had taken twenty-one years to produce a response in a form that could travel through channels she hadn't anticipated, a specific piece of intelligence that arrived in Usher's apparatus through a chain of intermediaries so long and so unlikely that the confirmation took three months to verify.

But verified.

Someone on Old Earth had found the chip. Someone on Old Earth had spent years developing the capacity to respond. Someone on Old Earth had chosen to reach across whatever infrastructure they'd developed to say: we're here.

She was in the Sphinx kitchen when Carson came to tell her, which was where she was often at seven in the morning, and he came in with the expression she recognized from Hades, the expression of someone who had been right about something for thirty years and was standing in the presence of that rightness fully confirmed.

She read the confirmation three times.

She looked at Carson.

He said: "We found them."

She said: "They found us."

He held her gaze.

She said: "It took thirty years."

He said: "The Clinkscales family kept faith for four generations."

She held his gaze.

She said: "Set up the communication framework. Whatever they can receive and send, we match." She held his gaze. "I want to talk to them."

He said: "Yes."

She said: "And Carson." She held his gaze. "Write this down."

He said: "I'm already writing."

She looked at him, at the man who had been the Vigil's keeper of the record since before it was the Vigil, who had been doing what his family had done for four generations and was now watching the thing the four generations had kept faith with bear fruit in ways that Thomas Clinkscales had spent his life hoping for and had not quite seen and which his grandson was seeing now.

She thought about Thomas. He'd been gone for three years. She thought about him with the warmth of someone who had known and loved someone and had the knowledge that the loving had been real and the knowing had been real and the absence was real and none of those three things displaced the others.

She thought: he would have been glad.

She thought: I'll be glad for both of us.

She said: "Call the full Vigil. Everyone. This is the kind of news that's told in person."

Carson said: "I'll call."

She stood up from the table.

She looked out the window at the Sphinx garden, at the established garden, the thing that had been growing for nine years and was doing what things did when they had enough time and enough care and enough ground to grow in.

She thought about the lineage on Old Earth, carrying the modification and the dreams and the Girl's ancient presence without the vocabulary for any of it, doing what the lineage had always done, which was continuing.

She thought about what she was going to say to them.

She thought: you're not alone.

She thought: we've been looking for you.

She thought: the door was never closed. We just needed time to find each other.

She went to tell the Vigil.

She went, and the morning was the morning and the garden was the garden and Vigil was in the canopy and the bond was the bond, and she was here, fifty-one, alive and building and still going forward, and the lineage was connected, and the work was ongoing, and it was good.

It was very, very good.

And she was here.

She was always here.

Forward.

Always, always forward.

Arc Five: Old Earth

Chapter 60: The communication framework took four months to establish.

Not because the technical challenges were insurmountable. Because the social challenges were. The people on Old Earth who had made the contact were careful in the specific way of people who had been operating without external support for a very long time and had developed the caution that came with that, the specific wariness of having been alone long enough that contact with something claiming to be on their side required verification rather than automatic trust.

She respected this entirely.

She didn't push the pace. She let the framework build at the pace the other end was comfortable with, which was slow and incremental and which produced, over four months, something that was reliable enough to sustain a real conversation.

The first real conversation happened on a Thursday.

She was in the Vigil's working space with Carson beside her and the communication link open to a relay that eventually reached a system she wasn't going to specify even in the document and from there to a receiver on Old Earth that she also wasn't going to specify, and on the other end was a woman named�

She was going to call her the woman, in the record. For reasons that were both practical and that the woman had specifically requested.

The woman was sixty-two.

She had been doing what the woman did for forty years without a name for it, which was to say she had been the Earth lineage's equivalent of what Buffy had been in Sunnydale before she'd known she was the Slayer, except that on Old Earth there had been no Watcher arriving to give the name and the context and the specific terrible gift of knowing what you were.

Forty years.

She thought about thirty and fifty-one and what she'd built and what this woman had been doing in the same forty years without any of it.

She said, to the link: "Thank you for reaching out."

The woman's voice was the voice of someone who had been managing something alone for a long time and was deciding to speak. It came through the relay with the slight delay of the communication chain and the slight compression of the technology, but it was clear enough.

She said: "The chip was in the cave for years before I found it. I didn't know what it was for a long time." A pause. "I knew the cave. I'd been going there since I was a girl." Another pause. "Something there."

Buffy said: "The interface."

The woman said: "I didn't have a word for it. I still don't entirely." A pause. "Your chip had words."

Buffy said: "I know. I wrote them for people who didn't have words yet."

The woman was quiet for a moment.

She said: "There are others here. Who have�the thing. What I have."

Buffy said: "I know. The lineage has been present on Earth for as long as it's been anywhere. Longer." She paused. "We've been looking for you."

The woman said: "How long?"

Buffy said: "In this specific form? Twenty years." She paused. "In the larger sense � longer than that. The looking was built into the work from the beginning."

The woman said: "The Vigil."

Buffy said: "Yes."

The woman was quiet for a longer moment.

She said: "The things in your chip. The record. It describes what we are."

Buffy said: "It describes what we know so far. The record is ongoing."

The woman said: "You describe the lineage's origin. As being here. On Earth."

Buffy said: "Yes."

The woman said: "There are stories here. Very old ones. About the first one. The first of what we are."

Buffy held the link.

She said: "Tell me."

The woman told her.

The stories were old in the way that oral traditions were old, carried through the lineage's subclinical carriers over two thousand years without the institutional framework that would have maintained them as doctrine, and therefore transformed in the way that oral traditions transformed, the specific details changed and the underlying truth intact.

The story of the first Slayer was there.

Not with that name. With a different name in a different language that the woman translated as something close to the First One Who Ran Toward Rather Than Away, which was accurate in the way that translations of very old things were accurate, capturing the essence rather than the surface.

The story of the Hellmouth was there. Called differently. The story of the place where the boundary between what was real and what was waiting to be real was thinnest, where the things that wanted the world had been held and defeated and held again.

The story of the Slayer line was there, compressed into the lineage's oral tradition, the specific knowledge that each generation produced someone who was more than the others, who could do what the others could not, who ran toward rather than away.

She listened to all of it.

She thought about the Clinkscales archive and about Thomas sitting a boy down and telling him what the family kept and about four generations of faith and about what two thousand years of oral tradition passing through the lineage's subclinical carriers without institutional support had produced.

It had produced truth.

Not the same truth as the archive. A different angle on the same truth. And together, the archive and the oral tradition, they produced something more complete than either alone.

She said: "I want to write this down. With your permission."

The woman said: "Write it."

She said: "And I want to know more. Everything you have. The full oral tradition, in as much detail as you can give it."

The woman said: "That will take a long time."

Buffy said: "I have time."

The woman was quiet for a moment.

She said: "You sound like someone who has been doing this for a long time."

Buffy said: "Thirty-three years. Counting from when I was called."

The woman said: "Called."

Buffy said: "When the channel opened fully. When I became the fully expressed version." She paused. "You have the word for it in your tradition."

A pause. Then the woman said a word in the language of her tradition.

Buffy held it.

She said: "Yes. That."

The woman said: "I am not that. Not the fully expressed."

Buffy said: "I know. But you found the chip. You built the communication. You've been doing this for forty years." She paused. "The work doesn't only belong to the fully expressed. I spent a long time building the infrastructure to make that point formally. You knew it informally before I said it."

The woman was quiet.

She said: "There is someone here. Young. Who may be what you are."

Buffy held the link.

She said: "Tell me."

The woman told her.

Seventeen. A girl in a city that had been built over what had been the central California coast in the time before the orbital period, the centuries of environmental disruption and managed recovery and eventual reconstruction into something different from what Buffy remembered and the same in its underlying geography.

Seventeen and fast and strong and dreaming.

She thought about herself at seventeen. She thought about Yeva at sixteen. She thought about the fourteen-year-old on Grayson who had asked what the good part was.

She said: "Does she know what she is?"

The woman said: "She knows the tradition. We teach the tradition to all of them. She knows she is one of the carriers." A pause. "She does not know yet that she is�more."

Buffy said: "Is she all right?"

The woman said: "She is seventeen."

Buffy said: "Yes." She paused. "That's an answer."

The woman said: "She needs�" she stopped. "What you needed, I think. What your record says you needed."

Buffy said: "Someone to tell her what she is. Before she figures it out alone." She paused. "Before the alone costs too much."

The woman said: "Yes."

Buffy said: "We'll get information to her. Through you. The record, the biology, the practical knowledge." She paused. "And when the next window opens�" she held the link. "We're going to come. In person. Not through the channel. Actually there."

The woman was quiet for a moment.

She said: "When is the next window?"

Buffy said: "Three years."

The woman said: "Three years."

Buffy said: "We'll be ready."

The woman said: "So will we."

She held the link.

She thought about three years and what three years would contain and who would be there in three years and what it was going to mean to stand in the Hellmouth's underground space with someone from the other side of the connection, the two ends of the thread finally in the same room.

She thought: thirty-three years from being called.

She thought: this is what it was building toward.

Not this specifically. This wasn't the end of the building. But this � the contact, the connection, the thread completing a circuit that had been open for two thousand years � this was the thing that made the building visible in a way it hadn't been before.

She said: "I'll speak to you again next week. There's more I want to ask and more I want to tell you."

The woman said: "I will be here."

She said: "Good." She paused. "One more thing."

The woman waited.

She said: "The seventeen-year-old. When you tell her what she is � tell her this first. Tell her she's not broken. There's a name for what she is. And she gets to decide what she does with it."

A pause.

The woman said: "Those are your words. From the chip."

She said: "Yes."

The woman said: "I'll tell her."

She said: "Good."

The link closed.

She sat in the quiet of the working space for a moment.

Carson, beside her, said nothing. He'd been there for the full conversation and he was doing what he did when he'd received something significant, which was hold it in the specific Clinkscales way before speaking about it.

She looked at him.

She said: "The oral tradition."

He said: "I heard."

She said: "Two thousand years of the lineage on Earth keeping the story alive without the archive. Without the institutional framework. Without any of it." She held his gaze. "They had the truth anyway."

He said: "The Girl doesn't forget." He held her gaze. "What's in the channel doesn't require an institution to persist. It persists because it's the lineage's nature to persist."

She held his gaze.

She thought about what Allison had found, the channel as the vehicle for the accumulated lineage consciousness, the Girl always there.

She thought about the oral tradition as the lineage's own record-keeping, the specific way the biology's channel had expressed itself through the stories people told rather than through institutional documentation.

She said: "We've been keeping the record in the archive. They've been keeping it in the telling. Both are real."

He said: "Both are in the Vigil now."

She said: "Yes."

She looked at the communication equipment.

She thought about the woman on Old Earth who was sixty-two and had been doing this for forty years and who had found the chip and spent years developing the means to respond.

She thought: I want to know her name.

She thought: I'll ask. When the time is right. She'll tell me when she's ready.

She stood up.

She went to find the rest of the Vigil and tell them what had been said.

Chapter 61: Three years.

She spent them the way she spent all the years now, which was fully, in the work and the life and the people, the texture of the ongoing thing rather than the waiting for the significant thing.

The Vigil at fifty-one was the Vigil that she could look at and recognize as the thing she'd been building toward in the early conversations with the Queen and the late nights in the first year's residence and all the iterations between. Not finished. Still becoming. But recognizably itself, the character of it established in the way that characters established themselves through being tested enough times that the testing confirmed rather than changed.

Yeva was the operational leader in full now, which meant Buffy's operational role was advisory rather than primary, which she'd thought was going to feel like loss and which felt instead like the specific satisfaction of something you'd built finally running on its own momentum.

She still took the highest-complexity operations. Not because she couldn't delegate them but because the highest-complexity operations required the most complete synthesis of the Slayer sense and the channel and the lineage knowledge and her thirty-three years of doing this, and that synthesis was still specific to her in ways that Yeva's was not yet. The yet was still honest. Yeva's ceiling continued to not be visible.

She advised on the rest.

The advising was different from the leading in a way she hadn't fully anticipated. Leading required the full engagement of everything she was at the level of execution. Advising required the full engagement of everything she was at the level of understanding, which was a different skill and one she was finding she was well suited to, the accumulated knowledge of what things looked like from the inside making her a particular kind of useful to the people inside them.

She talked to the woman on Old Earth every week.

The woman's name, when she shared it, was Adisa. She shared it in the fourth month, which was the right timeline, the point at which the communication had built enough that the name was an extension rather than an exposure.

Adisa was sixty-two when they'd first spoken and was sixty-five when the third window approached, and in those three years Buffy had learned Adisa the way she'd learned the people who became important in her life, through the accumulated specific weight of many conversations about many things.

Adisa was brilliant.

Not in the way of credentials or formal training, though she had both. In the way of someone who had been thinking about a specific set of problems for forty years with the intelligence and care of someone who had no choice but to be thorough because there had been no one else to be thorough for her.

The oral tradition she'd described in the first conversation was the surface layer. What the conversations over three years had revealed was a theoretical framework she'd developed for the lineage's nature that was, in some ways, more advanced than what Allison and Fritz had developed from the biology, because Adisa had been working from the inside of the experience rather than from the outside of the data.

Allison, when Buffy had begun summarizing the conversations, had said: "I need to talk to her directly."

She'd arranged it.

The first conversation between Allison and Adisa had lasted six hours and produced, by the end, the specific quality of two people who had arrived at the same place from completely different directions and were discovering that the convergence was real.

They'd been in regular communication since.

The seventeen-year-old the woman had mentioned � the one who might be fully expressed � had been told what she was.

Not by Buffy directly. By Adisa, using the words from the chip and the years of conversations with Buffy as the framework. The telling had gone, Adisa reported, the way tellings went when they arrived at the right time to a person who had been ready for them, which was with the specific quality of something clicking into place rather than something foreign being installed.

The girl had said: I knew something was different.

Adisa had said: yes.

The girl had said: is it always going to be this much?

Adisa had said: no. It gets different. The amount changes. You learn to hold it.

The girl had said: how do you learn?

Adisa had said: you talk to people who already have.

The girl had said: are there people who already have?

Adisa had said: yes. Many. On many worlds. And you're going to meet them.

She'd reported this conversation to Buffy and Buffy had read the report and thought: yes.

She thought: that's the thing.

She thought: you're going to meet them.

The third window opened in October.

She went with Yeva and Eris and two others.

Not for an operation. For the meeting.

~~~

Old Earth from the surface was something she hadn't anticipated.

She'd thought she'd known what to expect. She'd read everything that had been compiled about Old Earth's current state � the environmental history, the two millennia of human civilization building and adapting and rebuilding on the planet that had originated them all. She'd thought the reading would prepare her for the looking.

It hadn't.

The surface of Old Earth was not Sunnydale and it was not the Earth she'd read about in history and it was not the Earth of the stories and it was entirely, specifically itself, a planet that had been through more than most planets and was still here, still inhabited, still the specific place where humanity had begun even if almost nothing she recognized remained of the specific version of it she'd known.

She stood outside the cave entrance, which was recognizable only through the Slayer sense and the data from the previous visits, and she looked at what was around her.

The California coast. Changed beyond recognition in terms of its specific features and entirely itself in terms of its fundamental character, the light and the angle of the land and the quality of the air in October, which was wrong in the ways of two thousand years of climate change and managed recovery and was still October light in California in a way she felt in the specific part of her that was still from here.

Adisa was there.

And the girl, who was seventeen and whose name was Mara and who looked at Buffy with the expression that Buffy had seen on the faces of seventeen-year-olds who were newly told what they were, which was a specific combination of recognition and overwhelm and the specific alertness of someone whose understanding of what they were had just dramatically expanded.

Mara looked at Buffy and said: "You're the one from the chip."

Buffy said: "Yes."

Mara said: "You're from here originally."

Buffy said: "Yes."

Mara looked at the landscape around them.

She said: "What was it like? When you were here?"

She thought about Sunnydale and the high school and the library and Giles and Willow and Xander and her mother and the specific quality of that time, the weight and the grief of it and the love and the work.

She said: "It was a lot. I was seventeen and it was the whole world and it was very large." She paused. "It turned out the world was larger than I knew. But that was the world I knew at the time."

Mara said: "Do you miss it?"

She held the question.

She said: "I miss the people." She paused. "The place is � present. In me. The origin thing doesn't go away. But what I miss are the people and they're not here anymore in the sense of being reachable, so the missing is what it is." She paused. "I built other people. The missing didn't prevent the building."

Mara looked at her.

She said: "That's what Adisa says. That the loss doesn't prevent the building."

She said: "Adisa is right."

Adisa, beside them, had the specific quality she'd come to know through three years of conversations made fully visible for the first time in person. Sixty-five and present in the way of someone who had been doing the long work for forty years without support and had found the support and was now in the specific quality of a person receiving confirmation that what they'd built alone was real.

She looked at Adisa.

She said: "I owe you an apology."

Adisa looked at her.

She said: "The chip arrived thirty years ago. The response took twenty years to reach us. That's fifty years you spent doing this without the connection that the Vigil was built to provide." She held Adisa's gaze. "We should have found you sooner."

Adisa said: "The window physics�"

She said: "Yes. I know the window physics. I'm not talking about the windows. I'm talking about the forty years before the first window. The framework should have been in place before the first transit." She held Adisa's gaze. "It wasn't and I know why it wasn't and it was still a failure of what the Vigil was built to do. I wanted to say that directly."

Adisa was quiet for a moment.

She said: "You were building it while I was here. You didn't know I was here."

She said: "I know. The not-knowing doesn't change the fact of the gap." She held Adisa's gaze. "What you built here in the gap � the oral tradition, the framework, the communication network � is real and I'm not minimizing what you did without us. I'm saying you shouldn't have had to do it without us." She paused. "We're here now."

Adisa held her gaze.

She said: "Yes." She paused. "You are."

They held each other's gaze in the October light of the California coast on Old Earth and Mara stood between them with the alertness of someone who was taking in more than she could fully process and who was going to be processing it for years.

She looked at Mara.

She said: "You're going to come to Manticore."

Mara looked at her.

She said: "Not immediately. When you're ready. There are people there who need to meet you and things you need to learn that are better learned in the place where the Vigil exists." She paused. "And there are people there who are your age who are doing the same work. You should know them."

Mara said: "What are they like?"

She thought about the current generation of fully expressed candidates, the ones who had come through the Vigil's training in the past several years, who were older than seventeen and younger than thirty and who were the future of the work in ways she was still discovering.

She said: "They're remarkable. Each of them in their own specific way." She held Mara's gaze. "You'll fit." She paused. "Differently than any of them. You always fit differently than any of them. That's the nature of this." She held her gaze. "But you'll fit."

Mara looked at her for a long moment.

She said: "How do you know?"

She said: "Because you found the cave. Because you've been doing this for two years without anyone telling you what it was. Because when Adisa told you what you were, you recognized it." She held the girl's gaze. "The ones who fit recognize it. The ones who don't, don't recognize it." She paused. "You recognized it."

Mara held her gaze.

She said: "Okay."

She said: "Yes."

Yeva, who had been present and quiet through all of this, said to Mara: "I want to hear about what you can do. Your specific version of it." She held Mara's gaze with the direct quality that was specifically Yeva's. "No one's version is the same. I want to know yours."

Mara looked at Yeva.

She looked at Buffy.

She looked back at Yeva.

She said: "Okay."

She started talking.

Buffy listened to her talk and thought about seventeen and what it was and what it had been and what it was now, the specific quality of a generation that had been born into the world the Vigil had been building and who didn't know what it had been like before the building and who were going to do things with what they had that she couldn't predict.

She thought: good.

She thought: that's the shape of it.

She thought about the work and the document and the seven things she'd written and the data chip on the cave floor and the letter she'd left and the woman who had found them and was now here, in this light, in this specific October.

She thought about coming back to here and what here had always been and what it was now, not the home of her memory but the origin of her and everything that had come from her.

She stood in the October light of Old Earth and let it be what it was.

The Keeper was very present.

The Girl was present.

The lineage was running through her and through Mara and through Adisa and through the channel and through the oral tradition and through the archive and through every version of the knowledge that had been kept and was being kept and would be kept.

She thought: always forward.

She thought: this is what always forward looks like.

The California light did what it always did, the specific quality of it in October, and she was here, fifty-four years after she'd been born in a version of this place that no longer existed, and she was here, and it was good.

It was very good.

Chapter 62: She was sixty when she gave the document to Mara.

Not the full archive. The document, her document, the one she'd started in the working space on Manticore and finished in the Sphinx kitchen and which had been in the archive for twenty-nine years.

Mara was thirty-five.

She'd come to Manticore at eighteen, which had been a year after the third window meeting, and she'd done what she'd done with everything, which was to take it in completely and then do with it what could only be done with it from the inside, which was to become what she was in the context of the people who already were what they were.

She was thirty-five now and the Vigil was her home in the same way it was Yeva's home and in the same way it was Buffy's home, the place that held the work and the people and the meaning.

They were in the Sphinx kitchen.

Mara had been to Sphinx many times. She'd been coming to Sphinx since she was twenty, the first time Buffy had brought her to see the garden, and she'd taken to the planet with the specific recognition of someone who was encountering something they hadn't known they'd been looking for.

She had opinions about the garden.

Strong ones.

She and Sascha had developed a relationship around the garden that Buffy found both gratifying and slightly intimidating, the two of them agreeing about things and disagreeing about things with the mutual respect of people who took the same subject seriously from different positions.

The garden in its twenty-fourth year was something that Buffy looked at with the specific feeling that established things produced, the feeling of something that had found its full form, the accumulated weight of two decades of growing.

She sat across from Mara in the kitchen with the garden outside the window.

She put the document on the table.

Mara looked at it.

She said: "I know what this is."

Buffy said: "Yes."

Mara said: "You're giving it to me."

Buffy said: "I'm giving it to the Vigil. I'm giving it to you to carry."

Mara held her gaze.

She said: "Why me specifically."

Buffy said: "Because you know both ends of the thread." She held Mara's gaze. "You're from Old Earth and you've been in the Vigil for seventeen years and you carry both of those things simultaneously in a way no one else currently does." She paused. "The record needs someone who can see it from both sides. The origin and the work." She paused. "That's you."

Mara held her gaze for a long moment.

She said: "You're not done."

Buffy said: "No. But you're the next carrier. The document doesn't belong to one person. It passes forward." She held Mara's gaze. "I'm passing it."

Mara looked at the document.

She said: "Adisa should have it too."

Buffy said: "Adisa has a copy. The archive has a copy. Mara�" she held her gaze� "this is the original. The carrier version. The one that continues to be written." She paused. "Add to it. What happens in your time. What you know that I don't know. The thread continues."

Mara looked at the document.

She put her hand on it.

She said: "I'll carry it."

Buffy said: "I know."

She said it with the specific quality of something settled. Not resigned, not elegiac. The specific quality of someone who had built what they'd built and was now watching the building continue without requiring her hand on every part of it.

She thought about Thomas Clinkscales who had kept the record for thirty years and handed it to his grandson. She thought about the first Clinkscales entry and the sitting-down of a boy to tell him what the family kept.

She thought about the document and the seven things and all of it in Mara's hands now alongside her own.

She thought: forward.

She thought: yes.

Vigil was in the canopy.

She felt the bond, warm and specific and present, the specific quality of it after twenty-four years of being what it was between them.

She looked out at the garden.

She thought about sixty and what it looked like and what it felt like and what the distance was between here and the jungle on Hades where she'd come back to herself in the mud with Honor's voice reaching her.

The distance was very large.

She was not the same person.

She was recognizably the same person.

Both of those were true and she held them without needing to resolve the contradiction.

Mara said: "What do you need? From me. Now."

She looked at her.

She said: "Keep writing." She held Mara's gaze. "Don't let the document close. It's not finished. It's never finished while the work is ongoing." She paused. "Add to it. Your version. What you know. What you learn." She held her gaze. "And when the time comes, find the next person to carry it and hand it forward."

Mara said: "How do I know who the next person is?"

She thought about it.

She said: "You'll know. The same way I knew it was you." She held her gaze. "They'll fit. Differently than anyone else. But they'll fit."

Mara held her gaze.

She said: "Okay."

She said: "Yes."

She looked at the garden.

She thought about what was ahead. The sixty years that had been and the years that were still coming, the work that was still ongoing and the things that were still building and the long work in its geological patience moving toward whatever it was moving toward in the centuries ahead.

She thought: I'll be here for some of it.

She thought: they'll be here for the rest.

She thought: that's the shape of the Vigil.

That's what it was always supposed to be.

She breathed the Sphinx air and the garden was outside and Vigil was in the canopy and the kitchen held them both, Mara with the document in her hands and Buffy across from her, and this was the passing of something forward that had been passed forward before and would be passed forward again.

She thought: forward.

She thought: the thread continues.

She thought: always.

~~~

She was sixty-seven when Vigil died.

Not dramatically. Not in the way of things that were lost suddenly. In the way of things that had lived well and long and were ready, the specific quality of a very old treecat in the winter of a long life, the bond's quality changing over the months of the last year, becoming quieter and more inward, and then one morning in February in the Sphinx garden simply not there.

She was in the garden when it happened.

She'd known. Not precisely when, but the imminence of it, the way the bond had been for the past several weeks, the specific quality of something that was completing rather than continuing.

She'd been in the garden every morning.

She'd been in the garden when it went quiet.

She stood in the February garden and held the quiet.

The bond was not there.

It was not there the way a sound was not there when the room that had held it became empty, the specific shape of an absence that was defined by what had been present.

She stood in it.

She breathed.

She thought about Nimitz and what Honor had described, years ago now, about the treecats who chose and about the bond and about what the bond was.

She thought about twenty-four years of Vigil.

Twenty-four years of the garden and the canopy and the approaching and the bond's ambient quality and the specific communication of the touch and all of it, the accumulated specific weight of a relationship that didn't have a human category but was real in the way that all real things were real, present and specific and gone.

She stood in the garden for a long time.

Then she went inside.

Scotty was in the kitchen.

He'd been getting up early for months, the specific way he'd been present in the early mornings during the last year, the unspoken quality of someone who knew what was coming and was choosing to be there for it.

She came in and he looked at her face.

He said: "Yes."

She said: "Yes."

He came to her and she held on to him in the kitchen in the February morning and let the grief be what it was, the full grief of a real thing that had been present for twenty-four years and was now not present, the specific and irreplaceable quality of what had been there in the bond that would not be there again.

She held on.

He held her.

After a while she said: "Twenty-four years."

He said: "Yes."

She said: "I thought I knew what the bond was. I didn't fully know until it was gone."

He said: "You'll know it again. Treecats choose."

She said: "I know." She held him. "Not soon. Not immediately." She paused. "The grief needs time to be what it is."

He said: "As long as it needs."

She held him.

She thought about the bond and what it had been. The specific gift of it, the being fully seen and not alone in the seeing. She thought about what it had given her across twenty-four years and what it had shown her about herself, the things she wouldn't have known without the reflection.

She thought about what she'd owed Vigil, the carrots she'd thought of on the day she'd first understood what the bond was, the specific small thought she'd held.

She thought about being sixty-seven and the grief of a treecat and what grief felt like now compared to what it had felt like at seventeen in Sunnydale or twenty-one in Manticore or thirty on the portal's eve.

The grief was grief. It didn't get smaller with age, the individual griefs. What got different was the knowing that grief was a form of love and that having it was the evidence of having had the real thing.

She thought: twenty-four years of the real thing.

She thought: yes. It was worth every piece of it.

She held Scotty in the kitchen in February and the garden was outside and Vigil was not in the canopy and would not be in the canopy and the garden was still the garden and the work was still ahead.

She thought: forward.

She thought: yes.

She thought: I'm going to need some time.

She gave herself the time.

Chapter 63: Spring came again.

She was in the garden on a morning in early April when the treecat appeared at the edge of the property.

Not Vigil. A different one. She knew immediately and without needing to think about it, the Slayer sense reading the quality of the presence and finding it familiar in the register of the lineage and the channel and what treecats were, and unfamiliar in the specific individual way.

Young. Younger than Vigil had been when Vigil had first come to the property's edge.

It sat at the edge of the canopy and looked at her.

She looked back.

She was sixty-seven and she'd had the bond for twenty-four years and lost it two months ago and the specific quality of the grief was still present and would be present for a long time and was not incompatible with the looking.

She looked.

The young treecat looked.

She thought: not yet. I'm not ready yet.

She stayed where she was.

She said, quietly, to the edge of the canopy: "I see you."

The treecat looked at her.

She said: "I'm not ready yet. But I see you."

She went back to her work in the garden.

The treecat stayed at the edge for a while.

Then it went back up into the canopy.

She worked in the garden and felt the presence at the edge, not the bond, not yet, but something in the vicinity of the bond's channel-frequency, a presence that registered in the Slayer sense in the register that treecats registered.

She thought: when I'm ready.

She thought: it'll be there.

She thought: forward.

The garden was around her and the spring was doing what spring did and she was here, sixty-seven, alive and building and grieving and present, and the work was ahead and the life was here.

She was here.

She thought: still here.

She thought: yes.

She planted what needed planting and the morning moved and the spring held her in its Sphinx specific way and the treecat was somewhere in the canopy and the bond was waiting for when she was ready and the Keeper was present and the document was in Mara's hands and the archive was growing and the Vigil was doing the work and she was here.

She thought: this is what after looks like at sixty-seven.

Not the same as after at thirty or after at fifty-one.

But after.

Still, consistently, always after, and always also the beginning of the next part.

She planted and the spring came in and she was here.

Forward.

She was always, always here.

Forward.

Bonus Chapter: The Honest Trailer

A black screen. Stars emerged one at a time: unfamiliar, indifferent, and two thousand years too late.

Then the voice arrived—the one normally reserved for wars, legends, and sequels that inherited an impossible amount of unfinished business.

“In a universe where humanity has conquered the stars, perfected interstellar war, and developed several thousand new ways for governments to ruin a perfectly survivable week…”

The stars tilted. A prison planet filled the screen.

“…one seventeen-year-old girl will discover that being ripped out of Sunnydale and dropped into the Honorverse is somehow not the worst thing that happens to her.”

Buffy opened her eyes on Hades: injured, displaced, connected to something older than language, and surrounded by people who had already used up their allotted lifetime supply of desperate escapes.

“Meet Buffy Summers.”

Quick cuts: Buffy healing too fast. Buffy eating enough for a platoon. Buffy giving Honor blood. Buffy staring up at stars that were not hers.

“She’s the Slayer. She’s a temporal refugee. And according to the medical staff, she’s also where all the calories went.”

Honor Harrington watched her with the grave patience of a woman whose résumé already included naval command, political catastrophe, assassination attempts, and adopting traumatized strays with terrifying combat reflexes.

Nimitz bleeked.

“Correct,” said the narrator. “Another one.”

Watch as a teenage supernatural weapon meets a military culture that understands duty, sacrifice, and turning personal damage into an administrative structure.

“It’s Buffy the Vampire Slayer…”

Buffy drove something monstrous into the ground.

“…with orbital mechanics.”

A warship fired.

“And Honor Harrington…”

Honor made a decision that would alter several governments.

“…with vampires.”

A room full of officers went very still.

From Hades to Manticore, from Grayson to Beowulf, follow Buffy as she searches for the answer to the oldest Slayer question:

What am I when nobody is giving me something to kill?

The answer, regrettably, requires decades of institutional design.

“Because defeating a monster is easy.”

Buffy hit a monster.

“Defeating the system that keeps producing monsters requires committees.”

Buffy sat at a table with Haverstock, Prieto, Allison, Tomas, and Carson.

She looked as if she would prefer the monster.

“So thrill as the Chosen One becomes the Founding One—building the Vigil, assembling the Keeper’s Archive, tracing a lineage written through blood and memory, and discovering that ancient cosmic intelligences are perfectly happy to communicate in profound emotional concepts instead of usable sentences.”

The Keeper touched the edge of her awareness: recognition, patience, witness, continuity.

Buffy waited.

More qualities followed.

“Great. A mystical interface with no search bar.”

But this is not just a story about the Keeper, the Constant, the Seal, or the slow excavation of a hidden supernatural history among the stars.

It is the story of a girl once treated as a disposable weapon learning how to build something that will outlive the emergency.

The music softened.

Buffy stood beneath an alien sky and grieved the constellations she remembered. Honor sat beside her without trying to solve it. Nimitz offered the quiet certainty of presence. Carson became family by the ancient and highly technical process of continuing to show up.

The narrator cleared his throat.

“We will not be making fun of that part.”

A beat.

“We have standards.”

The music immediately became dramatic again.

“Instead, prepare for—”

The screen began counting.

One prison-planet escape.

Several governments that desperately need adult supervision.

Two primordial intelligences with very different approaches to personal space.

One sentient species of empathic murder-cats.

Nimitz showed his teeth.

“Affectionate empathic murder-cats.”

An entire archive assembled because nobody in human history thought to leave the Chosen One a decent operations manual.

And enough food to make a naval quartermaster whisper, ‘Dear God.’”

Starring:

Buffy Summers—as Lady Buffy of Sunnydale, because if the universe insists on giving her titles, she is at least going to make them nervous.

Honor Harrington—as the only person in the room whose life is complicated enough to make Buffy feel comparatively normal.

Nimitz—as Emotional Support Apex Predator.

Carson Clinkscales—as Found Family, Grayson Edition.

Allison Harrington—as Science, Curiosity, and Several Questions Buffy Was Hoping Nobody Would Ask.

The Keeper—as An Entire Cosmology Delivered Through Vibes.

The Constant—as Proof That Finding One Ancient Mystery Only Unlocks the Next Research Tree.

And the Vigil—as the revolutionary proposition that Slayers should receive backup before the funeral.

The screen filled with alternate titles:

Buffy the Vampire Slayer: Deep Space Nine Millennia Later.

The Long Patrol.

Lady Buffy and the Extremely Complicated Filing System.

The Chosen One Builds Human Resources.

The narrator paused.

“No.”

The alternatives vanished.

“Some titles carry more than one person’s work. Keep this one.”

The title appeared against the stars.

# HONOR’S SLAYER

Continued with love, grief, and the stubborn refusal to let a good journey end unfinished.

The screen faded—then returned on Sphinx, fifty years after Hades.

Buffy, sixty-seven, worked in her garden. The Archive was growing. The Vigil was doing the work. Somewhere in the canopy, a treecat waited without demanding that she be ready before she was ready.

“In most stories,” the narrator said quietly, “the hero saves the world and gets an ending.”

Buffy pressed another seedling into the soil.

“This one lets her have an after.”

She looked toward the trees.

Still here.

Forward.
